# US cross-source trend synthesis v1

**Checked:** 2026-09-25
**Status:** living cross-source synthesis; no single causal chain claimed  
**Scope:** the current machine-readable trend/case registry and its linked
analysis layers

## Latest workplace meaning rotation: purpose can improve firm performance while shifting who stays

The current [BLS ATUS flexibility record](records/us-bls-atus-job-flexibility-family-time-2024-2025.json)
anchors the route in U.S. official statistics. In 2024–25, 57% of
wage-and-salary workers could vary when they began and ended work, 69% said an
hour or two off for personal or family matters would not be difficult, and 9%
knew their schedule one day or less ahead. Parents with children under 18 were
more likely to work at home, while workers with less education were more likely
to receive short notice. This is access and exposure, not a causal estimate of
care adequacy or welfare: nominal flexibility can coexist with low pay, high
workload, or unpaid care transfer.

The [NBER meaning-at-work record](records/us-nber-meaning-at-work-purpose-intervention-2025.json)
adds a randomized worker-centered firm route to the workplace-control lane. In
a two-year rollout among 2,976 white-collar employees, the intervention
increased performance; low performers either improved or left; the meaning–pay
tradeoff flattened as low-meaning/high-pay workers exited; and parental-leave
behavior and bonuses changed. This is stronger than a stated firm purpose or
AI-adoption measure because it observes worker-level performance and retention
paths. It still does not show whether workers gained durable control, bargaining
power, household security, or political voice. Firm performance and worker
welfare can move together, diverge, or redistribute risk through exit.

The comparative [IZA job-flexibility record](records/comparative-iza-job-flexibility-intrahousehold-child-wellbeing-2026.json)
extends the worker-control lane into the household. In 464 Dutch couples with
children, a collective structural model links parent-specific schedule and
telecommuting flexibility to market work, childcare, chores, household
resources, and modeled child well-being. Simulations generally reduce gender
gaps in paid and domestic work, yet the child-well-being result is not uniform:
lower wages accompanying flexibility reduce household resources, and the
parent targeted matters. The comparison shows why “more flexibility” cannot be
treated as a single welfare outcome; the open U.S. test is whether control is
usable without transferring pay risk or unpaid care back into the household.

The [NBER autonomy-of-labor record](records/us-nber-autonomy-labor-risk-prevention-2022.json)
adds a more tightly identified task-control mechanism. In a randomized online
labor experiment with 476 U.S.-located workers, high autonomy increased
preventative effort by 9.7 percentage points because workers could distribute
skippable work across two task sets. The result shows that discretion can alter
risk management and later effort, not merely reported job satisfaction. It is
still a short task experiment: the open questions are earnings, schedule
control, household time, health, bargaining, and whether autonomy was usable in
the worker's broader labor market.

The next decisive workplace test should follow the same worker from purpose or
system exposure through actual task/schedule control, pay and care tradeoffs,
voice or appeal, retention/exit, and later household security. The intervention
should not be coded as universally beneficial merely because average performance
or bonuses rise.

## Latest cultural rotation: stable aggregate arts engagement can conceal a public/private shift

The [ATUS arts-engagement record](records/us-atus-arts-engagement-behavioral-phenotyping-2003-2023.json)
adds a national cultural baseline to the meaning, time, and participation
lanes. A 2026 analysis of 236,270 nationally representative American Time Use
Survey respondents covering 2003–2023 found that daily arts engagement was
stable overall, while participatory activities such as dancing, performing,
arts, crafts, and writing rose slightly and receptive activities such as
attending performing arts or museums and watching dancing declined slightly.
People also increasingly did arts activities alone at home, with differing
trends by sex, age, and income.

The usable claim is therefore about composition and setting, not cultural
collapse: an unchanged daily aggregate can contain a movement from public or
receptive settings toward private or participatory ones. The diary design does
not identify whether the shift reflects access, cost, time pressure, technology,
health, or preference, and it does not measure durable belonging, organizational
continuity, trust, or political action. The next end-to-end test should retain
activity type, setting, companionship, and subgroup position, then join them to
local cultural infrastructure, social connection, and later collective or
political behavior.

## Cultural-to-civic bridge: positive associations are real but context-sensitive

The [NEA Great Connector record](records/us-nea-great-connector-arts-civic-community-engagement-2026.json)
tests the open middle between cultural participation and civic action using
three national surveys. In the July 2022 SPPA analytic sample, arts
participation was associated with a 4.86 incidence-rate ratio for
civic/community engagement after demographic and socioeconomic controls; in
the February 2025 HTOPS analytic sample, the corresponding estimate was 1.17.
The report also finds stronger SPPA relationships for consuming art (IRR 2.77),
creating art (1.35), and multi-mode participation, with educational attainment
remaining a major predictor.

The important counterweight is the GSS Arts Module: the adjusted overall arts
participation relationship was effectively null (IRR 0.999), while creating or
performing art remained positively associated (IRR 1.17). The cross-survey
spread means the program can retain a cultural-to-civic association without
claiming a universal mechanism or causal mobilization effect. Survey frames,
measurement windows, analytic samples, education, local opportunity, prior
social networks, and selection may all matter. The next end-to-end test should
follow a named arts setting and participant exposure through repeated
participation, companionship, local institution, civic behavior, and later
political or policy feedback.

## Public engagement is plural: action, attention, community, and trust diverge

The [Pew public-life engagement record](records/us-pew-public-life-engagement-groups-2025.json)
adds a recent national map of the action endpoint without treating
“engagement” as a single ladder. Pew’s late-2025 Cross-Sectional Engagement
Survey classified adults from 19 political, civic, religious, and news
measures into Mobilizers (9%), Connectors (28%), Spectators (31%), and
Outsiders (31%). Mobilizers reported the highest political action: 65%
contacted an elected official and 77% volunteered. Connectors reported 87%
2024 voting and 65% group membership but much less direct political action.
Spectators followed national news at 84% while only 7% contacted an elected
official, and Outsiders followed news at 41% while 41% still reported voting.

The pattern is important for the broad material-to-meaning chain because it
separates attention, community participation, institutional action, and voting.
The follow-up ATP analysis also found roughly one-in-five trust in the federal
government across all four groups, while trust in national news ranged from
64% among Connectors to 45% among Outsiders. These are cross-sectional cluster
associations, not causal types or evidence that hardship produced withdrawal.
The next end-to-end test should attach a dated public-system, workplace,
consumer, or household episode to a distinct action route, then retain the
actor, attribution, response, recovery, and persistence variables.

## Read this synthesis first

## Latest household-room rotation: assistance can coexist with pressure, and the pooled pattern can reverse by context

The new [SIPP housing-transition timing audit](projects/us-census-hud-lehd-assistance-employment-trajectories/sipp-housing-transition-timing-report-v1.md)
adds a real public-use person-month backbone to the housing, work, and
household-room lanes. The streamed 2025 SIPP file retains 379,215 person-month
rows for 32,052 people in 13,910 households across all 12 months. Its key
finding is a timing boundary: the main housing-assistance fields are
reference-period or residence-spell measures, not monthly receipt histories.
The genuinely monthly `EOTHAS_MNYN` field supports a narrower same-person
descriptive screen.

That screen observes reported next-month rent/mortgage payment difficulty of
9.15% among month-t assistance-exposed pairs and 4.17% among unexposed pairs,
while job-count and poverty-band movement are close. The result is compatible
with assistance reaching people already under greater pressure; it is not an
assistance effect. The follow-up [tenure and resource counterexample audit](projects/us-census-hud-lehd-assistance-employment-trajectories/sipp-housing-assistance-work-stratified-v1.md)
shows why: among renters below twice the poverty line, exposed cells have
lower reported next-month difficulty than unexposed cells, while the direction
reverses in higher renter resource bands. Owner exposed cells are small and
imprecise.

| Evidence layer | What it adds to the cross-source map | What it does not establish |
|---|---|---|
| SIPP public person-month timing audit | A lawful same-person clock for assistance context, work, resources, and reported housing difficulty | Monthly HUD receipt, benefit amount or adequacy, application effort, landlord response, recovery, trust, or political action |
| SIPP weighted assistance/work screen | Assistance context beside a following-period household-security measure | Causation, program identity, arrears, eviction, homelessness, or verified protection |
| SIPP tenure/resource stratification | A direct counterexample to treating the pooled contrast as one universal effect | Why the direction differs, because exposure remains a combined clothing/housing-assistance field |
| HUD–LEHD and HEARS linked records | Separate linked evidence on labor trajectories and exit-type outcomes | A valid join to the SIPP person-months or one combined household story |

The cross-cutting implication is stronger than “assistance works” or
“assistance fails.” **Support and pressure can coexist, and the meaning of a
support route depends on starting resources, tenure, timing, and the form of
assistance.** This connects four atlas themes: household room, public-system
access, housing/place, and unequal exposure/status. It also sharpens the
broader subtheme of **usable help**: a program indicator is not the same as an
adequate amount, a reachable alternative, or a durable reduction in risk.

The result also reinforces a general measurement rule. A field's presence is
not enough; the atlas must preserve its clock. Reference-period assistance,
residence-spell status, monthly work, and next-month reported difficulty cannot
be silently treated as one dated benefit episode. The correct broad-program
finding is therefore a distributional and methodological one: public support
can appear alongside continuing constraint, and aggregate contrasts can hide
protective, neutral, and adverse paths in different resource contexts.

The next cross-source test is not another assistance percentage. It is a
program-specific or matched route that retains amount, adequacy, alternatives,
notice or application effort, institutional response, and a later housing or
security outcome. Until that exists, keep the SIPP bridge, HUD–LEHD trajectory,
and HEARS exit-type evidence as layered records rather than a pooled causal
claim.

## Latest cross-scale rotation: dependence is shared, but leverage is conditional

The new [dependence, control, and leverage synthesis](US-BROAD-DEPENDENCE-CONTROL-AND-LEVERAGE-SYNTHESIS_V1.md)
connects labor-market concentration, platform defaults and exit, connected-
vehicle data control, large-load infrastructure, and U.S. arms-transfer
concentration. These sources do not share a unit or causal design. Their
cross-source value is structural: each shows an actor facing a route whose
cost of refusal, switching, delay, or non-use may be unevenly distributed.

The comparison supports a bounded distinction:

```text
dependence = exposure to the cost of refusal
control = ability to shape another actor's available route
leverage = observed change in that actor's behavior, decision, or autonomy
```

The worker and platform sources show that dependence can enter political
meaning, beliefs, and behavior. The PJM and Virginia records show that
infrastructure dependence can produce operating and regulatory response before
household incidence is visible. SIPRI shows supplier concentration without
proving delivery, sustainment, recipient obedience, or deterrence. The search
experiment is a counterexample to permanent lock-in: temporary exposure to an
alternative can update quality beliefs and produce some continued preference.

The cross-cutting subtheme is **unequal replaceability**. Formal choice is not
equivalent to practical agency when alternatives require losing income, time,
information, safety, service quality, local belonging, or strategic readiness.
The next decisive evidence is therefore not another concentration or use-rate
measure; it is an attributed response, correction, substitution, refusal,
acceptance, or durable change for the affected actor.

## Latest linked housing-route rotation: assistance entry and exit have different labor clocks

The [HUD–LEHD trajectory record](records/us-census-hud-lehd-assistance-employment-trajectories-2026.json)
adds a genuine longitudinal administrative bridge. Census CES links HUD
assistance records from 2005–2022 to LEHD earnings records from 2000–2023 and
follows employment and earnings around entry and exit over 14-year windows.
Among 2.086 million first-time entrants, the earnings slope changes from -$112
per year before entry to +$174 after entry; the $286 slope break exceeds the
adjusted ACS comparison by $228 per year, while the employment slope rises 1.45
percentage points per year. Among 1.233 million final leavers, the post-exit
employment and earnings slope changes are -1.05 percentage points and -$244 per
year, respectively, relative to more stable adjusted stayers.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Linked HUD participant and LEHD employment/earnings records | 2.086 million first-time entrants and 1.233 million final leavers; longitudinal entry/exit trends and adjusted comparisons | housing-assistance transition → same linked unit's employment/earnings path → possible household-room change | causal effect, assistance amount/timing, rent burden, housing quality, alternatives, exit reason, remedy adequacy, health, trust, and action |

This is stronger than a participation total because the linked units are
followed across time, but it is not a complete household recovery episode.
Entry and exit are selected events; the next test must add housing conditions,
assistance details, work/care trade-offs, and the reason and consequences of
exit before interpreting the labor trajectory as program effect or security.

## Latest housing-exit rotation: exit type separates stability from loss of support

The [HUD HEARS exit-type record](records/us-hud-hears-exit-type-outcomes-2025.json)
adds the missing housing-side comparator. In linked data from Seattle Housing
Authority and King County Housing Authority, only 13.5% of 8,266 heads of
household had positive exits, while 31.6% had negative exits. Within one year,
homelessness or unstable housing was recorded for 3.1% of people with positive
exits, 14.5% with neutral exits, and 25.4% with negative exits. Negative exits
were also associated with about twice the odds of a behavioral-health crisis
versus neutral exits; positive exits had lower emergency-department-use odds than
negative exits.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Linked PHA exit, wage, Medicaid, behavioral-health, homelessness-service, and HMIS records | 8,266 heads of household and 16,301 linked individuals; classified exit type and one-year outcomes | assistance exit reason/type → same linked person's housing stability, health, behavioral-health, and wage outcomes | national generalization, causal exit effect, assistance adequacy, rent burden for every unit, later trust/action, and durable security |

This is the crucial correction to treating “exit” as a single success or
failure endpoint. It is a regional observational study, and the categories are
partly constructed from administrative reasons, but it demonstrates that the
same institutional transition can transfer either autonomy or residual risk.

## Latest consumer-pressure rotation: budget exposure differs before hardship is visible

The [BLS Consumer Expenditures record](records/us-bls-consumer-expenditures-income-quintile-2024.json)
adds a population-level composition and distribution layer. In 2024, average
annual expenditures were $78,535 per consumer unit; housing was 33.4% of the
average budget, and the published average housing expenditure increased 3.3%
from 2023. BLS's grouped comparison also shows that income-before-tax and
expenditure changes varied across income quintiles, with the lowest-quintile
expenditure increase concentrated in housing.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| BLS Consumer Expenditure Survey consumer-unit annual averages and income-quintile groupings | Nominal average budget composition, component changes, and grouped income/expenditure changes | resource position and budget composition → different observed spending movement and potential exposure to essential costs | a dated bill or price, quantity change, substitution, liquidity, debt, food/housing adequacy, time cost, trust, and action for the same unit |

This is a distributional consumer-budget signal, not a hardship rate or a
causal price effect. A larger expenditure can reflect income, quantity, quality,
household composition, or price; a large housing share does not establish that
any particular consumer unit faced an unaffordable payment. The record becomes
more informative when held beside SHED's reported adaptations and food-security
sources, but those surveys remain separate populations rather than a common
household trajectory.

## Latest consumer-recourse rotation: removing a fee is not the same as restoring financial room

The [NBER bank-fee record](records/us-nber-bank-fees-household-financial-wellbeing-2017-2022.json)
adds a transaction-level policy-change bridge. Its study of staggered large-bank
NSF-fee eliminations and overdraft-policy relaxations finds immediate reductions
in the targeted fees, but later reductions in late fees, interest payments,
account-maintenance fees, and alternative-financial-service use are observed
only among more liquid households. The source reports no substantial reduction
in financial stress among more vulnerable households.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Individual transaction data around staggered large-bank fee-policy changes | Targeted fee changes and liquidity-differentiated subsequent account/alternative-credit outcomes | fee rule → immediate charge reduction → unequal later financial-room indicators | original bill or need, merchant/service result, household trade-off, family support, welfare, trust, switching, and durable recovery |

This is stronger than a generic fee complaint because it observes a policy
change and later account outcomes, but it is not a full consumer-recovery
episode. The safe recurring trend is that a visible cost can be removed while
the underlying liquidity gap continues to shape who gains later room.

The payment-resilience evidence adds a distinct disruption version of the same
problem: during outage conditions, digital payment can become unavailable and
cash access is associated with more completed transactions. The fee-policy and
outage studies use different populations, shocks, and outcomes, so they do not
form one common resilience rate. Their shared implication is narrower:
financial fallback capacity—liquidity, cash, credit, and practical access—is a
distributed condition for continuity, not a property of a payment system alone.

## Latest usable-option rotation: formal routes fail at timing, visibility, and correction

The recent public-system records converge on a mechanism that is broader than
any one program. A formal option becomes usable only when the affected person
can identify it, reach it in time, and obtain a meaningful result:

| Route | What is visible | What remains open |
|---|---|---|
| Federal reentry | GAO found earned time was not fully applied toward RRC or home confinement for 21,190 of 29,934 reviewed people; capacity and late contractor payments constrained the route | Person-level transfer receipt, housing/work continuity, treatment, later justice contact, and trust |
| Community supervision | BJS counted 3.6819 million adults under probation or parole at year-end 2024 and distinguished completion, incarceration, absconding, and other exits | Supervision intensity, condition burden, service use, household effects, and durable inclusion |
| Accessibility complaints | GAO recorded 41 GSA-building complaints, 25 closed and 16 in process; stakeholders reported low public awareness of the route | Barrier prevalence, route discovery, correction quality, restored access, and later participation |
| Digital public contact | Census Household Panel evidence suggests text can accelerate initial response, while consent and access differ across demographic groups | Accurate representation, privacy cost, trust, policy use, and civic action |

Across these routes, the institution can report an eligibility rule, a contact
channel, a complaint closure, or a population decline while the person still
experiences delay, uncertainty, or no practical alternative. This is a working
interpretation, not a pooled causal estimate: the records use different units,
agencies, and outcomes. The next decisive test is a same-person route ledger
that retains notice or eligibility, attempted access, time and effort, usable
alternative, institutional response, verified correction, protected or
sacrificed outcome, and later trust, action, switching, or exit.

## Latest administrative-contestability rotation: procedure distributes access, voice, and legitimacy

Several recent records make the procedural layer more specific than a general
“friction” concept. They show different ways that an institution can decide
who reaches a benefit, who can inspect or correct a record, who contests an
assessment, and who receives a remedy:

| Source unit | Observed or estimated stage | Safe cross-source connection | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| NBER SNAP administrative records using ABAWD age exemptions and waiver periods | Work requirements associated with higher procedural and overall denials; study interprets administrative burden as participation deterrence | policy rule/exemption → paperwork or compliance burden → denial → reduced participation | notice, claimant effort, appeal, restored benefits, food security, work quality, trust, and action |
| U.S. property-tax field experiment | Information about average peer tax payments increased perceived fairness and reduced tax appeals | comparative information → fairness interpretation → institutional contestation | payment, service quality, compliance, durable legitimacy, resignation versus acceptance, and household welfare |
| DOD/VA toxic-exposure record system | Claims staff, clinicians, and researchers use ILER while service-member and veteran access/correction functions roll out | institutional visibility → affected-person access and correction → possible claim/care consequence | verified exposure, correction success, claim approval, treatment, health, appeal, and trust |
| Medicare Advantage skilled-nursing authorization review | Denials, appeals, and high reversal among appealed requests | initial administrative decision → contestation → formal reversal | interim care, admission timing, functional recovery, caregiver burden, and plan switching |
| FTC/Amazon Prime redress program | Automatic payment design and aggregate issuance reduce some claim-form burden | remedy design → payment reachability → possible receipt | eligible-person denominator, acceptance, account correction, adequacy, and recovery |

These records do not measure one common administrative-burden rate. Their value
is comparative: **procedure is not merely an interface around policy; it is a
distribution mechanism that can create or remove practical voice.** The same
formal right can have different meaning depending on notice, documentation,
time, information, representation, reversal, and the availability of an
alternative. A denial, appeal, correction, or payment should therefore be
read as a stage in an actor's route, not as the final state of eligibility,
care, food, privacy, or financial recovery.

The cultural and political implication remains conditional. Comparative
fairness information can reduce contestation; administrative burden can deter
participation; personal record access can make institutional decisions more
contestable; and a reversal can signal responsiveness. None of these stages by
itself proves trust, legitimacy, political action, or durable use. The decisive
next evidence is a same-person or same-account ledger that joins notice,
attempt, effort, alternative, institutional response, verified receipt or
correction, and later interpretation or action.

The [FAA simulator-oversight workload record](records/us-gao-faa-flight-simulator-oversight-workload-1990-2025.json)
adds a physical-safety capacity version. The simulator inventory grew by more
than 500% from 1990 to 2025 while National Simulator Program staffing stayed
steady and evaluation activity was relatively steady in 2019–2024. The
Extended Evaluation Interval program can lengthen qualifying simulator review
intervals from 12 to 36 months; six of ten selected sponsors raised
communication concerns, two cited potential safety concerns, and GAO found
unaddressed mission-critical skills gaps. Workload, interval design, sponsor
communication, and oversight staffing are visible system conditions—not an
accident-risk, simulator-error, pilot-performance, or causal safety estimate.
Risk-based extensions may be efficient for high-performing simulators, and the
selected-sponsor concerns cannot establish unsafe determinations.

## Latest health-record rotation: administrative visibility precedes personal control

The [GAO toxic-exposure record](records/us-gao-toxic-exposure-record-access-2026.json)
adds a data-governance stage to the health and benefits route. GAO reports that
the Individual Longitudinal Exposure Record links DOD and VA exposure
information for clinicians, researchers, and disability-claims staff, with use
increasing after the PACT Act. Service members gained access and could add
self-reported updates in March 2026, while veteran access and correction were
still in rollout.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| ILER staff-use data, VA screening estimate, and selected stakeholder interviews | About 3.2 million of 6.8 million screened veterans were estimated to have a potential exposure; claims staff were the largest user group; access/correction rollout was reported | exposure/screening → linked record → claims/clinical use → possible personal access and correction | verified exposure, correction success, claim approval, treatment, health improvement, household effect, appeal, trust, and action |

This makes “having a record” and “having practical control over the record”
separate currencies. It does not show that ILER is inaccurate or that access
changes improve benefits. The next decisive test is a named record followed
through access, correction, verification, claim or care decision, treatment,
health, and later appeal or trust.

## Latest same-household meaning rotation: cash changes relationships without a uniform stability result

The [guaranteed-income intrahousehold record](records/us-nber-guaranteed-income-intrahousehold-dynamics-2026.json)
reports a randomized experiment in which 1,000 recipients received $1,000 per
month for three years and 2,000 controls received $50 per month. The experiment
found no aggregate household-stability effect, while other household income
fell by about $1,700 per year; partners were less likely to be promoted or move
to a better job, transfers to friends and family increased, and relationship
quality and trust in partners improved suggestively. Other household members
were more likely to pursue education or training, and recipients were more
likely to report that someone forwent healthcare, an ambiguous measure that may
reflect newly visible unmet need rather than a confirmed care loss.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Randomized recipient/control households over three years | Household income, partner advancement, transfers, relationship/trust measures, education/training, and healthcare reports | cash exposure → intrahousehold redistribution and changed relationship signals → mixed work, care, and meaning outcomes | political action, post-payment persistence, actual healthcare receipt, durable security, household decision power, and which person experienced each trade-off |

This is a same-household bridge, not a political-action result. It shows why
the atlas keeps material improvement, household stability, trust, and action as
separate endpoints: a transfer can alter relationships and perceived support
without producing a uniform improvement in work or security.

The [transitory-income durable-purchase record](records/us-nber-transitory-income-durable-purchases-vehicle-financing-2008.json)
adds a consumer-finance conversion route. Using variation from the 2008
Economic Stimulus Act, an NBER study estimates that households spent about 80%
of a payment on durable goods within three months, largely motor vehicles, and
borrowed on average about 40 cents for each stimulus dollar to finance those
vehicle purchases. A temporary income gain can therefore become both a durable
good—potentially preserving mobility, work, or care access—and a future loan
obligation. The study does not establish purchase necessity, loan terms,
delinquency, insurance/fuel/repair burden, later cash margin, or whether the
purchase improved or weakened household security. Its 2008 policy and vehicle
market setting should not be used as a current consumer-credit forecast.

The [ACS state transportation-context record](records/us-acs-transport-state-income-context-2024.json)
adds an ecological counterexample to a single mobility-burden scale. When
states, DC, and Puerto Rico are pooled into quartiles by state median household
income, zero-vehicle household shares run 6.90%, 6.77%, 11.52%, and 8.16%; the
corresponding shares of workers not working from home with commutes of 30 or
more minutes are 33.43%, 35.48%, 41.22%, and 42.31%. State median income is a
place context, not household income: higher no-vehicle shares can reflect dense
urban form and transit availability, while longer commutes can coexist with
higher income. These aggregate tables do not identify affordability, service
quality, trip success, care access, missed activity, or whether a commute is
chosen or required.

## Latest causal material-to-politics rotation: income does not automatically become civic action or institutional trust

The [guaranteed-income political-attitudes and behavior record](records/us-nber-guaranteed-income-political-attitudes-behavior-2024.json)
analyzes the political outcomes of the same broad guaranteed-income experiment.
Among 1,000 low-income treatment participants receiving $1,000 per month for
three years and 2,000 controls receiving $50, the study finds no detectable
effect on political participation or engagement, largely no change in political
preferences, and no detectable change in trust in government, polarization, or
support for democracy. It reports small improvements in affect toward racial
groups and some politicians, alongside evidence that treated participants viewed
work as more important or as a requirement for accessing government programs.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Randomized low-income adults receiving three years of differentiated cash transfers | Political participation, engagement, preferences, institutional trust, affect, and work-attitude outcomes | cash/resource increase → tested political endpoints; no automatic mobilization or trust shift detected | household mediation, attribution and fairness, subgroup mechanisms, post-payment persistence, other transfer designs, and political effects of more salient shocks |

This is one of the strongest current causal boundaries in the atlas. It rejects
a simple “more resources produce more participation” rule without rejecting
the possibility that material conditions matter through other mechanisms,
contexts, or politically attributed events. The political outcomes must remain
separate from the experiment's intrahousehold work, relationship, and
healthcare outcomes.

## Latest fairness-to-institution rotation: comparative information can change contestation without proving compliance

The [property-tax fairness and appeals record](records/us-nber-perceived-tax-fairness-appeals-2024.json)
uses a U.S. property-tax field experiment that disclosed the average tax rate
paid by other households. Higher perceived peer contributions increased
perceived fairness and reduced the likelihood of filing a tax appeal. The
authors estimate that each additional dollar paid by the average household
increased an individual taxpayer's willingness to contribute by $0.43.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Property-tax households receiving comparative tax-rate information | Experimental fairness perception and tax-appeal outcomes; contribution estimate | comparative public burden → fairness judgment → administrative contestation | payment, compliance, ability to pay, public-service quality, durable legitimacy, political participation, and household security |

This adds a concrete action endpoint between material obligation and public
meaning. It also prevents an overclaim: appealing a tax assessment, declining
to appeal, paying, complying, trusting government, and participating politically
are different behaviors with different costs and meanings.

## Latest policy-uncertainty rotation: expected relief can change behavior before relief exists

The [student-loan policy-uncertainty record](records/us-nber-student-loan-policy-uncertainty-beliefs-spending-2026.json)
studies the 2022 forgiveness and payment-pause uncertainty using an information
experiment linked to credit, employment, and nondurable-consumption records.
Borrowers more optimistic about future forgiveness reduced loan payments by $40
per month and increased nondurable spending by $80 per month. Those more
optimistic about future pauses were 7.5 percentage points more likely to be
delinquent after payments resumed; the paper's calibrated model estimates a
possible cost of up to 6.9% of the initial loan balance for overly optimistic
beliefs.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| U.S. student-loan borrowers in an information experiment linked to administrative records | Beliefs, payments, spending, employment linkage, and post-resumption delinquency | policy uncertainty → belief → household payment/spending adaptation → later payment risk | actual relief, spending purpose, housing/health/family trade-offs, attribution, trust, political action, and durable security |

This is a strong belief-to-material-behavior bridge, but it is not evidence that
borrowers received forgiveness or changed political identity. An expected policy,
a payment decision, a spending shift, a later delinquency, and realized relief
must remain separate clocks.

## Latest contested-safety rotation: shared goals can produce different political tools

NBER Working Paper 34962 surveys more than 5,400 U.S. respondents and runs
randomized information treatments about firearm costs, safe storage, and
non-lethal alternatives. Owners and non-owners can share a goal of protecting
themselves or their families while reporting different safety perceptions and
preferring different means. Information about private costs increased concern
about harms and support for safe storage; information about non-lethal tools
increased willingness to pay for alternatives and support for incapacitating
tools, but did not increase current owners' stated willingness to give up lethal
firearms.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| U.S. firearm owners and non-owners in survey and randomized information arms | Shared safety goal, divergent perceptions, tool preferences, storage/alternative attitudes, and persistence patterns | common protection objective → different risk interpretation and tool choice → bounded attitude movement | actual acquisition/use/storage, injury or crime, durable policy support, public safety, and political action |

This is a cultural-political correction to treating policy disagreement as a
simple disagreement about goals. People may agree on the desired outcome while
disagreeing about the safety possibilities they believe different tools create.
Stated willingness, storage preference, ownership, and realized safety remain
separate endpoints.

## Latest disaster-liquidity rotation: credit can protect against collapse without proving recovery

NBER Working Paper 32845 links Federal Disaster Loan applications from
households with uninsured disaster damage to pre- and post-shock credit
records. Exploiting the program's debt-to-income approval discontinuity, the
study reports that approved liquidity reduced bankruptcy likelihood by 61% over
the following three years and increased car purchases even three years after
receipt. This is a stronger shock-to-financial-outcome bridge than an aggregate
disaster-loan total, but it remains a selected applicant route and a debt-based
intervention.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Federal Disaster Loan applicants with uninsured damage linked to credit records | Quasi-experimental approval effect on bankruptcy and later car purchase | disaster damage → liquidity approval → financial-collapse avoidance and possible mobility preservation | repayment burden, repairs, insurance and other aid, work/care continuity, non-applicant access, housing security, protected versus displaced needs, trust, and action |

The result sharpens the program's hidden-trade question: a loan can preserve
mobility or prevent bankruptcy while moving future repayment risk into the
household. A later car purchase is not by itself proof of repair, adequacy, or
durable recovery.

## Latest attribution rotation: people can share the hardship judgment while splitting on cause and remedy

NBER Working Paper 32497 uses a U.S. online survey with conjoint and
information experiments to distinguish perceived inflation harm from perceived
causes, policy trade-offs, and remedies. Respondents could regard inflation as
harmful while assigning responsibility to government, firms, supply chains,
labor markets, oil prices, or the Federal Reserve; partisan differences shaped
those attributions, and the tested beliefs were difficult to move with
information.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| U.S. survey respondents in conjoint and information experiments | Perceived harm, cause ranking, trade-off beliefs, policy preferences, partisan contrasts, and experimental belief movement | price pressure as publicly understood → attribution/fairness/remedy judgment → possible political meaning | respondent-specific price exposure, messenger trust, durable belief change, spending, policy implementation, turnout, vote choice, and whether perceived responsibility matches the economic cause |

This closes an interpretive middle that generic inflation or hardship measures
cannot supply. It does not establish that a stated cause is correct or that a
policy preference became political action; those remain separate links.

## Latest work-security rotation: safety has a price, and insurance can change who can move

NBER Working Paper 35580 combines a representative survey of employed U.S.
workers with a general-equilibrium search model. Workers require an average
1.63% pay increase for each additional percentage point of annual unemployment
risk in a new job. In the model, complete markets raise job mobility by 12%
and productivity by 0.19%; unemployment insurance encourages acceptance of
riskier, more productive offers while also increasing modeled job loss and
unemployment.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Employed-worker survey plus calibrated job-search model | Stated wage-for-risk trade-off and modeled mobility/productivity/UI mechanisms | job opportunity → security premium → constrained mobility; insurance can expand practical outside options | actual offer, acceptance, switching, layoff, household liquidity, benefits, care/health, worker voice, and realized security |

This makes “mobility” a conditional resource rather than an unqualified
good: moving can improve wages or productivity, but workers with little ability
to insure unemployment may rationally remain in safer jobs. The survey result
is observed preference evidence; the macro effects are model-dependent and do
not establish a universal realized UI effect.

The [Census race/context/job-mobility record](records/us-census-homophily-race-job-mobility-2026.json)
adds a social-composition condition to that practical-option question. In a
restricted ACS analysis across commuting zones, Black and Hispanic workers were
less responsive than White workers to changing job opportunities, while their
responsiveness rose when opportunities were in destinations with a higher share
of their own race. The result does not identify a simple preference for
same-race locations: networks, expected discrimination, safety, information,
family support, job matching, and moving costs remain competing mechanisms.
Nor does it show that a move occurred, that the destination offered better job
quality or earnings, or that any later household security, belonging, or
political outcome changed.

## Latest platform-agency rotation: a choice screen can change use, but implementation determines how much choice becomes real

The [NBER browser-choice record](records/comparative-nber-browser-defaults-active-choice-2026.json)
studies browser usage around the European Union's Digital Markets Act
choice-screen mandate. From month 15 onward, Firefox usage was 91% higher on
iOS and 13% higher on Android relative to a no-mandate counterfactual. The
platform difference is consistent with rollout: iOS reached existing users,
while Android primarily reached new devices; market shares also moved away from
Safari on iOS and Chrome on Android.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| EU browser usage and market-share data around DMA choice-screen rollout | Difference-in-differences estimates and platform-specific market movement | default/choice architecture → observed alternative-browser use | U.S. generalization, comprehension, accessibility, privacy/data-sharing outcomes, durable switching, welfare, and whether users experienced practical exit |

The result corrects a simple “more options means more agency” story: regulation
can weaken a default, but the effect depends on when and to whom the
intervention is delivered. Browser use is still not proof of informed choice or
improved privacy.

## Latest U.S. platform-learning rotation: active choice is weaker than learning by trying an alternative

The [NBER search-engine learning record](records/us-nber-search-engine-market-power-field-experiment-2025.json)
uses a randomized field experiment with approximately 2,300 U.S. desktop Google
users. Requiring active choice increased Bing's share by only 1.1 percentage
points. By contrast, users paid to try Bing for two weeks updated positively
about its relative quality, and 33% preferred to continue using it. The paper's
model estimates larger market-share and consumer-surplus effects if demand
frictions and incorrect quality beliefs were removed, but those estimates are
counterfactual rather than observed universal outcomes.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Randomized U.S. desktop-user search experiment plus demand model | Active-choice effect, paid-trial behavior, quality-belief updating, continued preference, and modeled friction-removal counterfactuals | incumbent default → choice or temporary exposure → use, learning, and continued preference | durable switching, privacy/security, accessibility, consumer welfare, data-sharing, complaint route, and political action |

This separates nominal choice from practical agency: an interface can ask a
person to choose without changing behavior, while actual exposure can teach
some users that an alternative is better for them. Continued preference is
still not verified durable exit or proof of improved privacy.

## Latest platform-ownership rotation: removing a platform-owned product can reduce short-run consumer surplus

NBER Working Paper 34135 reports a field experiment that hides Amazon-owned
brands in selected product categories. Shoppers choose similar products along
observable dimensions, without increased search effort or increased shopping
on other retail websites. Model-based counterfactuals estimate a 5.4% short-run
consumer-surplus reduction when private labels are removed, with substantial
category heterogeneity; demoting Amazon brands in search did not produce a
detected welfare gain in the tested setting.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| U.S. Amazon shoppers and selected categories in a field experiment plus calibrated model | Product choice, search effort, cross-platform substitution, and category-specific short-run surplus estimates | platform-owned availability/placement → observed product choice and search effort → heterogeneous short-run consumer welfare | long-run seller entry/exit, ranking fairness, future price/quality, data use, durable dependence, household welfare, and political response |

This is a useful counterexample to a one-directional anti-platform story:
removing a platform-owned option can harm some current consumers even if the
same platform may possess longer-run gatekeeping power. Short-run product
choice and long-run competitive structure must remain separate endpoints.

## Cross-source platform-agency synthesis: choice has at least four different clocks

The current platform evidence supports a more precise practical-agency model.
The sources do not measure one common consumer or one common welfare outcome,
but they separate four mechanisms that are often collapsed into “choice.”

| Mechanism | Evidence surface | What changes | What remains unproven |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Prompted choice** | EU browser choice-screen rollout | Alternative-browser use changes, with strong iOS/Android rollout heterogeneity | comprehension, accessibility, privacy, durable switching, U.S. transferability |
| **Learned choice** | U.S. paid search-engine trial | Temporary exposure updates quality beliefs and produces continued preference for some users | durable exit, privacy/security, broad welfare, complaint or correction route |
| **Consent friction** | Cookie-banner field experiment and structural model | Click burden, defaults, dismissal, and repeated decision time shape data-sharing behavior | informed consent, actual downstream data use, equal accessibility, realized privacy |
| **Owned-option value** | Amazon private-label field experiment | Removing a platform-owned option can reduce short-run consumer surplus without increasing search or cross-platform substitution | long-run seller competition, ranking fairness, future prices/quality, platform dependence |
| **Data-access governance** | Platform data-neutrality model | Equal data quantity and price can widen seller access and downstream competition while reducing modeled data-production incentives | actual market implementation, data quality, privacy/security, enforcement cost, seller survival, and consumer welfare |

The synthesis implication is that practical agency is not a single binary
state. A person may receive more nominal options, learn that an alternative is
better, remain inattentive to consent, or value an incumbent-owned product—all
at different clocks. The decisive missing design is a protected user-level
panel that joins interface exposure, comprehension and accessibility, actual
use, data-sharing, support/complaint, retention, switching, and welfare. Until
then, market-share movement, click behavior, continued preference, and
consumer-surplus estimates must remain separate endpoints.

The [platform data-neutrality record](records/us-nber-platform-data-neutrality-market-competition-2026.json)
makes the governance tradeoff explicit. In the model, a weak rule requiring only
equal data amounts can be offset through discriminatory pricing. A stronger
rule equalizing both data quantity and price broadens access and intensifies
downstream competition, but can lower the platform's incentive to refine and
produce data; the reduced-data effect can outweigh competition benefits in the
modeled consumer-welfare comparison. This is a theoretical mechanism, not an
observed U.S. platform result or a rule recommendation. It does not measure
data accuracy, privacy, security, compliance cost, actual seller outcomes, or
consumer welfare in any particular market.

## Latest property-mobility rotation: mortgage lock-in makes staying an active constraint

The [mortgage-lock-in and housing-mobility record](records/us-nber-mortgage-lockin-housing-mobility-2022-2024.json)
combines individual mortgage data with a spatial housing-ladder model. It
reports a 25% causal reduction in borrower mobility associated with mortgage
lock-in, with disproportionate reduction in moves down the housing ladder.
The separate model counterfactual estimates house-price and rent increases of
4.4% and 1.5% relative to no lock-in; those price estimates are not observed
household payments.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Individual mortgage data plus spatial housing-ladder model | Study-specific lock-in/mobility evidence and modeled equilibrium effects | rate change and low-rate mortgage → constrained sale/buy option → staying or altered move | desired move, replacement housing, repair/insurance, care/work, household security, trust, and exit |

This complements managed-retreat evidence: an official buyout can produce a
move, while a financial condition can make staying the adaptation. Neither
endpoint alone proves durable housing security.

## Latest workplace-control rotation: request, grant, and job structure are different stages

BLS reports that 5.9 million wage and salary workers requested or made a
change to help them do their job better in July 2024. Schedule requests were
fully granted 70.1% of the time, partially granted 21.9%, and not granted
7.5%; full-grant shares were lower for training and workplace accessibility.
Separately, the 2025 Occupational Requirements Survey reports pause control
for 53.5% of workers, self-paced workload for 18.5%, and employer-driven
schedule variability for 48.3%.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| CPS worker requests/grant outcomes plus ORS job requirements | Worker-request and employer-grant distributions; establishment-based job-structure estimates | need or desired improvement → requested change → full/partial/no grant; job-level control context | same-worker usability, persistence, pay, health/care, voice, retention, household security, and exit |

This advances the implementation-to-control arrow without treating a request,
a grant, or a job-level requirement as a complete worker outcome.

## Latest remedy rotation: cashing and reissue are a middle stage between formal payment and recovery

The FTC's 2024 Annual Report on Refunds to Consumers documents a six-step
refund process and reports that 2,794,012 of 4,350,411 consumers in its
first-distribution table had cashed a payment to date (64.2%). In programs
closed during 2024, 1,162,743 of 1,770,214 consumers sent a payment had cashed
at least one (65.7%). A named Amazon Flex reissue using Zelle produced 2,806
successful payments and raised reported participation from 90.3% to 92.3%.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| FTC 2024 refund distributions and Amazon Flex reissue | Administrator-reported payment, cashing, reissue, additional-distribution, and closure stages | formal remedy → payment usability and administrative reach | individual adequacy, underlying correction, continued work/service, trust, switching, exit, and durable household recovery |

This is stronger than a settlement order or check-sent count, but cashing is
not the same as full compensation or restored practical options.

## Latest material-to-action rotation: hurricane exposure can move policy and create electoral risk

NBER Working Paper 25835 uses the universe of federal disaster declarations
from 1989–2014 and reports that representatives from hurricane-hit districts
were more likely to support environmental regulation and control in the next
year. The agenda change persisted and was associated with an electoral penalty
in following elections, with stronger responses among safer districts, more
experienced representatives, and representatives with stronger environmental
records.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hurricane-hit congressional districts, representatives, legislative records, and subsequent elections | Study-level disaster exposure, lagged representative response, persistence, and electoral association | material disaster → institutional agenda/action → later electoral consequence | household damage, aid/insurance, attribution, trust, voter awareness, turnout/vote reason, implementation, and recovery |

This is one of the clearest current event-to-institution-to-political-outcome
routes, but it is not a same-voter household-to-ballot chain.

## Latest capability-to-external-response rotation: agreement commitments are not replaceability

The USTR fact sheet on the February 2026 U.S.-Taiwan Agreement on Reciprocal
Trade reports Taiwan tariff and non-tariff commitments, a Taiwan-linked plan
for U.S. semiconductor, electronics/AI, and energy clusters, and named planned
purchases for 2025–2029. The new [Taiwan response record](records/us-ustr-taiwan-reciprocal-trade-capacity-response-2026.json)
keeps those commitments separate from the prior record's TSMC Arizona
production and workforce observations.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| USTR agreement and referenced AIT-TECRO MOU | Officially reported bilateral terms and plans; the $84.8 billion total is a transparent sum of three planned categories | domestic strategic-capacity pressure/alignment → external partner trade, investment, and procurement commitments | approval, contracts, payment, delivery, output, supplier switching, local burden, persistence, and changed strategic behavior |

This advances the external-response stage without calling a negotiated plan a
realized capability or a concession that proves durable U.S. leverage.

## Latest property-risk rotation: assistance does not guarantee a reachable housing route

GAO-26-108154 reports more than $3 billion in FEMA Individual Assistance to
1.2 million individuals and households affected by selected recent disasters.
The program can cover temporary lodging, rental housing, and home repairs, but
the review also reports long helpline waits, confusing eligibility letters,
constrained post-disaster housing, direct-housing setup delays, and access
concerns after delivery changes toward state/local centers and away from
door-to-door canvassing.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| FEMA assistance totals plus selected helpline, letter, housing, and institutional evidence | Agency-reported assistance scale and selected implementation barriers | disaster need → assistance route → possible lodging/rental/repair support → housing-reach constraint | survivor eligibility, notice, application, award, receipt, repair, safe occupancy, move/stay, durable security, trust, and action |

This advances the property-risk lane from protection and hazard toward a named
public recovery route. It does not turn aggregate dollars or selected official
accounts into a survivor-level recovery rate.

The [GAO FEMA assistance/housing-reach record](records/us-gao-fema-assistance-housing-reach-2026.json)
preserves the delivery and federalism boundary behind that route. GAO reviewed
FEMA data and 56 interviews or written responses and reports that officials in
four Helene- and Milton-affected states lacked their own individual-assistance
programs. Federal and state officials also emphasized that state and local
governments need preparation time because they rely on significant federal
support. This is evidence of selected institutional dependence, not a
representative measure of local capacity or an argument that federal pooling is
ineffective. It does not link a survivor through eligibility, contact,
application, award, payment, repair, occupancy, or durable housing security.

## Latest disaster-capacity rotation: federal support meets uneven local capability

The [state/local disaster-response capability record](records/us-gao-disaster-state-local-response-capability-2025.json)
adds an upstream institutional layer to the FEMA assistance route. GAO's
selected-state review found wide variation in response capability and reported
particular challenges for rural or less-resourced jurisdictions, even inside
otherwise well-resourced states. Officials emphasized clear guidance, time to
prepare, and federal coordination when responsibilities change; federal
officials also described a continuing federal-support floor for catastrophic
disasters.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Selected state preparedness assessments plus federal, state, and local official interviews | Capability variation, federal-support dependence, rural/resource constraints, and role-transition requirements | hazard → jurisdictional capability and federal support → coordination and implementation conditions → possible assistance reach | response speed, survivor contact, eligibility, receipt, repair, safe housing, local economic recovery, trust, and political action |

This sharpens the distinction between formal program availability and usable
capacity. It does not establish that federal dependence is inherently harmful:
federal pooling may be efficient, and variation may reflect hazards or local
responsibilities. The next decisive test is a named jurisdiction and event
linking capability, support, role transition, response timing, survivor reach,
repair, and durable recovery.

## Latest service-route rotation: work requirements can become procedural denials

The [SNAP procedural-denial record](records/us-nber-snap-work-requirements-procedural-denials-2026.json)
uses SNAP administrative data and difference-in-differences comparisons around
age-based exemptions for ABAWDs aged 50 and older and periods of
work-requirement waivers. The study reports significant increases in procedural
and overall denials under work requirements and interprets the result as
participation deterrence through administrative burden.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| SNAP administrative records in study-selected jurisdictions and ABAWD age/waiver comparisons | Policy-associated procedural and overall denial changes | work requirement/exemption → administrative burden → denial → participation deterrence | claimant notice, effort, appeal, restoration, food security, health, work, later trust, and political action |

This advances the public-system route toward remedy without treating denial as
the same thing as ineligibility, final loss, or household recovery. The public
abstract does not disclose a full denominator or every jurisdiction and does
not provide a claimant-level follow-up.

## Latest experimental rotation: surveillance effects depend on managerial clarity

NBER Working Paper 33348 randomizes digital-surveillance use and whether its
use is explained according to workers' baseline productivity in an online labor
market. The study reports no statistically significant average performance
effect from surveillance alone, but a significant output reduction when the
presence or removal of surveillance is not explained on the basis of worker
performance.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Randomized online-labor-market workers in surveillance and explanation arms | Randomized monitoring, rationale, worker-performance grouping, and output response | surveillance implementation → managerial explanation/worker interpretation → output response | national exposure, pay, autonomy, voice, appeal, remedy, health, retention, exit, household security, and long-run effects |

This gives the broader GAO review a causal mechanism without treating online
labor-market output as ordinary employment quality. It also makes transparency
and rationale an observable implementation variable rather than a soft
interpretive add-on.

## Latest workplace-implementation rotation: surveillance changes the stakes of measurement

The [GAO digital-workplace-surveillance record](records/us-gao-digital-surveillance-workers-federal-oversight-2025.json)
reviews 122 studies published from 2020 through 2024 and interviews
stakeholders from 11 organizations. It describes digital-surveillance tools as
capable of improving safety or identifying health problems while also pressuring
workers to move faster, increasing injury risk, creating anxiety, and exposing
workers to flawed benchmarks or incomplete task measures. Those implementation
failures can feed into low evaluations, lower pay, discipline, or termination.
GAO identifies EEOC, NLRB, and DOL OSHA as relevant investigative routes and
reports that several agencies had rescinded or were reassessing prior guidance
in 2025.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| GAO review of 122 studies plus 11 stakeholder organizations | Reviewed worker-effect patterns and agency roles; no worker-level prevalence estimate | monitoring design/transparency/targets → possible safety, mental-health, evaluation, and employment-risk pathway → institutional oversight route | named worker exposure, consequential decision, appeal, remedy, pay or health outcome, job exit, household security, later trust, and collective action |

This is an implementation bridge, not proof that surveillance is uniformly
harmful. The same technology can support safety, while opaque or inaccurate
measurement can transfer uncertainty and contestation costs to workers. It
therefore advances the work-control lane without collapsing tool adoption,
worker exposure, employer authority, voice, and realized household outcomes
into one observation.

## Latest trust rotation: care-seeking depends on which institution is trusted

NBER Working Paper 32028, revised in April 2026, uses a nationally
representative U.S. adult survey and a model of care-seeking through perceived
usefulness, expected disutility, out-of-pocket cost, and claim denial. The
study separates trust in doctors, nurses, hospitals, insurers, public-health
authorities, and the health-care system overall. It reports that higher trust
is associated with more retrospective and anticipated preventive and elective
care-seeking and less care avoidance, with an association comparable to
education and income gradients. Structural estimates describe trust as more
substitutable across entities overall but more complementary within provider
domains; the reported simulations assign the largest effect to losses of trust
in doctors.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nationally representative U.S. adult trust and care-seeking survey | Entity-specific trust, retrospective/anticipated care, mechanism associations, structural estimates, and counterfactual simulations | health need/care decision → institutional trust → perceived value, disutility, cost, and denial → seek, delay, or avoid care | dated trust shock, appointment/treatment receipt, affordability, health, debt, later trust, remedy, switching, and political action |

This supplies a behavioral middle to the trust-and-meaning lane. It also
prevents institutional trust from being treated as one pooled attitude:
provider, hospital, insurer, and public-health trust can have different routes.
Because the evidence is survey and model based, it does not prove that a trust
change caused a later decision; access, resources, prior experience, and health
need remain alternative explanations.

## Latest public-capacity rotation: disaster assistance depends on workforce reach

GAO-26-108427 reports that FEMA averaged about 25,134 employees in FY2025,
while more than 4,300 separated, a 55% increase over FY2024. GAO also reports
that FEMA rescinded its strategic plan and did not base key 2025–2026 workforce
decisions on a workforce analysis; FEMA officials described lost institutional
knowledge and longstanding staffing challenges. During the Helene and Milton
response, FEMA deployed 13,500 employees, and only 4% of the incident-management
workforce was available to deploy in November 2024 afterward. Anticipated DHS
surge support for the 2026 hurricane season fell to about 240 personnel from
600 in 2025. GAO connects reduced surge and call-center staffing to long waits
and unanswered calls reported for Texas flood survivors.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| FEMA workforce, cadres, deployment, and selected disaster-assistance cases | Staffing counts, separations, planning condition, deployment, anticipated surge capacity, and selected contact evidence | concurrent disaster demand → workforce/planning constraint → surge/call-center/grant reach → access friction | representative contact failure, application/notice, assistance receipt, housing and income continuity, health, trust, and action |

This adds an upstream public-capacity mechanism to the flood and wildfire
records. Federal dollars and private firm capacity can exist while the route to
an answer remains constrained by staffing, institutional memory, and competing
disasters. The selected GAO cases do not prove that staffing reductions caused
any individual survivor outcome; demand, communication systems, policy, and
application complexity remain alternative explanations.

## Latest disaster-capacity rotation: federal finance can change which firms survive a flood

The [flood disaster-aid, firm-reallocation, and productivity record](records/us-census-flood-disaster-aid-firm-reallocation-productivity-2026.json)
studies large federally declared floods with confidential plant-level Census
data, business credit, and county-income measures. It reports that exits were
concentrated among the least productive plants; machinery released by exits
moved toward more productive entrants; surviving plants upgraded machinery and
productivity; and federal disaster spending expanded financing access for young
and small firms. The recorded estimates include a 3.6-percentage-point increase
in shutdown within two years, more than 11% higher survivor productivity by year
six, about 14% higher entrant productivity, and about a 3.1% increase in bank
loans after declared floods. In contrast, severe non-declared floods had higher
exits and about a 0.73% decline in aggregate TFP without the same productivity
selection.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Flood-affected plants, firms, credit, and counties in CES-26-52 | Event-study estimates of exit, capital reallocation, productivity, lending, and county income across declared, non-declared, and denied-request floods | flood shock → federal finance → firm selection, credit, and productive capacity → local-income signal | worker continuity, wages and job quality, firm ownership, relocation, customer/service continuity, housing and insurance recovery, trust, and political action |

This adds a firm-and-public-finance realization stage to the disaster lane.
It is important because emergency capacity is not only a dollar total: finance
can affect which organizations remain and which machines move. But selective
exit and higher productivity are not the same as universal local recovery, and
the study's manufacturing and infrequent-inland-flood setting limits the
generalization.

## Latest infrastructure rotation: broad water coverage coexists with uneven household responsibility

The [EPA/CDC drinking-water coverage and replacement-risk record](records/us-epa-cdc-drinking-water-coverage-replacement-risk-2026.json)
reports $625 billion in public drinking-water needs over 20 years, including
$422.9 billion for distribution and transmission. EPA's approximately four
million lead-service-line estimate combines three million reported lead or
galvanized lines requiring replacement with one million lines predicted to be
lead from unknown-material reporting; neither count is completed replacement
or household exposure. CDC reports that about nine in ten Americans receive
water from community systems, while about one in eight use private wells;
private-well owners are generally responsible for safety, and about one in five
sampled private wells had a potentially health-affecting chemical.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| EPA infrastructure assessment and CDC coverage indicators | National need, projected service-line inventory, public/private coverage, governance responsibility, sampled-well contamination | infrastructure need → exposure/governance route → public replacement or household testing responsibility | named household exposure, notice, replacement, cost, remediation time, health, mobility, trust, and action |

This adds a domestic-capability boundary: broad public coverage and large public
investment needs can coexist with private responsibility for testing and safety.
The units are national systems and sampled wells, not a common household panel.

## Latest time-and-care rotation: care capacity is a differentiated time buffer, not one shared route

The Census 2023 SIPP working paper examines first-time parents who worked
before and after their first biological child's birth. Among the 2014–2023
cohort, any leave was reported by 89.2% of mothers and 63.9% of fathers; paid
leave was reported by 51.6% and 49.3%. Paid leave, no-leave shares, and return
within less than one month differed across race/ethnicity-by-sex groups.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2023 SIPP first-time-parent work/leave universe | Descriptive leave, paid-leave, no-leave, and return-timing comparisons | birth/work-family need → leave route and timing → differentiated care/work option | employer/job mechanism, leave adequacy, wages, childcare, job retention, caregiver/child outcomes, security, trust, and action |

This strengthens the time/care lane by separating nominal leave from a paid,
usable, and timely option. It remains a selected employed-parent comparison,
not a causal racial or gender explanation and not an all-parent access rate.

The [dependent-coverage and parental-job-retention record](records/us-nber-dependent-coverage-parental-job-lock-2022.json)
adds a causal benefit-to-work bridge. Exploiting the ACA's birth-month
discontinuity in the duration of dependent eligibility, the employer-claims
study finds that extra eligibility increased dependent take-up and a proxy for
parental job retention. The effect was strongest among parents nearer a job-exit
margin, families placing more value on dependent coverage, and workers at firms
offering a broader set of insurance options. The result shows that a child's
coverage can shape a parent's labor-market decision; it does not show whether
staying was desired or harmful, the retained job's pay, safety, schedule, or
quality, plan affordability or network adequacy, actual care continuity, or
family wellbeing. Coverage-related retention is therefore neither automatically
coercive job lock nor automatic household security.

The newer Census child-care and BLS eldercare layers extend the same
conversion problem beyond first birth. Census reports that some households
with children ages 0–13 had an adequate-care problem that prevented more work;
among affected households, reported adaptations include paid or unpaid leave,
reduced hours, non-search, job quitting, and supervision while working. BLS
separately estimates 38.2 million eldercare providers age 15+ in 2023–24 and
reports care-day time among employed providers. The leave, childcare, and
eldercare records have different populations, clocks, and measures, so they
do not yield one national care-shortage or work-loss rate. Together, they
support the narrower trend: care capacity is a time buffer whose practical
availability varies with workplace terms, income, household resources, and
care arrangements.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Census households with children 0–13; BLS eldercare-provider population; BLS wage-and-salary leave module | Child-care work-prevention and reported adaptations; eldercare prevalence and care-day time; formal leave access and unmet leave need | care requirement → differentiated time, work, and leave adaptations across separate source units | common household trajectory, care quality/safety, verified work loss, earnings, recipient outcome, wellbeing, and durable security |

## Latest place rotation: redevelopment can improve children's opportunity without adult earnings gains

The [Census CES HOPE VI record](records/us-census-hope-vi-neighborhood-social-isolation-child-outcomes-2026.json)
examines $17 billion of redevelopment across 262
distressed public-housing developments. Using matched difference-in-differences
with anonymized tax records, plus movers-exposure and sibling comparisons, it
reports lower neighborhood poverty, no causal earnings effect for adults living
in public-housing units, and better long-run earnings, college attendance, and
incarceration outcomes for children raised in revitalized units. Increased
interaction with surrounding peers—especially higher-income peers—is reported
as an important mechanism.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| HOPE VI developments and linked tax outcomes | Matched redevelopment comparison, adult earnings null, child long-run outcomes, movers/sibling mechanism tests | place redevelopment → social interaction and changed neighborhood exposure → intergenerational opportunity | resident retention/displacement, housing quality, rents, safety, services, adult security, resident control, trust, and political action |

This is a stronger place-to-long-run-outcome bridge than a neighborhood poverty
snapshot, but a lower aggregate poverty rate and improved child outcomes do not
establish that every original household stayed, benefited, or gained control.

## Latest platform rotation: AI choice is task-specific, while privacy action depends on knowledge and replaceability

Jennifer Zou's June 2026 preprint surveys 1,999 U.S. adult AI-assistant users
with population-weighted estimates and an embedded choice experiment. The
reported market is concentrated, but smaller assistants occupy task niches;
about 43% of respondents intentionally route different tasks to multiple
platforms. Trust rankings differ between users and aware non-users, consistent
with trust being learned through use rather than reputation alone. Privacy
concern is widespread, but the study reports a knowledge/action gap and higher
stated willingness to pay to avoid human review than several other data-use
features.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| U.S. AI-assistant survey and choice experiment | Weighted platform/task shares, reported multihoming, trust comparisons, privacy knowledge and tools, hypothetical feature valuations | task exposure → provider choice and replaceability → experiential trust/knowledge → stated or reported protective behavior | verified settings, actual data exposure, causal knowledge effect, durable switching, provider response, wellbeing, and practical exit |

This adds a current U.S. task-level agency layer between platform use and
privacy control. It complements the account-exit, collective-exit, and DROP
records without treating their different users, systems, or outcomes as one
longitudinal history.

## Latest platform rotation: privacy control becomes more observable without becoming fully durable

California's Delete Request and Opt-Out Platform (DROP) adds an implementation
route for data-broker privacy control. CalPrivacy reports more than 325,000
consumer sign-ups/submissions and more than 600 active brokers. A single
