verifiable request is hashed, brokers must match it against their records, and
the public status vocabulary distinguishes deleted, exempted, opted-out,
not-found, and pending. Brokers must access the mechanism at least every 45
days, while status updates can take up to 90 days to appear.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| California DROP implementation | Program scale, hashed request architecture, broker clock, status categories, and optional matching fields | privacy concern/right → centralized request → matching and processing → visible partial-success states | consumer-level match and deletion distribution, residual data, re-collection, exemption fairness, trust, switching, and durable control |

This complements the account-exit and collective-exit studies by showing a
formal privacy route whose administrative status is more visible than before,
while the lived outcome still depends on matching, waiting, exceptions, and
persistence.

Walmart's company-reported California metrics supply a named firm-level
processing layer: access, deletion, and sale-opt-out requests are separately
counted as received, completed, or denied, with separate median completion
times. The FTC's review of nine large social-media and video-streaming companies
is a different system-level counterweight: it reports broad sharing, potential
indefinite retention, and that some reviewed companies did not delete all user
data after deletion requests. These sources cannot be converted into a shared
consumer completion or failure rate. They sharpen the pattern instead:
submission, matching, administrative completion, and durable data control are
different stages, and a visible status or completed request is not proof of
full deletion, non-recollection, trust, or practical exit.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| California DROP system; Walmart's California request metrics; FTC review of nine companies | Centralized request/status route; company-reported request processing; platform-practice retention and deletion findings | privacy concern/right → request and administrative processing → bounded, observable control status | individual deletion verification, downstream copies, re-collection, exemption fairness, trust, switching, and durable control |

## Latest platform rotation: individual exit can help while collective exit remains costly

The [social-media deactivation and collective-exit record](records/us-whr-social-media-deactivation-collective-exit-2026.json)
retains two separate U.S.-anchored study layers summarized by the World
Happiness Report 2026. In the Allcott et al. Facebook experiment, 2,743 users
were recruited and eligible participants were randomized to four weeks of paid
deactivation or continued use; the chapter reports higher happiness, life
satisfaction, and lower anxiety and depression after deactivation, alongside
lower later use and more app deletion. The continuing-use group had higher
political knowledge on the reported measure. Separately, a U.S. college-student
Instagram/TikTok study found stated willingness to pay to eliminate the
platform from one's academic community even while individual deactivation was
assigned a positive stated exit cost.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Facebook randomized deactivation experiment | 2,743-user recruitment, four-week treatment/control design, measured wellbeing and later-use outcomes | individual deactivation → short-run wellbeing improvement and lower later use, with a political-knowledge trade-off | account closure, data erasure, durable non-use, social/information loss, and political behavior |
| U.S. college-student Instagram/TikTok valuation study | stated individual and community-wide willingness-to-pay judgments summarized by WHR | individual exit cost plus social dependence → stated preference for coordinated community exit | actual coordination, collective deletion, persistence, substitution, and durable welfare |

This is a product-trap and coordination mechanism, not a single pooled study.
It complements the Korean qualitative non-use source by adding a U.S.-anchored
experiment and a stated collective-exit contrast.

## Latest platform rotation: practical non-use is not the same as durable exit

The [social-media non-use and practical-exit record](records/us-sage-social-media-nonuse-exit-2026.json)
adds a lived-experience bridge to the platform-exit lane. In interviews with 20
young Korean adults,
participants described time, app, account, and routine-level strategies for
limiting or stopping Instagram use. The sample included 11 active users, 2
deactivated accounts, 5 deleted accounts, 1 inactive account, and 1 partially
deactivated account at interview. The authors report UX, psychological, and
social challenges, nonlinear and reversible non-use, exclusion, incomplete
disconnection, and compensating use of other platforms alongside relief and
greater control for some participants.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| 20 Korean interview participants | Account-status composition and reported non-use strategies, frictions, and consequences | platform utility and connectivity norms → non-use attempt → experienced friction → reversible non-use, exclusion, or platform substitution | population prevalence, verified deletion/data erasure, durable non-use, wellbeing, remedy, U.S. generalization, and collective action |

This is comparative qualitative evidence, not an account-deletion rate or proof
that Instagram deliberately obstructed exit. It complements the platform-use,
privacy, age-restriction, and AI-companion records by adding the user-experience
middle between formal exit availability and durable practical exit.

## Latest geopolitical rotation: dependence can produce negotiation before coercion

The [international-power and negotiation record](records/us-nber-international-power-negotiation-2025.json)
adds a dependence-to-bargaining mechanism. It constructs a model-implied
international-power measure from asymmetric bilateral import dependence weighted
by sectoral trade elasticities, then combines it with bilateral engagement
events and high-frequency geopolitical relationships. The paper reports more
engagement and negotiation when measured power rises, and trade adjustment when
geopolitical relationships worsen, consistent with countries building future
leverage before a dispute.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| NBER international-power panel | Model-implied dependence measure combined with country-pair engagement and geopolitical data | asymmetric dependence → bargaining condition → negotiation and anticipatory trade adjustment | named demand, threat, concession, firm/public incidence, retaliation, alliance response, and strategic result |

This is a comparative model-and-panel mechanism, not proof that a particular
country successfully coerced another. It complements the conflict-risk paper’s
trade-disruption pathway by adding what may happen before open sanctions or
retaliation.

## Latest geopolitical implementation rotation: partner capacity can fail before capability is usable

The [GAO Section 333 partner-capacity record](records/us-gao-section-333-partner-absorptive-capacity-2023.json)
adds a planning-to-use stage beneath security-assistance funding. In GAO’s
selected review of 46 train-and-equip proposals, 42 did not fully document
partner absorptive capacity, sustainment, or measurable objectives. The program
had allocated nearly $5.6 billion across FY2018–2022, but that total is not
evidence of delivery, partner use, or strategic effect. GAO also found uneven
State participation and an unresolved joint DOD–State planning process in its
April 2026 follow-up.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| GAO Section 333 review | Selected proposal documents, allocation totals, interagency process, and recommendation status | authorization/funding → planning and partner-fit requirements → documented implementation risk | named-project delivery, training completion, operational use, sustainment, partner response, local legitimacy, and deterrence |

This is a process-risk finding, not a partner-failure rate. It complements the
international-arrangements record by showing that even after an assistance
authority exists, the missing middle can be partner fit, sustainment, measurable
objectives, and a defined interagency owner.

The [GAO human-rights training record](records/us-gao-security-assistance-human-rights-training-oversight-2019-2021.json)
adds a legitimacy stage that capability records cannot supply. DOD initially
did not systematically track mandated human-rights training, and DOD and State
had not evaluated whether the training was effective. Follow-up documented new
tracking fields, quarterly Section 333 status reports, and DOD/State monitoring
and evaluation plans. These are administrative reforms, not evidence of changed
conduct or civilian protection; the composite chain must keep rights oversight,
equipment reliability, partner use, and external strategic effect separate.

The [GAO Northern Triangle police-training record](records/us-gao-central-american-police-training-human-rights-controls-2014-2025.json)
adds a local institutional endpoint. In 22 State/USAID police-training projects
across El Salvador, Guatemala, and Honduras, none of the reviewed State or USAID
project documents had explicit human-rights objectives, and State lacked
reliable consolidated training counts. Later follow-up added human-rights
language and indicators, but neither reform establishes changed police conduct,
civilian safety, complaint resolution, or public legitimacy. The geopolitical
chain therefore distinguishes security-assistance funding, planning, delivery,
reliability, rights oversight, local policing, and lived legitimacy.

The companion [GAO Section 333 delivery record](records/us-gao-section-333-delivery-delay-evaluation-quality-2023.json)
extends the route to delivery and evaluation. GAO found 75% of Section 333
deliveries late relative to DOD estimates as of FY2022. Of six reviewed project
evaluations, four reported capability improvement and two reported no improvement
because equipment was unsuitable or unreliable, while the evaluation evidence
generally failed GAO's quality criteria. The safe composite is therefore:
funding and planning can coexist with delay, reliability problems, and weak
learning systems. It remains open whether a named partner used the capability
continuously, sustained it, or changed an external actor's choices.

## Latest technology-security rotation: export controls create compliance capacity before strategic effect

The [GAO advanced-semiconductor export-control record](records/us-gao-advanced-semiconductor-export-control-compliance-2022-2024.json)
adds a technology-security implementation layer to the domestic-capability and
geopolitical-dependence routes. GAO reviewed three 2022–23 rules and found
Commerce working with six other federal agencies on technical review,
licensing, foreign-partner outreach, border verification, investigations, and
enforcement. Affected firms updated compliance programs and self-reported
potential violations; GAO identified ten reported compliance challenges,
including unclear classifications, and documented BIS outreach, briefings,
definition changes, and rule updates.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| GAO advanced-semiconductor export-control review | Rules, interagency implementation, reported firm compliance, and administrative clarification | national-security rule → multi-agency and firm compliance surface → implementation learning | partner adoption, shipment/substitution response, domestic incidence, PRC capability change, deterrence, and U.S. leverage |

The implementation stage matters because a control can become administratively
real without producing the intended external result. The review itself points
to later enforcement and foreign-partner engagement work; it does not establish
that China substituted away from controlled technology, that allies aligned,
or that another actor changed its choices. This is a capability-to-response
bridge, not a strategic-effect claim.

The [BIS foreign-fab VEU record](records/us-bis-foreign-fab-veu-export-control-closure-2025.json)
adds a second technology-security stage: policy can preserve current operation
while narrowing future expansion. BIS required former VEU foreign-owned fabs in
China to obtain licenses, intended to permit existing-fab operation, and stated
that it would not license capacity expansion or technology upgrades. The cross-
source implication is not that the control succeeded; it is that a transition
clock and an expandable-capability boundary can be observed before firm-level
production, sourcing, partner alignment, or external behavior.

| Evidence | Observed stage | Still open |
| --- | --- | --- |
| GAO implementation review plus BIS VEU closure | interagency/firm compliance, policy revision, license transition, existing-operation continuity, and stated expansion restriction | affected-fab license outcomes, shipments, production, substitution, partner response, PRC capability change, deterrence, and U.S. leverage |

The formal endpoint is now partly observable: BIS's September 2025 final rule
removed Intel Dalian, Samsung China Semiconductor, and SK hynix Semiconductor
(China) from the VEU authorization list, effective December 31, 2025. This
closes the administrative loop for authorization status while leaving the
operational loop open. A removed general authorization is not the same as a
denied individual license, stopped fab, changed production line, or altered
external behavior.

Company-side certification adds a limited continuity signal: Samsung's Xi'an
site is certified for 12-inch flash-memory manufacture and related
packaging/testing through October 14, 2026, while SK hynix's current quality
page lists Wuxi 12-inch integrated-circuit manufacture through August 2, 2027.
These are not output series or license records. They support the narrower claim
that formal authorization revocation can coexist with a currently listed
operational scope; they do not show unchanged production, technology access,
or external response.

The first external-response signal is now directly attributed rather than
inferred. China's Ministry of Commerce opposed the VEU removals, warned of
serious global semiconductor supply-chain harm, urged U.S. correction, and said
it would take necessary measures to protect Chinese enterprises. This advances
the rule → formal revocation → official response chain, while leaving enacted
countermeasure, firm behavior, production, negotiation, and strategic effect
open.

The [EU dual-use list record](records/us-eu-commission-semiconductor-dual-use-control-list-update-2025.json)
adds a partner-alignment stage after the U.S. implementation and Chinese
response layers. The European Commission adopted common EU controls covering
semiconductor manufacturing/testing equipment and advanced-computing items,
with a stated basis in multilateral regimes and member-state commitments. This
supports list-level coordination, not identical policy, national enforcement,
firm adaptation, or strategic leverage.

The Commission's 2026 implementation report adds a system-level enforcement
stage: €77.6 billion in EU-authorized dual-use exports and 487 security-risk
denials in 2024, with China and the United States representing 25% and 21% of
authorized-export value. Because the report summary does not isolate
semiconductor items or member-state cases, this cannot be treated as a measured
effect of the 2025 list update or as proof of strategic alignment.

The Commission's underlying 2024 dataset adds a category-level authorization
stage. For 3EC4 equipment for manufacturing and testing semiconductor devices
or materials, the listed Member State rows sum to €721.1 million and 156
individual authorizations, with the largest reported values in Austria and
Germany. This improves measurement of semiconductor-specific licensing
exposure, but it still does not identify destinations, denials, exporters,
shipment completion, firm adaptation, or strategic effect.

The same Commission dataset also reports 415 full-time-equivalent control
staff, 154 outreach events, 1,353 compliance audits, 270 reported
infringements, and 144 penalties or fines in 2024. This adds a system-capacity
and enforcement endpoint after list adoption and authorization, while keeping
item category, destination, firm, shipment, deterrence, and geopolitical effect
unresolved.

The [Applied Materials settlement record](records/us-bis-applied-materials-semiconductor-equipment-settlement-2026.json)
adds a named enforcement route: BIS reported approximately $126 million in
illegal semiconductor-equipment shipments through Korea to China, imposed an
approximately $252 million penalty, and required continuing audits and annual
certifications. This is a realized violation-and-remediation stage after the
general EU and U.S. implementation layers; future compliance, production,
substitution, deterrence, and leverage remain unmeasured.

The [Cadence enforcement record](records/us-bis-doj-cadence-eda-export-enforcement-2025.json)
adds a second named firm route at the semiconductor-design layer: 56 BIS-
admitted violations and at least 59 DOJ-described exports through a PRC alias
to NUDT and related entities. The coordinated civil/criminal resolution
included a $95 million BIS penalty, DOJ penalties and forfeiture, a net amount
above $140 million, account freezes, an export hold, and remediation. This
shows enforcement reach across equipment and design tools, not a measured
change in Chinese capability or deterrence.

The [Alpha and Omega BIS order](records/us-bis-alpha-omega-huawei-power-semiconductor-settlement-2025.json)
adds a power-semiconductor route involving approximately 1,650 items across
15 unauthorized occasions sent through an AOS China facility for Huawei after
the Entity List addition. The $4.25 million penalty was tied to continued
export-privilege eligibility, while the order separately reports later
elimination of the Digital Power business unit. This adds a firm-operational
endpoint without attributing the exit to enforcement or inferring deterrence.

The [Bosch settlement record](records/us-bis-bosch-foreign-produced-mems-huawei-settlement-2026.json)
adds the foreign-produced and allied-firm layer: approximately $72.4 million
in MEMS sensors and automotive software exported from abroad to Huawei under
the Foreign Direct Product Rule without authorization. Bosch self-disclosed
and cooperated; BIS imposed a $36.18 million penalty and DOJ disgorgement was
approximately $3.6 million, credited against the BIS penalty. This shows
cross-border enforcement reach and remediation, not a measured change in
Huawei capability, supply, or deterrence.

The [BIS H200/MI325X licensing record](records/us-bis-h200-mi325x-case-by-case-china-licensing-2026.json)
adds a policy-reversal/conditional-opening layer. Certain advanced-computing
exports to China or Macau can receive case-by-case review when applicants meet
U.S.-supply, foundry-capacity, end-use, comparative-TPP, and independent-testing
requirements. No application approval or shipment is reported; the rule is a
formal conditional route, not evidence of market reopening or capability gain.

The [NVIDIA H200 license realization record](records/us-nvidia-h200-china-license-no-revenue-2026.json)
then supplies a realized administrative transition: NVIDIA reported a U.S.
license for small H200 shipments to specific China-based customers in
February 2026. The same filing reports zero H200 licensing-program revenue to
date and uncertainty over whether China would allow any imports; U.S. inspection
and a 25% tariff condition remain. The evidence therefore moves the chain from
formal conditional policy to license approval, while preserving the gap between
approval and shipment, import, revenue, capability, or market recovery.

The [NVIDIA partial H200 shipment record](records/us-nvidia-h200-partial-china-shipments-prc-restriction-2026.json)
provides the subsequent endpoint. In its fiscal Q2 2027 filing, NVIDIA reported
that PRC restrictions prevented all licensed sales, yet a fraction of permitted
H200 shipments had occurred and represented less than 1% of quarterly Data
Center revenue. The company continued to report a $0.4 billion H200 inventory
and purchase-obligation charge, tariff absorption, and no China data-center
compute revenue in its forward outlook. The chain therefore reaches physical
delivery while remaining commercially and politically constrained.

The [MOFCOM H200 non-confirmation record](records/us-china-mofcom-h200-shipment-nonconfirmation-2026.json)
adds a state-side information boundary rather than a new substantive control.
When asked on July 16, 2026 about U.S. claims that a small number of H200s had
been sold to China, a Ministry of Commerce spokesperson said he did not know
about the situation. The response neither confirms nor denies NVIDIA's filing;
it leaves Chinese import clearance, customer authorization, and official
acceptance of the reported shipments unresolved.

The [SAMR NVIDIA-Mellanox investigation continuity record](records/us-china-samr-nvidia-mellanox-investigation-continuity-2026.json)
adds a parallel state-capacity clock: SAMR's September 2025 notice initiated
further investigation after a preliminary finding, and SAMR's April 2026
review continued to list the NVIDIA case. The sources do not report a final
decision, penalty, or remedy. This supports regulatory continuity and unresolved
institutional exposure, not a measured causal effect on H200 shipments,
customers, competition, or Chinese capability.

The [Hygon DCU competitive-adaptation record](records/us-china-hygon-dcu-h200-competition-adaptation-2025-2026.json)
adds the competitor-response stage. Hygon said H200 entry would intensify
domestic high-end-chip competition and described a response centered on
performance, ecosystem compatibility, security compliance, cost-performance,
submarket focus, and domestic customer cases. It also reported existing DCU
applications and expected 2026 share gains. The record advances from state and
firm constraint to adaptation, while leaving actual substitution, customer
switching, market share, and capability effects unmeasured.

The [Hygon realized growth and deployment record](records/us-china-hygon-dcu-realized-growth-deployment-2026.json)
then supplies a firm outcome: Hygon reported RMB 9.099 billion in first-half
2026 consolidated revenue, 66.52% year-over-year growth, and DCU deployment in
multiple key industries and hundreds of application scenarios, with more than
400 mainstream large models adapted. The report strengthens the domestic
adaptation-to-deployment bridge, while leaving DCU-specific revenue, customer
switching, displacement, and causal export-control effects unresolved.

The [Hygon large-model cluster realization record](records/us-china-hygon-dcu-large-model-cluster-realization-2026.json)
extends the chain from deployment to reported operation: Hygon said a
10-trillion-parameter model had achieved preliminary stable operation on a
cluster of tens of thousands of Hygon DCUs, alongside a DTK stack with more
than 2,000 operators and 100-plus framework components. The evidence is useful
as an operational milestone, but lacks independent benchmarks, customer
acceptance, utilization, cost, or substitution evidence.

The [Hygon Kimi K3 adaptation record](records/us-china-hygon-dcu-kimi-k3-adaptation-deployment-verification-2026.json)
adds a model-specific realization stage: a 2026 listed-company interim report
states that Hygon completed full-process Kimi K3 adaptation, deployment, and
performance verification on its DCU platform. The report does not supply
independent benchmark values, a named customer or site, production duration,
utilization, or evidence that the model displaced another accelerator.

The [Hygon Kimi K3 performance-claim record](records/us-china-hygon-dcu-kimi-k3-reported-12-percent-performance-loss-2026.json)
adds a quantitative performance-claim stage: a technical-news report relayed
an official Hygon figure of performance loss within 12 percent and described a
Sugon 8000 distributed training and multi-card inference path. Because the
baseline, metric definition, test configuration, and artifacts are absent,
this is a vendor-reported result rather than independent parity evidence.

The [Hunan public-system integration record](records/us-china-hunan-compute-platform-hygon-dcu-resource-integration-2026.json)
adds an external infrastructure endpoint. Hunan's provincial platform reported
trial operation, Hygon DCU compatibility within a multi-accelerator scheduler,
and first-batch resource access for the Changsha National Supercomputing Center,
operator compute centers, and Changsha AI Innovation Center. The evidence moves
the chain toward public-system acceptance while leaving utilization, procurement
value, workload success, and substitution unmeasured.

The [Changsha AI-center workload record](records/us-china-hunan-changsha-ai-center-hygon-dcu-model-deployment-2026.json)
adds a named application endpoint. Hunan's inventory describes a 200P mixed
Ascend/Hygon center whose Hygon-based module sits alongside an Ascend module,
and lists support for a visual foundation model, an agricultural Q&A model,
and a tourism model. This is stronger than compatibility alone, but still does
not isolate Hygon workload share, production duration, performance, or
substitution.

The [MOFCOM analog-IC investigation record](records/us-china-mofcom-analog-ic-antidumping-investigation-2025.json)
adds a formal trade-remedy stage after the attributed response. China opened an
anti-dumping investigation into specified U.S. analog IC imports following a
domestic-industry application and reported preliminary claims of rising import
quantity and falling import prices. This is not evidence that the VEU removals
caused the investigation; it is a separate institutional response in the same
semiconductor conflict. Final ruling, duty, import, firm, and strategic effects
remain open.

The [MOFCOM anti-discrimination investigation record](records/us-china-mofcom-integrated-circuit-antidiscrimination-investigation-2025.json)
adds a broader legal-contestation stage after the VEU objection and analog-IC
trade-remedy initiation. China opened a formal investigation into U.S.
integrated-circuit measures, specified a legal basis, and invited affected
parties to participate. This is observable institutional action, but not a
finding, remedy, direct causal response to the VEU removals, or strategic effect.

## Latest care rotation: a work incentive can create an unfilled care gap

The [EITC parent-care-gap record](records/us-nber-eitc-parent-care-gap-2026.json)
adds a policy-to-care substitution test. Using Health and Retirement Study data
and simulated EITC generosity, it estimates that a $500 increase in EITC
generosity reduces care from eligible daughters to older parents by 13–24 hours
per month, driven by less intensive care. The study reports no increase in
financial transfers and finds that other children, spouses, or paid providers do
not fill the measured gap.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| NBER EITC-parent-care study | Simulated policy variation, family-care measures, and tested replacement channels | work-contingent benefit → daughter care allocation → reduced intensive care → no measured replacement in tested channels | care quality, unmet need, health, safety, daughter earnings, formal services, and total household welfare |

This is a bounded U.S. mechanism, not proof that every work incentive harms
older parents. It complements the family-care, paid-leave, and childcare
records by showing that the missing middle can be substitution failure rather
than simply insufficient time measurement.

## Latest redistribution rotation: similar cash does not imply similar recovery currency

The [NBER parental-investment record](records/us-nber-unconditional-cash-parental-investment-heterogeneity-2025.json)
adds a randomized cash-to-adaptation comparison. A large multisite U.S. study
reports similar treatment effects on net household income, earnings, and total
expenditures across racial and ethnic groups, but different child-investment
routes: Latino families increased child-focused spending by nearly one-third of
the transfer without detectable changes in maternal employment or time with
children, while Black families reduced maternal work hours and increased time
with children on early-learning activities without higher child-focused
expenditures.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| NBER unconditional-cash study | Randomized monthly transfer with subgroup-specific money/time outcomes | cash support → subgroup-specific spending or time reallocation → distinct care/investment route | treatment amount, compliance, care quality, stress, durable employment, broader household welfare, trust, and action |

This is a counterexample to treating redistribution as a single recovery
currency. The subgroup pattern should not be read as a racial mechanism or
generalized to all cash programs; it shows why the same transfer can buy goods,
buy time, or leave measured child time unchanged depending on household
context and the income source's meaning.

## Latest household-room rotation: immediate relief is not balance-sheet recovery

The [NBER consumption/balance-sheet record](records/us-nber-unconditional-cash-consumption-balance-sheets-2024.json)
adds a two-state randomized test of cash support and durability. Assignment to
$1,000 per month rather than $50 per month for three years increased household
expenditures by at least $300 per month, with the largest effects in housing,
food, and car expenses. Housing-unit and neighborhood mobility, self-reported
financial health, and credit scores also increased. Yet asset gains were offset
by higher debt, producing a near-zero net-worth effect; credit limits,
delinquencies, utilization, bankruptcies, and foreclosures did not change.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| NBER two-state cash-transfer study | Random assignment, spending, mobility, credit, assets, and debt | transfer → short-run spending/room and financial-health improvement → no clear durable net-worth gain | bill-level relief, care/time substitution, adequacy, stress persistence, trust, political action, and exit |

This is a direct warning against collapsing consumption, mobility, credit,
financial health, and net worth into one recovery variable. The transfer can
create usable room now while leaving the household's longer-run balance sheet
largely unchanged.

## Latest care-quality rotation: better inputs can coexist with reported stress

The [NBER parenting/stress record](records/us-nber-unconditional-cash-parenting-stress-2025.json)
adds a care-quality counterexample from a randomized three-year transfer of
$1,000 per month versus $50. The treatment increased spending on children and
reported parenting behavior, with the largest parenting improvements among the
lowest-income families and increased use and quality of non-parental childcare.
At the same time, parents reported more child developmental difficulties and
stress; the authors note that closer monitoring may partly explain that result.
Parental satisfaction and several other household outcomes did not change.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| NBER parenting/cash study | Random assignment with parenting, care, child-stress, mobility, and household outcomes | cash → spending/supervision/formal-care inputs → mixed observed care and wellbeing signals | clinical meaning of reported stress, care quality measurement, persistence, household time, trust, and political action |

This prevents “more parenting,” “more childcare,” or “more spending” from being
treated as interchangeable with improved wellbeing. A policy can improve an
input, increase detection of problems, and leave satisfaction or downstream
outcomes unchanged at the same time.

The [cash-transfer recovery-currency crosswalk](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/cash-transfer-recovery-currency-crosswalk-v1.md)
now consolidates this redistribution branch. Across the retained U.S. studies,
cash can become consumption, mobility, credit, child spending, maternal time,
formal childcare, or reduced unpaid care; these currencies cannot be pooled as
one recovery score. The common missing middle is still same-household adequacy
and persistence, followed by fairness/attribution, trust, political action, or
exit.

The [NBER family-income/voting record](records/us-nber-family-income-intergenerational-voting-2018.json)
adds the first retained U.S. cash-to-voting bridge in this redistribution
rotation. Exogenous unconditional cash transfers increased adult children's
voting propensity among those raised in initially poorer families, while
parents and children from initially better-off families showed no effect. The
effect is therefore delayed and intergenerational: it supports a resource,
human-capital, or social-capital route into later participation, not a claim
that a current payment immediately changes the adult recipient's turnout,
trust, approval, or vote choice.

The [APSR Moving to Opportunity record](records/comparative-apsr-moving-to-opportunity-voting-2024.json)
adds a place-based counterexample. Random housing-voucher offers reduced
neighborhood poverty and improved some child socioeconomic outcomes, but later
children's turnout did not increase, adult effects were null, and teen voting
and registration declined under both voucher conditions. This shows that
material/place improvement is not sufficient for political participation:
developmental stage, disruption, dosage, social integration, and persistence
remain part of the mechanism.

The [Social Problems welfare-participation record](records/us-social-problems-welfare-receipt-political-participation-2022.json)
adds a contemporary program-design counterexample. In nationally representative
young-adult data with fixed-effects models, cash assistance showed little
association with participation; food stamps were positively associated with
registration but had countervailing voting associations by political
attentiveness. This is not a causal transfer estimate, but it prevents the
political endpoint from being coded as one welfare effect: benefit type,
administrative experience, attention, registration, turnout, and trust remain
distinct.

The [NBER welfare-reform voting record](records/us-nber-welfare-reform-womens-voting-2016.json)
adds a positive adult-voting contrast: greater exposure to 1990s reform was
associated with roughly a four-percentage-point relative voting advantage among
low-income women, concentrated in presidential elections and stronger in
Democratic states and states with stronger work incentives. The proposed path
runs through employment, education, and income. This should not be averaged
with the NBER EITC employment-instrument negative estimate: benefit retraction,
work-incentive design, exposure, comparison group, election type, and estimand
differ.

The [Health Affairs Scholar safety-net stigma record](records/us-health-affairs-safety-net-stigma-government-perceptions-2024.json)
adds direct trust/meaning measurement to this political branch. SNAP
participation and EITC receipt were associated with greater reported social
stigma, overall government perceptions did not differ, and food-insecure SNAP
respondents additionally showed program stigma and discrimination. The study is
cross-sectional and convenience/snowball based, so it is not a causal or
national trust estimate. Its cross-source value is the boundary itself:
material support, stigma, government perception, and political action are
different currencies and should not be inferred from one another.

The [Oregon Medicaid voter-participation record](records/us-oregon-medicaid-lottery-voter-participation-2019.json)
adds a causal public-system-to-action contrast. Randomized coverage access
raised November 2008 presidential turnout by 2.5 percentage points, but did not
raise turnout in later elections through November 2010. The early effect is
consistent with a route through registration, administrative contact, health or
financial security, or election-specific mobilization; the study does not
identify which route and does not show a generalized trust change. It therefore
strengthens the action endpoint while preserving the open meaning and durability
arrows.

The [UAS economic-insecurity record](records/us-oxford-economic-insecurity-political-preferences-2023.json)
adds an observational mobilization counterexample. Greater income-history
insecurity predicted higher reported voting probability and Trump support in the
2016 U.S. election context, conditional on current resources, while other
surfaces show lower contact or action under hardship. Because the insecurity
index uses modeled income bands and the outcomes are reported probabilities,
this does not identify causality or a uniform response. It does establish that
the material-to-action arrow can point toward mobilization or polarization as
well as withdrawal, depending on timing, identity, attribution, and outcome.

The [urban-poor government-contact record](records/us-social-problems-urban-poor-government-contact-activism-2001.json)
adds a local encounter mechanism: among 462 low-income respondents, severe
hardship and formative contact with government agents were associated with
greater willingness to participate, while resources and demographics also
structured activism. This is not a causal or national estimate, but it makes
the interface visible. Hardship does not carry one political meaning apart from
the institution, agent, explanation, and efficacy experience through which it
is encountered.

The [Social Security and intergenerational-migration record](records/us-nber-social-security-intergenerational-migration-2026.json)
adds a distinct historical causal setting. Using early Social Security coverage
variation across industries within occupations and linked parent--child records,
the study estimates that greater predicted parental coverage led sons to move
farther from childhood homes, earn more, and later live in better neighborhoods;
the corresponding migration, earnings, and neighborhood effects were not found
for daughters, whom the authors describe as providing less easily replaceable
support. The historical design does not observe every exchange of care, money,
or obligation and cannot establish a present-day national Social Security
effect, a single migration mechanism, or that moving itself produced each later
outcome. It nevertheless shows that family support can shape the practical
mobility margin rather than merely offset a contemporaneous care gap.

## Latest platform rotation: formal access restriction does not equal complete control

Australia’s eSafety implementation record adds a policy-to-platform-action
bridge. Platforms reported about 4.7 million age-restricted accounts removed or
restricted by mid-December 2025 and more than 300,000 additional accounts
prevented from signing in by early March 2026. In eSafety’s pulse survey of 898
parents and carers, reported account ownership among children aged 8–15 fell
from 49.7% before the restriction to 31.3% afterward. Yet among children with
pre-existing accounts, substantial platform-specific retention remained, and
eSafety flagged five platforms for age-assurance and new-account-prevention
concerns.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Australian eSafety implementation | Platform-reported account actions, parent pulse survey, regulator testing and compliance concerns | legal rule → platform process → reported access change and residual retention → enforcement | unique-child exposure, privacy and false-positive cost, migration, safety, wellbeing, trust, and durable cultural effect |

This is comparative implementation evidence, not a U.S. estimate or a causal
child-safety result. It complements the existing platform-use and control
records by showing that aggregate action counts and lived access can diverge.

## Latest infrastructure rotation: emergency reserve capacity is conditional on operation

GAO-26-106918 adds an operational state-capacity layer to the atlas. As of
December 2025, DOE estimated effective Strategic Petroleum Reserve drawdown at
61% of design rate and fill at 56%. The reserve had met drawdown directives to
date, but aging infrastructure, construction outages, low inventory, and
limited commercial pipeline and storage spare capacity constrained the route
from nominal reserve to realized emergency market effect.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| GAO SPR capability review | Point-in-time effective fill and drawdown estimates, reserve operating history, and reported infrastructure/market constraints | reserve asset and maintenance → effective emergency operating capability → conditional distribution capacity | release-specific prices, refinery receipt, household/firm incidence, replenishment, deterrence, and geopolitical effect |

This is an operational-capability finding, not evidence of a current shortage or
of energy-security success. It complements the Puerto Rico grid and
transmission-capacity records while preserving different infrastructure,
commodity, and downstream units.

## Latest geopolitical rotation: economic coercion can become a trade and retaliation pathway

NBER Working Paper 35077 adds a comparative state-to-market bridge. Its
GDELT-based monthly bilateral indicator separates kinetic fighting, military
posture, sanctions-context hostility, and routine diplomacy. The paper reports
that the trade-weighted share of hostility routed through sanctions contexts
nearly doubled over the past decade. In its gravity-trade estimates, kinetic
and trade-context hostility reduce bilateral trade, routine diplomacy has no
detected aggregate trade effect, and directed retaliation compounds losses over
several months. A pre-escalation counterfactual places roughly $334 billion of
bilateral trade at risk, with the U.S.–China pair accounting for about half.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| NBER bilateral conflict-risk panel | GDELT events calibrated to human-curated ground truth; decomposed hostility and gravity-trade estimates | sanctions-context hostility → bilateral trade disruption → lagged retaliation | U.S.-specific firm and household incidence, product substitution, alliance behavior, legitimacy, and strategic success |

This is a comparative mechanism and counterfactual estimate, not an episode-level
proof that a particular U.S. coercive action worked. It complements the CRS
timeline of 2025 foreign retaliation while keeping government response, firm
compliance, public cost, and political outcome as separate stages.

## Latest public-systems rotation: public benefit can coexist with incomplete individual recovery

The Los Angeles shelter study adds a public-good layer to the atlas. Shelter
availability and unsheltered exposure are connected to neighborhood crime,
psychiatric emergency-room visits, and short-run mortality, while future
homelessness does not improve relative to outreach or other non-shelter
services. The result prevents a single “shelter success” endpoint from hiding
different beneficiaries and clocks.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Los Angeles shelter availability | Daily shelter beds/occupancy linked to crime, hospital, and mortality records; availability shocks used for comparison | shelter capacity → public safety and short-run health benefits; shelter entry → short-run mortality | access, safety, service quality, durable housing, future homelessness, trust, and collective response |

This is a public-capacity and health realization finding, not evidence that
temporary shelter is adequate or that all residents receive equal benefit.

## Latest depth rotation: average recovery can hide a negative subgroup

The Nevada job-search-assistance experiment adds a durable work-to-housing
route. A randomized offer combining counseling, search planning, enforcement,
and referrals is followed through administrative employment, earnings, UI,
disability, tax, and homeownership outcomes for up to eight years. Average
outcomes improve, but roughly 40% of the sample is estimated to have negative
long-run employment treatment effects.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nevada randomized UI intervention | 4,673 offers in a 32,751-person UI population; later employment, earnings, UI, tax, disability, and homeownership outcomes | bundled public employment route → heterogeneous long-run work and housing-related outcomes | component effects, job quality, health, care burden, housing adequacy, coercion, trust, and action |

This is a durable outcome bridge, not proof that counseling alone caused the
gain or that aggregate employment gains equal welfare gains for every person.

## Latest depth rotation: emergency assistance can reach a later work outcome without proving housing recovery

The [NBER emergency-financial-assistance record](records/us-nber-emergency-financial-assistance-work-outcomes-2026.json)
adds a stronger same-study bridge than a cross-sectional association: Chicago
homelessness-prevention hotline callers experiencing a housing crisis are
linked to federal tax records, and quasi-random variation in funding
availability supports a comparison of families receiving and not receiving
roughly $900–$1,000 in one-time rent or utility help. The study reports about
5,300 eligible callers referred to funds versus about 2,800 not referred, with
estimated receipt of 71% versus 12%; employment and earnings gains are reported
up to four years later, especially among the lowest earners.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| NBER emergency financial assistance | Hotline callers linked to tax records; assistance availability used for comparison; later employment and earnings gains reported | housing-crisis assistance route → later labor-market outcome | housing continuity, same-job retention, job quality, adequacy, displacement, household security, trust, and action |

This is an important open-middle advance, but earnings are not a recovery
score. Address-change evidence is suggestive but not robust, and the source
does not establish that the housing break was prevented or that the same
household's housing, health, debt, or meaning stabilized.

The [emergency-rental-assistance lottery record](records/us-nber-emergency-rental-assistance-lottery-four-cities-2024-2025.json)
adds a pandemic-era intervention counterexample. Across five lottery-based
programs in four cities distributing more than $200 million, assistance
increased rent payment and reduced reported eviction concerns; self-reported
mental and physical-health improvements were suggestive. Yet the study detected
little effect on its housing-stability and financial-distress measures. That is
not evidence that assistance failed: eviction moratoria, expanded safety-net
programs, and tenant-favoring market conditions may have weakened the usual
link between rent payment and displacement, while rent payment or reduced
anxiety can matter on their own. The evaluation does not establish national
eviction prevention, durable tenure, subsequent bills, debt, health, or
household recovery beyond the program-specific follow-up.

## Latest cultural rotation: local social infrastructure is a resource, not a guaranteed outcome

The American Neighbor Survey adds a place-level meaning and capacity layer to
the atlas. It reports regular neighbor conversation and neighborhood
problem-solving alongside trust, third-place access, and emergency childcare
comfort, with visible education and housing gradients. This supports a bounded
proposition: social infrastructure can create opportunities for contact and
mutual aid, but usable support is distributed unevenly and cannot be inferred
from amenities or stated goodwill alone.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| American Neighbor Survey | 40% report regular neighbor conversation, 40% neighborhood problem-solving, and 72% fair/great neighbor trust; 27% with no listed third places versus 43% with six or more report weekly neighbor conversation | place resources and proximity → contact/trust → stated informal capacity and belonging | selection, actual aid, time/money burden, adequacy, recovery, and collective or political action |

This is a weighted cross-sectional panel survey, not a causal neighborhood
experiment or a household crisis episode. It complements the existing Pew
neighbor-trust layer without pooling the two surveys.

The Census 2023 SIPP child-wellbeing report adds a family-time and cultural
opportunity distinction. Reference-parent reading and family-dinner measures
were broadly similar to their 2014 comparison values, while organized activity
participation was uneven: among children ages 6–17, 1.2% of those in families
below poverty participated in sports, lessons, and clubs versus 8.8% of those
at 200% of poverty or higher. This supports a bounded proposition: relatively
stable routine does not make access to organized social and cultural
opportunity equal.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Census 2023 SIPP child-wellbeing report | Age-specific parent-child routine, outings, organized activity, school-engagement, material-hardship, and poverty-gradient measures | household resources and time context → reported routine and access to organized activities → unequal opportunity surface | activity cost, transport, supply, preference, quality, belonging, confidence, educational attainment, and same-child later outcome |

The measures use different child-age universes and reference periods; stable
dinner or reading rates do not establish equal relationship quality or support,
and the activity gradient does not identify why participation differs. See the
[SIPP child cultural-participation record](records/us-census-sipp-child-day-cultural-participation-2023.json).

The CDC Community & Connection indicators add a measurement correction to the
social-infrastructure lane. The page reports that 58% of youth in its
2021–2023 indicator received needed social and emotional support, 82% of adults
did so in a separate 2021 indicator, and 12% of adults reported health-related
difficulty participating in social activities in 2024. The usable proposition
is not an age trend: perceived support and practical ability to participate are
different social outcomes, and neither is interchangeable with loneliness,
delivered help, or belonging.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| CDC Community & Connection summary indicators | Separate youth and adult self-reported support indicators and an adult health-related social-participation-difficulty indicator | perceived support and participation constraint as distinct community-connection surfaces | common-year/person comparison, adequacy or reciprocity of help, loneliness, invitation/access barrier, delivered support, belonging, civic action, and recovery |

The summary page does not retain one linked survey sample or full denominators.
Higher adult reported support than youth support is not a valid age comparison,
and participation difficulty does not identify a particular barrier or prove
isolation. See the [CDC community-connection record](records/us-cdc-community-connection-support-participation-2026.json).

The [CIRCLE youth political-homes record](records/us-circle-political-homes-youth-civic-capacity-2026.json)
adds a civic-belonging version of the social-infrastructure lane. Young people
reporting access to a political home had about 20-percentage-point higher civic
action and civic self-efficacy than peers without reported access. CIRCLE
describes those settings as potentially supplying safe third spaces, belonging,
civic information, food, financial compensation, and transportation, while
also reporting a relationship between strong connections and lower mental-health
costs of political involvement. The result makes practical support, belonging,
self-efficacy, wellbeing, and action visible as separate but adjacent surfaces;
it is not a randomized assignment, a turnout estimate, or evidence that a
political home caused later participation, durable trust, or policy influence.

The [CDC loneliness/social-support record](records/us-cdc-loneliness-social-support-2022.json)
adds a single analytic sample with a different social-connection surface. In
the 2022 BRFSS analysis of 218,915 adults in 26 states, 32.1% reported always,
usually, or sometimes feeling socially isolated and 24.1% reported sometimes,
rarely, or never receiving needed social and emotional support. Isolation was
43.3% among ages 18–34 and 47.9% among households below $25,000; reported
prevalence was also higher among bisexual and transgender subgroups. Adjusted
associations with frequent stress, frequent mental distress, and depression
history do not show that isolation caused those outcomes, nor do they identify
the social disruption, help received, care route, belonging, civic action, or
recovery path for any person.

The standardized ATUS eldercare-provider comparison adds an everyday time
profile rather than a survey attitude. In both 2024 and 2025 diary samples,
providers had about 64 more eldercare minutes than matched-cell nonproviders,
plus more household-work, care-for-people, and travel time and less
socializing/communicating time. The age, sex, and labor-force-status
standardization preserves the profile across two annual samples, while the
paid-work difference remains imprecise.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| ATUS 2024 and 2025 standardized diary comparisons | Provider/nonprovider differences in eldercare, household work, care for people, travel, socializing/communication, secondary childcare, and paid-work minutes | eldercare-provider status → distinct one-day time allocation/social-participation profile | care onset, recipient need, health, family structure, schedule control, employer accommodation, causal displacement, adequacy, wellbeing, recovery, trust, or action |

The years are separate samples, not a panel; residual selection can reflect
family, health, occupation, diary timing, or recipient need. Lower
socializing/communication time does not by itself show isolation or worse
wellbeing. See the [standardized eldercare-provider record](records/us-atus-eldercare-provider-standardized-2024-2025.json).

SHED's 2025 care/living-arrangements tables add the household organization
layer that neither the Census child-care problem screen nor one-day ATUS diary
can supply. Thirty-three percent of adults reported living with at least two
adult generations. Among parents of children under 13, 25% reported paid
childcare in the prior week and 44% reported unpaid childcare; paid-care use
was lower in the lowest income group, but that difference cannot distinguish
unaffordability from unpaid support, nonemployment, availability, or preference.
The same source reports an 18% adult unpaid-caregiving prevalence and an
association between regular adult care and not having paid work among prime-age
adults. Care arrangement, gendered caretaker role, work status, and meaning
are separate surfaces, not a causal household trajectory.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Federal Reserve 2025 SHED care/living-arrangements tables | Co-residence, paid/unpaid child care, child-care payment comparison, caretaker-role and adult-care/work-status distributions | household composition and resource position → reported care-arrangement and work-status surface | dated need, provider availability/quality, hours, schedule control, employer response, causal work displacement, family reciprocity, recovery, and later trust/action |

The SHED, Census, and ATUS records use different recall windows, units, and
conditional bases. Their combined implication is limited but important: care
capacity is assembled through co-residence, paid services, unpaid networks, and
time adaptation; none is a direct proxy for adequate, chosen, or durable care.
See the [2025 SHED care/living-arrangements record](records/us-federal-reserve-care-living-arrangements-2025.json).

The [GAO unpaid-household-work record](records/us-gao-unpaid-household-work-2026.json)
adds a national accounting surface beneath those arrangements. GAO estimates
that most people spent almost four hours a day on unpaid household work in
2021–2024; using selected 2024 wage proxies, it values that work at roughly
$70 per day and at least $5.6–$6.0 trillion annually (19.2%–20.2% of GDP).
Women made up 54% of people performing unpaid work versus 51% of the population,
and women and married people spent more time on childcare in the reported
comparisons. Replacement-value accounting makes a large hidden productive
capacity visible, but it does not measure care quality, exhaustion, reciprocity,
choice, bargaining, paid-work displacement, health, household security, or
whether outsourcing would improve wellbeing.

The work-control corpus adds an upstream opportunity layer. An NBER field
experiment connecting college students with 10,000 working professionals found
that female students received substantially more work-life-balance information
than male students in broad career exchanges, with a gap that persisted when
they explicitly asked about work-life balance; the paper links this information
pattern to career intentions. This is direct evidence that career information
can be gendered before a job, wage, care arrangement, or employer policy is
observed—not evidence of later labor-market inequality by itself.

Two different AI/work records sharpen the next stages. SHED reports that AI use
among workers differs by education and reported task control, alongside softer
job-mobility measures; an NBER occupation-level framework separates potential
AI exposure from adaptive capacity and retains revised working-paper versus
published-chapter counts separately. Neither establishes individual
displacement, retraining success, job quality, or bargaining power.

| Evidence unit | What is directly visible | What remains open |
|---|---|---|
| Randomized career-information exchange | Requester-gender differences in work-life information and a reported career-intention mechanism | Actual occupational choice, employer practices, pay, hours, care, discrimination, and later wellbeing |
| 2025 SHED employment/AI/control tables | Labor-market friction, mobility, schedule, and education/control-stratified reported AI use | Employer policy, task exposure, productivity, pay, displacement, and causal control effect |
| Occupation-level AI exposure/adaptive-capacity framework | Exposure/capacity overlap and clerical/support concentration under stated coverage versions | Person-level resilience, job loss, retraining, earnings, care constraints, and usable transition options |

The supported subtheme is **anticipated work is socially and institutionally
mediated before it becomes a realized career outcome**. The studies do not
share a sample or a causal chain. See the [career-information/advice record](records/us-nber-gender-gaps-career-information-advice-2026.json), [SHED employment/AI-control record](records/us-federal-reserve-employment-ai-control-2025.json), and [AI exposure/adaptive-capacity record](records/us-nber-ai-exposure-adaptive-capacity-2026.json).

AP-NORC's State of the Facts comparison adds an information-legitimacy layer.
Reported trust in federal-government information fell between its 2024 and 2026
issue-specific estimates for inflation/cost of living, health care, and
elections/politics, while separate 2026 measures reported difficulty evaluating
candidate claims and low confidence in the reliability or easy verification of
AI-generated information. This places institutional information trust,
candidate claim evaluation, and AI-information judgment on separate surfaces.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| AP-NORC 2024/2026 State of the Facts survey comparisons | Issue-specific reported government-information trust change; 2026 perceived candidate-information/claim difficulty and AI-information reliability/verification attitudes | institutional information environment → reported trust and evaluation-difficulty surface | factual accuracy, source exposure, search time, AI use, correction attempt, persuasion, turnout, vote, policy action, or causal explanation for trust change |

The items do not measure government performance or factual accuracy directly.
Question context, salience, political interpretation, and composition can shape
the comparison; perceived difficulty may reflect volume, interest, access, or
scrutiny. See the [AP-NORC information-trust record](records/us-apnorc-state-of-facts-information-trust-2026.json).

The Gallup/With Honor anniversary survey adds a distinct national-meaning
route. Among adults who planned to celebrate the 250th anniversary, 66% said
they intended to spend time with family or friends, 32% intended to join a
neighborhood or community event, and 17% intended to attend an official
America250 program. In the separate civic-or-educational recognition subgroup,
history learning and listening to personal stories were more common than arts,
service, or travel routes. Together with the local-infrastructure evidence,
this supports a bounded subtheme: cultural legitimacy can be practiced through
ordinary relationships and local stories as well as through official ceremony.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gallup/With Honor 250th-anniversary survey | Planned recognition: 66% of all adults planned some celebration; activity intentions among planner subgroups, including family/friends, community event, official program, learning, and personal stories | national commemoration → intended relational, local, educational, or formal ritual route → stated meaning/pride surface | completed participation, cross-group contact, learning, agreement about history, institutional trust, civic action, and durable identity |

These are nonexclusive plans, not a participation count or a measure of shared
political belief. Family celebration can coexist with disagreement about
history, institutions, or policy; official-program nonattendance is not civic
withdrawal. See the [Gallup anniversary meaning record](records/us-gallup-america250-social-ritual-meaning-2026.json).

AP-NORC's separate America 250 survey sharpens the distinction between shared
ideals, perceived threat, and expected opportunity. Respondents widely named
speech and voting rights as important to national identity while differing on
whether those rights face a major threat; only 33% said the hard-work pathway
of the American Dream still holds. These are attitudes about meaning and
security, not a legal-rights audit, mobility measure, or action record. See the
[AP-NORC America 250 identity/rights record](records/us-apnorc-america-250-identity-rights-dream-2026.json).

## Latest trust-and-care rotation: institutional meaning can enter a care decision without proving access

The NBER health-care trust study makes a specific behavioral middle stage
visible. In a nationally representative adult survey, higher trust in the
health-care system was associated with greater reported and anticipated
preventive and elective care seeking and with less care avoidance. Its model
also distinguishes trust in doctors, nurses, hospitals, insurers, and
public-health entities rather than treating trust as a single attitude. That
supports a bounded social/institutional theme: the meaning people assign to
particular care institutions can be associated with whether they approach or
avoid care.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| NBER nationally representative health-care trust survey | Entity-specific trust, retrospective and anticipated care-seeking/avoidance, demographic and political heterogeneity, and model-based counterfactuals | institution-specific trust/expected usefulness → reported or anticipated care-seeking versus avoidance | causal trust change, appointment completion, affordability, provider availability, treatment quality, health outcome, remedy, durable trust, and political action |

This is survey-and-model evidence, not a trust intervention. Lower reported
care seeking may also reflect cost, time, transport, provider supply, health
need, or prior care quality; anticipated care is not completed treatment. See
the [health-care trust record](records/us-nber-trust-health-care-seeking-behavior-2026.json).

Pew's separate source-use and influencer studies make the upstream information
environment visible. In the 5,111-adult source-use survey, 85% said they got
health information from providers at least sometimes, 66% from people with a
similar health issue, 36% from social media, and 22% from AI chatbots. Half
reported at least some difficulty judging accuracy and 54% reported at least
some difficulty knowing what to trust when information conflicted. The
influencer study separately found that 40% of adults ever got health/wellness
information from influencers or podcasts; the profiles mix self-described
health professionals, coaches, and entrepreneurs. Read with the NBER result,
these sources identify a source landscape → credibility judgment → possible
care-seeking route, not a same-person causal chain.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pew health-information source survey | Source-use frequency, perceived accuracy, conflicting information, and difficulty judging/trusting information | provider, peer, platform, or AI information environment → reported credibility problem | particular claim, verification, clinical discussion, decision, care, spending, adherence, or health result |
| Pew health/wellness influencer profile and audience surveys | 6,828 prominent influencer profiles; self-described professional/coach/entrepreneur identities; audience reach, trust, and perceived understanding | platform authority cues and commercial/professional identities → audience interpretation | verified credentials, sponsorship, accuracy, individualized advice, purchase, clinical decision, and health result |

The records must not be pooled with the NBER trust survey: their samples,
questions, and measures differ. Provider information may be more highly rated
yet still costly, delayed, or inaccessible; use of peer, social, or AI sources
does not establish misinformation or harm. See the [health-information source
record](records/us-pew-health-information-source-trust-2025.json) and
[health/wellness influencer record](records/us-pew-health-wellness-influencer-authority-2025.json).

Pew's topic-diversity survey broadens that information-route result beyond
health. The main reported source differs by subject: news media lead for
foreign policy, immigration, the economy, and technology/media regulation;
government sources are jointly most common with news media for Social Security;
social media and friends/family remain visible but smaller primary routes in
the published topic distributions. The atlas can therefore treat information
infrastructure as topic-specific: a person may encounter different authorities
when deciding about a consumer, public-benefit, political, or geopolitical
question.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pew topic-specific information-source survey | Self-reported primary information source for health care, elections, immigration, economy, Social Security, foreign policy, and technology/media regulation | topic → declared information route among news, government, social, personal network, school, other, or none | actual exposure, accuracy, ranking, trust, persuasion, consumer choice, policy understanding, civic action, or legitimacy |

Naming a main source is not exclusive use, agreement, or decision reliance.
Different topic bases and cross-sectional self-report prevent a claim that one
route caused a later belief or behavior. See the [topic-diversity information
source record](records/us-pew-information-source-topic-diversity-2026.json).

The AI-assistant choice study adds a platform-choice and privacy-control
surface. In its weighted U.S. adult AI-user sample, about 43% reported
intentionally routing different tasks to more than one assistant; platform
choice and trust varied by task and user experience. More than 80% reported
concern about conversation-data use, while the study's regression associated
knowledge of a training policy more strongly with reported protective-tool use
than an incremental increase in concern. Its embedded choice experiment also
measures stated willingness to pay for different data-handling conditions.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Weighted AI-assistant-user survey and embedded choice experiment | Task-specific primary assistant, reported multihoming, trust rankings, privacy concern/knowledge/tool use, and hypothetical data-handling preferences | task and platform environment → stated routing, trust, knowledge, and privacy preference/action surface | telemetry, actual payment, privacy disclosure/loss, causal protective behavior, durable switching, provider retention, dependence, wellbeing, or productivity |

Reported multihoming can coexist with a dominant default assistant, and stated
willingness to pay is not a purchase. The cross-sectional survey cannot show
whether use caused trust or policy knowledge caused protective behavior. See
the [AI-assistant choice, trust, and privacy record](records/us-ai-assistant-platform-choice-trust-privacy-2026.json).

The comparative NBER cookie-consent experiment adds a causal interface
mechanism, not a U.S. prevalence estimate. It randomized consent-banner design
and found that placing options behind extra clicks shifted users toward more
accessible alternatives; the associated structural analysis treats repeated
site-specific consent decisions as an attention/time cost. This shows why a
formal data choice can be shaped by the route through which it is presented.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Comparative cookie-consent field experiment and structural model | Randomized banner/interface effects on acceptance, dismissal, and accessible alternatives; modeled site-versus-browser consent/time comparison | consent design/default/friction → observed interface choice and repeated-attention-cost mechanism | U.S. prevalence, informed consent, comprehension, actual data use/protection, service trade-off, fraud, welfare, or durable exit |

An acceptance or dismissal is not an informed privacy choice, and the modeled
browser-level benefit relies on preference, time-cost, and data-value
assumptions. See the [cookie-consent choice-architecture record](records/comparative-nber-cookie-consent-choice-architecture-2025.json).

The [GAO digital-homebuying record](records/us-gao-homebuying-proptech-oversight-fair-lending-2025.json)
adds a housing-market version of that interface problem. Its review spans online
home search, automated valuations, automated underwriting, and electronic
closing: these tools may supply information, financing options, lower costs, or
faster mortgage approval. But the same route can collect sensitive data for
personalization and marketing, enable algorithmic advertising or chatbots that
may steer protected-class consumers, and make valuation inputs or errors hard
to inspect or contest. GAO also found product-specific oversight concentrated
at FHFA while other reviewed agencies generally had not conducted it, alongside
an open recommendation to clarify enterprise fair-lending expectations. This is
not an estimate of discriminatory treatment, privacy loss, loan approval,
affordability, completed purchase, or stable housing; it establishes that
transaction speed and information access do not close the contestability and
fairness stages.

The [federal AI privacy-guidance record](records/us-gao-ai-privacy-guidance-gaps-2026.json)
adds a public-system version of the same control gap. GAO's expert panel
identified ten selected challenges spanning security, transparency, secondary
use, consent, privacy technology, performance measurement, impact assessment,
workforce capability, and privacy-performance tradeoffs. In its January 2026
comparison, OMB's government-wide guidance fully addressed two of the ten;
GAO found it did not specify the known privacy-risk types agencies should
consider in policy development and issued two open recommendations. This is a
guidance-coverage result, not a score for agency compliance or a count of
breaches, misuse, affected people, privacy harm, trust change, or successful
remedy. Agencies may use controls that the government-wide guidance comparison
does not capture.

The privacy and digital-safety records now add a fuller contestability middle.
California's DROP reports system reach, broker-level statuses, processing
clocks, and deletion by at least one broker for nearly all reported registrants;
Walmart separately reports received, completed, denied, and median-time
privacy-request metrics. GM/OnStar, the TAKE IT DOWN Act, and Google Search
make formal data/removal, exception, deadline, tracking, and escalation routes
visible. The common result is not that every user has recovered control: it is
that request visibility and administrative completion are now observable stages
that must remain separate from verified deletion/removal, no recurrence,
safety, trust, and exit. See the [DROP deletion record](records/us-calprivacy-drop-data-broker-deletion-2026.json), [DROP contestability record](records/us-cppa-drop-status-contestability-2026.json), [Walmart privacy-request record](records/us-walmart-ccpa-privacy-request-completion-2025-2026.json), [GM/OnStar control record](records/us-ftc-gm-onstar-data-control-order-2026.json), [TAKE IT DOWN Act record](records/us-ftc-take-it-down-act-platform-removal-2026.json), and [Google image-removal record](records/us-google-search-explicit-image-removal-control-2026.json).

Gallup's financial-guidance survey adds a social and consumer-information
route. Among U.S. adults who sought guidance, internet research was the most
commonly reported source, while family, professional advisors, and AI were
separate nonexclusive routes. Financially stressed guidance seekers reported
family and AI use more often than financially fulfilled seekers, yet reported
confidence in AI's financial expertise remained low. This identifies guidance
seeking as a mixed human-and-digital support system rather than evidence that
AI has replaced human advice.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gallup/Edward Jones U.S. financial-guidance survey | Reported source mix, financial-fulfillment subgroup differences, and confidence comparisons | financial-guidance need/status → reported family, professional, internet, or AI guidance route → stated confidence surface | question, recommendation, advice quality, decision influence, fee, loss, financial improvement, human substitution, trust change, switching, or recovery |

The guidance sources are nonexclusive and the status/use/confidence measures
are cross-sectional. More family or AI use among stressed respondents may
reflect greater need or multiple-source search, not source effectiveness. See
the [financial-guidance and AI-trust record](records/us-gallup-financial-guidance-ai-trust-2026.json).

A randomized Chilean consumer-credit trial supplies a mechanism comparator,
not a U.S. estimate. Among 112,063 loan seekers, belief elicitation increased
search and produced lower interest rates, while a regulator-linked comparison
tool increased negotiation and lowered rates without increasing search; it also
made participants more likely to borrow. This is a valuable consumer-power
distinction: better price information can improve a term while increasing loan
take-up, so lower rates do not by themselves establish lower total cost,
repayment success, or household security. The experiment's products, market,
and follow-up are not the U.S. guidance survey's population or outcome path.
See the [comparative consumer-credit search/negotiation record](records/comparative-nber-consumer-credit-search-negotiation-2026.json).

For the fuller source-by-source treatment across health, finance, AI, and
public/political information, see [information authority and practical
judgment](US-BROAD-INFORMATION-AUTHORITY-PRACTICAL-JUDGMENT-SYNTHESIS_V1.md).

## Latest rotation: capacity is filtered through selection, maintenance, learning, and validation

The latest rotation adds five different units without treating them as one
population. Together they sharpen a cross-domain proposition:

> A formal offer or resource becomes a lived outcome only after it passes
> through selection, operating capacity, feedback, and meaning. The same
> resource can expand one form of agency while leaving another burden or risk
> untouched.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or estimated | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Federal duplication and fragmentation portfolio | GAO identifies 610 remaining matters, 97 new matters, $774.3B reported cumulative savings/revenue gains, and >$100B potential additional gains | fragmented responsibility → coordination and navigation cost → possible service and accountability consequence | household benefit, service quality, transition cost, and legitimacy |
