| AI public-finance model | Two-stage framework models erosion of labor-income/consumption bases and possible taxation of autonomous production | AI transformation → tax-base and redistribution problem → public capacity and political conflict | realized adoption, revenue, ownership, incidence, feasibility, and legitimacy |
| Public-university admissions metrics | In one large urban public system, high-school GPA predicts first-year outcomes and graduation better than SAT; separate Ivy-Plus evidence differs | metric choice → institution-specific classification and gatekeeping → possible access and legitimacy consequence | admissions reform, fairness, completion causality, earnings, and trust |
| K–12 AI diffusion gaps | School-principal survey finds lower AI integration associated with student disadvantage; training, guidance, and policy lag adoption | AI availability/use → institutional support capacity → unequal integration and possible student/teacher consequence | causal learning, wellbeing, privacy, safety, and durable inequality |
| AI economic forecasts and policy preferences | Five groups broadly expect capability advances; disagreement centers on economic consequences, while experts favor retraining and the public also supports broader income or job interventions | capability expectation → perceived distributional risk → preferred insurance or redistribution → possible legitimacy and action | realized AI effects, policy adoption, household security, and political behavior |
| Cybersecurity-regulation harmonization | Seven selected industry representatives reported overlapping controls, definitions, and incident-reporting burdens; 11 of 12 earlier federal coordination recommendations were implemented and one partially addressed | private infrastructure exposure → regulatory burden and reporting capacity → possible security and service consequence | national prevalence, compliance cost, breach reduction, consumer harm, and service outcome |
| Water-sector cybersecurity oversight | Internet-connected operational technology expands remote access; systems vary in capability and face workforce, legacy, funding, and legal constraints; EPA has a risk plan but identified authority and requirement gaps | digital infrastructure dependence → prevention capacity and governance gap → possible physical-service and public-health exposure | named incident, outage, household burden, compliance, and remediation |
| Medicaid eligibility correction (selected states) | States used training, guidance, and system updates; CMS did not consistently verify plan elements or learn which fixes worked across states | administrative error → correction attempt → incomplete institutional learning loop | individual coverage restoration, continuity, beneficiary time, health, trust, and appeal |
| DOD installation maintenance | $285B estimated backlog; about 80% of identified needs funded against a 90% goal; department-wide effectiveness data are weak | appropriations and maintenance capacity → facility/risk visibility → possible readiness and quality-of-life exposure | realized readiness loss, personnel harm, recruitment/retention, and adversary response |
| Medical-school selection | weak outside options shifted entry cohorts; later cohorts billed more per Medicare beneficiary and used more revenue-generating services without detectable health improvement | labor-market selection → professional incentives and setting → service intensity and measured outcome | patient-level welfare, household cost, coding, trust, and mechanism separation |
| Historical U.S. risk preferences | crop-choice-derived risk estimates correlate with leverage, Liberty Bond participation, local risk sharing, and tractor adoption | inferred risk orientation → different collective and technology choices | preference versus constraint, contemporary transfer, and causal institutional trust |
| Comparative household employment experiment | direct female work experience shifted later employment preferences, with unequal partner-wellbeing effects | lived experience → norm revision and expressed preference | U.S. transfer, durable employment, safety, care substitution, bargaining, and political action |
| AI measurement methodology | AI can make structured variables from unstructured data cheaper, while plausible construct choices can support different conclusions; explicit criteria and validation are required | scalable measurement → construct choice and validation → institutional visibility or allocation | named-system accuracy, subgroup error, appeal, trust, and downstream resource effects |
| AI coding and software production | coding activity rises much faster than projects, releases, and total marketplace usage | tool exposure → task activity → project/release realization → use | quality, review/security work, worker time and pay, firm profit, and consumer value |
| Housing-tax allocation model | tax treatment can lower capitalized prices and young downpayments while raising older holding costs and changing tenure | tax design → price and holding-cost changes → age- and wealth-conditioned housing access | observed reform incidence, rentals, displacement, public services, and political response |
| Jail healthcare accreditation RCT | standards, coordination, screening, and oversight improved in participating jails, with lower 12-month mortality and no added capital or labor inputs | institutional route → operating practice → care and mortality | post-release continuity, trust, detention exposure, vendor-wide performance, and generalizability |
| American Indian heterogeneity essay | pooled averages and changing definitions can hide historical harm, tribal variation, and gains associated with self-governance | historical policy and place → identity, governance, and measurement → observed human-capital and health outcomes | tribal-specific causal mechanisms, current policy effects, privacy, and sovereignty-aware measures |
| Managed-LTSS cross-payer comparison | Florida hospitalization and vaccination changes diverged from New York after managed-LTSS rollout | Medicaid delivery design → preventive care and utilization → Medicare-financed health outcome | individual quality, implementation mechanism, functional status, household burden, and generalizability |
| AI asset-management evidence | AI hedge-fund adoption grows, early outperformance decays, and return comovement is lower than among non-AI peers | disclosure and labor signals → firm capability → performance and strategy structure | causal alpha, investor welfare, leverage, and systemic-risk consequences |
| Emerging-technology governance foresight | GAO reviews neural implants, general-purpose robots, and orbital-debris removal as potentially transformative technologies and identifies unresolved privacy, autonomy, environmental, and legal questions | emerging capability → governance timing, human oversight, and legal capacity → future public and infrastructure consequence | adoption, distribution, realized harm, employment, public acceptance, and geopolitical response |
| Founder-tax risk-sharing model | startup exit value is highly concentrated; accrual taxation trades successful-founder dilution against refundable-credit insurance | venture ownership → tax timing → payoff distribution and entrepreneurial risk | realized reform, employee ownership, innovation, public revenue, and household effects |

The [NBER AI-measurement/validation record](records/us-nber-ai-measurement-validation-inference-2026.json)
grounds the table's methodological row. Its review distinguishes discovery,
construct definition, and observation: AI can turn unstructured text or images
into structured variables at lower cost, while shifting the hard question from
whether a measure can scale to which plausible construct should be used and
how it is validated. The framework requires explicit criteria and designed
validation, but is not a population estimate or an evaluation of any named AI
system. It does not establish accuracy, subgroup error, discrimination, appeal,
trust, agency practice, or downstream allocation for a public or private user.

The [AI public-finance tax-base record](records/us-nber-ai-public-finance-tax-base-2026.json)
grounds the table's fiscal row as a two-stage theoretical mechanism. In the
first stage, if AI displaces human labor, the model treats labor-income and
human-consumption bases as vulnerable and makes consumption or differentiated
commodity taxation more salient. In a later hypothetical stage where autonomous
systems produce most value and absorb more resources, the model argues that
taxing human consumption becomes inadequate and frames taxation of autonomous
production as an optimal-harvesting problem. This is not a realized AI adoption
path, revenue forecast, tax recommendation, incidence estimate, or feasible
international policy design; its implications depend on assumptions about
displacement, ownership, production, consumption, transfers, and political
capacity.

The [AI asset-management record](records/us-nber-ai-asset-management-performance-comovement-2026.json)
grounds the table's finance row in an empirical adoption screen. Across 116,000
investment-adviser disclosures from 2012–2024, the paper identifies steadily
growing AI-driven investing concentrated among hedge funds. AI hedge funds
outperformed non-AI peers initially, but that advantage declined over time,
including among early adopters; their reported return comovement was lower.
The result describes adoption, reported performance, and strategy structure,
not causal AI alpha, ordinary-household investment returns, productivity,
leverage, or systemic-risk reduction. Disclosure classification, novelty,
selection, changing strategies, hidden common exposures, and market adaptation
remain plausible alternative explanations.

The [water-sector cybersecurity-capacity record](records/us-gao-water-sector-cybersecurity-capacity-2026.json)
grounds the table's water-infrastructure row. GAO describes roughly 170,000
water and wastewater systems where operational technologies controlling valves
and pumps are increasingly connected to internet-enabled devices and remote
access. It identifies uneven system capability, workforce shortages, hard-to-
update legacy technology, and limited resources; EPA completed a sector risk
assessment and risk-management plan, while identifying gaps in assessment
requirements and federal authority. This is a risk-governance and operating-
capacity stage, not evidence of a successful attack, outage duration, household
water-safety effect, operator failure, compliance rate, or remediation result.

The common pattern is not that capacity is absent. It is that capacity is
converted unevenly and represented through contested measurement. A state program can correct an error without proving a
beneficiary regained continuity; a funded installation can retain a backlog
without a measured readiness failure; a medical profession can expand spending
without detectable health gain; caution can reduce leverage while increasing
local risk sharing; and a household can revise a gender-work preference after
experience without yet changing durable employment.

This rotation therefore strengthens the atlas's middle layer—selection,
maintenance, feedback, and interpretation—while preserving the end-to-end
stopping rule. The next decisive evidence must follow named people, cases,
facilities, or institutions across the open middle rather than infer the final
outcome from an upstream allocation or reported response.

This atlas is currently strongest at showing how pressures and capabilities
are distributed, how people and institutions adapt, and where the next link in
the chain is still unmeasured. It is not yet a single model that explains
political or geopolitical behavior from household conditions. The useful
reader question is therefore: **which stage is directly observed, which stage
is compared across sources, and which stage remains an open test?**

| If you want to understand | Start here | The current safe conclusion |
|---|---|---|
| How pressure reaches daily life | [Material room and household findings](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-013.md) and [energy-room findings](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-034.md) | Income timing, assistance, prices, energy spending, disconnection, repair risk, and adaptation are connected questions, not one stress score. |
| How time and care enter the story | [The household calendar integration program](US-HOUSEHOLD-CALENDAR-INTEGRATION_V1.md) | Work, care, travel, health, and recovery run on different clocks; the same-family trigger-to-outcome chain is still the key acquisition target. |
| How institutions shape practical options | [The public-system and consumer route findings](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-014.md) and [CFPB route findings](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-021.md) | A complaint, notice, or benefit receipt is an institutional route, not by itself a remedy, recovered security, trust change, or exit. |
| How local economies and infrastructure matter | [The firm/place finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-011.md) and [infrastructure/state-capacity bridge](projects/ai-work-control/domestic-capacity-dependence-state-leverage-cross-source-bridge-v1.md) | Applications, openings, jobs, capacity, revenue, ownership, and replaceability are separate stages; entry or investment is not automatically broad prosperity or leverage. |
| How meaning, politics, and geopolitics enter | [The cost/trust/politics program](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/README.md) and [comparative capability context](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-073.md) | Surveys and international comparisons identify meaning, confidence, capability, and context; they do not by themselves prove attribution, collective action, or external behavior change. |
| How consumer participation becomes social power | [Consumer culture, trust, status, and everyday power](projects/us-consumer-culture/consumer-culture-trust-status-layer-v1.md) and [consumer recourse visibility/remedy](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/consumer-recourse-visibility-remedy-synthesis-v1.md) | Buying, sharing, reporting, help-seeking, switching, and staying are distinct participation surfaces; trust, visibility, remedy, dignity, and practical exit must not be substituted for one another. |

The rest of this page moves in four passes: the joint proposition, the
evidence by arrow, the segments that are genuinely end-to-end, and the next
research cycle. Each section keeps a direct observation beside its limitation
and next test so that a compelling theme does not silently become a causal
claim.

## Current broad-program rotations

The latest rotation cycle deliberately moved across the full program rather
than deepening one household lane. The continuity ledger records 56 documented
rotations; the 46 numbered entries below are the cross-source synthesis
artifacts currently carried in this table. They are bounded syntheses, not
estimates of one pooled phenomenon:

The new partisan-identity layer adds a caution about political coalitions. Pew's
2026 NPORS shows a small national Democratic advantage after leaners are
included, but larger uneven differences by gender, race, and education. Many
people call themselves independent before leaners are assigned. This supports
the claim that political identity can organize belonging and interpretation
without proving a vote, a policy position, a switch, or a durable realignment.

| Rotation | Cross-source artifact | What it adds | Arrow still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Consumer power | [Practical exit](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/practical-exit-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md) | CFPB, household loss, CPSC, and platform-remedy evidence show institutional visibility is easier to observe than recovery or exit | Verified remedy, switching, trust, and practical exit |
| 2. AI/platform agency | [AI use, work, contact, and exit](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/ai-use-work-contact-exit-synthesis-v1.md) | Everyday adoption, work-time effects, synthetic contact, companion exit behavior, and governance inquiry remain distinct routes to control | Who captures the gain, who bears error, and who can stop, correct, delete, or switch |
| 3. Public systems | [SNAP route divergence](projects/us-safety-net-access/snap-route-divergence-food-security-political-feedback-synthesis-v1.md) | Entry, exit, food security, administrative ease, and institutional confidence diverge | Notice, amount, remedy, recovery, trust, and action in one episode |
| 4. Firm/infrastructure/state capacity | [Capacity without exit](projects/ai-work-control/capacity-without-exit-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md) | Physical capacity, fiscal revenue, and rules can grow before public benefit or replaceability is proven | Net incidence, portability, switching, legitimacy, and external response |
| 5. Material/time/care | [Time as hidden price](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/time-as-hidden-price-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md) | Households can pay in unpaid care, waiting, extra work, debt, travel, or reduced use | Dated trigger, substitution, protected/sacrificed outcome, and recovery |
| 6. Unequal exposure/meaning | [Unequal optionality](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/unequal-optionality-status-meaning-synthesis-v1.md) | Inequality is unequal room to absorb, contest, reinterpret, or leave—not exposure alone | Attribution, alternatives, trust, and action by the same unit |
| 7. Political action | [Action is a menu](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/political-action-menu-not-ladder-synthesis-v1.md) | Voting, contact, volunteering, complaint, organizing, switching, and withdrawal have different barriers | Same-event action, institutional response, and later legitimacy |
| 8. Place capacity and practical exit | [Local capacity is not practical exit](projects/us-local-business-place/place-capacity-practical-exit-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md) | Establishments, providers, public routes, and infrastructure create alternatives only when they are reachable, usable, replaceable, and contestable | Dated resident route, attempted use, fallback, restored access, stay/switch/move, and local meaning |
| 9. Financial capacity and household room | [From national growth to household room](projects/us-financial-intermediation/macro-to-household-financial-capacity-synthesis-v1.md) | Macro growth, provider infrastructure, public monitoring, credit, and household room are different stages; capacity can expand while practical access narrows | Dated obligation, alternatives, payment/record/fee route, household consequence, remedy, trust, and exit |

Housing security and cost of staying | [The cost of staying put](projects/us-housing-insurance-risk/cost-of-staying-put-synthesis-v1.md) | Payment, coverage, hazard, energy, and public backstops can compound without being interchangeable | Matched property/household episode with notice, claim/repair, assistance, mobility, recovery, and stay/move outcome |

Route-specific institutional friction | [Institutional friction is route-specific](projects/us-household-constraint-cascade/institutional-friction-route-specific-outcomes-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md) | Prescription delay, authorization denial, benefit interruption, complaint routing, and debt remedies have different objects and downstream currencies | Same-case route, alternatives, verified remedy, protected/sacrificed outcome, and later trust/action/exit |

| 13. Across them, issuance, repair, access restoration, receipt, adequacy, durability, and later trust/exit remain separate clocks | Identifier-compatible follow-up preserving exposure, alternative, effort, remedy receipt, protected outcome, repeat harm/correction, and later use, switching, trust, or exit |

### The common pattern

Across the 56 documented rotations, the strongest recurring theme is **unequal
optionality under institutional mediation**. A condition or capability changes
the options available to people, consumers, workers, firms, places, or states;
an institution then routes, prices, classifies, delays, corrects, funds, or
governs the response. The visible endpoint is often not the human or public
outcome but the institution's own record: a complaint label, a benefit
transition, a time measure, a tax receipt, a policy filing, a vote, or a
production milestone.

The broad map therefore needs one discipline at every scale:

```text
observed condition or capability
  -> available alternatives and control
  -> adaptation and redistribution of money, time, risk, data, or power
  -> institutional and firm response
  -> meaning, trust, action, switching, or exit
  -> sector, state, or geopolitical consequence
```

The rotations strengthen the middle of this chain and sharpen the open-arrow
list. They do not prove one grand causal theory, and they do not make the
unavailable PSID/UAS respondent files a program-wide dependency.

The newest cases make the same idea visible at different scales. Nursing-home
inspections can change care effort without showing complete resident wellbeing;
the CHPE line can operate without showing household affordability; a TAKE IT
DOWN complaint route can exist without showing removal or safety; a USMCA
labor remedy can report reinstatement and backpay without showing durable worker
power; and a SNAP office closure can reduce participation without showing the
full food-security consequence. These are not one causal sequence. Together
they support a narrower subtheme: **institutional visibility often arrives
before usable control, adequate protection, or durable trust**.

The [safe-drinking-water investment and health record](records/us-nber-water-works-safe-drinking-water-health-2026.json)
adds a public-infrastructure-to-health version. Across 266 million readings in
48 states, the share above current standards fell by about half from 2003 to
2019; the study nevertheless estimates that roughly 10%–20% of Americans still
receive water violating standards. Safe Drinking Water Act loans were associated
with lower pollution and lower mortality among older Americans in the study's
estimates, at an estimated average cost of $46 per person per year to remove
excess pollution. Local hotspots, household exposure, private treatment,
monitoring, equal receipt, and each household's private cost remain separate
from the system-level trend.

The [water-resilience record](records/us-gao-water-infrastructure-resilience-vulnerable-communities-2025.json)
adds the financing-and-place middle. Fourteen EPA, FEMA, and USDA programs
provided about $35 billion in grants and $29 billion in direct loans, yet
selected utilities still reported rate resistance, repayment burden,
maintenance costs, and difficulty making results visible to the public. Service
areas also did not line up cleanly with community boundaries. Federal capital
can therefore expand infrastructure capacity while local affordability,
maintenance, targeting, and dependable household service remain unresolved;
funding and project counts do not establish resilience or equal protection.

The CBP port-partnership record adds a public-private border version. More than
1.15 million people and 88,500 containers entered daily through 328 ports in
FY2024, while reimbursed overtime and donated lanes or equipment expanded the
capacity route. Partnership dollars and equipment do not by themselves show a
shorter queue, fairer inspection, lower trade cost, or preserved accountability.

The [retained same-unit bridge matrix](US-BROAD-RETAINED-SAME-UNIT-BRIDGE-MATRIX_V1.md)
clarifies the strongest longitudinal evidence currently retained. MEPS closes
an event-to-later-health/work bridge; SHED closes a pressure-to-adaptation and
partial-reversal bridge; CCES closes a prior-disruption-to-later-judgment or
action bridge. Read together, they produce a useful cultural result without a
false join: adaptation, institutional approval, contact, political action, and
recovery are different currencies. Hardship does not automatically become
backlash, and continued use does not automatically mean satisfaction. The
next decisive evidence must measure the person's perceived control, reason for
the choice, institutional response, and later outcome in the same episode.

The case-level audit sharpens the boundary further: no retained episode has
both a clearly observed alternative/non-use choice and recipient-level remedy
receipt. Meadley comes closest on an official reported payment, but the
worker's alternative is not recorded. Platform cases add reactivation,
substitute earnings, or post-restoration work, but separate lost-pay receipt,
durability, and later recovery remain open. This is why the atlas treats
formal remedy, implementation, receipt, and durable recovery as separate
clocks rather than one “resolved” outcome.

The latest SHED additions make the optionality pattern legible across three
everyday institutions. Banking access shows that formal entry can coexist with
nonbank substitution and incomplete fraud recovery; care and living
arrangements show that households can convert cost into co-residence, unpaid
care, and gendered work tradeoffs; employment and job quality show that weaker
outside mobility coexists with unequal access to AI and task discretion. Read
together, these are not one household-level causal sequence. They are three
bounded demonstrations that the same visible behavior—using an alternative
payment route, providing unpaid care, or adopting a work tool—can represent
preference, support, constraint, or some mixture. The decisive missing fields
remain the dated trigger, feasible alternatives, actor response, verified
outcome, and later trust, action, recovery, or exit in the same unit.

The [NASBO state-budget finding](projects/us-state-fiscal-capacity/findings/us-nasbo-state-budget-buffer-adjustment-2026-001.md)
adds a public-capacity layer between macro conditions and household-facing
services. Proposed fiscal 2027 general-fund spending grew only 0.6% at the
median even as states reported targeted cuts, vacant-position reductions, and
revenue increases; most states still expected dollar rainy-day balances to
hold or rise while total balances declined as prior surplus was spent. This
supports a sharper institutional subtheme: **fiscal room can remain visible
while adjustment is pushed into staffing, program choice, timing, or local
delivery**. It is not evidence of a service cut or household burden. The next
test is a named state service or shock followed through enacted policy,
implementation, access, and resident or firm outcome.

The [Pew state rainy-day capacity record](records/us-pew-state-rainy-day-capacity-fy2025.json)
adds the state-buffer stock beneath that budget-planning surface. Fiscal 2025
rainy-day balances totaled $174.2 billion; the median covered 47.8 operating
days (13.1% of annual spending), down from 54.5 days in FY2024. Capacity ranged
from 320.2 days in Wyoming to zero dedicated rainy-day days in New Jersey, with
24 states increasing and 26 decreasing their cushion. Total rainy-day plus
ending balances were $346.9 billion, yet median ending balances declined 17.4%.
These descriptive reserve ratios do not show legal availability, program need,
service quality, tax incidence, disaster response, household benefit, or trust.

### Three currencies of everyday optionality

The three latest SHED-based findings are useful together because they expose
different forms of room to maneuver without pretending that the respondents
or episodes are the same. The comparison is a mechanism map, not a pooled
index:

| Lane | Unit and measured surface | What the evidence supports | What it does not establish |
|---|---|---|---|
| Banking and fraud recourse | 2025 SHED adult respondents; formal account status, overdraft, nonbank transaction routes, reported fraud loss/recovery, provider contact, and reported switching | Formal entry can coexist with reliance on alternative payment routes and incomplete or uneven recourse; non-switching is an observed behavior, not proof of satisfaction | A dated account event, provider fault, quality of the alternative, verified dollar recovery, usable liquidity, trust change, or practical exit |
| Care and living arrangements | 2025 SHED adult respondents; multigenerational living, paid and unpaid childcare, childcare cost relative to housing, adult caregiving, gendered caretaker roles, and caregiving-related employment status | Households may convert money pressure into co-residence or unpaid labor, and care responsibilities can coexist with employment differences; these are distinct resource and time allocations | A same-family care trigger, available substitute, schedule control, displaced work/time, employer or public response, recovery, or political meaning |
| Employment and AI control | 2025 Federal Reserve SHED workers/adults; young-adult job-finding difficulty, layoffs, voluntary exits, new-job starts, work location/schedule variation, AI use, perceived time saving, education, and task-control differences | Outside mobility and workplace control are separate dimensions of the AI transition; adoption and perceived time saving can be distributed by education and decision latitude | Causal AI displacement or productivity, pay or benefit change, task-level implementation, worker bargaining, household welfare, trust, or political response |

The common analytical point is that an alternative is not just a second
product or a second place to live. It can be a payment channel, a family
member's unpaid time, a schedule accommodation, a job change, or the ability
to refuse a tool. Each alternative carries a different price in money, time,
privacy, status, dependence, or future bargaining power. The available data
show the surfaces separately; they do not tell us which option a particular
person considered, whether it was genuinely usable, or what outcome it
protected.

This is why the atlas should code the following as distinct states:

```text
formal access       != usable access
alternative route  != equivalent substitute
continued use      != satisfaction
AI adoption        != worker gain
co-residence       != voluntary preference
non-switching      != practical power
```

The next decisive design is therefore a same-unit event or repeated-person
comparison that records the initiating need, the alternatives actually
available, the choice and its money/time cost, the actor controlling the next
step, and a protected or sacrificed outcome. Only then can the three lanes be
connected to recovery, trust, action, or exit without converting population
patterns into one synthetic household story.

The [Amazon Flex tip-refund follow-up](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-087.md)
adds a named worker-remedy stage to the same distinction. The FTC publicly
reports earlier payment rounds totaling more than $60.6 million and a current
Zelle route for eligible drivers who did not cash their checks. This is stronger
than a compensation obligation or reserved fund, but it still does not provide
the complete eligible-driver denominator, every individual's receipt or
remaining loss, continued platform work, alternative work, household recovery,
or bargaining and exit outcomes. The worker remedy is therefore coded as
administrator-reported distribution with downstream lived recovery open.

The [CFPB Ombudsman redress-process trend](records/us-cfpb-ombudsman-redress-process-friction-2025.json)
adds a recipient-side middle stage to that distribution boundary. The FY2025
report records 2,584 Ombudsman inquiries, with questions and complaints as
distinct categories, and names redress-list, address-update, and check-reissue
problems among the recurring process topics. Its illustrative servicemember
example documents an expired check, a correct reissue request, administrator
friction, and Ombudsman facilitation. This makes effort and institutional
escalation visible without turning inquiry volume into a failure rate or
facilitation into verified receipt, cashing, recovery, trust, or exit.

The [FTC refund-administration benchmark](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/remedy-durability-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md)
adds a route-specific receipt measure: its 2019 report gives average check-
cashing rates of 64% for known-customer mailings, 93% for claims-process cases,
and 89% when Consumer Sentinel supplied the recipient list. The rates are not
one current remedy-success rate; they show that identification route,
contactability, claims friction, and reissue rules shape what “receipt” means.

This is the current end-to-end reading of the atlas. It is deliberately a
bridge across different units, not a merge of respondents, households,
complaints, workers, places, or countries. The [machine-readable trend
registry](us-trend-observations.md) is the source index; each record's boundary
controls how far its evidence can travel.

The [automatic-saving multi-currency bridge](findings/us-automatic-saving-multi-currency-policy-bridge-001.md)
adds a policy-to-household sequence across NBER/CRI Auto-IRA studies, Federal
Reserve SHED liquidity and credit, and BEA aggregate income and spending. It
does not pool the estimates: the policy studies use different treatment designs
and balance-sheet definitions, while the Federal Reserve and BEA layers are
context rather than treatment outcomes. The surviving claim is that automatic
saving can move current liquidity, future assets, and retirement timing on
different clocks; the net-security and meaning/action arrows remain open.

The [SIPP utility-credit-savings finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-006.md)
now adds a sharper household-room bridge inside the material and institutional
route layers. In the same person-record/month diagnostic, reported utility
payment difficulty co-occurs with more carried credit balances and less savings
account ownership, while higher-resource respondents within the difficulty
group are more likely to carry balances—an access counterexample, not evidence
that they face less hardship. The separate [following-month utility-to-work
finding](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-028.md)
places utility difficulty or energy-assistance status beside next-month earnings
and hours. These are adjacent descriptive stages, not a bill-to-credit-to-work
causal estimate.

The [directional SIPP work/resource finding](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-030.md)
sharpens the work/time bridge: earnings changes can rise or fall, while hours
are unchanged for most adjacent-month pairs. Work-limitation and resource
position condition the direction distribution, but no bill, health event,
employer decision, care substitution, or later meaning/action is observed.

The new [ATUS work-location finding](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-029.md)
adds a published 2025 work-location surface to the time architecture. Home and
workplace indicators overlap, and their sex and education gradients are visible,
but location does not identify schedule control, total workload, care conflict,
or worker voice. The federal-shutdown coverage warning in the 2025 release is
retained as a measurement caveat.

The new [SIPP tenure/resource finding](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-031.md)
adds a replicate-weighted intersection to the household-room surface. Renters
report more housing and utility-payment difficulty than owners within each
displayed resource band, including at the highest band, while the food-hardship
measure follows a different pattern and has its own universe. This sharpens the
alternative-space question—resources do not erase tenure-linked exposure—but
does not identify rent, local price, repair, insurance, debt, or a dated bill as
the mechanism.

The new [household-room layer](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-032.md)
puts that SIPP comparison beside the Federal Reserve's emergency-liquidity and
linked-credit measures. It separates resource position, tenure, hypothetical
emergency capacity, and realized card-balance exposure rather than treating
them as one security index. The combined reading is that household room is
about available alternatives when a dated cost arrives; the same-person event,
time/care substitution, protected outcome, and later recovery are still open.

The new [multi-clock material/time/care synthesis](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-033.md)
connects that open middle to the longitudinal evidence already acquired. SIPP
shows monthly resource and work transitions for the same people; MEPS follows
health spending, coverage, employment, wages, and perceived-health change in a
panel; SHED and ATUS add emergency routes, care, and time allocation. The
combined evidence supports distinct material, time, health, and work clocks,
not a pooled household stress score. A complete dated trigger → alternative →
protected/sacrificed outcome → recovery → meaning/action chain remains the
program's acquisition target.

The [SHED coverage-to-care-foregoing comparison](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/shed-panel-coverage-care-foregoing-paths-v1.md)
adds an important condition inside that middle. In the recontact panel,
stable insurance has the largest no-foregoing share (69.12%) and the lowest
persistent-foregoing share (14.53%), while persistent uninsurance has the
highest persistent-foregoing share (37.35%); coverage loss and gain remain
smaller cells with different entry and exit profiles. The [reproduction audit](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/shed-panel-coverage-care-foregoing-reproduction-audit-2026-09-16.md)
confirms these cells from the existing local artifacts. This is a route
condition, not an insurance effect: the annual fields do not observe plan
adequacy, network access, a dated need or bill, treatment continuity, or
recovery.

The [SHED care-foregoing/adaptation record](records/us-shed-2025-care-skipping-adaptation-association.json)
adds a cross-sectional household-pressure surface. Among respondents reporting
that they skipped at least one listed kind of care because of cost, 42.2% had
medical debt, 64.0% reduced savings, 34.0% increased borrowing, 74.3% delayed
a major purchase, and 33.0% worked more or got a job; the corresponding
no-foregoing shares were 10.0%, 33.1%, 9.6%, 35.6%, and 11.5%. The respondent
link makes the co-occurrence visible, but it does not establish a dated bill,
direction of causation, care alternative, treatment outcome, coverage
adequacy, remedy, or recovery. Prior scarcity, health need, access, coverage,
and reverse timing can produce both care foregoing and adaptation.

The [SHED coverage and care-skipping association record](records/us-shed-2025-care-skipping-coverage-association.json)
adds a coverage-conditioned snapshot. Among insured respondents, 23.8% reported
skipping at least one listed care type because of cost; 42.6% of that foregoing
group also reported medical debt, versus 10.0% of insured respondents reporting
no listed foregoing. The corresponding medical-debt shares were 40.1% and 9.9%
among uninsured respondents who did and did not report foregoing, respectively.
Coverage therefore conditions the care-foregoing surface but does not eliminate
concurrent debt. These are same-respondent, cross-sectional comparisons without
replicate-weight standard errors, bill timing, plan generosity, care need,
clinical outcome, or causal insurance effect.

The new [MEPS expenditure-conditioned finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-002.md)
deepens the health-cost/work bridge without turning spending into a burden
score. Baseline 2022 expenditure bands have different perceived-health and
employment-continuity profiles, but the differences remain confounded by need,
age, insurance, retirement, and service mix. The correct next question is not
whether high spending “caused” work loss; it is which treatment, coverage,
out-of-pocket, unpaid-care, and baseline-employment paths explain the distinct
outcomes.

The new [public-system route-to-judgment synthesis](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-014.md)
extends the safety-net chain through institutional meaning without pooling
incompatible respondents. USDA/FNA measures administrative timeliness, WBNS
measures recipient route barriers, SIPP measures following material hardship,
and Gallup measures institution-specific confidence. Together they support a
layered route/buffer/judgment architecture; they do not show that one notice
caused one hardship or changed one person's trust or political action.

The [Medicaid-expansion safety-net-spillovers record](records/us-medicaid-expansion-safety-net-spillovers-2014-2019.json)
adds a cross-program route mechanism. In border-county and restricted-ACS
comparisons, a typical 0-to-138%-of-poverty Medicaid expansion reduced
uninsurance by about 2.2 percentage points and was associated with about 0.6
additional SNAP participants per 100 people (0.49 percentage points in the ACS)
and higher TANF participation, particularly among single parents; the preferred
EITC estimate was small and not statistically distinguishable from zero. The
authors' leading interpretation is reduced information or transaction costs for
participation conditional on eligibility. The repeated cross-section and
county-pair designs do not follow a named household through notice, application,
amount, food security, care use, trust, action, or recovery, and cannot make
those downstream outcomes part of the expansion effect.

The [LIHEAP reach/restoration record](records/us-liheap-reach-restoration-fy2024.json)
adds a public-capacity comparator to this route. HHS reports broad FY2024
program reach and distinct restored-energy instances, but those aggregate
endpoints do not reveal whether service remained on, whether the next bill was
affordable, or which food, medicine, housing, health, or work trade-off was
avoided. Program reach, restoration, and durable household recovery therefore
remain separate stages.

The [Clean Energy Connector record](records/us-doe-clean-energy-connector-low-income-bill-credit-2024-2026.json)
adds a different eligibility-to-bill route. In six pilot jurisdictions, DOE and
HHS describe identifying LIHEAP recipients for community-solar subscriptions;
eligible households opt in, subscription managers complete enrollment, and the
program requires at least a 20% difference between the household's financial
benefit and participation cost. Clear communication, disclosure, data, and
consumer-protection requirements are visible program design. The page does not
report the number of eligible households, successful opt-ins, subscription fees,
bill-credit timing, net savings, retention, service reliability, or energy
security. A required discount is therefore a condition of a potential route,
not a measured household outcome.

The new [CFPB route-vintage audit](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-021.md)
adds a measurement-integrity result to the consumer-power lane. In live
2024/2025 product samples, `timely` does not substitute for elapsed routing
time, and the returned `has_narrative` field is unavailable in the re-query.
The public API therefore supplies a route/schema diagnostic, not a remedy or
consumer-welfare trend; omitted fields remain missing rather than zero.

The [CFPB complaint-publication record](records/us-cfpb-complaint-publication-visibility-2026.json)
adds a later institutional consequence to that visibility boundary. The Bureau
described two-factor and routing controls after concerns about third-party and
AI-assisted complaint activity, then ended discretionary publication of
unverified complaint narratives and visualizations while retaining collection,
monitoring, response, and secure regulatory sharing. This is not evidence that
the complaint route became less useful or more useful to any individual. It
does establish that administrative integrity and public consumer voice can move
in opposite directions: a more controlled intake can coexist with a less
visible public narrative layer, while correction, payment, trust, switching,
and exit remain unobserved.

The [CFPB narrative-filter API record](records/us-cfpb-narrative-filter-api-boundary-2026.json)
adds a narrower public-data boundary. In a live query of 21,425 matching
credit-card complaints carrying the company label `Closed with monetary relief`,
the capped returned payload exposed 15 administrative fields but no usable
narrative field. Adding `has_narrative=true` returned the same total, count,
and 25 IDs as the control query. This establishes only that the tested public
API route did not expose a narrative/meaning stage; it does not show that
consumers lacked narratives, that the filter fails everywhere, or that monetary
relief was received, adequate, or associated with account correction, repeat
effort, trust, switching, or exit.

The [federal shared-services record](records/us-gao-federal-shared-services-adoption-leadership-data-2026.json)
adds a public-administration version of the same interface problem. Agencies
can be offered centralized cybersecurity, financial, grants, and human-
resources services while legal fit, legacy systems, integration, leadership,
and performance data remain uneven. Centralization may reduce duplication, but
the review does not establish government-wide savings, service quality, user
experience, or mission improvement. A marketplace or shared platform is a
route choice; it is not proof that the work became easier or better for the
agency using it.

The [GAO federal-cloud security-control record](records/us-gao-federal-cloud-security-control-gaps-2026.json)
adds a system-level control test. Across eight selected cloud systems in four
agencies, three fully performed continuous monitoring, five fully implemented
the selected service-level-agreement practice, and three agreements lacked
consistent performance metrics, measurement, and enforcement. The remaining
five systems only partially implemented the three reviewed practices:
continuous monitoring, incident response/recovery, and enforceable provider
terms. GAO issued 12 open recommendations. This selected-system review does
not establish federal prevalence, a breach, outage, data loss, provider fault,
or public-user harm; it identifies the governance work that must exist before
cloud capacity can be treated as monitored, recoverable, and accountable.

The [NNSA Strategic Partnership Projects record](records/us-gao-nnsa-strategic-partnership-infrastructure-capacity-2026.json)
adds a realized shared-infrastructure version. NNSA sites performed more than
$15 billion in reimbursed partner work across FY2019–24, and GAO reports about
$1.4 billion in FY2024 indirect costs reimbursed by partners while specialized
facilities were used for mission and research work. This moves the evidence
from available capability to operating use, staffing, maintenance, and cost
allocation. It also exposes a reciprocal dependence: federal partners may lack
substitutes, while NNSA programs may rely on partner-funded overhead and
utilization. Some sites limited SPP growth because core mission work and
facility or staffing capacity competed for the same resources. Net savings,
facility condition, equitable allocation, substitute capability, delivered
mission effect, and geopolitical leverage remain open.

The [cross-agency performance-goals record](records/us-gao-cross-agency-performance-goals-coordination-2026.json)
adds the coordination layer. GAO participants said four-year shared goals can
create ownership and energy across agencies, while implementation still runs
into uneven leadership, limited resources, inconsistent measurement, and
transition problems. Coordination is not only a shared objective; it requires
stable authority, staff, data, and a way to survive administrative change. The
report does not show that any specific goal improved service or public trust.

The [USAspending supplier-concentration record](records/usaspending-jassm-lrasm-supplier-concentration-2024-2025.json)
adds a quantitative replaceability check to the geopolitical/state-capacity
lane. One JASSM/LRASM subaward extract has 51 reported recipient names, but
the top five receive 49.50% of returned amount and the top ten 74.60%. The
result keeps component diversity, financial concentration, production,
replaceability, and external leverage as separate stages.

The [entity-parent-facility reconciliation](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-076.md)
adds an official identity layer to that supplier test. DLA CAGE records make
the apparent BAE route legible from the Merrimack recipient to BAE Systems,
Inc. and BAE Systems plc, and provide a parallel General Dynamics-OTS to
General Dynamics Corp route. This improves the jurisdiction and facility
denominator without resolving the central capability question: parentage is
not facility-level assignment, production, acceptance, delivery,
replaceability, or external leverage. The next evidence must join the exact
identifiers to procurement and performance records.

The follow-up [recipient-identifier ambiguity audit](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-077.md)
tests that missing join directly. A name search returns 17 BAE recipient rows
across 10 UEIs and three General Dynamics-OTS rows, while the retained
JASSM/LRASM subaward response exposes no UEI or CAGE. One General Dynamics UEI
overlaps the selected DLA facility set, but that intersection does not assign
the $80.55 million subaward to a plant. The evidence therefore strengthens
the measurement boundary: legal identity, facility identity, award action,
and production location remain separate until an identifier-bearing performance
record appears.

The richer [subaward-to-UEI and location finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-078.md)
then closes an important measurement step. The official award-search route
returns UEI, recipient-location, and primary-performance-place fields for all
74 rows, including exact identifiers for the selected BAE and General
Dynamics-OTS actions. The locations are Nashua and Niceville, not automatically
the previously selected DLA CAGE facilities. This turns the next question into
a real CAGE-to-site and production/acceptance/delivery join rather than a name
search, while preserving the operational and geopolitical arrows as open.

The [facility-capability context finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-079.md)
adds a meaningful middle layer. Official BAE quality material places military
electronics design/production/servicing scope at the Nashua address, and
official General Dynamics-OTS material names Niceville Operations and
advanced-warhead capability. The sources make those locations plausible
industrial sites without proving that the selected subaward work occurred
there. Production, acceptance, delivery, alternatives, and external response
remain separate gates.

The [realization-stage milestone finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-080.md)
adds dated movement beyond procurement intent: a DoD JASSM/LRASM
production-support IDIQ, Lockheed Martin capacity investment/ramp-up evidence,
and a completed first phase of LRASM F-35C flight-science testing. The Air
Force FY2026 exhibit adds a government-reported Troy, Alabama minimum
sustained rate of 396 combined JASSM/LRASM missiles, allocated as 336 JASSM
and 60 LRASM, attributed to a new operational production facility. These
stages strengthen production-capacity evidence while preserving the missing
realized throughput, accepted quantity, delivery, fielding, maintenance,
replaceability, and external-response links.

The [Poland JASSM-ER delivery non-observation](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-081.md)
keeps the foreign customer stage honest. The May 2024 agreement's 2026–2030
window and the later 2028–2030 schedule reference conflict without a verified
amendment, and the reviewed official source set still lacks a delivery,
acceptance, fielding, training, or inventory event for the order. The absence
is bounded to public-source review; it is not evidence that no delivery has
occurred.

The [historical Polish audit-control finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-082.md)
adds an institutional-observability layer: NIK found that a JASSM-ER
production-cost settlement relied on milestone/process material without a
numeric value tied to the settlement. This is evidence about documentation and
control of realization, not a missile quantity or delivery estimate. The
separate 32-unit comparator is retained as a general Polish defence-contract
example and is not attributed to JASSM.

The [PSID 2023 field-audit finding](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-036.md)
closes a different acquisition gap. Official documentation confirms a large
panel family file with work, unpaid labor, care, perceived time pressure,
health, income, wealth, insurance, and utility fields. The fields make a
longitudinal material/time/care test feasible, but they do not yet supply a
dated event, attribution, recovery, trust, or political-action result.

The [FMS timing-asynchrony finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-083.md)
shows why the realization arrow needs multiple clocks. NIK records four F-35
deliveries in December 2024 and January 2025 while the related settlement forms
arrived in 2025, and states that FMS delivery schedules are estimates. The
comparator improves the measurement design but does not establish a JASSM-ER
delivery or operational capability.

The [historical GSS finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-024.md)
adds a long-run meaning countercheck: financial dissatisfaction is associated
with lower trust and fairness expectations in each selected anchor year, but
the gap and overall trust level change across decades. This supports a
relational meaning route without turning financial position into a stable causal
explanation of social trust or political action.

## What the evidence jointly supports

| Stage | Current evidence | Safe cross-source reading | Arrow still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Material room and exposure | BLS food-at-home prices; USDA food security; SHED food pressure, liquidity, credit, and financial paths; SIPP adjacent-month hardship and SNAP transitions; FTC reported fraud losses; LBNL national energy load; CFPB published complaints | Material price pressure, household resource gradients, liquid-buffer and credit exposure, basic-security severity, reported consumer loss, and infrastructure demand are measurable, but each source sees a different unit and exposure surface. | A dated household, customer, worker, or place event causing the next response and showing which buffer or institution absorbed it. |
| Adaptation and time | SHED repeated adaptations; SHED care/work cells; SIPP SNAP-transition/child-care time-loss and child-care-work/hardship comparisons; USDA family-shielding and assistance observations; ATUS eldercare prevalence, care-day intensity, 2024–2025 recipient-roster comparison, provider/nonprovider time profiles, age/sex/labor-force-standardized contrasts, and richer child/education-standardized contrasts; AI workplace time findings | People and institutions absorb pressure through substitution, borrowing, care, work, waiting, assistance, and other time or resource transfers. The [SIPP SNAP/child-care time-loss record](records/us-sipp-snap-childcare-time-loss-2024.json) shows child-care work prevention is higher in the stable-SNAP comparison (7.43% versus 3.42%). The separate [SIPP child-care/work-hardship bridge](records/us-sipp-childcare-work-hardship-bridge-2024.json) places annual work prevention beside following-month hardship within stable SNAP-status pairs: utility hardship is 16.9% versus 7.8% in the no-SNAP comparison and 32.4% versus 19.4% in the SNAP comparison. These are descriptive contrasts, not a care-to-hardship effect: the work-prevention cells contain 71 and 23 valid pairs, respectively, with wide intervals and separate hardship universes. ATUS adds that roughly four in five eldercare provider respondents list a non-household recipient and that care, household work, and travel differences remain after richer composition controls, while social-time ordering varies by year and paid-work differences are uncertain. | Whether a specific exposure displaced paid work, health, rest, family time, or civic availability for the same unit, and whether the support route caused or relieved that displacement. |
| Institutional route | CFPB case-route timing, response endpoint, and visibility through 2025, including six matched product slices and a student-loan timing-field audit; SIPP SNAP transitions; USDA assistance participation; BLS union membership/coverage | Access and protection are shaped by the route through a firm, benefit system, or workplace institution—not just by the headline outcome. The CFPB annual record shifts toward explanation and lower narrative visibility in 2025, but product routes diverge sharply. The student-loan audit shows that the broad `timely = No` field and narrower company-response untimely category are different endpoints; these are institutional records, not verified consumer outcomes. | Notice, effort, appeal, remedy adequacy, practical control, and later exit or trust in the same episode. |
| Unequal capacity and power | BLS age/occupation/state variation; SHED financial paths; ATUS age/employment differences; SIPP transition cells; AI adoption and education-gap comparisons; NHTS mobility options | The same shock or tool can land differently because cash, time, care capacity, skill, representation, transport, and institutional access are distributed unevenly. | A valid matched or longitudinal design identifying which capacity changed the outcome. |
| Meaning and political action | CPS schedule/health/administrative/transport/interest/candidate barriers; ANES/CCES layers including joint trust/action quadrants; Pew engagement/source/influencer measures; national ANES immigration cross-tabs; Chicagoland local immigration-attitude case | Political participation contains distinct access and interpretation routes; material and institutional experiences can be relevant inputs without being treated as votes. The CCES joint layer shows that trust and civic action can diverge across material/work proxy cells, so distrust cannot be equated with withdrawal. Local and national surveys can measure meaning and policy preference without proving the local cause. | Attribution, fairness, identity, trust, organizing, turnout, and vote measured after a defined exposure. |
| State and geopolitical consequence | SIPRI military-resource allocation; IMF WEO defense-boom fiscal estimates and US projections; defense procurement and realization records; Prince William county fiscal-revenue series (TY2012–2025); Virginia large-load governance and reliability record; LBNL infrastructure scenarios; domestic large-load rate governance; Romania–Malaysia project milestones and ownership changes | Digital capacity, public revenue, defense-resource allocation, fiscal/social-spending trade-offs, and physical dependence can create public/private control questions at the same time, while domestic regulators and alliances specify who carries financial, infrastructure, and strategic risk. Procurement intent, operating milestones, macro-fiscal estimates, local fiscal visibility, and regulatory response are separated as distinct stages. The TY2025 county-linked report records $465.9M in data-center tax revenue alongside 621 MW of new capacity, while SCC evidence records large-load events, a GS-5 framework, and a modeled Rider T1 allocation comparison. | Verified production/delivery, actual utility cost allocation, observed household or community incidence, financing/social-spending incidence, practical replaceability, and an observed external response. |

The [CFPB product-response-routes record](records/us-cfpb-product-response-routes-2024-2025.json)
puts numbers behind the institutional-route distinction. In 2025 published
records, explanation was the recorded endpoint for 84.5% of checking/savings
complaints, 94.9% of mortgage complaints, and 73.3% of debt-collection
complaints; monetary relief was respectively 10.6%, 1.7%, and 0.16%. The
student-loan slice also separates two timing fields: 15.9% carried a
company-response `untimely` label while 30.4% had the broader `timely = No`
field. These are product-filtered published complaint records, not customer
harm or remedy rates: product and issue mix, complaint propensity, publication,
routing, firm coding, and the 2025 taxonomy can all shape the shares. An
explanation, relief label, or timing field does not establish correction,
payment receipt, debt or account restoration, residual loss, switching, or
trust for a complainant.

The [payment-resilience record](records/us-nber-payment-method-resilience-disaster-outages-2026.json)
adds an outage version of the material-room and infrastructure rows. In the
source's disaster and outage evidence, digital payments fell and cash use rose;
nearly half of surveyed consumers could not use their preferred electronic
payment during an outage, and greater cash access was associated with more
completed transactions. This shows a practical fallback route, not a complete
household outcome: the record does not establish whether people obtained food,
medicine, fuel, or care, avoided income loss, or recovered without debt strain.

The [2024 CFPB case-route sample](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/cfpb-case-route-sample-2024-v1.md)
deepens the institutional-route row only through the first handoff: receipt to
company routing, channel, narrative/public-response visibility, and response
labels. Its capped retrieval-order design cannot establish route probabilities
or remedy; effort, verified outcome, repeat contact, trust, and exit remain open.
The [25-case public event-ledger finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-019.md)
turns that handoff into an explicit case-level contract: receipt, routing,
lag, channel, narrative presence, timeliness, and response labels can share a
de-identified record, while verified correction and later choice remain absent.
The [public event-ledger acquisition audit](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/cfpb-public-event-ledger-acquisition-audit-2026-09-14.md)
now proves that a de-identified episode ledger can be built from the public API
without overstating those fields. Its 25-record student-loan extract preserves
receipt, routing, channel, narrative presence, timeliness, and response labels;
it explicitly does not turn a company label into verified correction, recovery,
repeat effort, switching, trust, or exit.

The [SNAP data-governance finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-016.md)
extends the institutional route backward to control over the records that
define eligibility, benefit levels, and program integrity. DOJ's 2026 lawsuit
announcement reports a federal demand for five years of applicant data from
four states after 29 jurisdictions reportedly shared data; the related 2025
court order temporarily restrained threatened funding disallowance for most
plaintiff states and noted that the record did not show significant fraud or
eligibility errors in compliant states. This creates a useful two-sided
governance stage—oversight authority versus privacy, statutory, and state
control—without converting either agency claims or a temporary order into
applicant-level benefit, food-security, or trust outcomes.
The June 2026 USDA system-of-records notice adds the implementation surface:
FedRAMP High hosting, need-to-know access, authentication, encryption, and
legally bounded routine uses were specified after more than 450 public comments,
including roughly 132 privacy or mishandling concerns. Those are stated
controls and public concerns, not observed privacy safety or applicant behavior.

The [SHED annual fraud-comparison record](records/us-shed-fraud-annual-comparison-2024-2025.json)
adds a second annual household sample to the consumer-power row. In 2024,
non-credit-card fraud exposure was 6.85% for respondents age 18--29 and 10.25%
for those 45--59; in 2025 it was 7.13% and 8.38%, respectively. Among fraud
reporters, the unrecovered-money share was 53.78% versus 22.57% in the
$10,000--$24,999 and $100,000--$149,999 household-income bands in 2024, and
50.67% versus 28.29% in 2025. Separate weighted annual samples and unreproduced
replicate-weight variance mean these are descriptive comparisons, not a panel
trend or causal age/income effect. They do not join household loss to CFPB
response or establish remedy, trust, or exit.

The [2024 SHED household-fraud recovery record](records/us-federal-reserve-household-fraud-recovery-2024.json)
separates the loss and recovery currencies in the underlying survey. Twenty-one
percent of adults reported some financial fraud; among adults reporting
non-credit-card fraud, 63% lost money, 32% said some money was not recovered,
and about 30% spent ten or more hours recovering or dealing with the incident.
The Federal Reserve estimated $84 billion in non-credit-card losses, $21
billion recovered, and $63 billion borne directly by consumers. Those are
self-reported, conditional survey measures—not verified case outcomes, firm
liability, remedy adequacy, or a common denominator with CFPB complaints.

The [GAO fraud-risk finding](records/us-gao-fraud-risk-management-public-trust-recommendations-2026.json)
adds an institutional-control layer to the household fraud evidence. GAO's
recommendation count and open-status share show unfinished control work and
its stated trust/mission link, but they do not measure fraud prevalence,
beneficiary recovery, or person-level trust change. Administrative integrity,
usable correction, and restored confidence therefore remain separate outcomes.

The [GAO Ukraine-assistance monitoring record](records/us-gao-ukraine-assistance-monitoring-evaluation-2023-2026.json)
adds an international assistance-accountability stage. State's MEASURE contract
covered oversight of $6.1 billion in nonhumanitarian, nonmilitary Ukraine
assistance through September 2025, including police and border-guard training
and nuclear-safety operations. GAO found that the department used some contract
information for oversight and budgeting and made reports more analytical, but
wartime data access and varied outcome clocks constrained evaluation; strategic
outcome indicators and cross-project evaluations were still incomplete in
November 2025. Better reporting and administrative use are therefore visible
state-capacity outcomes, not proof of program effectiveness, Ukrainian public
legitimacy, institutional resilience, nuclear-safety result, deterrence, or
changed Russian behavior.

The [domestic capacity and state-leverage bridge](projects/ai-work-control/domestic-capacity-dependence-state-leverage-cross-source-bridge-v1.md)
now integrates SIPRI resources, procurement transactions and modifications,
realization milestones, ownership context, and international infrastructure
cases. The resulting cross-source chain is staged rather than collapsed:
resource allocation and commitment can precede operation, and operation can
coexist with external control or weak replaceability. Leverage remains open
until another actor changes behavior.

The state-capacity synthesis now makes that distinction concrete in the
data-center lane. LBNL's 176 TWh 2023 estimate and 649 TWh 2030 reference
scenario describe national modeled demand; Prince William County's $465.9M
reported TY2025 tax revenue describes local fiscal capacity; Virginia filings
describe modeled residential-incidence illustrations and reported large-load
drop events. These are not one benefit/cost estimate. Together they show the
political economy of infrastructure: growth can create public revenue while
leaving customer allocation, interruption risk, inspection, portability, and
community incidence unresolved. The missing state-capacity test is not another
load forecast; it is observed cost allocation, local experience, control or
switching rights, and an external actor changing behavior.

Two current infrastructure records make the stage distinction more concrete.
NYPA reports Smart Path Connect completed and energized, with physical corridor
and substation outputs plus projected system/customer savings; its public
accounting and tariff materials separately expose a revenue-requirement and
formal cost-allocation surface. PJM reports that about 4,000 MW of data-center
load disconnected in a July 2026 event, requiring backup generation and grid
management, followed by proposed ride-through controls and further regulatory
standard-setting. Completion and live stress response are stronger evidence
than a forecast, but neither record supplies retail bills, customer-class
incidence, local environmental burden, outage experience, or durable
affordability.

| Infrastructure stage | Direct observation | Still open |
|---|---|---|
| Completed transmission | Project completion/energization, physical assets, reported or modeled system savings, and formal revenue/cost-allocation artifacts | Retail pass-through, household bill/reliability result, geographic and class incidence, acceptance, and persistence |
| Large-load grid event | Approximate load disconnection, operator/backup-generation response, proposed connection controls, and regulatory process | Facility attribution, final rule/compliance, residential interruption, bills, emissions, and local burden |

The records should not be combined into one grid-benefit number: one is a named
transmission realization with distinct reported and modeled value surfaces; the
other is a PJM operating and governance episode. See the [Smart Path Connect
realization record](records/us-nypa-smart-path-connect-realization-2026.json)
and [PJM large-load disconnection-response record](records/us-pjm-large-load-disconnection-response-2026.json).

The state-accountability records add a related but distinct implementation
pattern: **being able to count funding, reach, a current baseline, or a process
does not establish that the intended problem changed.** GAO found an estimated
$1.2 billion across roughly 470 State/USAID projects intended to counter
Chinese influence while agencies lacked readily available reliable portfolio
data and had no portfolio-wide results assessment. In a separate DHS review,
18 of 19 baselined major acquisitions met their *current* goals even though 15
had revised baselines and collective projected costs had risen $11.4 billion
from initial baselines. GAO's broader program-performance synthesis identifies
the same measurement boundary across federal programs and also documents a
counterexample: a DHS program improved its tracking/management process without
yet demonstrating preparedness impact.

| Administrative surface | What is visible | What remains open |
|---|---|---|
| Geopolitical funding portfolio | Funding, estimated project count, data/process gaps, and early/paused evaluation framework | Project result, partner consent, changed Chinese behavior, legitimacy, or strategic effect |
| Major acquisition baseline | Current-plan milestone status, revised baseline history, projected cost movement, staffing/oversight changes | Fielded capability, user performance, mission result, safety, or external consequence |
| Federal performance management | Goals/data/process coverage and selected tracking improvement | Recipient adequacy, problem resolution, durable effect, trust, or political response |

The sources do not show that funding, rebaselining, or performance management is
meaningless; they show why outcome claims need a later compatible measure. See
the [GAO countering-China-influence record](records/us-gao-countering-china-influence-project-results-2020-2024.json), [DHS acquisition-baseline record](records/us-gao-dhs-major-acquisition-baseline-oversight-2025-2026.json), and [federal program-effectiveness record](records/us-gao-federal-program-effectiveness-2026.json).

The NYC Universal Access to Counsel study provides a stronger, causal
consumer/housing-court middle. Using staggered zip-code rollout and detailed
court records, its main instrumental-variable specification estimates that
tenant counsel reduced possession judgments by about 33.4 percentage points,
monetary judgments, and eviction-warrant issuance by about 34.0 percentage
points. The executed-warrant result was smaller and not consistently
statistically significant in the full sample. This is a valuable correction to
calling all legal access merely formal: representation can alter a court
process. It is not a completed housing-recovery chain—the study does not follow
rent arrears, later moves, homelessness, credit, work, health, schooling, or
whether the underlying rent problem was solved.

| Evidence unit | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|
| NYC housing-court records with staggered counsel rollout | low-income tenant court case → counsel → lower modeled possession-judgment and warrant-issuance stages | payment/arrears, physical eviction, later stay/move, housing quality, debt/credit, household wellbeing, and generalization outside the setting |

The court-record effect is not a national eviction rate or a claim that every
represented tenant remained housed. See the [NYC tenant legal-representation
record](records/us-nber-nyc-tenant-legal-representation-housing-court-2016-2019.json).

The [eviction-order consequence record](records/us-nber-eviction-order-homelessness-health-earnings-credit-2022.json)
adds the downstream counterpart. Linked administrative data from Cook County
and New York City show tenants' earnings/employment decline and financial
distress/hospital visits rise before court; using random assignment to judges
with different leniency, the study then estimates that an eviction order adds
homelessness and hospital visits and reduces earnings, durable-goods
consumption, and credit access in the first two years, with later indebtedness
and lower credit scores. Reported housing and labor effects were driven by
female and Black tenants. An eviction order is therefore an additional causal
shock, not simply a label for the prior crisis. The two-city study does not
reconstruct the initial shock, aid and counsel route, landlord response, move
destination, household care network, later housing quality, or the effect of
preventing an order on durable housing security.

The carbon-tariff model adds a geopolitical economic-policy boundary. In its
counterfactual, a U.S. tariff alone raises modeled U.S. emissions while lowering
foreign emissions and achieves about half the global emissions reduction of a
combined tariff-plus-domestic-carbon-tax policy; it can nevertheless improve
modeled U.S. GDP and welfare. The result distinguishes domestic, foreign,
global, and welfare surfaces, and its multilateral tariff-only result is not
monotonic in membership. It is not an implementation study: actual tariff
incidence, firm relocation, household bills, retaliation, partner acceptance,
and leverage remain unobserved. See the [carbon-tariff/global-coordination
record](records/us-nber-carbon-tariff-domestic-emissions-global-coordination-2026.json).

The new [Poland reciprocity and co-production finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-042.md)
adds an official response-stage layer: Poland's 2026 announcements describe
potential buyer/seller access in a US-linked UAS/C-UAS arrangement and planned
regional Barracuda B500M co-production with the US, Estonia, and Latvia. This
is evidence of institutional and industrial response around a procurement
relationship, not evidence of output, delivery, public reception,
replaceability, or bargaining leverage. The next test is to observe the named
transaction, plant, workforce, component-control, delivery, or switching
event.

The July 6 Bydgoszcz implementation account adds a more specific stage inside
that response: assembly followed by production at PGZ/WZL-2, reported PGZ
exclusivity for a Polish version, and technology/know-how/competence transfer
language. Those are agreement terms, not commissioned output or independently
verified control rights.
An August 19 BBN follow-up with Anduril adds a dated senior-level governance
checkpoint focused on the Barracuda-500M program and Polish implementation
competencies. It still does not establish a production milestone, output,
delivery, or amended contract.
The Ministry of State Assets' detailed production page adds reported targets of
thousands of systems and staged Polish/European supplier localization. These
make scale and local-content denominators testable, but remain plans rather
than completed units or observed supplier shares.
The September 9 Polish-US FMF agreement adds a broader financing checkpoint:
Poland reports $4 billion in Foreign Military Financing and acceleration of
existing contracts, including Apache helicopters. It is not Barracuda-specific
and therefore belongs to the resource/implementation stage, not the production,
delivery, or leverage stage.
The [September realization watchpoint](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-043.md)
records the current public-source non-observation: no first assembly,
commissioning, completed quantity, acceptance, delivery, or measured
local-content share was located in the checked primary-source set.

The new [public-system buffer-and-credit bridge](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-012.md)
adds a financial-coping stage to the public-system row. SIPP shows that SNAP
transitions can sit beside declining resources, stable job counts, and
following hardship; Federal Reserve SHED shows that liquid capacity,
revolving balances, linked balance growth, student-loan difficulty, and
retirement actions are distinct coping layers. This is a cross-source
mechanism map, not evidence that a public-system transition caused credit use
or that financial pressure produced political action.

The follow-on [route-and-buffer synthesis](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-013.md)
and [public-system route-security triangulation record](records/us-public-system-route-security-triangulation-2026.json)
add the WBNS lived-route and charitable-food layers. Across 51 state/DC
aggregates, SNAP participation has near-zero simple correlation with application
timeliness (0.033) and only 0.211 with recertification timeliness, so it cannot
stand in for route quality. Separately, among 182 people reporting a
recertification-related interruption, 40% reported too little time after notice,
32% not receiving notice, and 22% difficult paperwork (overlapping responses).
These sources place notice, recertification, interruption, cash/credit capacity,
supplemental food, and unmet need in one staged public/private safety-net map
