without pooling state, respondent, person-month, and conditional-credit
denominators. The result is stronger evidence for incomplete protection and
route substitution, not a same-household causal chain or a political-trust
finding.

The [SNAP reason-to-food-security record](records/us-sipp-snap-transition-reason-following-food-security-2024.json)
adds a narrower same-person adjacent-month arrow: classified entry and exit
reasons lead to distinct following-month low/very-low food-security profiles,
with 26.12% after entry and 18.87% after exit in the aggregate classified
comparison. It strengthens the route-to-material-condition stage while
preserving the missing notice, effort, benefit amount, gap-day, remedy, and
recovery fields. Exit is therefore not treated as restored security, and the
reason cells are not ranked as program performance.

The [care-cost/time/work cross-source finding](findings/us-care-cost-time-work-currency-cross-source-001.md)
extends the care/health row by putting MEPS, SIPP, and SHED beside one another
without merging their samples. The layers jointly support a bounded social-
reproduction pattern: health and care costs can be transferred among system
spending, out-of-pocket payment, unpaid time, work capacity, borrowing, and
saving. The same-person dated event through household recovery, institutional
response, trust, or politics remains an open arrow.

The [IMF geopolitical fiscal-shock record](records/us-imf-weo-geopolitical-fiscal-shock-2026.json)
adds the April 2026 WEO defense-boom estimates. Across its historical
cross-country episodes, a typical defense-spending boom raised outlays by 2.7
percentage points of GDP over about two and a half years, with 67% financed
through deficits; the analysis reports a 2.6-point deficit increase and a
7-point public-debt increase within three years, with social spending falling
in wartime booms. The macro layer shows that fiscal and social-spending
trade-offs can appear between resource allocation and usable capacity, but
these cross-country estimates and the IMF's U.S. aggregate projections do not
establish a U.S. household burden or realized geopolitical leverage. The
required leverage test remains an observed refusal, switch, concession,
regulation, or external behavior change.

The [defense resource-to-capability finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-048.md)
now aligns that macro stage with SIPRI spending estimates, a bounded
USAspending JASSM/LRASM supplier extract, and the Poland-US procurement and
co-production sequence. It makes contract, supplier, implementation, and
realization stages more explicit while preserving the missing tests for
production, delivery, domestic incidence, replaceability, and external response.

## The strongest current composite proposition

The long-form reader version is [Unequal optionality is the recurring
structure behind pressure, adaptation, and power](findings/us-broad-program-unequal-optionality-path-001.md).
It carries this proposition through the household, institutional, work,
cultural, place, infrastructure, and geopolitical scales and records the
counterexamples and tests required before it can become a causal claim.

Across the current evidence, a defensible provisional proposition is:

> US social and political change is often organized by unequal optionality:
> households, workers, customers, public agencies, and states respond to
> pressure through different combinations of money, time, care, representation,
> institutional route, and ability to switch or refuse. The same visible
> outcome—continued consumption, benefit receipt, employment, care, voting, or
> infrastructure growth—can therefore coexist with unresolved burden or
> dependence.

This is a synthesis of recurring measurement patterns, not a causal estimate.
The current registry contains 1013 machine-readable records, 2357 observations,
and 164 source packets across 14 program themes. It does not claim that the
records describe separate evidence units rather than the same people or that one
mechanism explains all domains. The counterexamples are part of the
proposition: financial improvement can coexist with worse health; SNAP exit
can coexist with hardship; continued receipt can coexist with utility burden;
assistance participation can coexist with very low food security; adults and
children can experience different food-security states; care can occur across
work and financial paths; a reported response is not a verified remedy; and
infrastructure scale is not state leverage.

## New government-AI signal: adoption is outpacing operational accountability

The OECD Digital Government Outlook 2026 adds a public-sector technology and
governance route distinct from household or firm AI-use evidence. AI is used in
at least one government area in 35 of 36 OECD countries (97%), and 32 of 36
(89%) report government AI training programmes. Yet the enabling layer is less
complete: only 21 of 36 (58%) provide central AI-procurement support, and only
13 of 36 (36%) offer training for public-service use or policymaking. The
pattern is consistent with adoption concentrating first where processes are
structured and easier to standardize.

The accountability endpoint is thinner still. All 36 countries report at least
one AI guardrail, but only 14 (39%) require pre-deployment risk assessments, 11
(31%) conduct post-deployment audits, and 10 (28%) measure any financial or
non-financial impact of government AI use cases. Only 15 (42%) engage service
users and 8 (22%) have citizen feedback or complaint mechanisms. These are
country-level institutional indicators, not proof that any specific system is
unsafe or ineffective; they identify a recurring test for public-service
adoption: whether deployment is matched by procurement capacity, enforceable
controls, evidence of realized value, and a usable route for affected people to
contest or repair outcomes.

The state-administered-programs review adds a public-delivery version of the
same pattern. GAO's 20-program sample represents about $1.1 trillion in
FY2025 obligations, but funds and responsibility can pass through states,
Tribes, local governments, subrecipients, and contractors. Local flexibility
may improve fit and reach; the same chain can reduce visibility into risk and
correction. Only 5 of the 20 selected programs had documented evidence of
identifying and assessing fraud risks for action. This does not establish fraud
or service failure. It adds a cross-source test: when a public benefit travels
through more layers, ask whether the final recipient, the accountable agency,
and the repair route remain visible to one another.

The DHS acquisition review adds a geopolitical version of target movement.
Eighteen of 19 baselined programs met their latest goals, while 15 revised
those goals and collective projected costs rose $11.4 billion from the initial
baselines. Eight programs also lost at least 20% of staff, and department-level
acquisition oversight was reorganized. This is not evidence of failed defense
capability; it is a reason to keep original promise, revised target, resources,
oversight, and fielded use as separate evidence stages.

The older-worker review adds a person-level version of usable capacity. Older
adults were a substantial share of participants in six federal workforce
programs, yet used training less often and had lower post-exit employment than
younger groups in the selected comparisons. This does not establish age
discrimination or program failure. It shows why enrollment, service use, exit,
employment, job quality, and durable household security must remain separate
stages in the cross-source map.

The UI/concentration study adds a firm-power test to the work-and-benefit lane.
Extra search time lengthened nonemployment by about a week, but its earnings
response was weaker in more concentrated local labor markets. This does not
measure current household welfare. It shows why a benefit's practical value
depends not only on its duration but also on the worker's available employers,
alternatives, and bargaining room.

The [Census AI diffusion at firm, function, and task record](records/us-census-ai-diffusion-firm-function-task-2026.json)
adds a measurement and control split inside the technology lane. In the 2026
BTOS supplement, 18% of firms reported AI in a business function (32%
employment-weighted), while 23% reported workers using it in work tasks (41%
employment-weighted). Firm adoption, functional deployment, and worker task use
did not always coincide. Most adopting firms reported narrow scope—57% used AI
in three or fewer business functions and 65% in three or fewer tasks—and 66%
reported augmentation only; AI-related employment decreases appeared in 2% of
firms. Broader functional deployment and operational investment were associated
with reported employment decreases, while worker-task integration was not
significantly linked to headcount reduction after controls. These are
cross-sectional firm associations, not causal worker-level displacement,
productivity sharing, or control. AI capacity, worker use, and worker power
remain separate stages.

The Pew health-information survey adds an information-legitimacy layer. People
still rely most on clinicians, but also combine peer experience, websites,
social media, and AI; many struggle with conflicting claims. This is not a
measure of misinformation or medical harm. It extends the atlas’s recurring
split between available capacity and usable trust: an information source can be
available, convenient, or familiar without being judged accurate or converted
into care.

The influencer study adds a more personal authority mechanism. Large health
accounts mix professional credentials, coaching, entrepreneurship, and lived
experience; their audience may trust only part of the message while still
finding it useful. This does not establish misinformation or harm. It shows
that platform reach and relatability can generate practical legitimacy before
accuracy, sponsorship, and health effects are visible.

### What the latest broad rotation adds

The congestion-pricing and EMS rotation adds a public-service version of the
same pattern: a city policy can measurably improve emergency travel time while
patient outcomes, household charge exposure, and distributional fairness remain
separate questions. System speed is not automatically equal usable benefit.

The [Free Association compact implementation/oversight record](records/us-gao-free-association-compacts-implementation-oversight-2026.json)
adds the geopolitical version. The amended Compacts with the Federated States
of Micronesia, Marshall Islands, and Palau commit more than $6 billion through
2043 for grants, trust funds, health, education, and infrastructure while
providing U.S. military access and security rights. GAO also reports delayed
fund disbursement, rising construction costs, labor shortages, most required
reports arriving late, and all three countries' single audits late since FY2019;
delayed U.S. appointments further constrained committee work. Formal access and
funding commitment can therefore coexist with implementation and oversight lag.
The source does not establish completed projects, service quality, durable
self-sufficiency, local acceptance, migration effects, or U.S. strategic
success.

The rural-housing service-coordination rotation adds a social version: a
housing unit can become more usable through a local person who helps residents
cross paperwork, distance, and service barriers, while the official data system
cannot reliably count the people or evaluate the results. Visible housing
capacity and usable social support therefore also move on different clocks.

The [GAO rural-housing service-coordination record](records/us-gao-rural-housing-service-coordination-data-outcomes-2026.json)
supplies the institutional evidence behind that claim. GAO reports roughly
56,000 federally subsidized affordable-housing properties with more than four
million units, of which about 19% of properties and 9% of units were rural in
the reviewed inventory. FY2025 appropriations for two HUD coordinator programs
totaled $152 million; in 2024, ROSS funded 50 coordinators at 49 rural-headquartered
entities and the multifamily program funded about 113 rural grant recipients.
Site stakeholders reported help with eviction avoidance and Medicaid
applications, but four of five reviewed studies had mixed effects and HUD could
not accurately determine how many multifamily properties had coordinators or
routinely analyze required performance reports. Funding, coordinator presence,
navigation help, eviction prevention, enrollment, health, and durable housing
stability are thus distinct stages, not one measured outcome.

The remote-work residential-sorting rotation adds a place-choice version. A
quasi-random office-lease exposure design links remote work to more migration,
more space and ownership, different school use, and movement away from
income-taxing jurisdictions. This does not turn moving into wellbeing: the
paper does not follow the same household to later security, belonging,
displacement, or political action. It shows that removing one constraint—the
need to live near work—can make housing supply, money, schools, tax rules, and
the ability to move more important.

The Instacart rotation adds a consumer-market version: convenience and
platform access can coexist with hard-to-see fees, subscription consent, and
refund routes. The FTC announced $60 million in consumer refunds to settle its
allegations concerning fee disclosure, paid-membership enrollment, and
cancellation/refund access; its later inquiry extends the question to marketwide
food-delivery fees. This makes an alleged design → enforcement → announced
redress route visible, not a customer-level recovery rate. See the [Instacart
fees/refunds record](records/us-ftc-instacart-fees-refunds-rulemaking-2025-2026.json).

The [FTC AT&T data-throttling refund record](records/us-ftc-att-data-throttling-refunds-2026.json)
adds a service-quality version of that distinction. The FTC reports more than
$5.6 million in initial refunds in April 2024 for eligible former unlimited-
data customers and an August 2026 Zelle reissue route for people who did not
cash a check or accept a PayPal payment. The enforcement account describes
post-threshold speeds so slow that ordinary browsing and streaming were
difficult or impossible. A refund route and a service-access injury therefore
belong to different clocks: the public page does not disclose an eligible or
paid-recipient denominator, individual receipt, restored past connectivity,
substitute service, current provider choice, switching, or trust.

The [DC Solar for All crediting/remedy record](records/us-dcpsc-solar-credit-error-rate-remedy-2025.json)
adds a public-utility version of the same recovery problem. A DCPSC
reconciliation audit could not determine whether subscribers had been properly
credited because the billing data needed for that core question were not
provided in time. Consumer-side parties proposed $5.70 per subscriber per
month during the audit period as an error-rate remedy, and separate customer
guidance described front-loaded June/July credits and a planned September bill
adjustment. Program enrollment, a customer notice, a proposed remedy, and a
planned adjustment are not evidence of final commission adoption, a correctly
credited individual bill, actual savings, arrears avoided, energy security,
trust, or practical exit. The proposed average can over- or under-compensate
individual accounts when the account-level billing record is unresolved.

The [DCPSC monthly-credit-receipt record](records/us-dcpsc-solar-for-all-monthly-credit-receipt-2025.json)
adds an intermediate, project-level billing endpoint. Four listed
community-renewable-energy project rows reported every listed subscriber
receiving a monthly credit: 309/309, 303/303, 297/297, and 260/260. This is
stronger than a program promise but remains a selected four-row project slice:
it does not reveal an individual credit amount, Pepco bill line, subscription
charge, arrears, billing error, net savings, or continued receipt. It cannot
resolve the earlier account-level reconciliation gap or validate the later
program-wide savings estimate.

The [DC Solar for All reach/savings record](records/us-doee-solar-for-all-household-bill-savings-2025-2026.json)
adds a program-level endpoint that should remain separate from that audit. DOEE
reports nearly 10,000 households served and about $500 average annual bill
savings, or roughly $5 million annually across participants; the community-
solar subscription is free and credits are intended to appear on Pepco bills.
The route nevertheless begins only after enrollment is updated in Pepco's
system, credits are anticipated after about 60 days, and continuity depends on
income eligibility and an active account. Reported reach and average savings
are stronger than a design-only claim, but not evidence of a particular bill
line, correct credit timing, net savings after other charges, arrears avoided,
continued enrollment, energy security, or household recovery. These program
figures and the crediting-audit record are different source units, not a common
household outcome series.

The [FleetCor/Corpay fee-redress record](records/us-ftc-fleetcor-unauthorized-fees-redress-2026.json)
adds a small-business recourse route with a stronger legal finding but the same
recovery gap. A federal district court found FleetCor liable on all counts in
2023 and an appeals court upheld summary judgment in 2026; the FTC then
announced a proposed $100 million redress allocation and prospective controls
against billing without express informed consent and clear unavoidable-charge
information. The fund remains a proposed allocation subject to a final
Commission decision, with no published customer denominator, claim/distribution
ledger, receipt, restored liquidity, alternative fuel-card access, switching,
or business-survival outcome. A legally confirmed fee practice and a large
nominal redress pool are therefore not an account-level recovery measure.

The randomized meal-delivery customer-service study adds a different middle
stage. AI suggestions reduced response time and improved automated sentiment in
256,934 chats, but were least effective for repeat complaints where the
underlying issue was systemic; customers handed from an incomprehensible chatbot
to an AI-assisted human also had lower sentiment. Faster response and warmer
language can therefore improve an interaction without correcting the delivery,
billing, account, or product problem that generated it.

| Evidence unit | What is visible | What remains open |
|---|---|---|
| FTC Instacart case and fee inquiry | Alleged price/consent/cancellation practices, announced $60M redress, and marketwide rulemaking question | Exposure prevalence, individual fee, refund receipt/adequacy, grocery affordability, continued use, switching, and trust |
| Randomized meal-delivery customer-service experiment | Response time, automated sentiment, intent-specific AI performance, and chatbot-to-human handoff contrast | Actual refund/correction, repeat-contact resolution, retention, switching, and household welfare |

These sources do not describe the same firm, customers, or event. Their shared
consumer subtheme is narrower: **response quality is not remedy quality**. See
the [AI customer-service/recourse record](records/us-hbs-ai-customer-service-contextual-recourse-2020-2021.json).

The SNAP sugary-drink rotation adds a direct policy-to-consumption and
policy-to-meaning contrast: excluded purchases fell, but recipients' perceived
stigma rose. A policy can therefore be behaviorally effective while imposing a
social cost that a health or fiscal model does not fully count.

The [Urban WBNS food-insecurity persistence record](records/us-urban-wbns-food-insecurity-persistence-2019-2025.json)
adds a population hardship surface. Its weighted working-age series fell from
24.0% in 2019 to 19.9% in 2021, then rose to 27.7% in 2025; the brief's values
are unadjusted for sample-composition differences and its 2025 methodology
update means this is not a seamless causal time series. In the 2025 working-age
cross-section, food insecurity was 32.0% among adults in households with
children, 51.0% below 200% of the poverty line, and 52.1% among adults with
disabilities. The cross-sectional pattern identifies unequal hardship, not the
cause of any annual change, SNAP receipt, food price, charitable-food use,
dietary adequacy, health, or later political judgment.

The [charitable-food access record](records/us-urban-wbns-charitable-food-access-2019-2025.json)
adds an informal-support route that public-program measures can miss. In 2025,
16.1% of adults reported household receipt of free groceries or meals, rising
to 39.5% among food-insecure adults; 8.0% of adults and 28.8% of food-insecure
adults reported needing or wanting charitable food but not receiving it. Among
recipients, 51.6% reported at least one access difficulty. Among people with
unmet need, closed hours (45.5%), not knowing where to go (42.8%), discomfort
getting help (39.9%), unavailable needed variety (37.6%), and transportation
(35.2%) were all reported, with overlapping answers. Charitable food is thus a
real but incomplete buffer governed by time, information, comfort, transport,
variety, safety, and treatment—not a binary service-exists measure. The
cross-sectional survey does not establish food quantity or adequacy, provider
capacity, the causal effect of stigma, SNAP loss, later trust, or political
action, and it cannot show that charitable food substitutes for public aid.

The [California fast-food wage/small-chain record](records/us-nber-california-fast-food-wage-small-chain-entry-exit-2026.json)
adds a firm-side version of uneven adjustment:
aggregate employment can look modestly changed while small-chain entry slows
and exit rises. The incidence of a policy is therefore not visible in one
statewide average; it can move through firm survival, worker opportunity,
prices, and local choice on different clocks.

The [Healthy Diné Nation food-price and health record](records/us-nber-navajo-healthy-diet-fiscal-policy-health-null-2026.json)
supplies a place-specific counterexample to the price-to-health route. The
policy exempted healthy foods and beverages from sales tax while raising tax on
unhealthy foods and beverages, a reported 7--8% price wedge, but did not
produce a detectable improvement in the studied maternal and newborn
diet- and weight-related outcomes. The estimates could rule out gestational-
diabetes improvements as large as those reported for U.S. city sugary-drink
taxes. That null does not mean no one changed purchases; it points to the
missing middle of retailer implementation, cross-border shopping, cultural fit,
and exposure. It is not a universal test of fiscal nutrition policy or a
measure of store pass-through, purchases, household budgets, or trust.

The 988 rotation adds a crisis-care version: implementation improved the
front-door operation, but population mortality and broader mental-health
outcomes did not move detectably in the short run. The missing middle is the
care, housing, safety, and social-support system after the call.

The peer-loss rotation adds a social-network version of the same gap: a shared
event can produce more formal care for some young people and more offending for
others. Exposure, diagnosis, help-seeking, and recovery are therefore distinct
paths shaped by prior history and reachable support.

The comparative elite-network rotation adds a political-access version: in
historical Buenos Aires, Jockey Club membership was associated with a higher
chance of legislative office, while the advantage weakened after a
democratizing reform. Social connection can therefore become political access
when institutions reward personal networks, but the finding is not a present-
day U.S. estimate and does not show representative governance.

The organized-interests rotation adds a U.S. policy-responsiveness version. A
state-level NBER study links campaign contributions, party trifectas, a Gun Law
Index, and estimated constituent sentiment from 2000 through 2024. Contribution
and policy associations differ by party control, while the study's instrumental-
variable estimates do not find constituent sentiment itself causally predicts
policy change. Public preference, organized political capacity, party control,
enacted policy, implementation, safety, and legitimacy are therefore separate
stages; the study does not show that money always defeats public opinion or
that any individual constituent was represented or ignored.

The whistleblower-disclosure rotation adds an accountability-and-risk version.
GAO finds that selected agencies use different combinations of confidentiality,
retaliation investigation or relief, and awards, while workers may face fear,
identity exposure, or restrictive employment agreements and agencies may face
high report volume. Information, voice, protection, investigation, correction,
and trust therefore move on separate clocks; the presence of a reporting route
does not establish reporter safety, adequate remedy, or durable institutional
learning.

The Public Buildings Service rotation adds a reorganization-and-capacity
version. GAO reports that a 45% workforce reduction occurred without prior
strategic workforce planning, while performance measures and two-way feedback
were missing and selected tenant agencies reported service delays. Centralized
design, staffing, communication, service delivery, measurement, correction,
and customer confidence are therefore separate stages; later agency plans do
not yet show operational recovery.

The DOE nuclear-cleanup rotation adds an early-framing and contestability
version. GAO found that most of 21 reviewed large-project mission statements
named a particular solution before the full option set was tested, and selected
projects did not pursue cheaper technically sound alternatives after legal,
regulatory, or contractor-linked constraints entered the process. Need,
option generation, independent review, approval, construction, cost, safety,
and public consequence are therefore distinct stages; formal review does not
prove that realistic alternatives remained open.

The international-commitment rotation adds a geopolitical visibility version.
GAO found that 90 of 311 agreements and qualifying instruments reported to
Congress were late, while estimated public publication was about 86% overall
but about 53% within the 120-day deadline. Separate pages, identifiers, and
missing searchability further increased the work of finding and matching the
records. Formal commitment, congressional visibility, public usability,
partner understanding, implementation, and legitimacy are therefore separate
stages; delayed reporting is not by itself proof of concealment or invalidity.

The federal performance-review rotation adds a government-learning version.
GAO found that none of four selected agencies fully addressed new strategic-
review requirements in 2025, while OMB guidance covered 10 of 15 assessed
requirements. Formal goals, performance sites, and review rules therefore do
not yet demonstrate completed review, stakeholder learning, changed decisions,
cross-agency coordination, or improved public results.

The gig-work rotation adds a practical-exit version: access to one competing
platform made Uber drivers more responsive to conditions at the first platform
and lowered the study's implied monopsony markdown. A second option can create
worker leverage, but formal access is not the same as usable switching, better
net pay, or household security.

The dementia-coverage rotation adds a care-transition version: older adults
with probable dementia were more likely to have Medicare without supplemental
coverage before onset, while Medicaid eligibility rose without higher take-up.
Eligibility, enrollment, complete coverage, and actual care therefore remain
separate stages.

The neighborhood-opportunity rotation adds a place-market version: childhood
neighborhoods can shape later outcomes, while credit limits, search friction,
social spillovers, and behavioral barriers can prevent families from reaching
opportunity. A voucher or mobility service is therefore an offer that still
has to become a workable move.

The market-participation rotation adds a financial-culture version: investors
who leave markets are more pessimistic and see greater crash risk, and that
belief-driven sorting can concentrate risk among the investors who remain.
Participation, exit, realized wealth, and household security are still
different outcomes.

The consumer-credit rotation adds an information-and-bargaining version: a
comparison tool changed beliefs, negotiation, and loan rates, but also made
people more likely to borrow. Consumer power and consumer exposure can rise
together; a lower interest rate is not automatically a lower total burden.

The [historical competition-and-consumer-discrimination record](records/us-nber-competition-consumer-discrimination-historical-2025.json)
adds a different consumer-power mechanism. Using historical local-market data
from the decades before the Civil Rights Act, the study finds that increases in
nondiscriminatory businesses were concentrated in the least competitive markets.
Across more than 25,000 establishment prices, nondiscriminatory firms charged
more than discriminatory firms in the same local market, creating a documented
price cost for Black consumers seeking non-discriminatory service. This does not
turn a higher price into a welfare comparison or imply that discriminatory
service was equivalent; it shows why the availability of a formally open option,
its price, service quality, practical exit, and equal treatment must remain
separate. The historical design does not establish present-day discrimination,
online-market behavior, or a universal competition-to-fairness relationship.

The NNSA partnership-capacity rotation adds a geopolitical version: paid work
for agencies and partners can retain staff, maintain specialized facilities,
and support missions, while the same sites face limited capacity and growing
core nuclear-security workloads. Shared capability and scarce capacity can
therefore rise together.

The [weapons technology-maturity and delivery record](records/us-gao-weapon-systems-mature-technology-rapid-delivery-2026.json)
adds a delivery-stage version. In GAO's 104-program assessment, major programs
averaged more than 12 years to deliver a capability. The 23 most expensive
Middle Tier programs planned at least $49 billion; 18 of 40 entered that rapid
pathway with immature technologies, and technologies in seven of eight reviewed
programs that ended without a fieldable capability remained immature and needed
additional development. Program priority and budget therefore do not yet equal
fielded capability, reliable use, sustainment, operational effectiveness, or
deterrence; schedule and maturity data also cannot assign a delay to a particular
contractor, service, or reform.

The comparative [public-official integrity-norms and enforcement record](records/comparative-nber-social-norms-enforcement-public-officials-2026.json)
adds an institutional-culture version. In Singapore's universe-level housing
transactions, civil servants disproportionately bought homes near planned but
not-yet-announced MRT stations, especially one to two years before announcement;
the pattern was concentrated among mid-level officials, rail-planning-connected
agencies, and relatives. Stronger formal enforcement substantially reduced both
direct and indirect misconduct, alongside survey/model evidence consistent with
integrity norms that persist but remain fragile. Social trust and enforcement
may reinforce one another in this setting rather than substitute for each other.
This is not a U.S. estimate, a universal corruption mechanism, or direct
evidence of citizen legitimacy, service quality, reporting, or political action.

The federal shared-services rotation adds an institutional-capacity version: a
centralized marketplace can offer efficiency while agencies still face legal,
technical, integration, leadership, and measurement problems. Shared
infrastructure and usable performance therefore move on different clocks.

The BLS–Census establishment-frame rotation adds a measurement version of the
same problem. Two official frames can show sharply different numbers of
workplaces and different levels of firm complexity because they use different
administrative sources and parent-firm rules. The discrepancy is concentrated
in part in home-care and disability services and also changes policy-model
inputs about concentration and establishment size. Before treating a business
count as economic power or decline, the atlas needs to know which workers,
worksites, services, and households the frame actually represents.

The microenterprise-credit rotation adds a firm-consumer version: in a Chilean
trial, large loans increased treated-firm profits and estimated customer
surplus, with little evidence of simply stealing sales from other firms.
Business growth can therefore create customer benefit, but estimated surplus is
still not the same as household cash or durable security.

The [American Indian heterogeneity record](records/us-nber-american-indian-heterogeneity-human-capital-2026.json)
adds a measurement-and-culture version: U.S. economic, educational, and health
outcomes differ by community, geography, resource endowments, historical policy,
and self-governance, while tribal and racial identification, changing
definitions, and data limits can hide those differences. The historical
synthesis identifies long-lasting harm associated with forced relocation,
boarding schools, and assimilation policy, alongside reported improvement in
some self-governing economic-development contexts. It is an essay and
measurement critique—not a pooled causal estimate, ranking of tribes, or proof
of any current policy's effect. A national average can therefore conceal both
ongoing harm and local improvement.

The [nursing-home inspection-timing and care-quality record](records/us-nber-nursing-home-inspection-predictability-care-quality-2026.json)
adds an oversight-and-care version. Seventy-four percent of inspections occurred
40--60 weeks after the previous one, and the reported composite care-effort
measure was highest around inspection, declined afterward, and rose again as
the next one approached. A one-standard-deviation increase in that measure was
associated with a 0.2-percentage-point (5.4%) lower mortality rate. The separate
dynamic model estimates that making timing unpredictable would save about 92
additional lives annually versus the current regime, while reducing the
information available to regulators; that is a model comparison, not observed
reform impact. A formal check can improve performance and still invite
organizations to time their effort around it, without measuring complete care
quality, resident wellbeing, or family experience.

The work-and-technology rotation adds a useful distinction between exposure
and usable room to adapt. An NBER study finds that many workers in highly
AI-exposed occupations also sit in occupations with above-median adaptive
capacity, while a smaller group—especially in clerical and administrative
roles—faces both high exposure and low expected capacity. The working-paper
and published versions use different occupation coverage and counts, so they
are kept as separate observations. The stable cross-source insight is that a
visible pressure does not determine the outcome by itself: the practical exit
route, including skills, mobility, time, money, local demand, and employer
support, can divide resilience from vulnerability. The study does not observe
actual displacement, retraining success, wages, or household security.

The follow-on retraining rotation adds an observed route between that capacity
and an earnings result. In a U.S. workforce-program sample, estimated
quarterly earnings returns for trainees starting in highly AI-exposed work rose
from about $1,000 before 2020 to about $3,000 in 2022–2024 relative to matched
job-search-only users. The reported route was mainly movement into less
AI-exposed occupations, with a smaller role for AI-complementary training. This
is evidence of a possible adaptation route, not proof that training was equally
reachable, completed, voluntary, durable, or good for household security.

The latest cultural-place rotation adds BEA's outdoor-recreation account. It
shows a $696.7 billion national footprint and sharply different state shares,
while leaving resident access, affordability, local ownership, displacement,
conservation, and belonging unmeasured. A place can therefore gain a large
recreation economy without the account proving broad cultural access or shared
benefit.

The household-economy rotation adds BEA's expanded household-production
account. Unpaid household production represented about 16% of total output and
30% of consumption in 2024, with both shares gradually declining over roughly
20 years. This makes the market/nonmarket boundary visible without proving who
performed the work, who had a choice, or whether household security improved.
See the [BEA household-production account record](records/us-bea-household-production-account-2024.json).

The firm-finance rotation adds the Federal Reserve small-business credit route.
Application, lender choice, risk category, and approval are observable before
funding use, repayment, survival, jobs, or local benefit. This is a distinct
firm-side version of unequal practical agency: formal lender availability is
not the same as a usable amount on workable terms.

The new [local economic participation synthesis](US-LOCAL-ECONOMIC-PARTICIPATION-SYNTHESIS_V1.md)
consolidates the firm, disaster, investment, and place-economy routes. It
provides a stopping rule: another aggregate money or investment total adds
little unless it identifies local receipt, terms, use, distribution, durability,
or exit.

The geopolitical coordination rotation adds a process stage between agreement
and capability. GAO's DOD review shows a substantial international-arrangement
portfolio alongside decline, review delays, and communication gaps. The safe
cross-source conclusion is that formal cooperation and usable joint capability
run on different clocks; partner use, deterrence, and legitimacy remain open.


The latest rotation strengthens the same proposition across several different
scales rather than opening a new single-topic lane. The health-cost synthesis
shows that care pressure can travel through delayed care, borrowing, reduced
savings, work adaptation, credit exposure, and a short-lived civic response;
the sequence is informative but still combines compatible designs rather than
one person's complete bill-to-recovery history. The remedy rotation separates
reported receipt, repeat-remedy eligibility, institutional order completion,
and borrower or worker recovery. The firm/infrastructure rotation adds a US
GaN manufacturing route where a domestic production path and scheduled samples
precede qualification, scaled output, upstream material independence, and
strategic leverage. The worker and consumer routes therefore do not show one
universal failure of institutions: some stages are visibly delivered, but the
later question is whether delivery is adequate, durable, usable, and trusted.

This is the broader cultural reading: people and institutions do not respond
to pressure through one ladder from hardship to backlash. They conserve,
search, borrow, care, work, complain, accept, refuse, switch, stay, organize,
or withdraw according to the options available to them. The atlas is now
strongest at mapping these conversion choices and the power that shapes them;
it remains weakest at observing the same unit after the formal response.

The latest local-capacity rotation adds a bounded BDS county-sector screen and
two overlays. County business turnover diverges by retail, transportation,
health/social assistance, and accommodation/food rather than forming one
resident-facing capacity signal; the same-year BDS–PLACES comparison produces
near-identical routine-checkup and transportation-insecurity medians across
negative and nonnegative health-sector-flow counties; and the BDS–HRSA overlay
shows that employer flow and shortage designation do not identify provider
adequacy. The [three canonical records](records/us-bds-county-sector-divergence-2026.json)
preserve these as observed ecological stages with explicit cross-vintage and
resident-outcome boundaries.

The latest platform-remedy comparison adds the [Mansoor same-worker record](records/au-mansoor-platform-alternative-earnings-remedy-2025.json):
substitute earnings and a formal order are visible in one adjudicated case,
while payment receipt, actual access, durability, and later choice remain
open. This strengthens the alternative-to-response bridge without converting a
comparative case into a US prevalence or recovery estimate.

The [Waheed interim-payment record](records/au-waheed-interim-payment-obligation-2026.json)
adds a related legal-implementation stage: a consent-stay payment obligation is
visible even though receipt, actual platform access, final quantum, appeal
outcome, and later work remain unobserved. Together, the comparative cases
separate alternative work, remedy order, conditional payment, payment receipt,
and practical recovery rather than collapsing them into one outcome.

The [Al Hussein record](records/au-al-hussein-reactivation-deemed-work-2025.json)
adds a counterpattern in the same comparative lane: legal reactivation and
deemed work can be ordered while lost-pay quantification, payment, actual
access, and later work remain open. This keeps procedural remedy, legal fiction,
payment, and lived recovery as separate clocks.

The [Malaysia Act 872 institutional-response record](records/my-grab-act872-institutional-response-2026.json)
extends the comparison from adjudicated worker cases to governance design:
Grab's public page reports earnings, appeal/dispute, and social-protection
commitments, while the official Ministry and Tribunal route provides statutory
and procedural capacity without a public GrabCar case identifier, award,
payment, or enforcement outcome. The institutional route is therefore evidence
of available governance architecture, not evidence of worker uptake, remedy,
trust, or durable control.

The [CPSC consumer-safety surveillance/recall record](records/us-cpsc-consumer-safety-recalls-import-surveillance-fy2024.json)
adds the system-level stages behind those individual recall keys. In FY2024,
CPSC reports 333 negotiated and implemented voluntary recalls covering about
41 million units, 2,969 regulatory-violation notices, more than 58,000 products
removed from e-commerce platforms, and more than 65,000 import examinations.
Its revised recall response measure reports a 51% average correction/response
rate in FY2024 and 44.55% in FY2025, but the method change, product mix, and
firm reporting prevent interpreting that as a household action trend. Recall
counts, examined imports, takedown requests, firm-reported correction, and
emergency-department injury surveillance have different units and clocks; none
alone proves exposure, notice, remedy receipt, avoided injury, or restored
safety for a consumer.

The [VRURC recall-implementation record](records/us-cpsc-vrurc-recall-implementation-2023-2026.json)
adds a first recall-key implementation bridge. A July 2023 notice covered
approximately 190,000 portable chargers, reported one fire and four smoke-
inhalation hospital transports, and offered a free replacement route. An
August 2026 MPR row reports 64,963 consumer-level corrections and 29%, but its
256,845-product denominator differs from the notice scale; the mismatch may
reflect different scope, version, or denominator definitions. The joined key
therefore shows a dated hazard, remedy offer, and reported correction stage,
not unique purchaser exposure, notice receipt, replacement receipt, safe
restoration, residual cost, trust, or exit.
The [Honda Talon recall record](records/us-cpsc-honda-talon-recall-implementation-2023-2026.json)
adds a second recall-key implementation bridge: a dated safety notice and free
repair route are visible, followed by an MPR row reporting 790 consumer-level
corrections and 44%, but the API's approximately 34,000-unit scale does not
match the MPR's 2,047-product row. This strengthens the correction-stage map
without treating firm-reported progress as notice receipt, completed repair, or
safe household restoration.
The [Sauna360 repeat-remedy ledger](projects/us-marketplace-product-safety/data/sauna360-repeat-remedy-event-ledger-v1.json)
adds a durability-stage counterexample: a 2025 repair route is followed by a
2026 CPSC expansion stating that the previous repair was ineffective and
requiring another repair. This shows that a remedy can fail after
implementation, while still leaving owner notice, repair completion, safe use,
downtime, trust, and exit unobserved.
The [cross-key MPR heterogeneity record](records/us-cpsc-recall-mpr-progress-heterogeneity-2026.json)
extends the two named recall bridges to a 17-row purposive snapshot: reported
consumer-level correction spans 0%–44% and total correction 0%–50%, with one
blank consumer-level row. The range maps implementation heterogeneity, not
consumer recovery, because recall duration, mix, and denominators differ.

The [IER individual-remedy record](records/us-doj-ier-individual-remedy-followups-2026.json)
adds a cross-domain paid-remedy comparator: four official summaries report
individual back-pay or front/back-pay payments and one reports a substantial
offer, with employer training or policy changes. This is stronger than a
reserved fund or conditional obligation, but the public summaries still do not
show independent receipt, restored employment, durable compliance, household
recovery, trust, or exit.

The [Walmart Spark driver-remedy record](records/us-ftc-walmart-spark-driver-remedy-2026.json)
adds a platform-worker version. A court-entered $100 million judgment directs
$79 million to drivers, $10 million to FTC-administered driver distribution,
and $11 million in civil penalties to participating states; it also requires
ten years of earnings verification. Walmart reports some pre-announcement
payments and ongoing identification/payment processing, but does not publish
the eligible-driver denominator, payment receipt ledger, unresolved-payment
count, corrected earnings record, continued platform work, alternative work,
schedule control, switching, or exit. Correcting a past pay shortfall is
therefore not the same thing as restoring practical control over platform work.

The [Uber occupational-licensing consumer-outcome record](records/us-nber-uber-occupational-licensing-consumer-outcomes-2026.json)
adds an upstream rule-to-service route. Using trip-level data, proximity-based
dispatch comparisons around New York City/New Jersey, and Houston deregulation,
the NBER study finds that licensing changed the driver pool without consistent
improvement in its measured rider ratings and telematics-based driving outcomes;
after Houston deregulation, entrants were indistinguishable from previously
licensed drivers on those outcomes despite age and experience differences. A
licensing rule can therefore alter access to platform work without settling
average measured service quality. The study does not measure fares, wait times,
cancellations, driver income, disability access, rare severe safety incidents,
or trust, and its two-city evidence is not a national rule evaluation.

The [Kyei merits-dismissal record](records/au-kyei-merits-dismissal-2025.json)
adds a negative remedy counterexample to the platform lane: process deficiencies
were identified, but a valid safety reason was accepted and the unfair-
deactivation claim was dismissed. No restoration, payment, later access, or
exit is observed. This prevents procedural defect from being treated as either
automatic worker protection or automatic proof of platform unfairness.

The [GrabCar first-hearing record](records/my-grabcar-tribunal-first-hearing-2026.json)
adds the exercised-route stage to that comparison: a reported first hearing
and continuation for evidence/witness examination are visible, while the
primary case identifier, award, payment, access correction, and enforcement
remain unobserved. This is institutional use, not proof of effective review or
worker recovery.

The [Pew 2026 AI concern and expected-jobs layer](projects/ai-work-control/pew-2026-ai-concern-partisan-jobs-layer-v1.md)
adds a current meaning-and-politics endpoint to the AI/work route. In the
June 2026 survey, 52% of adults were more concerned than excited about AI's
increased use in daily life, up from 37% in 2021; Democrats were at 56% versus
49% of Republicans, and 75% versus 68% expected fewer US jobs over the next
20 years. These are public expectations, not realized displacement or a
partisan causal effect. They make the missing bridge sharper: link actual AI
implementation and control to worker experience, attribution, policy demand,
and later action.

The [Pew AI daily-life/control record](records/us-pew-ai-daily-life-control-2026.json)
adds the adoption-versus-governance surface. In February 2026, 49% of U.S.
adults reported ever using a chatbot and 24% reported daily use; 42% used one
for information, while 38% of employed adults reported work-task use. Yet 63%
said AI was advancing too quickly, 70% expected personal information to become
less secure, and 67% had little or no confidence in effective government AI
regulation. Reported use, task-specific usefulness, personal control, privacy
expectation, and public-governance confidence can therefore diverge rather than
move in a single pro- or anti-AI direction. These are self-reported survey
surfaces, not telemetry, actual privacy loss, dependence, productivity,
political behavior, or evidence that one attitude causes another.

The [NYC algorithmic-work measurement record](records/us-nyc-local-law-25-algorithmic-work-study-2026.json)
adds a municipal visibility stage. Local Law 25 mandates a city study of
algorithmic tools and automated employment decision tools, including hiring,
regular employee work functions, and employee feedback, followed by a report
and recommendations. As of the source recheck, implementation was still in
planning and no completed study or public report was located. Measurement can
make a worker-control problem administratively legible before it identifies a
tool, exposed-worker denominator, decision, notice, appeal, correction,
compensation, workload/pay change, or exit outcome. A study requirement is not
itself evidence of changed employment conditions or worker protection.

The [workplace AI partisan-composition record](records/us-nber-politics-ai-partisan-composition-2026.json)
adds a composition-to-meaning boundary. Democrats reported frequent workplace
AI use somewhat more often than Republicans in Q1 2026, but the gap became
statistically insignificant after education controls and reversed with
occupation and industry controls. Perceived displacement risk instead showed a
new 4.2-point partisan gap. Where people work and what skills they hold can
therefore explain reported use while political interpretation begins to
diverge; neither endpoint establishes ideology, actual job loss, control, pay,
or political action.

The [Pew 2026 press-government legitimacy layer](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/pew-2026-press-government-media-legitimacy-layer-v1.md)
adds a complementary information-institution route. Seventy-one percent of
adults described the administration-news relationship as bad, while 55% said
news organizations have too much political power; that latter measure was 69%
among Republicans and 43% among Democrats. At the same time, 68% said it was
acceptable for journalists to criticize politicians and 56% said it was
acceptable for politicians to criticize journalists. The pattern is not a
single trust score: shared conflict perception coexists with partisan power
attribution and asymmetric norms of criticism. Exposure, accuracy, censorship,
subscription switching, and political action remain open.

The [Pew press-government/media-legitimacy record](records/us-pew-press-government-media-legitimacy-2026.json)
keeps those three surveys surfaces separate: 71% called the relationship bad,
55% said news organizations have too much political power (69% of Republicans
versus 43% of Democrats), 68% found journalist criticism of politicians
acceptable, and 56% found politician criticism of journalists acceptable. The
measures are perceptions and norms, not evidence of censorship, accuracy,
ownership concentration, audience exposure, institutional behavior, or causal
media effects.

The [Gallup 2026 institutional-confidence divergence layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/gallup-2026-institutional-confidence-divergence-layer-v1.md)
adds a broader legitimacy benchmark. Average confidence across 14 core
institutions is 27%, but large technology companies sit at 20% while small
business is at 67%; Republicans report 13 points more average confidence than
Democrats, with a 70-point presidency gap. The important result is divergence,
not a single trust collapse: confidence, use, dependence, institution-specific
performance, switching, and political action are separate surfaces.

The [Pew 2026 public-life engagement groups layer](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/pew-2026-public-life-engagement-groups-action-layer-v1.md)
adds a structured action surface. Mobilizers report 58% extremely or very
interested in politics versus 9% among Outsiders, yet federal-government trust
is low across all four groups (17% to 22%). National-news trust ranges from
45% among Outsiders to 64% among Connectors, while 72% to 82% say news tends
to favor one side. The comparison shows that political interest, civic
participation, source trust, government trust, and fairness judgments form
different currencies; action is not a single ladder from trust to turnout.

The [federal-building accessibility record](records/us-gao-federal-building-accessibility-complaints-2026.json)
adds a rights-to-contestation example. GAO reports 41 Architectural Barriers
Act complaints about GSA buildings from October 2022 through June 2026, with
25 closed and 16 still in process, while interviewed stakeholders generally
said the public was unaware of the route. Complaint processing is therefore a
visible institutional stage, not proof that barriers were corrected or that
people could use the buildings afterward.

The [FDA third-party device-review record](records/us-gao-fda-third-party-device-review-audit-2026.json)
adds a delegated-regulation stage. Third parties supplied 617 510(k) reviews
from fiscal years 2018–2025, but FDA lacked target audit time frames and some
recent deficiency audits took more than six months to close. Delegated review
is therefore visible capacity, not proof of timely correction, device safety,
patient access, or public trust.

The [Pew working-class identity record](records/us-pew-working-class-identity-2026.json)
adds status meaning to the material-to-political route. Sixty percent of adults
said “working class” described them well, up from 54% in 2024; the rate was 50%
among both upper-income adults and college graduates, 67% among Republicans or
Republican leaners, and 55% among Democrats or Democratic leaners. Blue-collar
workers were more likely than other workers to adopt the label (77% versus
61%), but the identity still spans resources and occupations rather than mapping
cleanly onto either. It is a repeated-cross-sectional cultural and political
self-description, not proof of hardship, class position, class consciousness,
voting, or a causal change in identity.

The [NBER multidimensional-signaling model](records/us-nber-multidimensional-signaling-cultural-politics-2026.json)
adds a theory mechanism to this meaning layer. If voters cannot observe the
whole candidate and cultural signals are easier to read than economic ones,
candidates may emphasize culture even when the economy matters more to voters.
This helps explain how cultural themes can become prominent without proving
that voters stopped caring about material conditions. Because the source is a
formal model, it does not estimate current U.S. prevalence, persuasion, vote
choice, or policy change.

The [U.S. protest-effects finding](records/us-nber-protest-effects-public-opinion-policy-2017-2022.json)
tests the next stage with observed collective action. The study finds that
most protest waves produced strong online attention but limited movement in
broader measured outcomes, while the post-George-Floyd protests were a notable
exception for Democratic support and racial legislative activity. This keeps
being seen, changing minds, changing votes, changing official behavior, and
implementing policy as separate stages. It is a cross-wave result, not proof
that protest lacks local effects or that violence is required for influence.

The [trust-and-democracy finding](records/us-nber-trust-democracy-economic-crisis-political-turnover-2026.json)
adds a crisis-conditioned institutional layer. Across country, subnational,
and individual analyses, lower generalized trust is associated with greater
regular political turnover after recessions, including analogous U.S.-county
patterns. This suggests that the same economic shock can carry different
political consequences in different trust environments, but it remains a
cross-level relationship rather than a single U.S. household-to-election
causal chain.

The [aggregate-shocks review](records/us-nber-aggregate-shocks-preferences-beliefs-review-2024.json)
adds a life-course cultural mechanism. The reviewed literature links major
economic and non-economic shocks to later preferences, risk attitudes, and
institutional trust, with stronger and more persistent effects often reported
when exposure occurs during young adulthood. The review keeps shock type,
place, cohort, method, and persistence separate; it does not turn a shared
crisis into a universal political or cultural outcome.

The [zero-sum-thinking finding](records/us-nber-zero-sum-thinking-ancestry-political-preferences-2023-2026.json)
adds a group-competition interpretation layer. A 20,400-person U.S. survey
associates stronger zero-sum beliefs with several policy preferences, including
both redistribution and restrictive immigration, and relates the mindset to
individual and ancestral experience. This shows that perceived competition can
organize political judgment without proving that scarcity is imaginary, that
history transmits through one route, or that stated preferences become votes.

The [cultural-distance finding](records/us-nber-cultural-distance-media-consumption-time-attitudes-1965-2020.json)
adds a multi-surface measurement check. Political-ideology and some income
attitude differences widened, racial groups diverged in consumer behavior while
converging somewhat on attitudes, and many other dimensions stayed stable.
This means “polarization” cannot stand in for every cultural change: group
distinguishability, social conflict, contact, trust, and political action are
separate outcomes.

The [Gallup corruption-perception finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-068.md)
adds a legitimacy signal that cuts across party lines. Gallup reports that 89%
of U.S. adults see government corruption as widespread, including 91% of
Democrats, 90% of independents, and 83% of Republicans; the reported concern
is lower for business at 71%. Shared alarm is therefore not the same as shared
cause, target, remedy, or action. The measure records a public judgment, not
verified corruption or a later vote, protest, organizing effort, service exit,
or trust change.

The [Gallup workforce-diversity finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-069.md)
adds a firm-side counterpoint. Public belief that diverse workforces improve
profitability and innovation rose across parties, while views of DEI priority
and business performance remained divided. This separates perceived
instrumental value from legitimacy and implementation: an audience can believe
a practice helps firms without agreeing that firms are doing it well or that a
particular program is fair. Actual worker treatment, firm performance, and
worker voice remain open.

The [Census CPS ASEC nonresponse finding](projects/us-census-survey-measurement-bias/findings/us-census-cps-asec-nonresponse-bias-2026-001.md)
adds a measurement layer beneath the material-pressure map. Since 2020,
survey-only income has generally run 2%–4% above linked-record benchmarks and
survey-only poverty slightly below them, even though many year-to-year
directions remain usable. This means the atlas must treat who is visible in a
data source as part of the social pattern: an estimate can be technically
official and still miss people unevenly.

The [Gallup courage finding](projects/us-courage-social-risk/findings/us-gallup-courage-social-risk-2026-001.md)
adds a cultural-risk stage between values and action. Many respondents say
courage is weaker, place high value on leader courage, and worry about the
personal cost of taking a principled stand. This does not show less actual
action; it shows why silence, delay, private action, and public action should
not be collapsed into one measure of civic engagement.

The [Gallup courage/social-risk record](records/us-gallup-courage-social-risk-2026.json)
makes the three reported surfaces explicit: in its May 2026 national survey of
more than 3,000 adults, 67% said courage in American society was weaker than
20 years earlier, 79% called courage from elected leaders very important to the
country's future, and 44% reported concern about the personal costs of taking
a principled stand. Perceived decline, normative demand, and anticipated risk
need not move together, and none measures observed speaking up, protest,
workplace voice, retaliation, or a historical behavioral decline. The
20-year comparison is a current judgment, not a repeated-person trend.

The [Senate peer-proximity record](records/us-nber-senate-peer-proximity-voting-2026.json)
adds an institutional social-contact mechanism. An NBER working paper using
randomized Senate seating reports an 11.9-point voting shift associated with
close peer exposure, concentrated within roughly 19.6 feet, and a larger
cross-aisle effect. Physical access can therefore alter institutional behavior
without any formal rule changing. The study cannot tell us whether the route
was persuasion, information, timing, or coalition access, and it does not
establish ordinary-voter persuasion, durable trust, or broad democratic
legitimacy.

The [human-AI political-dialogue record](records/us-nature-ai-dialogue-voter-persuasion-2024-2025.json)
adds a randomized digital-influence stage. In preregistered experiments linked
to the 2024 U.S. presidential election and a Massachusetts ballot measure,
personalized conversations with a candidate-advocating AI changed stated
candidate or policy preferences; related experiments in Canada and Poland
reported candidate-preference effects as well. These are experimental preference
endpoints, not verified votes, turnout, durable belief change, scalable campaign
effects, or democratic harm. The article also reports that not all presented
facts were accurate, so influence and informational reliability must remain
separate measures.

The [women's work and Temperance mobilization record](records/us-nber-civil-war-womens-work-temperance-mobilization-2026.json)
adds a historical household-pressure-to-politics pathway. A linked-record
working paper reports higher labor-force participation among wives and
daughters of disabled Union veterans, and more town-level Temperance Crusade
activity where women's labor participation and disabled-veteran exposure were
higher; newspapers and proximity to other protest towns amplified the pattern.
Household responsibility can therefore become political capacity through work,
information, and networks. The historical town result is not individual
persuasion, present-day gender evidence, movement success, or durable political
power.

The comparative [women's-employment preference experiment](records/comparative-nber-household-preferences-womens-employment-field-experiment-2026.json)
adds a separate cultural-updating mechanism. In rural Bangladesh, a randomized
six-week job assignment to a wife improved her own reported wellbeing but not
her husband's; a year later, both partners in households with that prior female
work experience were more likely to prefer that the woman take a job and voiced
fewer concerns about it. Direct experience can therefore update a household's
expressed work preference without establishing that preference was the original
constraint or that it becomes durable employment. This is comparative evidence,
not a U.S. estimate: it does not transfer the effect across labor markets,
childcare, safety, income, bargaining, or institutional settings.

The [Pew 2026 Latino American-dream and mobility layer](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/pew-2026-latino-american-dream-mobility-meaning-layer-v1.md)
adds a second status-meaning reversal. Among Latino adults, 57% say their
standard of living is better than their parents' and 53% expect their children
to be better off, yet 18% say the American dream is out of reach and 27% say it
does not exist. Immigrant Latinos report stronger retrospective and prospective
mobility than US-born Latinos, while US-born Latinos are more likely to say the
dream has become harder to achieve. This is a cultural and intergenerational
meaning pattern, not an observed income-mobility, migration-causality, voting,
or consumer-behavior result.

The [BLS 2026 Q2 productivity and labor-share boundary](projects/ai-work-control/bls-2026q2-productivity-labor-share-boundary-v1.md)
adds a current macro-to-worker counterexample: reported productivity rose 1.4%
annualized while real hourly compensation fell 3.3% in the quarter, and labor
share was reported at 52.8%, the lowest in the series beginning in 1947. These
are aggregate production and compensation surfaces, not worker-level control,
household security, or causal firm behavior. The direct BLS page blocked local
automated retrieval, so this remains a provenance-bounded memo rather than a
canonical machine record until a reproducible small extract is available.

The [Pew 2026 Muslim-American discrimination, capacity, and visibility layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2026-muslim-americans-discrimination-capacity-visibility-layer-v1.md)
adds a long-run inclusion and legitimacy route. Muslim population and mosque
capacity have grown and political representation is more visible, while 42%
of US adults report a negative impact judgment, 59% of Muslim adults report a
lot of discrimination, and the partisan gap on whether Islam encourages
violence is 76% among Republicans versus 29% among Democrats. Presence,
institutional capacity, treatment, public judgment, and representation are
separate currencies; none alone establishes safety, equal influence, or causal
political behavior.

The [firearm-safety means record](records/us-nber-firearm-safety-means-2026.json)
adds a cultural-political version of contested safety. A survey of more than
5,400 U.S. respondents finds that owners and non-owners can share a protection
goal while reporting different safety perceptions and preferring different
tools. Randomized information about private costs and non-lethal alternatives
shifted safe-storage and alternative-tool preferences more than current owners'
willingness to give up lethal firearms. Shared safety language is therefore not
shared risk interpretation or a common policy route; the study does not observe
completed behavior, injury reduction, crime, or durable public safety.

The [BLS 2025 major-work-stoppage and collective-action boundary](projects/ai-work-control/bls-2025-major-work-stoppages-collective-action-boundary-v1.md)
adds an observed conflict event to the work/control lane. Thirty major
stoppages began in 2025 and involved 306,800 workers, with 98% of involved
workers in service-providing industries and 232,800 in state or local
government stoppages. The comparison also shows why event count is not power:
2023 involved 458,900 workers across 33 stoppages, versus 306,800 across 30 in
2025. Stoppage, settlement, service incidence, worker security, and political
meaning remain separate stages.

The [Pew press-freedom limits record](records/us-pew-press-freedom-limits-2026.json)
adds a conditional-legitimacy surface to the media lane. Seventy-three percent
say press freedom is highly important to society, but 53% prioritize preventing
inaccurate reporting even if freedom is limited. Perceived complete freedom
falls from 33% in 2024-25 to 28% in 2026, while the partisan pattern reverses:
41% of Republicans versus 18% of Democrats see the press as completely free,
while high concern is 29% versus 64%. These are public norms and perceptions,
not censorship, accuracy, platform-control, or media-effect measures.

The [BLS 2025 foreign-born work, earnings, and labor-room boundary](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/bls-2025-foreign-born-work-labor-room-boundary-v1.md)
adds a labor-market migration surface: foreign-born workers were 19.1% of the
civilian labor force, with 4.2% unemployment versus 4.3% among native-born
workers, but median weekly earnings of $1,059 versus $1,236 for full-time wage
and salary workers. Occupational, regional, gender, and care differences are
visible, but the release does not identify causal migration effects, worker
displacement, household welfare, or public-service demand. BLS's shutdown-year
comparability warning remains explicit.

The [BLS 2026 candy-price, supply-chain, and consumer-adaptation layer](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/bls-2026-candy-price-supply-chain-consumer-adaptation-layer-v1.md)
adds a bounded consumer route from global inputs to cultural purchase. From
December 2019 to November 2024, BLS reports confectionery-material PPI up
87.6%, end-product PPI up 13.9%, candy-and-chewing-gum CPI up 32.0%, and
import prices for sugar/confectionery manufacturing up 73.3%; downsizing added
a measured 2.1% price effect. The result is a price/quantity/ritual-adaptation
case, not a general household-welfare or food-security estimate.

The [Pew 2026 national mood and future-expectations layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2026-national-mood-future-expectations-layer-v1.md)
adds a temporal meaning surface. Current dissatisfaction (69%) and the view
that the country's best years are behind it (59%) coexist with personal hope
(68%), and 66% expect greater political division by 2050. Republicans and
Democrats are close on that long-run division expectation (66% versus 65%)
even while present-direction judgments are strongly president-linked. This
separates national inheritance, personal affect, institutional expectation,
and partisan alignment; it is not a forecast or a behavioral causal estimate.

The [Pew 2026 political typology, values, and coalition layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2026-political-typology-values-coalitions-layer-v1.md)
adds a value-based political segmentation surface. Nine groups built from 30
questions reveal a large mixed center, a financially stressed and politically
ignored left group, a low-attention tuned-out middle, and sharp cultural
divides within each partisan direction. The typology also links domestic
identity to geopolitical posture: 71% say US sole-superpower status is at least
somewhat important while 62% say US policy should account for allies even when
that requires compromise. These are coalition and value configurations, not
fixed identities or causal vote estimates.

The [Pew political-typology/values/coalitions record](records/us-pew-political-typology-values-coalitions-2026.json)
makes the composition and cross-pressure visible. Its 10,357-adult, 30-question
typology assigns 21% of adults to two highly ideological right-anchor groups,
17% to two highly ideological left-anchor groups, 23% to an unconventional
right, 18% to an order-and-opportunity left, and 9% to a tuned-out middle. Even
within right-leaning groups, for example, the share saying abortion should be
illegal in most or all cases ranges from 83% among Faith and Flag Conservatives
to 43% among the Unconventional Right. Group size, issue clustering, identity,
and power/alliance preferences are separate descriptive surfaces; the
cluster-derived categories do not establish fixed identities, turnout, vote
choice, policy influence, or a future realignment.

The [foreign-policy cross-currents record](records/us-pew-foreign-policy-typology-cross-currents-2026.json)
shows why the national averages cannot be read as one public mandate. The
groups combine power and alliance preferences differently: Leftward
Progressives are skeptical of sole-superpower status but strongly favor allied
consideration, while the two most pro-Trump groups favor dominance but more
often reject compromise when allies disagree. Other Republican-oriented groups
are more favorable toward NATO. This is a cultural interpretation of
legitimacy, not a forecast of policy, cost-bearing, alliance compliance, or
external behavior.

The [Pew 2026 midterm economy and political-attribution layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2026-midterm-economy-political-attribution-layer-v1.md)
adds a dated material-pressure-to-politics junction. Economy and affordability
are the leading issue voters want candidates to discuss (29% and 15%), but
neither party has a clear economic-policy advantage (37% Democratic versus 36%
Republican); 51% say neither party represents them on AI policy. Presidential
attribution and congressional legitimacy also diverge: 42% frame their vote as
against Trump while 72% view Congress unfavorably. This identifies political
salience and representation uncertainty, not a causal price-to-vote arrow.

The [EIA 2024 household energy-insecurity consequence layer](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/eia-2024-household-energy-insecurity-consequence-layer-v1.md)
adds an infrastructure-to-household endpoint. Among 132.54 million primary
residence homes, 43.56 million reported at least one listed energy-insecurity
condition, 32.89 million reduced or forgone food or medicine to pay energy
costs, and 16.19 million received a disconnect or delivery-stop notice. The
categories can overlap and do not establish a price cause, completed shutoff,
assistance response, or health effect; they do show why service continuity,
payment threat, substitution, equipment, and remedy must remain separate.

The [energy household-consequence/public-restoration synthesis](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/findings/us-repeat-energy-crises-001.md)
connects that EIA surface to LIHEAP reach/restoration and the retained SIPP
utility-to-following-outcome screen. It makes the missing middle explicit:
household sacrifice or notice is not verified assistance, program reach is not
prevention, restoration is not durable stability, and following hardship is not
a causal bill effect. The next test is a dated household bill or notice linked
to assistance, reconnection, next-bill status, and later recovery or action;
the three current sources remain non-pooled.

The [GAO 2026 FEMA disaster-assistance delivery and capacity layer](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/gao-2026-fema-disaster-assistance-delivery-capacity-layer-v1.md)
adds the institutional-response stage. FEMA provided over $3 billion to 1.2
million people and households across recent disasters, but GAO also reports
long helpline waits, confusing eligibility letters, post-disaster housing
bottlenecks, and access risks for older, disabled, rural, or offline survivors.
The distinction between authorized support, reachability, receipt, and recovery
is now explicit; aggregate assistance is not proof of a protected outcome.

The [New York insurance complaint/availability record](records/us-new-york-dfs-insurance-complaints-availability-2025.json)
adds a state-regulatory route to the cost of staying insured. DFS received
39,443 insurance complaints in 2025, closed 39,064, and reported more than
$121 million in recovery for consumers and providers. Within the combined
property-casualty/service-contract category, 498 of 2,869 closed complaints
had the agency's positive-consumer-outcome label and $17.95 million in reported
recovery. At the same time, DFS required insurer reporting on homeowners
availability, underwriting plans in catastrophe-exposed areas, and quarterly
terminations, which creates a market-observation route but not a published
availability result. Complaint closure, an administrative positive outcome,
reported recovery, insurance affordability, claim adequacy, repair completion,
replacement coverage, and staying in place remain separate endpoints.

The [GAO 2026 EDA disaster-economic recovery outcomes layer](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/gao-2026-eda-disaster-economic-recovery-outcomes-layer-v1.md)
extends the response chain from survivor assistance to community economic
capacity. EDA awarded about $2.1 billion in disaster-recovery grants across
FY2014–FY2024 and coordinated 45 economic-recovery missions in 2013–2025, but
GAO found that current measures cannot establish whether intended outcomes
occurred and that grantee-reported performance data were not independently
validated. This makes measurement capacity itself part of the trend: visible
obligations and projects can coexist with uncertain firm, worker, customer,
and resident outcomes. The bounded 55% no-jobs/private-investment result among
292 analyzed grants is a warning about reported performance, not a 55% failure
rate for the full portfolio.

The [disaster debris-contracting record](records/us-gao-disaster-debris-contracting-information-2026.json)
adds a preparedness-and-information stage. Six of 16 selected governments had
advance debris-removal contracts, while FEMA role confusion and sharply
different public-reporting timelines followed Los Angeles wildfires and
Hurricane Helene. A contract may speed field work and a map may improve public
legibility, but neither proves equitable cleanup, household safety, local
business recovery, or trust; physical recovery and visible recovery remain
separate clocks.

The [federal-program-effectiveness record](records/us-gao-federal-program-effectiveness-2026.json)
generalizes that measurement boundary across program types. GAO finds that
federal programs may track money spent, people reached, or internal performance
processes without having clearly defined goals or relevant outcome data. In a
selected set of 15 programs serving pregnant women, young children, and their
families, 12 had performance-management processes, but that did not establish
adequate or durable household outcomes; only about one-third of surveyed
managers reported access to robust evaluations. The DHS Securing the Cities case
is a counterexample: oversight improved goals, milestones, and financial
tracking from 2019 to 2024, while preparedness impact remained unmeasured.
Management capacity is therefore a real institutional change, not proof that
the underlying problem was solved.
