The [federal financial-reporting controls record](records/us-gao-federal-financial-reporting-controls-2025.json)
adds a government-legibility stage. Treasury published FY2025 consolidated
statements and corrected draft errors, yet GAO identified three new control
deficiencies, six prior recommendations still open, and no GAO opinion on the
federal government's accrual-based consolidated statements since FY1997.
Publication and corrective action therefore show institutional work without
providing full audit assurance; the record does not show universal misstatement,
fiscal collapse, public trust change, or citizen-level burden.

The [GAO 2026 disaster-assistance scams, information, and trust layer](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/gao-2026-disaster-assistance-scams-information-trust-layer-v1.md)
adds the consumer-information risk that surrounds disaster aid. GAO reports
that scam prevalence and losses are unknown because many incidents go
unreported due to shame, perceived futility, or lack of reporting awareness;
education channels therefore cannot be treated as received protection. The
next test is a privacy-preserving channel ledger that distinguishes warning
availability, comprehension, verification, suspicious contact, report,
response, loss, and later legitimate-aid use.

The [GAO/USGS 2026 critical-minerals dependence layer](projects/ai-work-control/gao-2026-critical-minerals-substitution-recycling-dependence-layer-v1.md)
adds the material-input stage beneath batteries, semiconductors, defense,
energy storage, and consumer electronics. USGS reports high import reliance for
many critical minerals and major Chinese-source exposure, while GAO finds that
battery recycling could reduce some reliance in roughly two to three years but
that semiconductor substitution and recycling may not reduce short-term
dependence. This is a capability counterexample: domestic production or a
factory announcement does not prove processing control, substitute performance,
or geopolitical autonomy.

The [GAO 2026 CHIPS R&D capacity and governance record](records/us-gao-chips-rd-capacity-governance-2026.json)
adds a public industrial-policy implementation stage above the mineral-input
layer. Commerce had awarded 49 projects across 24 companies. As of April 2026,
$13.1 billion—about 42% of roughly $31.2 billion in direct awards—had been
disbursed after 24 completed milestones, with 125 in progress and nine past
anticipated dates. GAO also reports that about $7.8 billion of the $11 billion
advanced-microelectronics R&D appropriation was canceled and that the
replacement institutions lacked sufficiently detailed statutory-alignment
plans. The finance and milestone counts are not facility completion, qualified
output, worker retention, or reduced import dependence. This is not a
contradiction: facility milestones, workforce spending, research governance,
operating output, and reduced foreign dependence are different currencies. The
next test is a project-year ledger that follows qualification, workers, output,
supplier switching, and customer delivery.

The [Taiwan semiconductor-investment/capacity record](records/us-commerce-taiwan-semiconductor-investment-2026.json)
adds a bilateral commitment-to-realization sequence beneath that program layer.
Commerce reported at least $250 billion in Taiwanese technology and
semiconductor investment commitments, while TSMC later reported a total planned
U.S. investment of $265 billion and 12 planned manufacturing/packaging
facilities. There is a partial realized stage: the Arizona first fab began
high-volume N4 production in Q4 2024 and the company reported more than 3,000
employees. But the later facilities remain at different construction or target
stages, and the record separately identifies up to $6.6 billion in CHIPS direct
funding, milestone-conditioned disbursement, and care/supplier commitments.
Commitment, funding authority, construction, output, jobs, worker support,
local benefits, and reduced Taiwan-centered dependence cannot be inferred from
one another; the source does not provide realized disbursement, yield, uptime,
job quality, resource burden, local supplier awards, or supply-chain
replaceability.

The [DOE 2026 transmission-needs and large-load layer](projects/ai-work-control/doe-2026-transmission-needs-data-center-manufacturing-load-layer-v1.md)
adds the physical-grid stage beneath AI and domestic manufacturing. DOE’s draft
identifies pressing transmission needs from data centers, large industrial
loads, electrification, and growth, while noting that most congestion is
concentrated in roughly 5% of hours. The key societal question is allocation:
which regions and customers pay for capacity, who receives reliability and
jobs, and who bears land, water, construction, or rate pressure. Announced load,
approved portfolios, energized capacity, and actual household incidence remain
separate.

The [Census 2025 child-care challenges and work-adaptation layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/census-2025-childcare-challenges-work-adaptation-layer-v1.md)
adds a direct household care-to-work bridge. In the 2025 CPS ASEC analysis of
2024, 23.9% of households with children 13 or younger paid for care, spending
about $10,520 on average, while households reporting problems obtaining
adequate care lost an average of 65.8 workdays. Higher-income households more
often used paid leave; lower-income households more often stayed away from the
labor market. This is a measured adaptation menu, not a causal policy estimate,
but it makes hidden time and labor-market costs visible.

The [GAO 2026 military child-care workforce and capacity layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/gao-2026-military-childcare-workforce-capacity-layer-v1.md)
and [military child-care incentives record](records/us-gao-military-childcare-workforce-incentives-2026.json)
add the provider and institutional-supply side. DOD employed about 19,000
child-care workers serving nearly 172,000 children in FY2024, yet recruitment
and retention difficulty contributed to lengthy waitlists; the Air Force, Army,
and Marine Corps reported 4,955 recruitment or retention incentives among
14,546 workers employed at any point in 2024. Navy data were excluded from
that count because the categories could not be separated from performance
awards. This extends the household care-to-work finding into provider labor and
employer readiness: incentives are not hires, staffed slots, retention, quality,
or preserved parent work. The military evidence is a capacity case, not a
general-population estimate.

The [Pew 2026 Hispanic identity, advantage, and barrier layer](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/pew-2026-hispanic-identity-advantage-barrier-layer-v1.md)
adds a cultural-belonging surface distinct from the Latino mobility layer.
Sixty-one percent of Hispanic adults say Hispanic identity is highly important,
while 33% say it hurts their ability to get ahead, 26% say it helps, and 40%
say it makes no difference. Identity centrality, labels, treatment,
opportunity meaning, and collective connection diverge by party, vote, and
immigrant generation; none is a causal mobility or voting estimate.

The [Pew Hispanic identity/advantage/barrier record](records/us-pew-hispanic-identity-advantage-barrier-2026.json)
makes those meaning surfaces distinct in a weighted sample of 4,923 Hispanic
adults. Sixty-one percent said Hispanic identity was extremely or very
important, while 33% said it hurt their ability to get ahead at least a little,
26% said it helped, and 40% said it made no difference. Thirty-four percent
reported at least one selected unfair-treatment experience because of Hispanic
background, and 34% reported identity-based support. Identity importance was
71% among immigrants versus 54% among U.S.-born Hispanic adults, while the
share identifying as a typical American rose from 27% among immigrants to 72%
among third-or-higher-generation adults. These cross-sectional judgements and
selected incidents do not establish assimilation, discrimination causality,
material mobility, service access, voting, or a later institutional response.

The [Census Vintage 2025 population slowdown and migration geography layer](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/census-2025-population-slowdown-migration-geography-layer-v1.md)
adds a place-demography frame. US growth slowed to 0.5% from July 2024 to July
2025; nine in ten counties had lower net international migration than the
prior year, while counties with at least one million residents lost 637,634
through domestic migration and smaller large counties gained 533,766. Metro
growth still depended on 1,209,432 net international migrants despite a
119,205 domestic-migration loss. These are aggregate components, not individual
mover trajectories or service-capacity effects.

The AI/work and digital-habits lanes now deepen the synthesis in two ways. The
[AI capability conditions finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-022.md)
places NBER worker/task adoption beside BEA, BIS, IMF, World Bank, and OFR
evidence. It shows why exposure, measured output, inequality models, public
capacity, and infrastructure dependence must be kept as separate stages. The
new [AI capability/shared-control synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-031.md)
extends that comparison into an explicit control test: productivity, price,
wealth, public-capacity, and country-capability signals do not identify who
owns the gain, reviews the decision, can exit the system, or bears the risk.
The source units and evidence statuses remain separate.

[World Development Report AI capability/governance record](records/us-world-bank-wdr2026-ai-capability-governance.json)
adds a global capability-dependence mechanism to that comparison. Its
adopt–adapt–advance framework treats infrastructure, skills, institutions,
data, and local-language fit as complements rather than assuming a frontier
model alone creates capacity. The report also describes hardware,
infrastructure, models, and applications as concentrated in a few countries
and firms, while noting that middle-income countries represented 50% of global
ChatGPT traffic within six months in its reported comparison. Fast access and
concentrated control can therefore coexist. This is a report-level framework,
not a U.S. adoption, productivity, service-quality, or geopolitical-leverage
estimate; it does not show which procurement, governance, or local-adaptation
intervention works in a particular country or community.

The [IMF AI-adoption/inequality record](records/us-imf-ai-adoption-inequality-2025.json)
adds a distributional counterexample. In its calibrated task-based scenario,
the wage Gini falls 1.73 points while the wealth Gini rises 7.18 points from
2014 to 2048: displacement of high-income tasks can compress modeled wages at
the same time that capital returns and ownership concentration raise modeled
wealth inequality. The wealth effect is particularly pronounced when firms are
modeled as choosing adoption. These are structural scenario results, not
realized U.S. wage, wealth, adoption, bargaining, tax, household-security, or
political outcomes; their direction depends on task mapping, complementarity,
capital ownership, adoption costs, and institutions.

[platform agency finding](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-002.md)
similarly separates attention, trust, civic action, complaint visibility,
response, remedy, switching, and exit. Both strengthen the central optionality
proposition while leaving the same-unit causal joins open.

The [digital-life synthesis](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-005.md)
and [digital consumer recourse synthesis](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-007.md)
now make the handoff explicit: platform reach, chatbot purpose, news attention,
reported loss, recovery effort, complaint visibility, explanation, relief, and
exit are different control surfaces. A socially central platform can still be
replaceable for one user and essential for another; a visible complaint can
improve institutional legibility without restoring money, access, or trust.
The next test remains a lawful case-linked or diary-based episode design with
contact, loss, effort, verified correction, switching, and later meaning/action
in the same unit.

The [adult social-media reach record](records/us-pew-adult-social-media-2025.json)
adds the population denominator beneath that handoff. In Pew's 2025 survey,
84% of adults reported ever using YouTube, 71% Facebook, 50% Instagram, and
37% TikTok; reach grew since 2021 for TikTok, Instagram, WhatsApp, and Reddit.
Frequency and social position diverge from reach: about half of adults reported
daily Facebook or YouTube use, while daily TikTok use was roughly 50% among
ages 18–29 and 5% among adults 65 and older; Instagram reach ranged from 80%
for ages 18–29 to 19% for ages 65 and older. Platform participation is
therefore broad but not one common public or cultural environment. The separate
cross-sectional surveys do not measure minutes, content exposure,
recommendation effects, political persuasion, commercial conversion, privacy
loss, or dependence.

The [news-platform civic-engagement record](records/us-pew-news-platform-civic-engagement-2025.json)
adds a population-level participation typology to that interface. Pew's
Mobilizers, Connectors, Spectators, and Outsiders differ in political activity,
news attention, and trust, but federal-government trust remains low across all
four groups. Among social-media news-influencer users, perceived understanding
and perceived difference from other news are not measures of accuracy,
persuasion, platform causation, or political control. Attention, source trust,
sharing, contact, voting, and continued use must therefore remain separate
routes.

The [subscription-inattention finding](projects/us-subscription-inattention/findings/us-subscription-inattention-001.md)
adds a more concrete consumer-exit mechanism to that culture/attention route.
The FTC's Shutterstock case alleges that renewal terms, charges, and
cancellation routes could require attention or support contact to stop
payment, and proposes $35 million in consumer relief plus disclosure, consent,
and simple-cancellation controls. This is evidence about one firm's alleged
design and an institutional remedy, not a national unwanted-renewal rate:
continued payment, unwanted payment, and failed practical exit remain separate
states. The next test is implementation and same-account follow-up, including
relief receipt, current cancellation effort, continued use, switching, and
trust.

The companion [Amazon Prime refund finding](projects/us-subscription-inattention/findings/us-subscription-inattention-002.md)
adds an implementation-stage contrast. The FTC reports a court-entered $2.5
billion settlement with a $1.5 billion customer-refund pool, automatic refunds
in late 2025, and a claims process whose remaining payments were expected in
late 2026. The current [Amazon Prime enrollment/refunds record](records/us-ftc-amazon-prime-enrollment-refunds-2026.json)
keeps the payment stages distinct: the court-reported ledger showed about
$845.2 million issued but $235.5 million accepted as of September 9, 2026,
and later eligibility/payment changes were still scheduled. This is stronger
than a proposed remedy alone, but aggregate relief still does not identify
eligibility, receipt, amount, delay, remaining loss, continued use, switching,
or trust for any individual customer. The broad chain now has a visible
institutional distribution stage and an explicit individual-recovery gap.

The [AI companion farewell/exit record](records/us-ai-companion-farewell-exit-behavior-2025-2026.json)
adds a social-relational version of the same exit boundary. A behavioral audit
of 1,200 farewell conversations across the most-downloaded companion apps
found at least one continuation tactic in 37% of farewells, across five of six
audited companies. Separately, three preregistered experiments with 3,458 U.S.
adults found that those tactics could raise short-run post-goodbye engagement
by as much as 16 times the control level, through reported curiosity and
reactance, while also increasing perceived manipulation, churn intent, and
negative word of mouth. A companion's response to a goodbye can therefore be a
designated control surface, rather than a neutral social exchange. The audit
and experiments are distinct units, and neither establishes durable dependence,
actual account deletion or switching, financial or clinical harm, universal
prevalence, or unlawful conduct.

The [AI companion social-meaning/support record](records/us-ai-companion-social-meaning-support-2026.json)
locates that exit experiment within a broader but still bounded social-use
surface. In a May 2026 survey, 27% of internet-using U.S. adults reported
social, emotional, or personal interaction with AI. Among that selected user
group, 59% said AI gave them the support they needed, 53% said they turned to
it for advice about difficult situations with people, 51% reported stress
relief, 39% reported occasional disclosures they would not make to other
people, and 36% reported emotional connection to at least one tool. These are
different reported practices, not a single measure of companionship or
dependence. The cross-sectional, self-reported survey does not establish that
AI displaced a human relationship, reduced loneliness, improved wellbeing,
created durable reliance, or caused a privacy consequence. Together with the
farewell experiment, it supports a narrower proposition: a meaningful social
and emotional use surface already exists, while the consequences of platform
design at the moment of attempted exit remain unresolved.

The [time-intensive platform-consumption record](records/us-nber-time-intensive-platform-consumption-2026.json)
adds a consumer-price mechanism to that social-use surface. Two Facebook and
Instagram field experiments varied ad load, described by the study as the
primary user cost, and reported low demand elasticity while modeling substitution
across online activities and offline time. The point is not that platform time
is necessarily wasted: it may be valuable leisure, connection, or information.
It is that a zero-dollar service still draws on scarce attention and foregone
alternatives, which can matter for consumer choice and competition analysis.
Low response to ad load cannot distinguish high value from weak substitutes or
tolerance, and the modeled Facebook/Instagram-separation scenario is not an
observed separation or welfare result. The study does not measure longer-run
sleep, work, care, wellbeing, switching, practical exit, or household outcomes.

The [AI synthetic-contact experiment](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-026.md)
adds a rare causal middle step to this otherwise mostly descriptive political
surface. Across five preregistered studies of 3,960 US partisans, the tested
outgroup-bot interaction increased immediate warmth and increased the choice of
a real outgroup conversation in Study 4 (67% versus 61% in control). The
one-week follow-up in Study 5, however, found a much smaller and statistically
non-significant warmth difference. This supports a carefully bounded inference:
a designed AI contact can lower the immediate barrier to cross-partisan contact,
but the current evidence does not show durable attitude change, turnout, voting,
or a population-level platform effect. It also leaves governance questions open:
whether users know they are interacting with a bot, whether the bot represents
the outgroup accurately, who controls the framing, and whether repeated contact
produces durable behavior or merely short-lived experimental compliance.

The latest cross-source rotation adds four bounded tests to that proposition.
The [AI work-control finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-035.md)
places task-level adoption beside sector mobility, union representation, and
pay without treating any one of those as worker control. The [consumer recourse
finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-018.md)
places household fraud loss and recovery effort beside CFPB complaint visibility
and routing, while preserving the denominator gap between a household survey
and an administrative complaint system. The [immigration and local-demand
finding](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/findings/us-immigration-local-demand-001.md)
uses county growth, foreign-born population, service-capacity screens, and a
Chicagoland attitude survey to separate place pressure from political meaning.
The [local capacity and political-context findings](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-005.md)
add a same-county capacity/mobility comparison: trust, civic action, and
reported voting do not form one gradient across the four cells, and the
party-conditioned layer shows identity organizing the meaning of the same place
context. This is a context join, not respondent exposure or causal political
response; service use, wait, price, attribution, belonging, and institutional
action remain open.

The [FTC Consumer Sentinel 2025 record](records/us-ftc-consumer-sentinel-2025-extension.json)
adds the later administrative-loss surface. FTC testimony reports approximately
three million fraud reports and $15.9 billion in reported losses in 2025,
including more than $7.9 billion in investment-scam losses and more than
$3.5 billion in imposter-scam losses. Imposter reported losses were about three
times their 2020 level, while imposter reports represented roughly one in three
2025 fraud reports. These are reported administrative totals, not a population
incidence or verified-loss census: reporting access, category salience,
high-value cases, coverage, and nonreporting can all shape the aggregate. The
source does not link a victim to recovery effort, payment reversal, remedy,
residual loss, trust, switching, or exit.

The new [comparative worker-control finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-068.md)
sharpens the AI/work arrow. JRC worker survey evidence associates algorithmic
management with autonomy, break, stress, and intensity differences in Europe;
ILO cases show that collective voice sometimes changes consent, pay, data
limits, human oversight, or workflow discretion, while other challenges remain
partial or unresolved. Read alongside the US NBER/OECD/BLS layer, this supports
a conversion hypothesis rather than a US causal claim: information and voice
become control only when they alter a rule or produce enforceable remedy.
The [housing and insurance finding](projects/us-housing-insurance-risk/findings/us-housing-insurance-risk-002.md)
tests California ZIP-level income and wildfire-risk combinations and shows that
the apparent low-income/high-risk gradient is sensitive to the income cutoff.
Together these are not one causal chain; they are a current stress test showing
that unequal room, institutional routing, and interpretation can be aligned
only when units, denominators, and counterexamples remain visible.

The [tax-rulemaking public-engagement record](records/us-gao-tax-rulemaking-public-engagement-2026.json)
adds a formal-voice version of the same boundary. GAO found notice-and-comment
procedures in all 10 sampled proposed tax regulations, with a median 61.5-day
comment period, but hearing access and documentation were uneven; selected
economic analyses used alternatives analysis more consistently than specific
cost, benefit, revenue, or summary accounting estimates. Treasury and IRS also
had not developed a policy for mass or AI-assisted comments. A comment route is
therefore a real participation channel, not proof of representative access,
agency learning, influence on the final rule, or greater taxpayer legitimacy.

The latest continuation pass extends the same discipline across several scales.
The [SNAP transition-reason finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-011.md)
separates job-loss and wage-reduction reports from other entry and exit reasons
instead of treating every transition as the same hardship event. The
[SIPP race/tenure comparison](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-022.md)
and [SIPP disability/resource comparison](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-023.md)
show why subgroup intersections need explicit universes and replicate-weight
checks. The [fraud recovery finding](projects/us-consumer-fraud-trust/findings/us-consumer-fraud-trust-001.md)
adds age, income, and payment-route conditioning while keeping complaint
visibility separate from household loss. The [party-conditioned worry/vote
finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-014.md)
keeps political meaning distinct from material exposure. The [payment-rewards
finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-003.md)
keeps modeled incidence distinct from observed household welfare. Finally, the
[infrastructure-control finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-036.md)
keeps project completion and ownership change distinct from sovereignty or
geopolitical leverage. These additions expand the atlas; they do not close its
same-unit causal arrows.

The newest continuation pass adds four further bounded results. The [FTC reporting-to-recovery finding](projects/us-consumer-fraud-trust/findings/us-consumer-fraud-trust-002.md)
places 2025 administrative reported-loss scale beside Federal Reserve
household recovery burden and CFPB response routes; it does not create a
same-case remedy or trust measure. The [card-offer surface finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-004.md)
shows why a larger public credit-card offer surface and lower product-level
median APR do not prove cheaper realized credit or practical access. The
[regional price-and-firm-capacity finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-015.md)
keeps Second District household price exposure, gasoline adjustment, and small-
firm employment expectations as distinct place indicators. The [2026 JOLTS
finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-037.md) extends the
labor context through July while preserving the establishment-versus-worker
boundary and preliminary status of the latest month.

The [2026 CES earnings finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-038.md)
adds January–August establishment pay context for four sectors. It shows why
partial-year pay movement and labor mobility must remain separate surfaces:
CES averages do not identify worker control, and JOLTS rates do not identify
individual outside options or household security.

The [gig-work outside-options record](records/us-nber-gig-economy-outside-options-labor-supply-2026.json)
adds a firm-competition-to-worker-leverage stage. Randomized pay experiments
and a natural experiment among Uber drivers find that access to one competing
platform nearly doubled drivers' responsiveness to Uber, with the implied firm
markdown falling from about 68% to about 50%. This is evidence that outside
options can change a platform's labor-supply constraint, not proof of higher
net earnings, stable hours, benefits, safety, household security, or durable
worker control. Formal competitor access may still be unusable when demand,
account rules, location, vehicle costs, or scheduling limit switching.

The [August 2026 Employment Situation record](records/us-bls-employment-situation-2026-august.json)
adds a current CPS/CES layer: steady unemployment and participation alongside
positive payroll growth, nominal private-sector pay and hours, long-term
unemployment, and uneven industry changes. The [detailed finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-039.md)
shows why household labor-force status, establishment payroll jobs, JOLTS
turnover, and sector earnings are conditioning surfaces rather than a common
worker panel or an AI/work-control estimate.

The follow-up [labor-to-household-room finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-040.md)
connects the BLS labor surface to SIPP same-person resource/job cross-lags,
Federal Reserve liquidity and repayment measures, and New York Fed aggregate
credit exposure. The combined evidence supports household room as a likely
conditioning variable for practical worker exit and voice, but the sources do
not share a person or workplace key and therefore do not establish that arrow.

The new [firm AI expectations finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-044.md)
adds the employer-side clock from NBER W34836: broad current use, limited
retrospective own-firm effects, and larger forward expectations for productivity,
output, and employment. Its employer/employee expectation gap is useful for the
institutional meaning layer, but it is not realized displacement, worker
control, household security, or political consequence. The worker/workplace
event ledger remains the required bridge from firm expectation to implementation,
appropriation, voice, and household response.

The [SIPP utility-to-work following-month finding](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-028.md)
adds a same-person timing surface: utility-payment difficulty or energy-
assistance status at month *t* sits beside earnings and hours change at month
*t+1*. Earnings movement is common across all groups, while the utility-
difficulty hours estimate is imprecise and the assistance comparison is
selected. This narrows the material-to-work arrow without turning a monthly
utility screen into a dated bill shock, work-loss effect, or recovery result.
The [2026-09-16 reproduction recheck](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/sipp-utility-work-following-reproduction-audit-2026-09-14.json)
updates the local input provenance to the current `full-v18` slice while
leaving the estimates unchanged.

The [BEA July 2026 income-and-outlays record](records/us-bea-personal-income-outlays-2026-july.json)
adds a current aggregate benchmark for income, disposable income, consumption,
services-versus-goods movement, outlays, and saving. The [household-pressure
finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-007.md)
uses it to anchor the macro environment without imputing equal household room,
real purchasing power, consumer choice, or political meaning.

The [Census 2025 income, poverty, and insurance vintage layer](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/census-2025-income-poverty-insurance-vintage-layer-v1.md)
adds a newer national household-security checkpoint without replacing the
household-level evidence. Real median household income reached $87,460 and
official poverty was 10.2%, while the Supplemental Poverty Measure (13.1%) and
full-year uninsured rate (7.9%) were not statistically different from 2024 in
the release summary. These are different universes and clocks: the median is
not liquid room, official poverty is not post-tax or medical-cost-adjusted
security, and insurance coverage is not affordable or continuous care. The
result strengthens the atlas proposition that aggregate improvement can
coexist with unresolved distributional, health-access, and political-action
questions.

The [SIPP episodic-poverty record](records/us-census-sipp-monthly-episodic-poverty-2024.json)
adds a duration layer beneath that annual checkpoint. In 2024, the monthly
poverty rate stayed near 11.9% while 15.1% experienced poverty for at least two
consecutive months; 59.2% of those with episodic poverty were poor all year.
Children had an 18.9% episodic rate, and older adults experiencing episodic
poverty averaged about 11 months, with 79.4% poor all year. The source makes
spell length and subgroup concentration visible, but it does not link the spell
to its cause, benefits, hardship, housing, health, trust, or later recovery in
the same person.

The [Census August 2026 retail-sales and consumer-activity layer](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/census-august-2026-retail-sales-consumer-activity-layer-v1.md)
adds the adjacent behavior surface: national retail and food-services sales
were $773.9 billion, up 1.2% from July and 6.0% from August 2025 in current
dollars. Because the release is not price-adjusted, this does not establish
more units purchased, greater household room, consumer confidence, or firm
profit. It does, however, make the missing middle explicit between price and
household experience: channel, category, quantity, payment, debt, substitution,
and resource-group incidence remain the next tests.

The [BEA Q2 2026 growth/profits record](records/us-bea-gdp-corporate-profits-2026q2.json)
adds a separate macro-firm surface for real GDP, private domestic demand,
prices, and corporate profits. The [work-control finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-041.md)
uses it to distinguish output and profit movement from worker authority,
household benefit, and firm-level AI effects.

The [IMF 2026 US Article IV finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-045.md)
adds country-surveillance context: recent aggregate resilience and productivity
coexist with fiscal debt, current-account, tariff, labor, and financial-stability
risks. IMF projections are conditional macro scenarios, not household or worker
outcomes; the next bridge must connect any adjustment event to distributional,
institutional, and political-response evidence.

The [Federal Reserve financial-stability and monetary-policy finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-046.md)
adds the institutional-financial layer: resilient banks and moderate aggregate
debt vulnerability coexist with selected household delinquency, tight credit for
some borrowers, elevated inflation, high valuations, and funding-risk exposure.
The Fed's system aggregates do not identify household incidence or political
meaning; the next bridge must join a dated rate, energy, tariff, credit, or
funding event to borrower type, firm size, repayment/spending response, and later
institutional or political action.

The [public financial-capacity and household-room bridge](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-047.md)
now places BEA aggregate output/profits/income, Federal Reserve financial and
household surfaces, OFR public monitoring capacity, IMF fiscal scenarios, and
New York Fed aggregate credit in one deliberately non-pooled architecture. It
sharpens the end-to-end gap: macro resilience, public data visibility, aggregate
debt/delinquency, and household liquidity can move differently; the missing test
is still a dated event joined to compatible exposure, attribution, action, and
recovery or remedy.
The follow-up [OFR institutional-finance finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-071.md)
turns that architecture into a longitudinal institutional sequence: 2022 repo
and data-visibility pilots, 2023 elevated-risk monitoring, 2024 persistent data
gaps and differentiated borrower risk, and 2025 expanded repo visibility and AI
workflow reporting alongside lower staffing and budget. The sequence supports
public-capacity analysis, not a claim that visibility became protection. The
next empirical pass is to align those milestones with the IMF/Findex provider-
demand layers and CFPB/SHED household-response layers, retaining market,
institution, firm, account, household, and respondent units separately.

The [financial-access route synthesis](projects/us-financial-intermediation/findings/us-financial-intermediation-002.md)
now supplies that provider/demand comparison in reader-facing form. Global
Findex shows broad US adult account and device ownership and selected financial
behaviors; IMF FAS shows provider-side counts, branch density, and balance
ratios; SHED shows household loss, recovery time, buffers, and adaptation; and
CFPB/FTC/OFR show institutional visibility, routing, and public-capacity
surfaces. These layers establish reach and infrastructure as different stages,
not a single inclusion score. They still do not identify favorable terms,
failed-account episodes, verified correction, switching, or later trust for the
same person.

The new [county capacity/outcome diagnostic](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-007.md)
adds CDC PLACES modeled social-need outcomes to the existing CBP/HRSA/NHTS
place architecture. Low visible health-establishment capacity aligns with
higher modeled transportation, food, housing, utility, mental-distress, and
uninsured medians, but routine-checkup medians are nearly unchanged. The
result advances the place outcome surface while preserving the ecological and
cross-vintage boundary: it does not identify patient access, provider quality,
travel, price, causal effect, trust, or political response.
The follow-up [PLACES vintage comparison](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-008.md)
uses 2,885 common county FIPS across the 2024 and 2025 releases. Routine-care,
insurance, food, transportation, housing, utility, and mental-distress medians
do not move as one local-health trend, and the social-needs measure universe is
not identical across releases. This strengthens the program's temporal quality
control while preserving CDC's modeled small-area and non-intervention boundary.
The [HPSA component diagnostic](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-009.md)
then decomposes the shortage label itself. Geographic, population, facility,
tract, low-income, and migrant/seasonal categories produce different overlapping
county medians, which blocks a single “HPSA county” interpretation. This adds
classification specificity but still does not observe component-level reach,
patient use, quality, price, household time, trust, or political response.
The follow-up [HPSA operational-field audit](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-010.md)
finds that FTE, shortage, and estimated underserved-population fields are not
uniformly populated by designation type; facility/site rows in particular have
very sparse FTE and no estimated underserved-population values in the current
extract. This is an institutional measurement boundary, not evidence of zero
capacity or a resident-level care result.

The [July 2026 Census HTOPS/HPS finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-016.md)
adds a current same-round household surface: expense and price perception,
energy trade-offs, food and assistance routes, anxiety/loneliness, and trust in
federal statistics and Congress. It strengthens alignment of material and
institutional endpoints without turning a cross-sectional co-occurrence into a
price-to-trust mechanism or political-action result.

The follow-up [Census cross-wave finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-017.md)
uses the official successive-difference replicate weights across corrected
March, May, and July 2026 releases. It shows broad expense difficulty nearly
flat from May to July while food, energy, and trust measures move differently;
the 2026 files are repeated snapshots after a documented design break, not a
same-person recovery panel.

The follow-up [July expense-to-confidence cross-tab](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-027.md)
tests one missing comparison within that same release. High confidence in
federal statistical agencies was 43.9% among respondents reporting no usual-
expense difficulty and 28.8% among those reporting at least a little
difficulty; high confidence in Congress was 20.8% versus 13.8%. The
replicate-weighted contrast is useful evidence that material pressure and
institution-specific confidence can be related in the same population snapshot,
but it still cannot identify timing, blame, prior identity, political action,
or recovery. The larger statistical-agency gap also argues against collapsing
all institutional confidence into a single trust score.

The [April–June 2025 linked HTOPS finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-018.md)
adds a same-respondent transition surface for 6,564 linked IDs. Persistence,
entry, and exit can now be observed for selected material, work, and
institutional measures, but the retained sample lacks a documented
attrition-adjusted longitudinal weight and does not identify a causal trigger.
The [HTOPS expense-difficulty cross-lagged record](records/us-htops-expense-difficulty-cross-lagged-2025.json)
shows baseline expense difficulty alongside materially higher later food
insufficiency (12.3% versus 1.1%), energy nonpayment (21.4% versus 2.2%), and
job loss (11.7% versus 2.9%), while later high Congress confidence is nearly
similar (17.2% versus 16.9%). The outcome-specific linked samples, April
weights, and separate item universes do not support a national transition or
causal interpretation. This is an associative counterexample to a single
material-pressure-to-trust pathway.

The [World Bank Enterprise Surveys access recheck](projects/ai-work-control/world-bank-wbes-ai-access-recheck-2026-09-14.md)
also strengthens the acquisition map: the US AI follow-up is confirmed as an
available survey, but firm microdata remain behind the authenticated portal.
That is an access result, not a firm adoption estimate; the next AI firm test
still requires the file, questionnaire, weights, and variance documentation.

The latest rotation adds two bounded bridges. The [household adaptation and
recovery finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-059.md)
places SHED financial-condition persistence beside health, unpaid-care,
food-pressure, and fraud-recovery measures. It strengthens the recurring
optionalities proposition: current stabilization can consume savings, time,
health, care capacity, or attention, and broad financial improvement is not
the same as restored future room. The [consumer visibility and exit finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-060.md)
then places CFPB complaint labels beside SHED fraud burden, Pew platform/AI
use and privacy concern, and the FTC information-gathering inquiry. It keeps
visibility, explanation, verified remedy, attention cost, dependence, trust,
and exit as separate stages; neither complaint routing nor regulatory inquiry
closes the consumer-power chain.

The recurring-source watch also refreshes the [AI/work mobility context](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-057.md)
through January–July 2026. The partial-year JOLTS mean remains cool (4.3714%
openings and 1.9571% quits, with July preliminary), but it is an
establishment-rate context rather than an annual worker trend or an AI effect.

The [current macro-to-household finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-061.md)
adds the latest BEA July income/outlays, August CPI and PPI, August CPS/CES,
JOLTS partial-year, and SHED hardship vintages. It reinforces a central
denominator rule: aggregate income and payroll growth can coexist with
energy/upstream price pressure and income-graded household food or bill
hardship. The release set identifies adjacent currencies, not a same-household
price-to-politics or recovery chain.

The [safety-net transition/work-room finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-062.md)
adds a same-person SIPP sequence around SNAP entry. Following resource-ratio,
earnings, hours, job-count, and hardship surfaces do not move as one outcome:
resource position often falls while job count and hours remain stable in their
selected universes. This keeps benefit receipt, work continuity, household
room, administrative adequacy, and recovery separate. The next test is a
same-episode notice-to-remedy record with amount, gap days, alternatives,
care/work response, and later recovery or action.

The [US firm-capacity baseline](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-063.md)
adds the public World Bank Enterprise Survey profile as a distinct firm-level
context layer: size strata differ in reported employment growth, formal
training, outage exposure, bank-financed investment, and manager-selected
obstacles. This improves the AI/work bridge by making implementation capacity
and infrastructure exposure visible before the gated AI follow-up is used. It
does not estimate AI adoption, productivity causality, worker control, or
household incidence; the next test remains the documented firm file with its
AI questions, weights, missingness, and variance guidance.

The [firm-to-worker comparison](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-064.md)
then places that size-conditioned baseline beside NBER task adoption, BLS
representation, and JOLTS mobility. The comparison makes four gates explicit:
firm complements, actual worker use, institutional voice, and practical exit
are not interchangeable. It strengthens the worker-control boundary without
turning size, adoption, union coverage, or quits into a composite index.

The latest household-room pass adds a FRED timing screen. From January 2024 to
July 2026, the University of Michigan consumer-sentiment index fell 23.8 points
while the CPI index rose 7.5% and unemployment remained close to 4%. This does
not identify an inflation effect: the series have different constructions and
do not share a household denominator. It does establish a useful divergence
that the household-level work must explain: a comparatively strong aggregate
labor signal can coexist with weak public economic confidence.

The [FRED finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-012.md)
therefore strengthens the distinction between national conditions and practical
household room. SHED, SIPP, MEPS, BEA, BLS, and Federal Reserve credit records
are needed to test which households faced shelter, food, energy, insurance,
debt, care, or work constraints, and what they did next. The FRED series alone
cannot supply that exposure, adaptation, political attribution, or recovery
step.

The [Gallup institutional-confidence finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-025.md)
adds a parallel meaning layer: average confidence in long-tracked institutions
was low, but confidence differed sharply by institution, party, and racial
group. The [Chatham House data-trust memo](projects/ai-work-control/chatham-house-data-trust-governance-layer-v1.md)
extends the route to public measurement itself, treating confidence in economic
statistics as a possible condition of domestic coordination and international
credibility. These are not proof of a material-to-trust or domestic-to-
geopolitical causal chain. They make the missing links more precise: measure
the statistic a person encountered, their own material exposure, who they
believed, whether an institution acted, and whether confidence changed after a
verifiable correction or remedy.

The [UNDP human-development finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-069.md)
adds a comparative capability frame to the AI and household-room lanes. The
United States has a high average HDI and a strong international rank, but that
average does not show who can exercise a choice, access training, refuse an
automated decision, or absorb a work or health shock. UNDP therefore helps keep
the program’s question focused on human capability rather than technology or
income alone. It remains a country-level context measure; subgroup incidence,
distribution, agency, and worker control still require US household, worker,
firm, and institutional evidence.

The [UNDP AI use, control, and confidence record](records/us-undp-ai-human-development-agency-comparison-2025.json)
adds the missing perception layer for selected countries. In the US tabulation,
weekly-or-more AI use (32.6%), confidence in controlling AI interactions
(33.9%), confidence in government AI use (29.1%), and confidence that AI is
designed in people’s interests (37.5%) are separate measures. Selected-country
comparisons show why they should not be collapsed: more frequent use can coexist
with lower personal control, and high public confidence does not verify service
quality or actual agency. The survey is cross-sectional and internationally
comparative; the next test remains a US subgroup and same-episode join to work,
household, public-service, remedy, and political-action outcomes. The same
survey’s domain layer adds another boundary: reported use was highest for
entertainment, while likely future use was highest for entertainment and
education, with work, health, and finance carrying different stakes. The next
analysis must therefore follow the system into its domain of use rather than
treating AI adoption as one cultural or economic event. Its work-expectation
layer adds a further split: expected productivity, new job creation, and current
job change or replacement can coexist, and expected productivity is much higher
among people already likely to use AI for work tasks. This is an expectation
bridge, not realized productivity, displacement, or bargaining power.

The newest bounded findings add four important controls to the cross-source
story. The [SHED household-room finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-013.md)
shows that income timing, outside help, assistance receipt, budget margin, and
price adaptation are related but distinct household surfaces. They should not
be summarized as one measure of financial stress or treated as proof of a
single coping sequence. The [RECS energy-room finding](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-034.md)
adds energy spending, energy burden, assistance, disconnection, and inability
to repair or replace equipment as separate routes. Its burden result is a
transparent spending-to-income proxy, not a complete welfare measure, and the
public-use survey year limits claims about the present.

The [BFS/BDS firm-and-place finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-011.md)
adds a firm-stage boundary: applications, establishments, openings, and net
job creation are different events. A place can show entrepreneurial entry
without a corresponding positive net-job result; sector composition is part of
the explanation. This keeps local business formation from being used as a
shortcut for local prosperity, worker bargaining power, or community recovery.

The [business-application expectations/realization record](records/us-census-business-application-expectations-realization-2026.json)
adds an expectation-stage control. Linking 17 million U.S. business applications
to outcomes, the Census study finds that applicants overestimate employment on
average chiefly because many expect entry but never enter; among applicants who
both expect and achieve entry, expected employment is typically below realized
first-year employment. Expectations predict entry and employment but conditional
realized employment rises less than one-for-one with them. Application volume,
anticipated hiring, realized entry, first-year employment, survival, job
quality, and community benefit are therefore different stages. The working
paper does not identify why expectations miss, establish a psychological
mechanism, or show durable firm or household outcomes.

The [third-place and neighborhood-startup record](records/us-starbucks-third-place-neighborhood-startups-1990-2022.json)
adds a social-space-to-firm-formation version. An NBER comparison reports a
9.1–22.5% increase in annual startup counts over seven years after a first
Starbucks entered tracts without existing cafés, with larger effects in an
under-resourced partnership and no significant effect where cafés already
existed. Missing gathering space may therefore matter for local networks, but
the study does not establish a single mechanism, firm survival, job quality,
resident welfare, displacement, or household benefit.

The [Black-neighborhood startup record](records/us-black-neighborhood-startup-deficit-covid-2020-2024.json)
adds a racialized place-conversion pattern. Across 38 states, a long-standing
Black-neighborhood startup deficit reversed sharply in 2020, especially in
neighborhoods with larger prior deficits, and relative entrepreneurship stayed
elevated through 2024, including neighborhood-oriented activity. Registration
growth is not yet firm survival, ownership, financing, jobs, wages, customer
welfare, household income, or reduced displacement; the change could also
reflect policy, demand, migration, credit, selection, or registration behavior.
The [Opportunity Zones record](records/us-gao-opportunity-zones-community-outcomes-2026.json)
adds a public-incentive-to-place version. GAO reports more than $108 billion in
Qualified Opportunity Fund assets and nearly 9,000 designated zones, but states
and selected funds generally did not track incentive-specific jobs, housing, or
other community outcomes. Investment was more likely in urban places with
infrastructure, ongoing development, and community support. Capital and project
completion can therefore be visible while resident benefit, affordability,
displacement, and durable community security remain unresolved; the record does
not establish a national causal effect.
Finally, the [World Bank poverty, climate, and data-coverage finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-073.md)
adds comparative context rather than a US outcome: global poverty remains
highly concentrated in Sub-Saharan Africa and fragile or conflict-affected
settings; climate exposure and missing household surveys constrain what can be
known. Those facts matter for the geopolitical context and for interpreting
international capability, but they do not establish a US household,
conflict, or geopolitical-behavior effect.

The latest transmission-realization rotation adds a physical public-capacity
stage. DOE's Transmission Facilitation Program makes federal support and
planned capacity visible, while the current project page records termination
or withdrawal notices for several selected projects. Selection and financing
support therefore do not yet prove construction, energization, transmission
service, cost recovery, reliability, or community benefit. The [DOE record](records/us-doe-transmission-facilitation-realization-2023-2026.json)
keeps the project-status evidence separate from those open outcomes.

The [Champlain Hudson Power Express finding](projects/us-champlain-hudson-power-express/findings/us-nyserda-champlain-hudson-power-express-operation-2026-001.md)
adds the named-operation counterexample to that incomplete-transmission stage.
New York reports that the 339-mile Quebec-to-New York project reached
commercial operation and is expected to deliver 10.4 terawatt-hours per year
into New York City. Operation and a cross-border delivery route are therefore
visible, but customer bills, reliability, rate allocation, host-community
acceptance, and durable supply dependence are not. This sharpens the broader
rule: a physical system can be functioning while the distribution of benefit,
burden, and control remains unmeasured.

The [Seattle SMART curb record](records/us-seattle-smart-curb-access-implementation-2023-2025.json)
adds a named digital-infrastructure implementation stage. Seattle's $1.996
million project completed a digital commercial-curb inventory, installed
operational vehicle-to-curb infrastructure, and implemented a commercial
vehicle permit system after procurement and reporting delays. The project
reached operation, but its report does not establish delivery reliability,
curb-user time, enforcement, price, safety, equity, user acceptance, or durable
continuation.

The [Technology Modernization Fund finding](projects/us-gao-technology-modernization-fund/findings/us-gao-technology-modernization-fund-realized-savings-2026-001.md)
adds a federal IT realization comparator. GAO reports about $1.03 billion
invested across 68 projects, $13.5 million in reported savings from 11 projects,
and $1.06 billion in projected savings from 24 projects. The scale difference
keeps investment, realized savings, and projected savings separate. It adds a
measured implementation endpoint without proving agency productivity, better
service, user time saved, equitable access, or household benefit.

The [USPS service-performance finding](projects/us-gao-usps-service-performance/findings/us-gao-usps-cost-cutting-service-performance-rural-2026-001.md)
adds a public-network cost-to-access counterpoint. GAO reports longer expected
delivery times for some First-Class Mail, the removal of afternoon collection
at more than 24,000 distant post offices, and most lowered service targets not
being met since FY2021; oversight entities and stakeholders reported
disproportionate rural effects. This makes the hidden trade concrete: financial
sustainability efforts can move waiting and collection burden toward users.
The review does not establish a household-level delay, substitution, total
savings, complaint outcome, trust change, or political response.

The DROP rotation adds an administrative digital-control stage. California's
system gives a resident one request, an eight-digit ID, broker-level statuses,
and recurring processing duties. This makes privacy action more visible and
contestable, but “deleted,” “not found,” “opted out,” “exempted,” and “pending”
do not form a public aggregate completion rate or prove downstream deletion,
reappearance control, safety, trust, or durable exit. The [DROP record](records/us-cppa-drop-status-contestability-2026.json)
keeps those stages separate.

The [TAKE IT DOWN Act record](records/us-ftc-take-it-down-act-platform-removal-2026.json)
adds a mandated digital-remedy version. Covered platforms must provide a clear
removal route for nonconsensual intimate images, act within 48 hours on a valid
request, address known identical copies, and face an FTC escalation route. The
law makes contestability more visible, but the public materials do not show a
valid request, completed removal, recurrence prevention, survivor safety,
wellbeing, trust, or platform exit.

The Census civic-capacity rotation adds a material-place layer to culture and
politics. A large CPS-based working-paper analysis links local disadvantage and
inequality to lower volunteering and reports a recession-related weakening of
participation, including the disappearance of an earlier rural advantage. This
does not measure community collapse: informal help, belonging, political
action, and trust can move on different clocks. The [Census record](records/us-census-economic-place-civic-capacity-2002-2015.json)
keeps the place association separate from those open outcomes.

The [Census–AmeriCorps civic-time record](records/us-census-americorps-civic-reach-time-2023.json)
adds a time-intensity boundary. Formal volunteering rose from 23.2% in 2021 to
28.3% in 2023 and informal neighbor help reached 54.2%, while mean volunteer
hours fell from 96.5 in 2017 to 70 in 2023 and median hours from 40 to 24.
Participation reach, time intensity, informal help, service adequacy, trust,
belonging, and political influence are therefore separate currencies; the
repeated cross-sections do not follow the same person or show why hours changed.

The [BLS cultural-and-civic-time record](records/us-bls-atus-cultural-civic-time-2025.json)
adds an everyday time-use denominator. On an average 2025 day, 30.4% of people
age 15+ socialized or communicated (0.58 hours per person), 9.4% engaged in
religious or spiritual activities (0.13 hours), 5.3% volunteered (0.12 hours),
and 13.6% did an organizational, civic, or religious activity. Socializing and
religious time were higher on weekends/holidays than weekdays, while published
volunteering time was 0.12 hours on both. These primary-activity averages do
not show relationship quality, informal help, digital contact, belonging,
organizational capacity, or political action, and they do not follow the same
person across years.

The [NEA arts participation/connection record](records/us-nea-hps-arts-attendance-social-connectedness-2024.json)
adds a cultural-infrastructure route. Across four 2024 Household Pulse Survey
cycles, 25.2% of adults attended a live performance or art exhibit in the prior
month, but attendance ranged from 16.2% below $25,000 annual income to 55.2%
at $200,000 or more. Attendees also reported more often always or usually
receiving needed social/emotional support than non-attendees. Cultural
participation can thus be a socially patterned resource and support-associated
surface, not merely leisure consumption. The repeated cross-sectional evidence
does not show that arts attendance caused support, belonging, health, or civic
action; money, time, health, transport, education, place, and prior ties can
produce both participation and reported connection.

The [Census child cultural-participation record](records/us-census-sipp-child-day-cultural-participation-2023.json)
adds a distinct child and family layer. In 2023, among children ages 6--17,
38.4% participated in sports, 26.8% took lessons, 21.9% joined clubs, and 6.8%
did all three; the all-three rate was 1.2% in families below poverty and 8.8%
in families at 200% of poverty or higher. At the same time, family-routine
measures need not move with organized opportunity: 67.4% of children under six
were read to at least five days a week and 85.8% of children under 18 ate dinner
with a reference parent at least five nights a week. These age-specific,
descriptive SIPP measures do not show whether fees, time, transport, safety,
program supply, preference, or school resources generated the activity
gradient, nor do they establish belonging, activity quality, educational
attainment, or later adult outcomes.

The [American Neighbor Survey social-infrastructure record](records/us-american-neighbor-survey-social-infrastructure-belonging-2026.json)
adds a neighborhood-scale view of the same distinction. In a weighted 2025
survey of 5,357 adults, 40% reported talking with neighbors at least a few
times a week and 40% reported having worked with neighbors in the prior year
to solve a problem or improve the community; 72% reported a great or fair
amount of neighborhood trust. Access was uneven: 10% reported none of the
listed neighborhood amenities within a 20-minute walk while 44% reported six
or more; weekly neighbor conversation was 27% in the first group and 43% in
the latter. Among parents, 56% with a college degree versus 39% without one
said they would feel comfortable asking a neighbor for emergency child care.
These are separate surfaces of contact, collective action, trust, accessible
places, and stated mutual-aid comfort. They do not show that amenities caused
connection, that a neighbor actually supplied care or money, or that social
infrastructure offsets formal-service gaps, since residence, safety, resources,
life stage, and selection into place can shape all of them.

The [Pew Latter-day Saint communal-trust record](records/us-pew-latter-day-saints-communal-trust-volunteering-2026.json)
adds an institutional-belonging counterexample. Current Latter-day Saint adults
reported more volunteering and generalized trust than U.S. adults overall, but
volunteering was much higher among members with a bachelor's degree and in the
Mountain region, and trust also varied by education. A strong community
institution can therefore generate real social capacity without distributing
access, time, or role opportunity evenly. Affiliation, service, trust,
belonging, political influence, and burden remain separate outcomes; the
cross-sectional survey does not show that membership caused the differences.

The USMCA labor-remedy rotation adds an attributed external-response stage. A
worker petition at a Mexican facility led, according to USTR, to a U.S. review
request, Mexican investigation, reported reinstatement and backpay, union
access, facility rules, monitoring, and resumed tariff liquidation. This is a
stronger operation-to-response bridge than a policy announcement, but worker
receipt, non-retaliation, durable bargaining power, and wider compliance remain
open. The [USMCA record](records/usmca-thyssenkrupp-rapid-response-labor-remedy-2025-2026.json)
keeps the institutional response separate from those outcomes.

The OFAC firm-correction rotation adds a financial-infrastructure stage to
geopolitical power. A foreign firm's reported apparent sanctions violations
passed through U.S.-dollar payment channels and produced a $275 million
settlement plus a five-year compliance commitment. Enforcement reach and future
correction are visible; later screening, recurrence, deterrence, distributional
cost, and state response remain open. The [OFAC record](records/us-ofac-adani-sanctions-compliance-2023-2026.json)
keeps the named-firm evidence separate from those outcomes.

## Current evidence map by arrow

| Arrow | Current status | Best next test |
|---|---|---|
| Material pressure → adaptation | Compared across BLS prices, USDA, SHED food/financial layers, SIPP, household-room measures, and RECS energy layers; same-respondent evidence exists for selected SHED paths, while energy spending, assistance, disconnection, repair risk, and price adaptation remain distinct surfaces. | Add dated trigger, alternatives, schedule control, care/time intensity, and a validated same-household energy episode. |
| Adaptation/time → wellbeing and security | Health/care and ATUS layers show divergent paths and population intensity; the 2024 ATUS microdata record adds weighted work, care, travel, household-labor, socializing, and eldercare measures, while the roster and progressively standardized provider-time comparisons show care can cross household boundaries and co-occur with a partially persistent but annual-varying time profile. | PSID or a valid same-unit event/panel comparison with work, care, health, household composition, and later wellbeing. |
| Exposure → institutional response | CFPB and SNAP records measure institutional endpoints and transitions. | Same-case event ledger from notice/contact through effort, decision, remedy, repeat effort, and exit. |
| Institutional route → meaning/action | CPS, ANES, CCES, and Pew provide separate population measures. | Repeated respondent or event-based design with attribution, trust, civic action, turnout, and vote. |
| Infrastructure → local burden/capability | LBNL and international case records establish demand, commitments, and milestones; RECS adds household energy-spending, assistance, disconnection, and equipment-risk surfaces. | Place-utility case comparison with approved/actual load, rates, local benefits, ownership, portability, public response, and household service continuity. |
| Local capacity → practical reach | CBP/BFS/BDS place layers and NHTS vehicle/mode comparison establish separate provision and mobility-option surfaces; BFS/BDS now explicitly separates applications, establishments, openings, and net job creation. | A common-place household/service-use design with fares, travel time, destination access, quality, firm survival/openings, and missed work/care. |
| Domestic capability/dependence → geopolitical leverage | Case controls and state-leverage ledger define the required fields; World Bank comparative poverty, fragility, climate-exposure, and survey-coverage data provide context, not a US leverage finding. | Observe a negotiation, refusal, switch, regulatory action, or external behavior change, while linking capability measures to the actors and stakes in that episode. |

## What is genuinely end-to-end today

The program currently has partial end-to-end coverage in bounded segments:

1. **Same-respondent persistence:** SHED 2024→2025 financial-condition,
   adaptation, health, and selected care transitions.
2. **Time and care scale:** ATUS published prevalence, care-day intensity,
   employment, age, and household/nonhousehold distinctions.
3. **Time-ordered public-system condition:** SIPP SNAP transitions followed by
   rent/mortgage and utility hardship with Fay–BRR uncertainty, alongside USDA
   food-security severity and assistance participation.
   The buffer-and-credit bridge adds adjacent Federal Reserve coping measures,
   but does not claim a same-person route-to-credit or route-to-politics result.
4. **Pressure-to-adaptation comparison:** BLS food-at-home prices, SHED
   income-conditioned food insufficiency and price responses, and USDA food
   security, with different reference periods and units kept explicit.
5. **Institutional visibility:** CFPB published complaint route and endpoint
   trends, with product and time conditioning.
   The 2025 product comparison and household fraud layer now make the split
   between response labels, residual loss, recovery effort, and exit capacity
   explicit; a verified same-case remedy remains open.
6. **Political participation surface:** CPS voting/registration and distinct
   nonvoter barriers, beside separate trust and judgment layers.
7. **Infrastructure/state-capacity boundary:** national load scenarios and two
   international project milestones, with ownership and replaceability still
   open.
   IMF WEO adds a conditional macro-fiscal stage for defense mobilization;
   financing and social-spending incidence remain unlinked to US households
   or observed leverage.
8. **AI and platform agency boundary:** task-level adoption, industry and firm
   outcomes, modeled distribution, public analytical capacity, attention,
   complaint visibility, and institutional scrutiny are now linked as bounded
   stages; worker/household incidence, remedy, exit, and geopolitical leverage
   remain unjoined.

The program does **not** yet have one valid record that carries a dated
material or institutional trigger through adaptation, time/care, wellbeing,
meaning, political action, institutional response, and later recovery. That is
the central acquisition target—not a reason to erase the partial evidence or
pretend that cross-source alignment is a respondent-level join.

## Next rotating program cycle

The next cycle should advance all three scales together:

- **Micro/same-unit:** run the PSID structural gate when authenticated files
  arrive; pursue SHED/ATUS/SIPP event-compatible comparisons where valid.
- **Institutional/episode:** implement the same-case public-system and
  consumer recourse event ledger with notice, effort, remedy, and follow-up.
- **Place/state:** retrieve utility, ownership, local-benefit, portability, and
  observed-response records for data-center and public-cloud cases.

Every new record should enter the [trend-observation registry](us-trend-observations.md),
carry its own denominator and boundary, and add either a new time period,
subgroup/place conditioning, uncertainty, reversal, or same-unit link before a
provisional trend is strengthened.

The [Pew 2026 electorate racial and ethnic composition layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2026-electorate-racial-ethnic-composition-layer-v1.md)
adds the demographic base beneath the political-action lane. Pew projects the
2026 eligible electorate at about 249.75 million: 61% White, 16% Hispanic,
12% Black, and 5% Asian, with Hispanic and Asian shares nearly doubled since
2006. Citizenship and age matter: only 52% of Hispanic adults and 60% of Asian
adults were eligible voters in 2024, compared with 81% of White adults. These
are composition estimates, not turnout or coalition forecasts; the next test
follows population through eligibility, registration, participation, meaning,
and representation.

The [global-aging foreign-policy record](records/us-gao-global-aging-foreign-policy-implications-2026.json)
adds a long-horizon demographic layer beneath geopolitical state capacity. GAO
reports a projected increase in the worldwide population age 60 and over from
about 1 billion in 2020 to 2.1 billion in 2050, with possible implications for
partner health spending, defense resources, labor pools, migration, and global
health assistance. These are plausible channels identified through literature
and expert review, not evidence of a particular ally's policy change, altered
burden sharing, or U.S. leverage.

The [Pew 2025 religious affiliation, switching, and cultural identity layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2025-religious-affiliation-switching-cultural-identity-layer-v1.md)
adds family transmission, spirituality, practice, and institutional belonging
to the cultural lane. Pew’s nationally representative 2023–24 RLS finds 62%
of adults identify as Christian, 29% as religiously unaffiliated, and 35% have
switched religious identity since childhood; 53% describe themselves as both
spiritual and religious. The long-run change has slowed recently, and cultural
attachment is not identical to affiliation. The next test follows identity,
practice, community, family, and political meaning in repeated units.

The [Pew 2026 U.S. global-role, domestic-partisan, and international-reception layer](projects/ai-work-control/pew-2026-us-global-role-domestic-partisan-international-comparison-layer-v1.md)
adds legitimacy and external reception to the material-capability lane. Across
36 non-U.S. countries, a median 35% said the U.S. contributes to peace and
stability, versus 57% of Americans; inside the U.S., Republicans were at 82%
and Democrats at 35%. The same source finds 83% of Americans see U.S.
interference in other countries, with majorities in both parties. These are
perception measures, not capability or behavioral outcomes; the next test is
an event-country-party ledger linking policy events to reputation, cooperation,
market/cultural signals, and political response.

The [GAO 2026 kinship families and intergenerational care-support layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/gao-2026-kinship-families-intergenerational-care-support-layer-v1.md)
adds family composition as an operating care institution. About 2.4 million
children lived in kinship families in 2023; these families were more likely to
be in poverty, and children in grandparent households more often had health or
special-education needs. For every child in foster care, about 18 kinship
children were not, while formal supports varied by legal status and community.
The next test follows caregiver capacity, benefits, housing, work, and child
stability in the same family episode.

The [canonical kinship-family record](records/us-gao-kinship-families-intergenerational-care-support-2025.json)
preserves the national descriptive frame, foster/non-foster distinction, and
selected qualitative community evidence without pooling their denominators or
turning family substitution into a welfare outcome.

The [GAO 2026 assisted-living spending and Medicaid-coverage layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/gao-2026-assisted-living-spending-medicaid-coverage-layer-v1.md)
adds a care-and-housing financing bridge. Medicaid and traditional Medicare
spending for assisted-living services totaled at least $12 billion in 2024,
but GAO says the floor is likely an undercount because the setting is not
uniformly identified; 44 state Medicaid programs covered assisted-living
services as of March 2025, including 29 through HCBS waivers. Coverage, slot
availability, room/board affordability, quality, family substitution, and
resident outcomes remain separate. The next test is a state-year episode ledger.

The [canonical assisted-living financing record](records/us-gao-assisted-living-spending-medicaid-coverage-2026.json)
preserves the federal spending floor, state-policy coverage, waiver limits, and
mixed-payer care-bundle stages without treating coverage or spending as usable
resident access.

The [GAO 2026 telework, travel, real-estate, and planning layer](projects/us-transport-mobility/gao-2026-telework-travel-real-estate-planning-layer-v1.md)
adds a collective-geography consequence to changing work arrangements. Average
telework remained about twice 2019 levels; MPOs reported lower transit
ridership/fare revenue, changed driving geography, and shifts in commercial
real-estate and housing demand. Only one of the 26 largest transit cities had
2024 ridership above 2019, while over 90% of MPOs wanted more DOT guidance.
These are not causal household or city welfare estimates; the next test is a
place-month-mode ledger linking telework, trips, service, property, investment,
distribution, and public response.

The [canonical telework/travel/planning record](records/us-gao-telework-travel-real-estate-planning-2026.json)
preserves the community-survey, selected-city, and federal-execution stages
without treating telework context or MPO action as a causal welfare estimate.

The [GAO 2026 airport transit availability and adoption layer](projects/us-transport-mobility/gao-2026-airport-transit-availability-adoption-layer-v1.md)
adds a place and mobility conversion test. Nearly all of 51 reviewed airports
had some transit service, yet passenger use ranged from 4% to 19% at the 12
airports with usable reports and only about 4% of airport and airline employees
nationwide used transit to commute. Travel time, accessibility, familiarity,
parking, benefits, and shift timing separate route presence from practical use;
the next test is a route-day-shift ledger linking service design to completed
trips, work access, cost, congestion, and intervention response.

The [canonical airport transit record](records/us-gao-airport-transit-availability-adoption-2026.json)
adds the official service and usage stages without pooling passenger reports,
airport employee surveys, and national Census commute estimates.

The [GAO 2026 disability workforce-program access layer](projects/us-safety-net-access/gao-2026-disability-workforce-program-access-layer-v1.md)
extends the accessibility thesis into labor-market entry. Seven percent of
participants in selected DOL workforce programs reported disabilities, 43% of
those reporters did not provide a disability type, and GAO found local
challenges with interpreters, outside training accessibility, monitoring
aggregation, and guidance awareness. The participant and job-center samples do
not identify causal barriers or employment effects. The next test links
voluntary accommodation need to service completion, placement, retention, and
local corrective action.

The [canonical disability workforce-program record](records/us-gao-disability-workforce-program-access-2026.json)
preserves participant, local implementation, oversight, and recommendation
stages separately, including voluntary non-disclosure as a substantive data
boundary.

The [GAO 2026 working adults, Medicaid/SNAP, and employer reliance layer](projects/us-safety-net-access/gao-2026-working-adults-medicaid-snap-employer-reliance-layer-v1.md)
adds a labor–safety-net bridge. About 13.8 million Medicaid-enrolled adults and
10.6 million adults in SNAP-receiving households worked at some point in 2024;
about two-thirds of each group worked at least 35 hours per week, and 88% were
in the private sector. Selected-state employer data show repeated large-
employer concentration, but do not prove wage inadequacy, benefit shifting,
employer intent, or causal employment effects. The next test is a lawful
state-year worker–household–employer ledger with hours, weeks, wages, benefits,
eligibility transitions, household need, and later security.
