The [GAO 2026 disability health-care accessibility and oversight layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/gao-2026-disability-healthcare-accessibility-layer-v1.md)
adds the usability stage after nominal coverage, facility capacity, or an
appointment offer. GAO identifies physical, sensory, communication, digital,
and bias barriers, while finding that none of 12 HHS population-health surveys
collect national-level data on most accessibility barriers. This strengthens
the interface thesis but does not estimate barrier prevalence or prove effects
on diagnosis, health, work, or provider exit. The next test is an encounter-
care, family/time burden, complaint, correction, and oversight response.

The [canonical disability accessibility record](records/us-gao-disability-healthcare-accessibility-oversight-2025.json)
preserves the barrier, measurement, oversight, and recommendation stages as
separate observations rather than treating formal coverage as usable care.

The [family-care public-capacity synthesis](projects/us-aging-care-strain/findings/us-aging-care-strain-004.md)
adds a cross-generational version of the same mechanism. Kinship families,
childcare arrangements, unpaid adult care, ATUS time allocation, and
assisted-living financing show that families often supply the missing care
capacity between need and formal service. The protective outcome—continuity,
belonging, or care received—and the sacrificed outcome—work, money, housing,
transport, health, or social time—remain separate. The next test is a
same-family care episode with alternatives, support receipt, schedule control,
and later stability; the comparison is not a pooled family-burden estimate.

The [domestic capacity/autonomy realization synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-085.md)
adds the material-input layer below infrastructure and procurement. Critical
minerals, CHIPS awards and milestones, data-center/grid records, and JASSM/LRASM
orders show separate commitment, capacity, operating, replaceability, and
leverage clocks. Domestic production or public spending can advance while
foreign processing, qualified output, delivery, ownership, or supplier
switching remain unresolved. The next test is one identifier-bearing
mineral/application or procurement event followed through output, local
incidence, substitute qualification, and an observed switch or external
response; no autonomy or leverage score is inferred.

The [three-audience legitimacy synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-093.md)
adds the interpretation layer above material capability. Pew separates foreign
public reception, Americans' national judgment, and domestic partisan meaning:
foreign publics were less likely than Americans to say the US contributes to
peace and stability, while Republicans and Democrats differed sharply inside
the US. The shared judgment that the US interferes abroad shows that common
descriptive perception can coexist with divergent legitimacy evaluations. The
next test is an event-country-party-year ledger linking a dated policy event to
cooperation, refusal, market/cultural response, or institutional action; polling
alone is not geopolitical leverage.
The measurement-quality rotation adds a necessary counterexample. A study
that applied the same occupational-exposure rubric to three language models
found a 3.6-fold difference in mean exposure estimates and downstream results
that changed materially with the annotator. The atlas should therefore treat
AI exposure as a measured construct with a model, rubric, and uncertainty—not
as one fixed fact. Exposure findings should be compared with observed adoption,
worker reports, and realized job or earnings changes before they are pooled.

The political-culture rotation adds a second caution: a raw partisan gap in
workplace AI use can mostly disappear after accounting for education,
occupation, and industry, while perceived displacement risk may still begin
to diverge by party. This separates political exposure from political meaning.
The source does not show durable polarization, policy demand, or action, but it
suggests that conflict may grow around what a technology means even when its
use is largely shaped by where people work.

The cultural-identity rotation adds a parallel legitimacy pattern. Pew's
Muslim-American review shows population, mosque counts, and congressional
representation growing while negative public judgments, perceived
discrimination, unfair media treatment, and reported anti-Islamic incidents
remain separate and substantial. Institutional presence can therefore grow
without proving equal safety, public legitimacy, or political power.

The infrastructure-geopolitical rotation adds the material-input layer. GAO
and USGS show that domestic research, recovery, or manufacturing projects can
coexist with high import reliance and application-specific substitution limits.
Recycling and substitution are possible routes, not operating alternatives;
the missing middle is qualified output, inventory, supplier switching, local
incidence, and external response. Domestic capacity therefore does not by
itself prove material independence or geopolitical leverage.

The platform-control rotation adds a measured administrative endpoint. Walmart
reports access, deletion, and sale-opt-out requests separately, including
completed, denied, and median-time fields. This is stronger than a policy-only
route, but it still does not verify complete deletion, downstream copies,
re-collection, appeal, or consumer trust. Completion is therefore evidence of
processing capacity, not automatically durable practical control.

The external-response rotation adds a separate geopolitical stage. The CRS
2025 tariff timeline shows China, Canada, and the European Union answering U.S.
pressure with countermeasures, negotiations, pauses, exemptions, and partial
reversals. A response proves interaction, not leverage: the missing tests are
firm pass-through, public incidence, coalition durability, and an attributed
policy change.

The [export-controls and domestic-firm-spillovers record](records/us-census-export-controls-domestic-costs-firm-spillovers-2010-2024.json)
adds a domestic-incidence stage to that trade-policy lane. The share of U.S.
exports subject to controls doubled between 2010 and 2024; using the 2014
Russia restriction as a natural experiment, the Census CES study finds affected
firms less likely to export controlled goods to Russia, but also reducing other
products' exports and exports to other destinations, with lower average payroll.
The targeted transaction and the firm-wide adjustment therefore cannot be
treated as the same effect. Firm payroll is not a worker-level layoff or
household-loss measure, and the study does not settle security effectiveness,
diplomatic outcome, consumer incidence, later adaptation, or the national
welfare balance.

The [foreign-sourced telecommunications procurement record](records/us-gao-foreign-sourced-telecom-procurement-information-2026.json)
adds a geopolitical supply-chain stage. Section 889 reduced obligations to five
named Chinese companies, and nearly 90% of active-contract companies publicly
represented compliance, but GAO found that DOD and GSA did not broadly share
affiliate information and implementation lessons with other agencies. Reduced
procurement exposure is therefore not the same as verified equipment absence,
avoided cyber events, operational continuity, or strategic autonomy.

The [federal telecom-transition record](records/us-gao-federal-telecom-transition-service-disruption-2026.json)
adds a modernization-to-continuity boundary. GSA's transition of 221 agencies
from legacy telecommunications contracts to Enterprise Infrastructure
Solutions ran more than three years past its revised deadline, with legacy
periods extended through May 2026. In six selected agencies, combined legacy
Networx billing rose from $13.4 million in February 2025 to $40.9 million in
March—a 206% increase—yet GSA did not know which agencies, if any, experienced
service disruptions. An extension can be a continuity safeguard while also
exposing agencies to cost and technology risk; neither the billing spike nor
the absence of a known disruption establishes vendor causality, an outage rate,
mission effect, employee effect, or whether the replacement contract improved
service.

The [aviation-spectrum cybersecurity record](records/us-gao-aviation-spectrum-cybersecurity-monitoring-gaps-2026.json)
adds a threat-identification-to-monitoring boundary. In its review of eight
spectrum-dependent National Airspace System systems, GAO found that FAA had
identified interference, spoofing, jamming, and related risks but had not
completed risk/mitigation assessments and updated security documentation, nor
defined real-time monitoring for all spectrum threats. FAA fully addressed two
of eight leading collaboration practices and partially addressed six; nine GAO
recommendations remained open. A known technical risk and partial coordination
are not evidence of a cyberattack, outage, flight delay, accident, passenger
harm, or a failure of any specific flight operation.

The connected-vehicle pass makes the control problem more concrete. FTC and GM
records establish access, deletion, opt-out, and location-disable routes, but
vehicle technology and safety exceptions can leave residual collection or a
privacy-versus-service choice. A formal control is not yet a verified privacy
or mobility outcome.

The cross-platform FTC review adds the system-level counterweight. Across nine
large social and video platforms, the agency reported broad collection and
sharing, potentially indefinite retention, and cases where not all user data
was deleted after a request. This is a platform-practice assessment, not a
user-level failure rate, but it shows why administrative completion cannot by
itself prove that data moving through affiliates and vendors is controllable.

The AI-assistant choice record adds a user-side version of the same control
gap. In a weighted survey of 1,999 U.S. users, people reported routing
different tasks to different assistants and learning trust through use, while
privacy concern was much more common than paid privacy protection or knowledge
of training practices. Reported multihoming and hypothetical willingness to
pay are not durable switching, verified privacy, or independence from a
dominant default.

The rural-housing coordination record adds a hidden social-infrastructure
version. Service coordinators can help residents navigate eviction and Medicaid
while HUD cannot reliably count all properties with coordinators or routinely
use performance reports to judge outcomes. A housing unit plus a referral is
therefore not the same as a usable service bridge, completed assistance, or
durable housing stability.

The press-legitimacy rotation adds a cultural meaning layer: many Americans
can agree that the government-news relationship is bad while disagreeing about
how much power news organizations have and whether journalists or politicians
should criticize one another. Shared diagnosis therefore does not equal shared
legitimacy or trust.

The Pell/FAFSA rotation adds a higher-education version of the same interface
problem: GAO found that formal Pell eligibility expanded after FAFSA
simplification, including maximum-award eligibility, even as fewer students
completed the application. The gains were uneven and some household
configurations could lose eligibility. Eligibility is therefore a measured
administrative stage, not proof of aid receipt, enrollment, persistence, or
college access.

The chronic-absence rotation adds a school-participation version: Department of
Education data count 13.4 million of 48.3 million students as chronically
absent in 2022–23, or 27.8%, with wide state variation. Enrollment and
attendance are therefore separate stages. The aggregate file does not explain
the absence or show school response, learning, safety, belonging, or return.

The [school-facility disruption record](records/us-gao-school-facility-disruption-2026.json)
adds a physical-continuity stage. GAO estimates that 19% of districts canceled
school for at least one day because of facility problems in 2024–25, affecting
about 2 million students and 3.5 million learning days, while 47% reported a
chronic facility issue in at least one school. Plumbing emergencies and extreme
classroom temperatures were among leading causes. Facility disruption is a
distinct route from attendance, but the survey does not measure student-level
learning loss, family burden, health, repair completion, or later opportunity;
closing can also protect students from unsafe conditions.

The MOEquity rotation adds an institutional-memory stage after emergency
funding. GAO found that Education's real-time guidance lacked written
MOEquity-specific procedures, data reliability problems limited verification of
which districts were protected, and lessons learned were not reliably
documented or shared. The rule and funding can therefore exist without durable
organizational learning or a verified student effect.

The tribal-water rotation adds a construction-to-maintenance boundary. GAO
found that property rules can exclude homes from an IHS-funded project, while
multi-agency grant navigation can consume substantial staff time and Tribes
remain responsible for operation and maintenance after construction. A built
asset is therefore not yet reliable household service, health protection, or
institutional autonomy.

The [rural-water funding record](records/us-gao-rural-water-funding-coordination-unserved-communities-2026.json)
adds the pre-construction bottleneck: 28 possible USDA and EPA programs do not
remove incompatible deadlines, matching-fund risk, limited data, or the need
for local administrative capacity and knowledge. A formal funding menu can
therefore coexist with an unusable route to a safe, operating system.

The [GAO broadband deployment, targeting, and sustainability record](records/us-gao-broadband-deployment-targeting-sustainability-2026.json)
adds a targeting-to-sustainability boundary. By February 2026, all nine
reviewed federal programs had begun distributing funds, but only three had
completed-project data available. The $42.5 billion BEAD program is expected to
run through 2030 and beyond; GAO found that provider-reported maps and public
challenges shape where funds go and that its long-term financial-certification
system still lacked detailed monitoring guidance. Funding, awards, and intended
locations therefore remain separate from completed, affordable, reliable, and
lasting digital access; missing completion data do not themselves prove project
failure.

The [FCC broadband availability/adoption/choice record](records/us-fcc-broadband-availability-adoption-choice-2022-2025.json)
adds the household conversion stages beneath that deployment route. At year-end
2023, 53% of households subscribed where 100/20 Mbps fixed terrestrial service
was available, versus 26% where 940/500 Mbps was available. In the FCC's 2022
provider-count comparison at 100/20 Mbps, 7.9% of households had no terrestrial
fixed provider, 37.4% had one, 36.6% had two, and 18.2% had three or more.
Availability, conditional subscription, and advertised provider count are
therefore not one access measure: none reveals price, installation, devices,
reliability, actual use, meaningful substitutability, or a household's ability
to switch.

The related [dig-once coordination record](records/us-gao-broadband-dig-once-highway-coordination-2026.json)
adds the physical-governance middle: 46 of 52 state contacts reported a
coordinator and 46 reported broad interagency coordination, while staff, IT,
provider-participation, and outcome-visibility problems remained. Coordination
can reduce the expected cost of a shared highway route without establishing
construction, activation, price, reliability, or household use.

The wearable-health rotation adds a data-to-care boundary. GAO identifies
potentially faster, more personalized, and more remote care, but also variable
device reliability, workflow and liability problems, privacy needs, and unclear
AI autonomy. Sensor output is therefore an input to care, not evidence by
itself of better health or equal clinical control.

The Marketplace-broker rotation adds a consent-to-continuity boundary. GAO
found that federal Marketplace registration and licensing did not reliably
verify consumer authorization or notify consumers of every broker action;
confirmed complaints tied to unauthorized enrollments and switches grew more
than fourfold from 2023 to 2025, and GAO estimated at least 160,000 likely
unauthorized application changes in the 2024 plan year. Selected state
Marketplaces' one-time-passcode controls provide a counterexample, but neither
route yet proves lasting coverage or care continuity. See the [Marketplace
broker-consent record](records/us-gao-marketplace-broker-consent-2026.json).

The No Surprises rotation adds a legal-protection-to-market-response boundary.
GAO found that in-network claim shares rose after implementation for three of
four selected specialties, while emergency payment trends largely continued
their pre-law directions. Provider participation, negotiated payment, patient
liability, affordability, and health outcome remain separate stages. See the
[No Surprises network-participation record](records/us-gao-no-surprises-network-participation-2026.json).

The [No Surprises arbitration/network-pricing record](records/us-nber-no-surprises-arbitration-network-pricing-2026.json)
adds a linked provider-market countereffect. The statute capped patient surprise
bill liability and created final-offer arbitration for insurer-provider payment
disputes; the NBER study reports high-volume, provider-favorable awards above
the statutory benchmark. Provider-network participation declined in the most
affected specialties and states, including after provider groups' first
favorable awards, while evidence that more-exposed insurers raised marketplace
premiums is suggestive. A patient-bill protection can thus alter provider
outside options and insurance-market terms without establishing a patient's
network adequacy, appointment wait, realized out-of-pocket spending, care
continuity, health, or household financial recovery.

The Part D rotation adds a public-subsidy-to-household-security boundary. GAO
found that the stabilization demonstration kept average standalone-plan
premiums near $42-$43 and enrollment rose 2%, while Medicare took on an
estimated $9.8 billion in 2025-2026 risk. The planned evaluation is still
needed to separate the demonstration's effect from other benefit changes and to
show medication or financial outcomes. See the [Medicare Part D premium-
stabilization record](records/us-gao-medicare-partd-premium-stabilization-2026.json).

The [Louisiana hepatitis-C subscription-model record](records/us-nber-hepatitis-c-subscription-outcomes-2026.json)
adds a more complete public-purchasing path. Using differences-in-differences
comparisons within Louisiana Medicaid, the study estimates that the 2019
subscription model tripled hepatitis-C treatment volume, reduced new diagnoses
23%, added $37 million in antiviral spending, and produced an estimated $266
million in five-year savings. This is a program-level treatment, diagnosis, and
fiscal outcome result, not proof that every eligible person was reached,
completed treatment, was cured, avoided reinfection, faced equal access, or
experienced lower household cost. The estimated savings may also reflect
outreach, diagnosis, provider behavior, and other implementation changes rather
than the contract design alone, and do not automatically transfer to other
states.

The federal eligibility-data rotation adds a measurement-to-accountability
boundary. GAO found data-quality issues in all nine selected sources and
cross-source inconsistencies in seven, while 2,074 identifier matches remained
leads rather than proof of improper awards. Public systems can therefore be
information-rich without being able to make a reliable case-level judgment.

The 2025 NHIS rotation adds a coverage-to-care boundary. Exchange-based
coverage rose from 4.3% of people under 65 in 2021 to 6.3% in 2025, while 28.0
million people remained uninsured and the adult uninsured rate stayed at
11.6%. Coverage expansion and persistent unequal practical protection can
therefore coexist; continuity, affordability, and care use remain open.

The [AI-exposed college-graduates record](records/us-census-ai-exposed-college-graduates-2026.json)
adds an education-to-first-job boundary. A Census CES working paper reports
that the most AI-exposed decile of college majors saw initial employment fall
five percentage points and full-quarter initial earnings fall 13% after ChatGPT
became available. About half of the earnings change came from lower pay within
sectors and the remainder from movement toward lower-wage sectors. The result
sharpens a broader pattern: education and credentials can remain available while
the first conversion into paid work becomes less reliable or less valuable. The
working paper does not establish permanent displacement, lifetime earnings loss,
household insecurity, or a ChatGPT-only cause.

The [AI coding production-stage record](records/us-nber-ai-coding-productivity-shipping-output-2026.json)
adds a task-to-realized-output boundary. In a matched event study covering more
than 500,000 GitHub developers, autocomplete tools were associated with a 30%
cumulative increase in commits, interactive agents with 180%, and autonomous
agents with 240%. For autonomous agents the corresponding increase was 80% in
projects and 30% in actual releases, while total usage across four software
marketplaces did not increase despite more new apps. AI-assisted activity,
shipped software, and software that users actually use are therefore distinct
clocks. The study does not measure code quality, security, worker pay or
bargaining, consumer value, firm profitability, or long-run survival; lower
release growth can also reflect review, quality control, demand, or deliberate
experimentation rather than a simple bottleneck.

The cross-border firm-network rotation adds a supply-chain version of the same
conversion problem. A Census CES working paper reports that foreign disasters
can reduce U.S. importers' purchase growth by up to 25 percentage points, while
non-MNC buyers contract roughly twice as much as buyers inside global
corporations; the intra-firm versus arm's-length comparison shows a similar
gap. Firm structure can therefore change who absorbs a foreign shock, but the
abstract does not show whether the result becomes substitution, delayed
production, higher prices, worker effects, or household burden.

The export-control rotation adds a state-policy version of the same split. A
Census CES working paper reports that the share of U.S. exports under controls
doubled between 2010 and 2024. In the 2014 Russia restriction case, affected
firms reduced targeted exports, exports of other products and destinations, and
average payroll. A geopolitical restriction can therefore create observable
domestic adjustment while its security or diplomatic result remains unknown.
Domestic cost is not proof of policy failure, just as policy intent is not proof
of strategic success.

The [regional hospital-capacity record](records/us-census-regional-hospital-capacity-2026.json)
adds a public-infrastructure version. A Census CES working paper reports
declining hospital counts alongside increasing hospital-industry employment and
larger establishments, with rural and regional differences, particularly access
challenges in rural Southern localities. System capacity and local reach can
therefore move in different directions. The working paper does not show whether
consolidation improved specialized care, increased travel and service gaps, or changed patient and
household outcomes.

The [congestion-pricing and EMS record](records/us-nber-nyc-congestion-pricing-ems-response-2026.json)
adds a policy-to-service-time version. An NBER study of New York City's
congestion-zone boundary reports an 18–21% traffic decline near the boundary
and a 63–70 second, roughly 5–6% reduction in total EMS travel time after
pricing began. A policy can therefore improve a measurable service route while
patient survival, treatment quality, neighborhood distribution, ability to
avoid the charge, and political acceptance remain open.

The rural-firm innovation rotation adds a complements version. A Census CES
working paper's rural/urban decomposition, summarized by Census as emphasizing
digital infrastructure, R&D, and human-capital deficits, challenges the idea
that rural location alone explains lower innovation activity. Place differences
may therefore be bundles of missing supports. The decomposition does not show
that adding one support would cause innovation, firm survival, jobs, or regional
convergence.

The [bankruptcy-venue record](records/us-census-bankruptcy-forum-shopping-delaware-employment-1990-2021.json)
adds a legal-institution-to-firm-survival pathway. A distance-based instrumental
variable design estimates that Delaware venue can reduce liquidation and
closure and preserve post-bankruptcy employment for the firms affected by
proximity. The result complicates a simple venue-abuse story, but it does not
establish individual worker security, consumer benefit, or effects for every
court and firm.

The [reusable-software record](records/us-census-nonrival-custom-software-firm-scope-concentration-2026.json)
adds a technology-to-firm-scope pathway. Empirical adoption patterns and a
calibrated model suggest that non-rival custom software can make multi-site
scope more attractive and contribute to concentration. The model does not
identify the resulting prices, wages, service quality, worker control, or
consumer ability to switch.

The [environmental lender-liability record](records/us-census-environmental-lender-liability-pollution-abatement-1996-2026.json)
adds a finance-to-environmental-behavior pathway. A legal change in lender
cleanup exposure was associated with a 14.7% reduction in affected firms'
abatement investment and higher pollution, alongside altered loan terms. The
result shows institutional incidence before the household and health middle;
firm emissions are not a household exposure estimate.

The [housing-wealth labor record](records/us-census-housing-wealth-womens-labor-supply-childcare-2026.json)
adds a same-household time-and-care pathway. Instrumented housing wealth is
associated with lower women’s labor-force participation, especially for mothers
of young children and women citing child-related reasons. Participation is not
the endpoint: childcare options, unpaid hours, job quality, household security,
and whether the arrangement is chosen or constrained remain open.

The [tariff-incidence record](records/us-census-tariff-quantity-discounts-incomplete-pass-through-2018-2019.json)
adds a transaction-scale layer to trade policy. Once lost quantity discounts are
separated from strategic pass-through, the 2018–2019 estimate assigns about 60%
of the tariff to scale-free import prices and a significant share to foreign
exporter markups. That does not identify retail, household, domestic-worker, or
strategic outcomes.

The [status-mismatch record](records/us-census-status-inconsistency-neighborhood-mobility-acs-2026.json)
adds a relative-status layer to residential mobility. Household income rank and
neighborhood standing are associated with different short- and long-distance
move patterns, consistent with spatial assimilation and relative deprivation
interpretations. The model does not identify whether movement reflects
belonging, opportunity, pressure, discrimination, or displacement.

The CTC/ACTC rotation adds a benefit-measurement version. Census's linked CPS
ASEC/IRS estimate reports 91% participation among eligible taxpayers but 85%
dollar participation in tax year 2021. The two figures should not be treated as
one take-up rate or as household relief: taxpayer participation, dollars,
timing, adequacy, and later material outcomes are separate stages.

The [Child Tax Credit sentiment record](records/us-nber-child-tax-credit-sentiment-benefit-loss-2026.json)
adds a policy-loss-to-perception stage. A study using monthly Consumer Sentiment
Survey data estimates a 1.7-point sentiment decline per $1,000 of lost 2021
credit, persisting nearly two years and appearing larger among lower-income
families with multiple children. Benefit receipt, benefit loss, household room,
and economic judgment can therefore move together across time, but the record
does not link the same households to spending, government trust, voting,
political identity, housing security, or a full recovery path.

The [founder-background and hiring record](records/us-census-entrepreneur-background-hiring-homophily-2026.json)
adds an identity-to-firm pathway. Linked Census and LinkedIn data estimate that
female-founded startups have a 36.4-point higher female employee share, while
the corresponding adjusted differences are 51.2 points for Black employees,
37.3 points for Hispanic employees, and 11.3 points for non-college employees.
Those differences persist at least 20 years across new-firm cohorts; the wage
decompositions attribute the relative pattern to labor demand and higher
relative wages for workers similar to the entrepreneur. Social similarity can
therefore become an organizational channel for opportunity, but the
decomposition does not identify intent or separate network information, trust,
skill, geography, labor supply, preference, and exclusion in every case. It
also does not establish firm performance, worker wellbeing, or intergenerational
mobility.

The WIC rotation adds a measurement-to-reach boundary. A Census-FNS-ERS linked
data project estimates eligibility and access across states, counties, and
demographic groups, while Census and USDA/FNS estimates differ in method and
scope. Better visibility can locate unequal reach without explaining the route
barrier or proving benefit adequacy, food security, or child health.

The public-pension/private-equity rotation adds a financial-institution-to-firm
boundary. A Census CES study reports lower returns and smaller fund matching
for more severely underfunded public pensions, alongside productivity decreases
in financed firms. Institutional funding pressure can therefore reach firms,
but the source does not show whether the final burden falls on workers,
beneficiaries, taxpayers, or firms—or whether the pattern is reversible.

The [minimum-wage and robot-adoption record](records/us-census-minimum-wage-robot-adoption-2026.json)
adds a policy-to-production version. A Census CES working paper reports that a
10% increase in the minimum wage is associated with roughly an 8% increase in
manufacturing robot adoption. A wage floor can therefore change the firm's
technology mix before the worker result is known. Robot adoption is not job
loss, and the public abstract does not settle pay, hours, safety, training,
bargaining power, or household security.

The affirmative-action-contracting rotation adds a policy-to-market-entry
version. A Census CES working paper reports that after states ended affirmative
action in contracting, existing minority- and women-owned contractors gradually
downsized, with larger reductions among Black-owned and larger firms, while
existing firms were not more likely to shut down. New MWBEs were relatively
less common. Firm survival, firm scale, and new entry therefore move on
different clocks; the result does not by itself establish worker harm,
community opportunity, or public-service effects.

The female-teacher rotation adds a long-run cultural-institution version. A
Census CES working paper links historical rural school exposure circa 1940 to
later records and reports higher educational attainment for girls exposed to
female primary-school teachers, followed by delayed marriage and childbearing,
better-educated husbands, higher household income, and longer life. The pattern
was stronger where parents had less education or women had fewer labor-market
opportunities. A local institution can therefore matter most where substitutes
are scarce, but the historical setting and public abstract do not settle the
mechanism or current transfer. See the [female teachers/female students
record](records/us-census-female-teachers-female-students-lifetime-2026.json).

The [women's education/career/children record](records/us-census-womens-education-career-children-2026.json)
adds a more local causal version. A Census CES study using a school-entry birthdate cutoff reports that women induced into
some college had higher employment and earnings, more professional and
higher-status jobs, more socially meaningful work, and better working
conditions; their children also had better early-life health and prenatal
inputs. Education can therefore carry both income and job-quality effects into
the household, while the design remains local to the affected schooling margin
and does not identify every mechanism.

The [grandparenting and mortality record](records/us-census-grandparenting-mortality-race-2000-2019.json)
adds a family-care heterogeneity version. A Census–Social Security linked
working paper following adults age 50 and older reports different mortality
associations for coresident grandchildren and primary-caregiving duration
across White, Black, Hispanic, and Asian groups: the same family arrangement
can coincide with higher, lower, or duration-specific risk. Coresidence is
therefore not one cultural meaning or one burden measure; it can reflect care,
reciprocity, housing need, obligation, or prior health. The working paper is
associational and does not identify the mechanism or the effect of public
services, income, health care, or family support.

The minimum-wage-career rotation adds an age and policy counterexample. A
Census CES study merging SIPP panels into LEHD reports strong later earnings
growth for minimum-wage workers, driven mostly by young workers, while older
workers show a different modest dip around the minimum-wage period. State
minimum-wage increases did not significantly alter the future careers of workers
who were on the minimum wage when the increases occurred. Starting wage, age,
firm adjustment, and policy effect therefore remain separate stages.

The local-demand-shock rotation adds a place-and-measurement version. A Census
CES study harmonizes several annual commuting-zone shock measures and finds
that they are weakly correlated and produce different adjustment stories:
trade-related shocks show little employment or migration response, while more
general Bartik-style shocks are associated with meaningful changes in both. A
local shock is therefore not one common object; migration is also only one of
several possible household responses.

The [Census neighborhood/firm/intergenerational-mobility record](records/us-census-neighborhoods-firms-intergenerational-mobility-2026.json)
adds a linked place-and-labor-market mechanism. Its linked Census and
administrative employment analysis reports that the between-neighborhood
intergenerational earnings relationship was about twice the within-neighborhood
relationship after adjusting for parent-income measurement error; mean parent
earnings captured more than 80% of modeled variation in unrestricted
neighborhood effects. The authors' decomposition distinguishes children’s
person effects, associated chiefly with parents' and neighbors' person effects,
from establishment premiums, associated chiefly with parents' and neighbors'
establishment premiums. Neighborhoods explained about 30% of the conditional
Black–White child-earnings gap in the model, while higher Black-share
neighborhoods had higher child adult earnings conditional on neighborhood
average earnings. These are modeled associations, not proof that moving a
family, racial composition, or a particular school, network, firm, or policy
caused an earnings outcome; adult earnings also do not establish wellbeing,
belonging, safety, or political capacity.

The [immigrant-student firm-placement record](records/us-census-immigrant-student-firm-placement-2001-2020.json)
adds an identity-to-firm version. A Census CES study following college students
from 2000 through 2020 finds disproportionate placement at both large firms,
especially multinationals, and small firms or self-employment. Degree fields
predict the large-firm route, while ethnic specialization helps explain the
small-firm route. Immigrant students who stay in the United States earn more
than native peers in the study, but selection, visa status, opportunity, and
constraint remain separate questions; the public abstract does not establish
whether either placement route is chosen freely, equally accessible, secure, or
beneficial for household wellbeing and belonging.

The multinational-services rotation adds a cross-border capability version. A
Census CES study of the 2008–2009 crisis reports that multinationals exporting
services as well as goods to the same destination had higher goods-export
growth than goods-only multinationals. The difference was driven by industrial-
process intellectual-property sales and accompanied by higher affiliate services
sales and domestic services employment. Cross-border capability can therefore
support domestic adjustment, while worker, local, consumer, and strategic
effects remain open.

The HOPE VI rotation adds a place-based social-connection version. A Census CES
study reports lower neighborhood poverty after revitalization but no causal
earnings effect for adults in public-housing units, while children raised in
revitalized units later earned more, attended college more, and were less likely
to be incarcerated. Increased interaction with surrounding peers was an
important reported mechanism. Neighborhood composition, adult income, child
opportunity, and resident control therefore move on different clocks.

The wealth-and-income mobility rotation adds an asset version of the place
story. Using the same parent-child links, Census estimates show that counties
can have different mobility patterns for income, homeownership, housing wealth,
and total wealth; income and wealth mobility correlations are below 0.5. The
Great Recession is associated with lower later income mobility broadly, while
wealth losses are concentrated more among children from poorer families. The
result is a descriptive map of unequal room, not a causal explanation by
schools, housing supply, segregation, debt, transfers, or local government.

The institutional rental-ownership rotation adds a firm-concentration and
denominator version. Institutional investors' holdings rose in all six selected
metros, but their share of all single-family homes remained 1% to 3% while
their share of the single-family rental subset reached 4% to 22%. A growing
actor can therefore matter at the rental interface without owning the whole
housing market. Ownership is not yet tenant experience, rent burden,
maintenance, eviction, homeownership access, displacement, or durable place
control. See the [institutional rental-ownership record](records/us-gao-institutional-rental-ownership-six-metros-2018-2024.json).

The HUD–LEHD trajectory rotation adds a longitudinal work-and-housing middle.
First-time assistance entrants moved from declining toward rising employment
and earnings trends, while eventual leavers' upward trends flattened after exit
relative to stayers. Because entry and exit are selected events with different
need and pre-trends, the study does not identify a clean causal benefit or loss.
It does show why housing assistance should be tested through work, care,
transport, and later stability rather than judged by receipt or earnings alone.

The house-price/firm-credit rotation adds a financial-channel version. A Census
CES study of the Great Recession reports large and persistent employment effects
at small businesses, especially young firms, with entry and exit contributing
to the pattern. Reliance on personal assets or home equity and local bank credit
shocks were associated with sensitivity to house prices. Housing can therefore
be both a household asset market and a local firm-finance channel.

The business-application rotation adds an expectations-to-capacity version. A
Census CES study links 17 million applications to outcomes and finds that
applicants overestimate employment overall because many expect to enter but do
not. Among those who expect and achieve entry, employment is typically
underestimated. Application, entry, hiring, local conditions, and learning are
therefore separate stages rather than one business-formation count.

The grandparent-care rotation adds a family-care heterogeneity version. A
Census-SSA study of adults 50 and older reports higher mortality for White
coresident grandparents, lower mortality for Asian coresident grandparents,
higher mortality for Hispanic long-term primary caregivers, and lower mortality
for Black short-term primary caregivers. Coresidence, caregiving duration,
family support, health, and cultural meaning therefore cannot be collapsed into
one burden measure.

The trade-welfare rotation adds a geopolitical-place version. A Census CES
study develops a welfare measure that includes unemployment, participation,
sector switching, and where workers live, then applies it across commuting zones
to China's PNTR shock. Trade welfare therefore cannot be read from wages alone;
relative local effects still do not settle household meaning, political
response, or the national welfare level.

The counter-China-influence audit adds a geopolitical measurement version. State
and USAID can report a large project portfolio and substantial funding while
missing complete project-status data and a shared assessment of what changed.
Strategic spending, partner implementation, and changed external behavior must
therefore remain separate stages.

The [U.S.-China global-role comparison](records/us-pew-us-china-global-role-comparison-2026.json)
adds a comparative legitimacy stage. Across 36 countries, China is viewed more
favorably than the U.S. in many publics and is more often described as reliable
in several middle-income countries, while the Philippines favors the U.S. on
reliability. Favorability, reliability, peace contribution, actual dependence,
and government alignment are separate outcomes; public reception alone does not
show a transfer of strategic influence.

The [Pew democracy-evaluation record](records/us-pew-democracy-dissatisfaction-reform-2026.json)
adds a domestic public-judgment counterpart. In the March 2026 survey, 68% of
U.S. adults said the country used to be a good democratic example but had not
been in recent years, while 24% said it was currently a good example; 69% were
dissatisfied with how democracy was working and 30% satisfied. Satisfaction
differed sharply by party (86% dissatisfied among Democrats and Democratic
leaners versus 51% among Republicans and Republican leaners). These are current
evaluations, not institutional-performance audits, evidence of a shared reform
program, or proof of turnout, protest, institutional withdrawal, or a causal
effect of party control, economic conditions, or any particular event.

The democracy-assistance rotation adds a civic-continuity version. When a host
government becomes part of the democratic risk, U.S. support may pivot toward
civil society, media, or private actors, but an unplanned pivot can interrupt
momentum and coordination. Funding, recipient safety, civic voice, and later
democratic change therefore remain separate stages.

The Ukraine-oversight rotation adds a wartime-learning version. State improved
the format and timing of reports and used some information for oversight, while
data access and delayed outcomes still prevented complete cross-project
evaluation. Better reporting, delivered assistance, and strategic effect run on
different clocks.

The [Iran protest-response record](records/us-nber-us-israel-strikes-iran-protest-reversal-2026.json)
adds a foreign-action-to-domestic-meaning version. An NBER working paper using
geocoded protest events and U.S.–Israeli strike exposure reports a shift from
anti-government toward pro-government and anti-U.S./Israel protest after
exposure, while total protest activity changed little. External force can
therefore redirect the meaning and direction of mobilization without increasing
mobilization overall. Repression, state messaging, fear, selective recording,
civilian effects, and later legitimacy remain unresolved, so protest direction
is not by itself strategic success or failure.

The economic-place civic-capacity record adds a cultural-material version. A
large CPS-based study links local disadvantage, inequality, and the Great
Recession to lower or changing formal volunteering, while the earlier rural
advantage disappears after the recession. Formal volunteering, informal help,
belonging, and political action remain separate forms of civic life.

The jail-exposure record adds a justice-contact version. Local-jail population
and admissions were lower than in 2014, yet 2024 still involved millions of
admissions, a large unconvicted population, and unequal racial exposure. Lower
aggregate custody, pretrial experience, conviction, and later reentry therefore
run on different clocks.

The [federal reentry-timing record](records/us-gao-bop-reentry-timing-2026.json)
fills in a missing administrative middle. BOP could not readily identify all
eligibility dates, and in a review of 29,934 people it had not applied all
earned time toward placement for 21,190; limited RRC capacity and late payments
to providers added operational friction. A formal reentry option is therefore
not the same as timely transfer, usable housing or work support, community
reconnection, or durable inclusion. The audit does not establish the individual
effect of delay on recidivism or trust.

The community-supervision record extends that route beyond the jail door. About
3.68 million adults remained under probation or parole at the end of 2024 even
as comparable-agency supervision fell over the decade. Completion, incarceration,
absconding, and other exits are visible administratively; work, housing, family
life, safety, and the meaning of freedom afterward remain open.

The [Native-housing and homelessness record](records/us-gao-native-housing-homelessness-2026.json)
adds a culturally specific legibility version. GAO identifies 30 federal
housing programs, but nearly all contacted Tribes and Tribal Designated Housing
Entities said funding did not keep pace with construction, rehabilitation, or
maintenance needs. Doubled-up living may be common, supportive, crowded, or
unstable and may not fit federal homelessness definitions. The preliminary
review does not measure national prevalence, duration, safety, tribal control,
application success, construction completion, or durable housing outcomes, so
program reach, cultural meaning, and safe housing remain separate.

The [Tribal-federal shared-decision record](records/us-gao-tribal-federal-shared-decision-making-2026.json)
adds a governance version of cultural legitimacy. GAO's five selected agreement
cases identify 11 design features that strengthen shared decision-making,
including consensus and dispute resolution, while agency authority and staffing
capacity remain uneven. Voice becomes more than consultation only when the
institution supplies decision rights, resources, and a way to correct conflict;
the review does not show equal power, community outcomes, or durable
self-governance.

The defense-arrangements record adds an alliance-administration version. DOD
established 303 acquisition-related international arrangements associated with
$64 billion in FY2021–2025, but personnel shortages, review delays, and uneven
communication slowed the route. Agreement count and dollar value are therefore
stages before implementation, joint use, partner response, and deterrence.

The weapons-acquisition record adds a rapid-pathway-to-capability version. GAO
found an average delivery time above 12 years across a selected portfolio, and
18 of 40 reviewed Middle Tier programs entered with immature technologies;
some ended without a fieldable capability. A fast acquisition label, budget,
or prototype is therefore not the same as reliable deployment, sustainment,
warfighter use, or deterrence.

The [Poland–US defense reciprocity/co-production record](records/us-poland-us-defense-reciprocity-regional-coproduction-2026.json)
adds a regional defense-industrial and sovereignty-meaning layer. Official
Polish announcements record a UAS/C-UAS cooperation agreement intended to
expand Poland's role beyond buyer to potential seller, a Poland–US–Estonia–Latvia
Barracuda-500M production and supply agreement, and a reported $4 billion
Foreign Military Financing agreement. But the later ministerial response makes
the implementation stage explicit: detailed production, licensing, and
technology-transfer obligations depend on confirmed Polish Armed Forces demand,
while working groups prepare arrangements and initial activity is described as
assembly and servicing before later localization. Official statements frame
domestic technological competence as resilience and sovereignty. Agreements,
financing, preparation, staged localization, and political meaning are each
observable, but none establishes a purchase order, licensed control rights,
commissioned plant, local-content share, produced or accepted unit, fielding,
supplier replaceability, bargaining leverage, or deterrence.

The [Task Force X-Baltic record](records/us-nato-task-force-x-baltic-2025-2026.json)
adds an experiment-to-alliance-capability version. NATO reports that a
three-week 2025 test fielded more than 70 uncrewed air, surface, and subsurface
systems, with 75% average availability for eight hours a day; eight Allies then
signed a Phase II commitment to nationally owned capabilities taskable by NATO,
with reported procurement and deployment following. That is a meaningful
conversion from test to institutional commitment and reported real-world use.
It does not independently show continuous coverage, detection quality, crisis
reliability, maintenance burden, cost sharing, public consent, adversary
adaptation, or deterrence.

The [technology-release and foreign-disclosure record](records/us-gao-technology-release-foreign-disclosure-2026.json)
adds a permission-to-capability version. DOD estimates about 7,000 foreign
technology-release cases annually, with roughly 70% continuing existing sales
or precedent, while policy discovery can take days to a year. Urgent Ukraine
reviews were compressed from roughly a year to weeks or hours through overtime
and deprioritizing other work. The route can protect sensitive technology while
still producing partner-visibility and trust problems; reform completion does
not establish faster approvals, transfer, partner use, interoperability, or
deterrence.

The [German brigade-in-Lithuania staffing record](records/us-germany-lithuania-brigade-staffing-social-capacity-2026.json)
adds a social-capacity version of alliance readiness. About 80% of reported
positions were filled by volunteers,
but selected mandatory measures were added because more personnel were needed
before the end-2027 operational target. Staffing, maintenance, family burden,
host-country acceptance, and deterrence are separate stages; a readiness target
is not yet a tested capability.

The [Germany/NATO hybrid-response and alliance-posture record](records/us-nato-germany-hybrid-response-alliance-posture-2026.json)
adds a later posture, training, and legitimacy context. NATO's September 2026
statement describes a permanent German armoured brigade in Lithuania, a new
eastern-flank command role for the First German-Netherlands Corps, and
solidarity after a reported hybrid attack at Leipzig airport. A separate
Freedom Shield 26-I report documents a brigade-level exercise in Lithuania with
about 2,900 personnel, 800 tactical vehicles, eight NATO nations, movement,
tactical training, and live firing. German 2025 opinion data record support
for higher defense spending (64%) and more personnel (65%), but only 10% said
they felt well informed about foreign missions; a 2023 Lithuanian poll reported
82% approval of permanent German brigade stationing. These different dates and
units do not establish consent for a specific 2026 response, full operational
capability, independent incident attribution, local burden acceptance,
adversary adaptation, or deterrence.

The [military-readiness and sustainment record](records/us-gao-military-readiness-sustainment-tradeoffs-2026.json)
adds a force-conversion version. GAO's cross-domain synthesis reports
maintenance, personnel, training, data, housing, fatigue, and availability gaps;
the Army watercraft example fell from 75% fully mission capable in 2020 to below
40% in 2024 against a 90% policy goal. Modernization and operational demand can
therefore coexist with weak sustainment at the point of need. This fleet example
does not establish overall force weakness, combat performance, deterrence
failure, ally willingness, or an external response.

The [Ukraine–JEF AURORA record](records/us-jef-ukraine-enhanced-partnership-aurora-2026.json)
adds a partner-integration version. Ukraine moved from coordination under the
JEF Enhanced Partnership to reported participation by Ukrainian drone operators
and specialists in Sweden's AURORA 26 exercise, with combat experience and
unmanned systems identified as exchange priorities. A partnership can therefore
convert into a named exercise contribution without becoming membership or a
security guarantee; completed tasks, sustained access, cost, public consent,
adversary adaptation, and deterrence remain open.

The [NATO resilience-baseline record](records/us-nato-resilience-baseline-requirements-2026.json)
adds a civil-military infrastructure version. NATO's 2026 requirements place
food, water, health, communications, transport, border crossing, supply chains,
and private-sector dependencies inside military-readiness planning, while also
requiring resource planning, Host Nation Support, whole-of-society governance,
and mitigation of effects on populations. A formal resilience framework can
therefore widen the alliance's scope without proving national implementation,
service continuity, public consent, cost distribution, crisis performance, or
deterrence.

The [Denmark host-nation rehearsal record](records/us-nato-host-nation-rehearsal-denmark-2026.json)
adds an observed coordination stage. NATO's Steadfast Discovery 26 account
reports a deployment rehearsal in Copenhagen and Herning with Danish authorities
covering logistics, transport, movement, reception, and allied sustainment. A
host-nation rehearsal makes civil-military interfaces visible, but the public
account does not report movement times, failed procedures, corrective action,
civilian continuity, cost sharing, public consent, or crisis performance.

The [KFOR local-security-integration record](records/us-nato-kfor-posture-local-security-integration-2026.json)
adds a responsibility-transfer version. NATO reported an improved Kosovo
security assessment and a gradual KFOR posture adjustment; Kosovo Police
personnel then began integrating into KFOR's Visoki Decani Monastery security
architecture, alongside a coordination surface naming EULEX, Kosovo
institutions, Serbian Orthodox Church representatives, and Serbian Armed
Forces. A mission can thus operationalize local institutional integration
before it establishes resident acceptance, actor agreement, incident reduction,
cost distribution, durable legitimacy, or a changed external behavior.

The carbon-tariff record adds a climate-geopolitical version. A quantitative
model finds that a U.S. tariff alone can lower foreign emissions while raising
U.S. emissions, whereas combining it with a domestic carbon tax produces a
larger modeled global reduction. Modeled GDP, household incidence, firm response,
and partner acceptance must remain separate.

The geoeconomic-coercion model adds a state-power-through-firms version. A
great power may pressure a foreign government, private firms, or both through
trade and financial links. A firm may be easier to pressure because it does
not internalize every national consequence, while a government may reach firms
that the great power cannot directly control. This is a modeled mechanism, not
an observed success rate: real compliance, firm losses, public cost, coalition
response, and strategic effect still need episode-level evidence.

The corporate-PAC record adds a domestic legitimacy version. After fatal
school shootings, firms reduced visible PAC giving to some Republican House
candidates, especially where consumer exposure made reputation more salient;
the reported effect was selective, did not show a shift to Democrats, and
returned toward baseline within about nine months. Visible political retreat
can therefore be real without proving changed lobbying, policy goals, consumer
trust, or durable corporate influence.

The [FEC campaign-finance record](records/us-fec-campaign-finance-2025-2026-15-month.json)
adds the disclosed-money scale to that visibility boundary. Federal committees
reported about $2.13 billion in congressional-candidate receipts, $1.12 billion
in party-committee receipts, and $6.30 billion in PAC receipts from January
2025 through March 2026, with independent expenditures separately reported.
Committee-level money is a capacity measure, not a count of unique donors,
citizen participation, communication exposure, persuasion, turnout, policy
change, or legitimacy; reporting and classification boundaries remain explicit.

The [EAC election-administration record](records/us-eac-election-administration-voting-2024.json)
adds the attempted-ballot-to-counted-ballot stage. More than 158 million ballots
were counted in 2024, while 1.74 million provisional ballots were cast; 73.8%
were counted and 25.1% rejected under differing state and local rules. More than
98% of jurisdictions reported paper or auditable records. This shows election
administrative capacity without proving equal access, improper rejection, cure
success, voter trust, persuasion, or later political action; a rejected
provisional ballot is not by itself evidence of wrongful exclusion.

The [employment-and-mothers political-outcomes record](records/us-nber-employment-mothers-voting-political-identity-2026.json)
adds a household-side political version. Among lower-income mothers, the
study's policy-induced employment variation was associated with lower turnout,
registration, political knowledge, and civic engagement, alongside more
conservative policy preferences. Effects were stronger for unmarried, younger,
less-educated, and nonmetropolitan mothers, and the reported political shifts
were concentrated among White women despite larger employment gains among
non-White women. Employment gains and political change therefore do not move
one-for-one. Work may add income and social contact while taking time from civic
life; the working paper does not identify the full care, workplace, income,
household-security, or long-term political mechanism.

## Latest breadth synthesis: usable participation is the common middle

The Census nonprofit-sector study adds a labor condition beneath community
institutions. Nonprofits employ roughly one in ten US workers, yet average
earnings are lower than in for-profit firms and the estimated gap varies widely
by industry. This does not establish weaker mission or service; it identifies a
material staffing and retention layer that must be connected to capacity,
client access, quality, and trust before making a social-infrastructure claim.

The Census ACS review supplies a public-data version of the same interface
pattern. Selected changes to layout, messaging, internet response, and mailings
raised response in particular periods, while a 2022 test was neutral and the
longer-run self-response rate continued to decline. Better institutional design
can therefore help without proving equal representation, accurate completion,
trust, or policy feedback.

The CDC Community & Connection page supplies a social-life version of the
usable-participation boundary. Most adults in the displayed 2021 indicator
reported receiving needed support, but the youth indicator was lower and a
separate 2024 measure found health-related difficulty participating in social
activities for 12% of adults. These are not same-year or same-person results;
they support a distinction between support, ability to participate, belonging,
and recovery rather than a causal claim about isolation.

The household-energy rotation makes the time dimension of usable participation
more concrete. SIPP's adjacent-month screen places utility difficulty and
tenure before childcare-related work prevention, food insecurity, housing
hardship, and resource-band movement on the following record. The renter cells
with utility difficulty and work-prevention care constraints show a particularly
high hardship surface, but they are sparse and the fields are not a dated bill,
shutoff, or newly occurring care episode. HHS and EIA evidence adds the
institutional counterpart: LIHEAP can reach households and restore service,
while public counts do not show whether the next bill, food, medicine, work,
or safe-temperature trade-off was avoided. The combined lesson is not that
assistance failed; it is that a payment can buy time without proving that the
household's next usable option expanded. Timing of application, approval,
payment, restoration, and the next bill should therefore be treated as separate
stages rather than collapsed into "served" or "restored."

The historical US selection record adds a domestic political-access layer.
Across Members of Congress born from 1830 to 1950, future officeholders came
from economically advantaged families, and the family-background gap held or
widened across cohorts. Four tested Progressive-Era reforms did not detectably
change who reached Congress. This does not prove present-day exclusion or bad
policy; it identifies a separate pre-vote stage where family resources,
education, networks, money, and time may shape political entry.

The Vietnam draft-lottery study adds an institution-and-identity counterexample.
Veteran status was positively associated with immigrant integration, but random
draft risk did not produce a comparable positive effect on naturalization,
language, residential integration, or cross-national marriage. Military service
did produce a college-completion benefit, especially for non-Western immigrants.
The broader lesson is that an institution can provide a concrete resource while
its symbolic promise of shared belonging remains unproven; selection into the
institution matters.

The newest cultural, household, public-system, and geopolitical rotations
make one recurring subtheme clearer: **formal availability is not the same as
usable participation**.

| Surface | What is visible | What remains open |
|---|---|---|
| Housing and work | HUD reports about 66 affordable units per 100 extremely low-income renter households, but only 38 affordable and available, and 34 affordable, available, and adequate; BLS finds leave access for 94% of wage and salary workers while 8% needed leave and did not take it | Search, acceptance, workload, income loss, care result, lease-up, and later stability |
| Culture and civic life | ATUS separates socializing, religious activity, and volunteering; NEA finds large income differences in arts attendance; Census CES links place disadvantage and recession to lower volunteering | Meaning, transport and time cost, informal help, belonging, continuity, and political action |
| Digital participation | A Facebook deactivation experiment finds short-run wellbeing gains while a separate study finds social pressure around collective exit; FTC reports $2.1 billion in 2025 reported scam losses where social media was the contact method | Durable deletion, replacement, recovery, platform responsibility, and later trust or exit |
| Public and geopolitical systems | BJS finds 23.3 violent victimizations per 1,000 people but 11.2 reported to police; a Secure Communities study estimates reduced reporting by Hispanic victims alongside increased offending, with reported crime unchanged as those changes offset; GAO finds allied space-coordination mechanisms alongside staffing and responsibility gaps; state budgets can retain reserves while adjusting positions and programs | Response quality, safety, operational coordination, service incidence, partner contribution, and legitimacy |

The [social-media scam-loss record](records/us-ftc-social-media-scam-losses-2025.json)
refines the digital-participation row. FTC reports $2.1 billion in 2025 losses
from fraud reports identifying social media as the contact method, up from
$261 million in 2020; 28% of loss reports identified social media as the
contact route, more than 40% of those reports began with a product/service ad,
and reported investment-scam losses initiated on social media totaled $1.1
billion. These are selected public and partner reports, not a population fraud
rate, verified household loss ledger, or measure of platform causation. The
record does not show individual exposure, platform response, refund/chargeback,
household recovery, or durable trust and exit.

The newer [Amazon Prime redress-route record](records/us-ftc-amazon-prime-redress-route-2025-2027.json)
adds a later remedy-design stage to the same receipt boundary. An FTC
September 2026 update reports $845 million in aggregate redress issued by that
point, a maximum of $200 per eligible consumer under the revised order, and
automatic payment channels through mailed check, PayPal, or Venmo. Automatic
distribution can reduce claim-form and notice-response burden, but the source
does not provide a common eligible-consumer denominator, individual receipt or
acceptance, account correction, continued use or exit, trust, or household
recovery; future payment stages remained scheduled. The right cross-source
comparison is therefore **low-friction remedy design versus verified recovery**,
not “money issued equals consumers made whole.”

The [allied-space coordination record](records/us-gao-space-allied-coordination-2025.json)
keeps that geopolitical example bounded. GAO found multiple cooperation
mechanisms with six partner nations, alongside staffing gaps, overlapping
responsibilities, difficult points of contact, and missing implementation
milestones; three recommendations remained open through the review period.
Formal cooperation is therefore not the same as linked mission coordination,
readiness, partner consent, or strategic effect. The review also does not show
alliance collapse or a named operational failure: an emerging cooperation system
can be incomplete while still functioning.

Lithuania's September 2026 air-policing record supplies a distinct later stage:
the official Lithuanian account reports Italian NATO fighters scrambling to
identify, intercept, and destroy a drone that entered Lithuanian airspace.
Lithuanian and Italian defense ministers then framed the response as alliance
unity and called for stronger European air defense, critical-infrastructure
protection, and detection/neutralization capability. This supports a narrow
geopolitical pattern: standing allied posture can become a reported protective
action and an official legitimacy claim before strategic consequence is known.

| Evidence unit | Directly observed or reported | What the atlas can safely connect | Still open |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lithuanian Ministry of National Defence incident account and follow-up | Named reported drone incursion; scramble, identification, interception, destruction; host-government alliance-unity framing and stated capability priorities | airspace incursion → reported multinational operational response → official political interpretation/capability priority | independent attribution/intent, full response sequence, cost, readiness depletion, civilian-risk reduction, public consent, procurement/delivery, deterrence, or adversary adaptation |

The source is a host-government operational account, not independent incident
verification or a measure of recurring mission effectiveness. A successful
interception does not establish the affordability, sustainability, escalation,
or legitimacy of repeated air-policing responses. See the [Lithuania drone
interception record](records/us-nato-lithuania-drone-interception-alliance-response-2026.json).

## Detailed cross-cutting map: from formal provision to lived agency, meaning, and power

The current corpus is now broad enough to support a more explicit social,
cultural, institutional, consumer, technological, and geopolitical map. The
sources should still not be pooled: they use different populations, units,
periods, designs, and outcome definitions. What can be compared is the
recurring conversion problem—how a formal resource, right, service, platform,
institution, or strategic capability becomes (or fails to become) a usable
option for an actor, and how that experience can acquire meaning and alter
subsequent action.

The most defensible common sequence is:

```text
material or institutional condition
  -> formal offer, capacity, or exposure
  -> usable route, attempted use, non-use, or exit
  -> receipt, burden, correction, remedy, or unresolved loss
  -> interpretation through fairness, trust, belonging, dependence, or control
  -> individual, household, organizational, civic, or geopolitical action
  -> later adaptation, legitimacy, substitution, resilience, or power shift
```

This is an organizing hypothesis and measurement discipline, not a claim that
every source observes every arrow. The atlas is strongest on the first two or
three stages, has partial evidence on receipt and correction, and is much more
fragmented on attribution, meaning, and durable downstream action.

### 1. The main cross-cutting themes and their subthemes

| Cross-cutting theme | Recurrent subthemes | What the current sources show | What remains unobserved or weakly linked |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Usable access rather than nominal access** | eligibility versus reachability; availability versus adequacy; application and timing friction; accessibility and information; attempted versus completed use | Housing, leave, utility, health, election, ACS-response, and public-benefit records repeatedly separate a stated route from a route that can actually be used. Medicare Advantage prior-authorization records show denial, appeal, and reversal as distinct stages; provisional-ballot records similarly distinguish casting from counting. | Whether the affected actor understood the route, whether a substitute existed, how much time or money was spent, and whether the final result protected the underlying need. |
| **Receipt, remedy, and recovery are separate stages** | payment versus usable relief; replacement authorization versus replacement received; refund issued versus refund accepted; correction versus restored status; service restoration versus durable security | SNAP EBT theft records, Amazon refund records, consumer-loss sources, Medicaid eligibility correction, and housing-exit evidence show why an approved remedy is not the same as recovered food, money, coverage, housing, or safety. | A linked household-level recovery ledger, adequacy of the remedy, later arrears or hardship, and whether the person returns to or exits the institution. |
| **Unequal optionality and replaceability** | cost of refusal; switching friction; lock-in; local scarcity; income and care constraints; strategic dependence | Mortgage lock-in, platform choice and non-use, job mobility, AI-assistant choice, large-load infrastructure, arms-transfer concentration, and social-media exit evidence all show that formal choice varies with resources and alternatives. | The counterfactual route actually available to each actor, the cost paid to switch, and whether a new option changes behavior beyond a short experiment or stated preference. |
| **Material pressure is mediated by time and care** | work-care conflict; eldercare and childcare; health limitation; disaster disruption; household adaptation; labor-market meaning | SIPP, ATUS, wildfire, SHED, NHIS, and employment-and-mothers records show that money, work, health, and care interact. A resource gain can coexist with time loss, unfilled care, or reduced civic participation. | Complete household production, intra-household bargaining, care quality, longer-run wellbeing, and the mechanism connecting time pressure to trust, identity, or political action. |
| **Institutional capacity is carried by people and infrastructure** | staffing and pay; maintenance; data governance; administrative learning; local implementation; interoperability; reliability | Nonprofit earnings, FEMA and county capacity, USPS performance, GAO modernization and cybersecurity, SNAP data governance, allied-space coordination, and infrastructure records show that formal responsibility does not guarantee operational reach. | Whether capacity gaps are visible to users, how responsibility is attributed, and whether learning or investment changes later service quality and legitimacy. |
| **Participation, belonging, and meaning are not interchangeable** | support versus participation; membership versus service; identity versus action; social connection; collective exit | CDC support/participation measures, Pew religious-institution and Latter-day Saint records, CIRCLE/civic-time evidence, Pew working-class and Latino-American-Dream records, and platform-exit studies distinguish belonging, perceived value, use, identity, and action. | Person-level pathways from received support or identity to durable participation, collective organization, trust, or political behavior. |
| **Information changes interpretation, but not automatically outcomes** | attribution; comparative information; prediction and advice; privacy knowledge; learning by trying; media exposure | AI political-advice theory, working-class and American-Dream identity, news-platform civic engagement, privacy/deletion records, search experiments, and health-influencer evidence show that information can change beliefs, perceived agency, or contestation. | Whether information is accurate, retained, acted upon, socially reinforced, and translated into material protection or institutional change. |
| **Public and private systems distribute responsibility** | state versus firm accountability; platform governance; fraud and recourse; administrative correction; contractor and partner dependence | Consumer remedies, AI companion inquiry, California broker deletion, FTC scam-loss reporting, Medicaid correction, and USPS/GAO records show that responsibility is often distributed across firms, agencies, contractors, interfaces, and users. | Which actor can actually correct the harm, whether responsibility is accepted, and whether correction changes future design or behavior. |
| **Geopolitical capacity becomes power only through use and response** | capability versus readiness; coordination versus operation; protection versus deterrence; supplier concentration; bargaining and coercion | SIPRI, allied-space coordination, NATO ISR/KFOR, large-load and energy-infrastructure, AI-compute, arms-transfer, and Lithuania air-policing records show capacity, dependence, coordination, and some visible response. | Partner consent, civilian burden, sustainment, adversary adaptation, deterrence, strategic substitution, and durable changes in counterpart behavior. |

### 2. The strongest recurring trend propositions

The corpus supports the following bounded propositions. “Trend” here means a
recurrent pattern across source families or a change documented within a source;
it does not mean that every proposition is a time-series trend or a causal
effect.

**Trend A — Formal provision is increasingly being tested at the interface.**
Across benefits, health coverage, elections, public data collection, digital
privacy, and strategic cooperation, the important question is moving from
“does a route exist?” to “can the relevant actor discover, afford, complete,
and rely on it?” The newest correction and remedy records make the middle
stages visible: denial, appeal, reversal; theft, replacement authorization,
receipt; request, deletion status, verified deletion; and formal cooperation,
staffing, contact, and milestone gaps. This is a measurement trend in the
atlas, not proof that institutions are universally deteriorating.

**Trend B — Recovery is a bottleneck and a distributional divider.** A visible
payment, refund, replacement, or correction can stop short of restoring the
thing that mattered: food, coverage, cash flow, housing stability, service
quality, privacy, or safety. The gap is likely to be largest where the actor
has little slack, few substitutes, or high costs of delay. The records support
this as a recurring process distinction; they do not yet estimate a common
recovery rate or show a universal decline.

**Trend C — Agency is conditional on replaceability.** Digital choice,
employment mobility, mortgage decisions, care routes, consumer remedies, and
state or alliance dependence all show that nominal choice can coexist with
high costs of refusal. A user may be able to deactivate, a worker may be able
to search, or a government may be able to diversify, while the practical cost
of doing so remains high. The key subtheme is not choice versus no choice but
**unequal replaceability**.

**Trend D — Material conditions reach culture and politics through time,
attribution, and social position.** The corpus does not support a simple
material-to-political-action law. Cash-transfer, employment, care, housing,
working-class identity, Latino mobility, news-platform, and religious-life
records show several possible pathways: more security, less available civic
time, changed identity, changed attribution, more or less belonging, or no
detected political shift. The same material change can therefore carry
different cultural meaning across subgroup, tenure, race, family status, or
institutional context.

**Trend E — Institutional legitimacy is produced in encounters, not only in
coverage or stated values.** Religious organizations may be viewed as
community-building while membership and participation are lower; a public
agency may offer a correction while leaving restored status open; an alliance
may frame an interception as unity while readiness and independent
verification remain open. Legitimacy is therefore best treated as an outcome
of visible competence, fairness, explanation, response, and alternatives—not
inferred from provision alone.

**Trend F — Capacity is becoming more networked, but accountability can become
more diffuse.** AI infrastructure, energy load, data brokers, fraud pathways,
public-benefit administration, nonprofit delivery, and allied operations all
depend on chains of firms, agencies, platforms, contractors, local systems,
and partners. Networks can add resilience and capability, but they can also
make failure attribution and remedy harder. The records establish distributed
capacity and responsibility; they do not prove that networked systems are
inherently less accountable.

### 3. Cross-lane mechanisms and counterexamples

The same apparent pattern should be tested against the following counterexamples
before being promoted as a general theory:
