1. **Assistance can coexist with pressure, but can also be protective in a
   narrower subgroup.** The SIPP housing screen reverses direction by renter
   resource band; pooled assistance contrasts therefore cannot be read as one
   effect.
2. **A remedy can be real without being complete.** Amazon refunds, EBT
   replacement authority, Medicaid correction, and prior-authorization reversal
   each show an institutional response, but not the same degree of receipt,
   adequacy, or recovery.
3. **Exit can improve wellbeing while collective exit remains costly.** The
   Facebook deactivation experiment, social-media non-use comparisons, and
   platform-choice records distinguish individual short-run relief from durable
   substitution, social pressure, and collective bargaining power.
4. **Capacity can produce a visible success without proving durable power.**
   The Lithuania interception and allied-space records show response and
   coordination claims, but not deterrence, sustainment, consent, or changed
   adversary behavior.
5. **Belonging can be institutionally meaningful without becoming formal
   participation.** Pew religious-community measures and civic-time records
   show that perceived social value, membership, attendance, volunteering, and
   political action are different outcomes.
6. **Material improvement can coincide with reduced civic capacity.** The
   employment-and-mothers record and care/work studies show why increased work
   or income cannot automatically be interpreted as increased agency or
   political participation.

These counterexamples are not exceptions to be discarded. They are the reason
the atlas keeps source-specific units, clocks, subgroup boundaries, and missing
arrows visible.

### 4. The current end-to-end status of the atlas

The atlas now has several strong partial chains, but not one fully observed
social-cultural-geopolitical chain:

| Chain stage | Current evidence strength | Representative examples |
|---|---|---|
| Material condition or exposure | **Strong in many bounded sources** | household finances, work/care, housing, infrastructure, platform exposure, capacity, military expenditure |
| Formal offer or institutional capacity | **Strong** | eligibility, coverage, public programs, platform controls, alliance mechanisms, infrastructure plans |
| Attempt, use, non-use, or exit | **Moderate and uneven** | leave not taken, provisional ballots, platform deactivation/non-use, care-seeking, privacy deletion request, participation time |
| Receipt, correction, remedy, or operational response | **Growing but fragmented** | appeal reversal, EBT replacement, refund/reissue, Medicaid correction, USPS performance, air-policing response |
| Adequacy, recovery, and protected outcome | **Weakly linked** | some housing, earnings, health, hardship, and exit-type outcomes; few complete remedy-to-recovery episodes |
| Attribution, meaning, trust, and belonging | **Observed in separate surveys/experiments** | institutional trust, identity, religious-community value, platform beliefs, perceived fairness, civic connection |
| Durable political, organizational, or geopolitical action | **Partial** | turnout and civic engagement measures, policy contestation, firm/platform response, alliance claims; causal persistence remains open |

The central research gap is therefore not a lack of facts. It is a lack of
linked episodes that preserve the actor, timing, alternatives, response,
interpretation, and later consequence in one record. The next high-value work
should prioritize such episodes over adding another unconnected percentage.

### 5. Priority next-pass questions

1. Which public or private routes convert a formal remedy into verified recovery
   for the same person or household?
2. When do people attribute a failed route to policy, institution, firm,
   frontline capacity, bad luck, or their own constrained options?
3. Which combinations of money, time, social support, information, and
   alternatives convert material relief into durable agency rather than merely
   temporary stabilization?
4. When does non-use indicate preference, protection, cost, fear, distrust,
   exclusion, or the absence of a substitute?
5. Which institutional investments—staffing, maintenance, interoperability,
   data governance, or local implementation—produce observable improvements in
   receipt, correction, trust, and repeat use?
6. In geopolitical networks, when does coordination become partner acceptance,
   sustainable readiness, deterrence, or bargaining power rather than only a
   formal agreement or one successful response?

This section is a comparative synthesis of bounded records. It should be
updated when a new source fills an arrow, supplies a counterexample, or changes
the unit of analysis; it should not be used to manufacture a pooled index of
social health, cultural cohesion, institutional legitimacy, or geopolitical
power.

## New cross-cutting bridge: distribution becomes meaning through attribution

Across the corpus, material and operational outputs acquire cultural or
geopolitical meaning through a further sequence that should not be assumed:

```text
offered protection, service, capacity, or coordination
  -> uneven receipt, burden, delay, visibility, and practical usability
  -> attribution: who is seen as responsible, capable, indifferent, or coercive
  -> interpretation as fairness, abandonment, dependence, solidarity, or control
  -> non-use, complaint, support, organizing, exit, partner acceptance, or resistance
  -> later legitimacy, trust, adaptation, or changed counterpart behavior
```

This bridge joins the cultural and geopolitical lanes without claiming they are
the same phenomenon. On the household and civic side, SIPP assistance and
housing-pressure records show that an offered benefit can coexist with
different next-month hardship paths; CIRCLE, CDC, and civic-time records show
that support, belonging, and participation have different distributions and
time requirements. The person may therefore interpret a public system through
whether it was reachable and adequate, not merely through the formal promise.
Those records do not identify the person's attribution or later political
judgment.

On the institutional side, nonprofit pay and staffing evidence, ACS response
experiments, and public-system records show that the organizations carrying
care, culture, representation, and administrative access have their own
capacity constraints. A visible service gap may be read as institutional
indifference even when the immediate cause is staffing, funding, timing, or
interface design. Conversely, a successful receipt can support confidence
without proving durable legitimacy. The source units do not permit a general
theory of public trust from output or coverage alone.

The geopolitical analogue is similarly staged. GAO's allied-space review
shows formal cooperation with staffing, responsibility, contact, and milestone
gaps; the NATO ISR and KFOR records show movement into operation and local
coordination; the Lithuania incident account reports a concrete allied
response and an official unity interpretation. Together they support a
capability-to-meaning pathway—coordination can become a visible protective
signal—but they do not establish partner consent, civilian burden, deterrence,
or changed adversary behavior. Official framing is evidence of a claimed
interpretation, not proof that all audiences shared it.

The same distinction applies to private and digital systems. FTC social-media
scam-loss reporting makes exposure and loss visible at an aggregate contact
route, while the platform records leave recovery, responsibility, and exit
open. A user may interpret the same platform as useful, risky, unavoidable, or
unaccountable depending on loss, remedy, alternatives, and information about
who could have prevented it. The source does not observe that interpretation
for each affected person.

**Cross-cutting finding:** distribution is not yet meaning. Meaning is formed
through the interaction of incidence, visibility, attribution, alternatives,
and response. This is the strongest current bridge between household security,
civic culture, institutional legitimacy, and geopolitical power, but the
corpus mostly observes these stages in separate source units.

**Next decisive test:** link a dated intervention or incident to the affected
person, place, organization, or partner; record who received what, who bore the
cost, what alternatives existed, what explanation or attribution was
available, and whether the response was used, contested, supported, or avoided.
Then follow later trust, belonging, political action, recovery, partner
acceptance, or counterpart adaptation. Without that linkage, the atlas should
label cultural and geopolitical meaning as an observed interpretation or open
mechanism—not as the automatic consequence of unequal distribution.

These sources are not a pooled dataset and do not describe the same people or
episodes. Their value is comparative. Across housing, leave, arts, volunteering,
digital life, public safety, state budgeting, and allied operations, the
important missing middle is the interface where a person, community, worker,
or partner must actually use the offered route. That interface carries money,
time, fear, social cost, information, staffing, and dependence.

This sharpens the program's cultural-geopolitical interpretation. Civic
capacity and alliance capacity are not only matters of stated values or formal
commitment; both depend on mundane organizational pathways being reachable,
understandable, staffed, and safe enough to use. Conversely, non-use is not one
meaning: it can reflect preference, protection, cost, fear, missing trust, or
the absence of a workable substitute.

The next decisive comparison is therefore not another availability count. It
is a repeated person-, household-, place-, or operation-linked record that
follows an offered route through attempt, alternative or non-use, response,
protected or sacrificed outcome, and later belonging, trust, action, recovery,
or exit. Until that exists, the atlas should describe this as a recurring
interface pattern, not a single causal social theory.

## Latest breadth rotation: time, consumer recourse, and geopolitical capacity

Three new primary records widen the active map without pretending to close the
same-unit gap. The 2025 ATUS release shows that household work and care remain
unevenly allocated by gender and employment status, while average socializing
and communication time is lower than in 2015 and games/computer leisure time is
higher. This is a time-allocation and cultural-participation signal, not proof
of social withdrawal or a causal digital substitution.

The 2026 GAO review of countering-Chinese-influence programs adds a state-capacity
and geopolitical evidence pattern: State and USAID reported an estimated 470
projects and $1.2 billion in allocations, but missing project metadata and the
absence of a portfolio-wide results-assessment process make strategic effect
harder to observe. Spending and proposal machinery therefore outpace evidence
of influence, partner response, or durable geopolitical leverage.

The September 2026 FTC/Amazon Prime redress revision adds a consumer-power
implementation shift: more than $845 million had been issued, eligibility was
expanded, and new payments were to become automatic rather than claim-based.
That reduces paperwork and may widen reach, but aggregate issuance still does
not show individual receipt, adequacy, continued use, switching, or trust repair.

**Rotation finding:** across care, geopolitics, and consumer recourse, formal
capacity is becoming more visible and sometimes more automated, while the
distribution of usable time, strategic effect, and lived recovery remains the
decisive unmeasured middle. These records strengthen the broad trend atlas;
they are separate units and must not be pooled into one causal story.

The next platform-and-AI rotation adds a sharper cultural and geopolitical
version of the same tension. The FTC's TAKE IT DOWN enforcement regime turns
platform safety into a request process with a 48-hour removal window and a
regulator complaint route, but the public record does not yet show completed
removal or non-recurrence for affected people. Pew's 2026 U.S. survey finds
chatbot use rising to 49% from 33% in 2024 while 67% report little or no
confidence that the U.S. government can regulate AI effectively. A separate
Pew cross-national survey finds median trust in China to regulate AI at 43%,
versus 35% for the U.S. and 34% for the EU. Adoption, domestic legitimacy,
and geopolitical regulatory trust are therefore moving on different clocks.

These are not evidence that one governance model is superior. They show that
platform rules, everyday use, perceived institutional control, and international
legitimacy are distinct trend surfaces that can diverge even as AI becomes more
embedded in daily life.

## Latest breadth rotation: civic participation, firm diffusion, and incidence

Four additional records extend the map across social participation, business
technology, household consumption, and trade. They point to a common mechanism
without implying a common population or effect.

Pew's 2025 engagement study separates adults into Mobilizers (9%), Connectors
(28%), Spectators (31%), and Outsiders (31%) using political, civic, religious,
and news behaviors. Voting and contacting officials vary sharply across the
groups, but the groups are politically mixed. This is evidence that civic
participation is multidimensional, not a single enthusiasm scale; it does not
show why a person enters a group or whether the classification persists.

Census BTOS data add a firm-side asymmetry to the AI story. Current AI use was
reported by roughly 20% of employer businesses overall in May 2026, compared
with 37% of firms with at least 250 employees, 32% of firms with 100–249
employees, and 39.7% in information. Fewer than 20% of firms with four or fewer
employees reported use. Adoption therefore appears to diffuse through firm
size and sector capacity rather than evenly across workplaces. The record does
not establish productivity, worker displacement, or redistribution of control.

The consumption channel is also organizational. An NBER working paper using
consumer-panel data finds that working from home shifts shopping online and
toward weekdays, increases trips while reducing shopping minutes, and can raise
prices paid partly through fewer deals and a redistribution of shopping
responsibility. The estimated grocery-price effect is about 1%, but the source
does not measure welfare or food hardship. The relevant trend is that work
arrangement changes household market behavior and the hidden price of
convenience.

Finally, the NBER tariff study traces geopolitical policy into domestic
incidence: about 26% of the tariff increase passed through to consumer prices,
with 64% of that consumer-price effect attributed to direct foreign-variety
effects and 36% to imported inputs and domestic markups. The indirect channel
arrived over roughly nine to twelve months. This makes attribution and timing
central: a policy can be formally imposed at the border while its costs are
distributed later through firms, products, and household purchasing decisions.

**Rotation finding:** civic capacity, workplace technology, household
consumption, and geopolitical trade policy are all stratified by the practical
capacity of the unit receiving or transmitting the change. Participation is
organized into different civic modes; AI adoption is concentrated in larger
and information-intensive firms; remote work reallocates shopping labor and
price exposure; and tariff incidence travels through supply-chain layers.
Together these records strengthen the atlas's broader claim that nominal
availability is not the same as usable capacity or equal incidence. They remain
separate source units: none supplies the missing link from exposure through
individual interpretation, recovery, or durable political action.

## New institutional-structure signal: public capacity is organized through many units

The machine-readable record `us-census-government-organization-units-1942-2026`
and the Census Bureau's 2026 Government Organization tables add a structural
public-sector signal to the source rotation. The United States contains 91,780
federal, state, and local government units in the 2026 table. The comparable
series was 155,116 in 1942, reached a low of 78,269 in 1972, and then rose to
91,780 by 2026. State totals also vary substantially, from 182 units in Alaska
to 4,547 in California (with the District of Columbia counted separately).

This is not evidence that fragmentation is good or bad. It shows that public
capacity is delivered through an historically changing and geographically
uneven organizational structure. That structure can support local tailoring,
but it can also create coordination, attribution, and route-discovery costs.
The counts do not measure budgets, staffing, service quality, resident trust,
or whether a person can successfully use any particular government route.

**Rotation finding:** the atlas now has a cleaner institutional bridge between
formal public capacity and lived access. Government-unit counts describe the
organizational layer; the household, civic, consumer, and remedy records
describe the encounter layer. The trend question is therefore not simply how
much government exists, but how organizational structure changes the visibility,
coordination, and contestability of the routes people and firms actually use.

## New mobility signal: emerging automation is already ordinary infrastructure

The machine-readable record `us-census-htops-autonomous-features-2026` adds an
everyday technology signal from the Census HTOPS transportation tables. In the
July 15–August 3, 2026 topical release, 129.4 million represented adults
reported driving a vehicle with autonomous features in the prior week—about
49.5% of the represented adult population. The table defines the features
broadly, including speed adjustment, lane steering, and braking; reported use
was about 42.0% among ages 35–49 and 56.5% among adults 65 and older.

This is not a finding that half of adults use fully autonomous vehicles. It is
an exposure-and-use measure for driver-assistance functions, and Census labels
the estimates experimental. It does, however, widen the technology trend from
firm adoption and platform use into ordinary mobility: automation can become
embedded in daily routines before public debate settles questions of safety,
trust, liability, access, or human control.

**Rotation finding:** technology diffusion is not only an enterprise or policy
event. It can become mundane household infrastructure, with the important
social and political questions moving to who gets access, who remains in
control, who bears failure, and how people interpret a system they use without
necessarily choosing it as a political or cultural identity.

## New comparative signal: global-order preferences are locally routed

The record `comparative-pew-india-pakistan-global-order-2026` adds a distinct
public-geopolitics mechanism: rival populations can assign threat and alliance
roles differently even while viewing each other as the central danger. Pew's
2026 survey finds that 54% of Indians name Pakistan as India's greatest threat,
while 43% of Pakistanis name India. Pakistanis most often name China as their
most important ally (75%); Indians most often name Russia (36%), followed by
the U.S. (16%).

The divergence is also internally structured. Indian Muslims are less likely
than Hindus to name Pakistan as India's greatest threat and more likely to view
Pakistan favorably. These are public judgments, not direct measures of
capability or alliance reliability, but they show that the global order is not
received as a single strategic map. National conflict, domestic identity,
religious position, and perceived economic or security partners route the same
external powers into different political meanings.

**Rotation finding:** geopolitical influence should be tracked through at least
three separate surfaces—material capability, elite alignment, and public
interpretation. A state can be formally partnered with one actor while its
public sees another as the key ally, threat, or economic reference point. The
gap is a potential source of legitimacy, bargaining, and policy constraint, not
proof of future realignment.

## New geopolitical meaning signal: the same conflict is attributed through different routes

The record `comparative-pew-israel-palestinian-coexistence-meaning-2026` adds
an attribution layer that operational and opinion-only records cannot provide.
In Pew's spring 2026 survey, only 17% of Israelis and 21% of Palestinians in
the West Bank and East Jerusalem said Israel and an independent Palestinian
state could coexist peacefully; 56% of Israelis and 68% of Palestinians in the
West Bank and East Jerusalem said this was not possible. Respondents were then
asked to explain their answers in their own words.

The explanations overlap around land, violence, and distrust, but the
attributed obstacle differs: Israeli respondents more often described an
independent Palestinian state as a security or existential threat, while
Palestinian respondents more often described occupation, control, checkpoints,
and political rights as barriers. That is not just a difference in sentiment;
it is a difference in the causal story each population uses to interpret the
same geopolitical arrangement.

The study was fielded during the Iran war, did not include Gaza, and observes
stated explanations rather than later action or reconciliation. Its value for
the atlas is narrower and stronger: geopolitical meaning is not produced by
capacity or violence alone. It is produced through attribution—who is seen as
responsible, what risk is visible, what remedy is credible, and whose security
or sovereignty is treated as the relevant unit.

**Rotation finding:** cross-source geopolitical analysis should preserve both
the shared condition and the competing causal narratives. A common event can
generate incompatible interpretations of protection, threat, occupation,
security, or inevitability; those interpretations are potential mechanisms of
future support, resistance, non-use, or bargaining, not interchangeable survey
opinions.

## New U.S. perception signal: sentiment can warm without strategic convergence

The record `us-pew-china-feeling-thermometer-2016-2026` adds a U.S.-specific
time trend to the geopolitical perception layer. Americans' average feeling-
thermometer rating of China rose from 28 in 2021 to 37 in 2026. Warm ratings
rose from 11% to 19%, neutral ratings from 20% to 27%, and cold ratings fell
from 68% to 54%; views remain more negative than positive overall.

The result should not be read as a simple normalization of China. Younger
adults and Democrats report warmer views than older adults and Republicans,
and a warmer feeling thermometer does not measure support for Chinese policy,
reduced security concern, or willingness to deepen economic dependence.

**Rotation finding:** geopolitical sentiment has its own clock. Public warmth
can increase while strategic competition, regulatory distrust, or policy
disagreement remain. The atlas should therefore keep affect, threat judgment,
material dependence, elite policy, and observed counterpart behavior as
separate trend surfaces rather than treating any one as a proxy for the others.

## New AI meaning signal: perceived economic risk is stratified by exposure and awareness

The record `comparative-pew-global-ai-job-inequality-expectations-2026` adds a
comparative perception layer to the firm-adoption and U.S. AI records. In 34 of
37 countries, more people expect AI to reduce jobs than to create them. The
median is 55% in high-income countries versus 36% in middle-income countries;
middle-income publics are more likely to say they are unsure. Similarly, the
median share expecting AI to increase inequality is 35% in high-income
countries versus 22% in middle-income countries, while the U.S. share is 46%.

The pattern is not simply optimism versus pessimism. It may reflect differences
in exposure to automation, awareness, labor-market structure, or the ability to
imagine the technology's consequences. In the U.S., adults ages 18–34 who are
more concerned than excited rose from 40% in 2024 to 55% in 2026. These are
expectations, not observed job losses or productivity effects.

**Rotation finding:** technology becomes politically meaningful before its
outcomes are settled. Firm adoption, public awareness, perceived job risk, and
inequality expectations can move on different clocks; the gap between them is
part of the social trend rather than noise to be averaged away.

## New Brazil signal: democratic satisfaction and external-partner meaning diverge

The record `comparative-pew-brazil-democracy-trading-partners-2026` adds a
pre-election public-meaning surface outside the U.S. In spring 2026, 37% of
Brazilian adults said they were satisfied with how democracy was working and
59% were not satisfied. Confidence was not uniformly low: 73% expressed at
least some confidence in college and university professors and 70% in
scientists, while 68% had little or no confidence in elected officials and 57%
in business leaders.

Brazilian views of its two major trading partners were nearly balanced in 2026:
47% favorable toward the U.S. and 46% toward China. But the balance had moved
over time—U.S. favorability fell from 64% in 2024, while China rose from 39% in
2023—and ideology routed the meanings differently. Left-leaning adults were
more favorable toward China; right-leaning adults were more favorable toward
the U.S.

**Rotation finding:** political legitimacy, institutional trust, and external
partner preference need not travel together. A public can be dissatisfied with
domestic democracy, trust scientific or professional authorities, and still
hold roughly balanced views of competing international partners. That is a
political-cultural configuration, not a single trust score or a forecast of
electoral alignment.

## New infrastructure legitimacy signal: AI capacity can become local burden before benefit

The record `us-pew-data-center-local-legitimacy-2026` adds a place-based
legitimacy layer to the AI infrastructure and firm-adoption surfaces. In the
Pew January-August 2026 comparison, the share of U.S. adults saying data
centers have a mostly bad impact rose from 39% to 54% for the environment,
38% to 50% for home energy costs, and 30% to 49% for nearby quality of life.
Perceived benefits also rose, but more modestly: local jobs went from 15% to
24% mostly bad and local tax revenue from 12% to 21% mostly bad. By August,
60% were not too or not at all comfortable with a new data center in their
area, while awareness of data centers had become nearly universal.

This is not evidence that communities reject AI capacity in the abstract. It
shows that the infrastructure can acquire a local burden meaning before its
benefits are trusted or evenly distributed. Rural environmental concern and
Republican concern about nearby quality of life both increased, so the issue
cannot be reduced to one partisan or urban political route.

**Rotation finding:** AI build-out has a siting-legitimacy pathway distinct
from adoption, jobs, or national competitiveness. The atlas should track
capacity expansion, local resource pressure, perceived benefit, awareness, and
comfort with siting as separate surfaces; otherwise public resistance or
acceptance will be misread as a generic attitude toward technology.

## New democracy signal: national frustration can coexist with local legitimacy

The record `us-gallup-local-national-democracy-gap-2026` adds a local-versus-
national legitimacy surface to the political and trust lanes. In the 2026
Gallup-Kettering survey, 56% of U.S. adults said national democracy was doing
very or moderately poorly and only 19% said it was doing well. Their city or
town received a less negative assessment: 34% said local democracy was doing
well and 21% said it was doing poorly. At the same time, 60% said democracy
was stronger 50 years ago, and 45% expected it to become weaker over the next
50 years rather than stronger (31%).

The gap is not simple optimism about proximity. Only 28% trusted that
political leaders would be held accountable to the nation's laws and
Constitution; 59% said the executive branch had too much power; and 60% rated
equal treatment under the law poorly. The source also reports that trust in
local leaders and neighbors is associated with more positive views of
democracy, but this is not a causal estimate.

**Rotation finding:** democratic legitimacy is geographically layered. People
can distrust national accountability while retaining more favorable local
judgments, suggesting that proximity, observability, and social relationships
are mechanisms to track separately from national institutional confidence.

## New cultural-consumption signal: local opportunity and cultural use do not align automatically

The record `us-census-htops-arts-cultural-participation-2026` adds a recent
cultural-practice layer using the Census HTOPS arts tables. During the four
weeks before the July 15-August 3, 2026 survey, an estimated 20.5% of adults
attended live music, dance, or theater, 9.5% attended an art exhibit, 22.6%
went to the movies, and 17.9% created, practiced, or performed personal art.
About 62.9% agreed that their neighborhood or community had plentiful arts and
cultural opportunities.

The age pattern is not a simple scarcity gradient. Personal art creation was
reported by 26.2% of adults ages 18-34 versus 13.8% of adults 65 and older,
while agreement that local opportunities were plentiful was higher among older
adults (71.5% versus 52.3%). Perceived local supply and recent cultural use
therefore need to remain separate surfaces; one does not establish the other.

**Rotation finding:** cultural participation is organized through both
opportunity and practice, with different age groups occupying different parts
of that route. The atlas should track local supply, recent attendance,
personal creation, cost, time, and social invitation separately rather than
treating cultural vitality as a single participation rate.

## New public-capacity signal: infrastructure access is shaped by rules and maintenance, not construction alone

The record `us-gao-tribal-water-infrastructure-access-maintenance-2026` adds a
public-service lifecycle layer to the infrastructure and unequal-exposure
themes. GAO reports that IHS estimated at least $6 billion was still needed as
of November 2025 for safe tribal drinking water and wastewater disposal, even
after major federal appropriations. The report identifies two distinct access
frictions: eligibility rules can exclude homes and community properties inside
tribal communities, and fragmented IHS, EPA, and USDA processes can turn a
small capital need into a large administrative route. In one example, IHS
officials reported spending more than 80 hours helping a Tribe obtain an
$8,000 grant.

The lifecycle does not end at construction. GAO reports that IHS generally
does not fund routine long-term operations and maintenance such as water
operator salaries and energy costs; one area office's four tribal utility
consultants were expected to support 104 Tribes. Eligible costs in the
sanitation-deficiency database rose from $4.7 billion in FY2023 to $5.9 billion
in FY2024, partly because of aging infrastructure and inadequate maintenance.

**Rotation finding:** public capacity should be modeled as eligibility,
application, construction, operating labor, energy, maintenance, and repair
stages. A completed project is not the same as reliable service, and a formal
funding program is not the same as reachable assistance for the people and
properties within a community.

## New health-AI signal: convenience can drive use while transparency and control lag

The record `us-pew-ai-health-information-control-transparency-2026` adds a
practical decision-making layer to the AI, health, and trust surfaces. In the
June 2026 Pew survey, 34% of U.S. adults had used an AI chatbot for at least
one health or medical reason. Speed was the most common stated motivation
(28%), followed by figuring out symptoms (25%), low or no cost (22%),
understanding a doctor's diagnosis (22%), and lab results (20%). Among these
users, 95% said the information was at least somewhat helpful.

The use signal is not the same as institutional acceptance. Seventy-two
percent said it was highly important for a provider to disclose AI use, 53%
said they had little or no say over whether a provider uses AI, and 63% wanted
more input compared with 21% who were comfortable with their current input.
Nearly half (46%) were unsure whether a provider had used AI, and among those
who said a provider had used it, only 22% understood very or extremely well
how it was used.

**Rotation finding:** AI can enter consequential decisions through a
convenience-and-cost route before visibility, consent, and control are settled.
The atlas should therefore track personal use, perceived usefulness, provider
disclosure, data comfort, and decision authority as separate stages rather than
using adoption as a proxy for legitimacy.

## New firm-side signal: input-price pressure is running ahead of the business outlook

The record `us-census-btos-business-price-pressure-outlook-2026` adds a
high-frequency firm-side layer to the household cost and business-capacity
surfaces. In Census BTOS data as of September 6, 2026, the current input-price
index was 70.1, while current revenue, employees, and hours indexes were 42.9,
48.1, and 46.6. The six-month outlook was also more price-heavy: future input
prices were 76.3, compared with 52.3 for future demand and 51.0 for future
employees.

This is not a direct consumer-price or employment estimate. It is a useful
mechanism signal because firms can translate input pressure through prices,
hours, staffing, quality, margins, or substitution, and different sectors may
choose different routes. The future indexes represent expectations, not
realized outcomes.

**Rotation finding:** household cost pressure and firm cost pressure should be
linked as a chain with an open transmission stage. The atlas should track
input prices, firm responses, consumer incidence, employment, and expectations
separately rather than treating a business outlook index as a realized
household trend.

## New household-material signal: improving headline indicators do not collapse the security surface

The record `us-census-2025-income-poverty-insurance-divergence` adds realized
household outcomes to the firm-price and cost-pressure lanes. Census reports
that real median household income reached $87,460 in 2025, up 2.6%, while the
official poverty rate fell to 10.2% and child poverty reached a historic low of
13.4%. But the Supplemental Poverty Measure remained 13.1%, not statistically
different from 2024, and 7.9% of people were uninsured for the entire year,
also unchanged statistically.

The route composition matters. Public coverage for adults ages 19-64 fell to
17.3%, driven by a Medicaid decline, while employment-based coverage remained
the largest route. Income at the 90th percentile increased 1.7% while the
10th-percentile estimate did not change significantly. These are not
contradictory statistics: they measure different resource definitions,
expenses, transfer systems, and coverage pathways.

**Rotation finding:** household security is a bundle, not a single prosperity
score. The atlas should keep pretax income, official poverty, after-tax and
transfer resources, medical and housing expenses, insurance route, and lower-
tail movement separate; a favorable headline can coexist with persistent
exposure in the channels households actually use.

## New multilateral signal: support for an institution can exceed confidence in its performance

The record `us-gallup-un-global-support-performance-gap-2025-2026` adds a
global-institution layer to the geopolitical and public-systems surfaces.
Across 137 countries in Gallup's 2025 World Poll, the median share approving
of the UN's job was 50%, versus 26% disapproving, with majorities approving in
67 countries. But the pattern was geographically uneven: net approval was
strongly positive in Southeast Asia and sub-Saharan Africa and negative in the
Middle East and North Africa; among Security Council members with data,
approval ranged from 79% in Denmark to 15% in Russia.

The U.S. role-specific evaluation is more critical. In September 2026, 45%
said the UN was doing a good job providing humanitarian assistance, while 73%
said it was doing a poor job maintaining international peace and security and
about seven in ten rated it poorly on human rights and international law. The
humanitarian rating was the strongest of the five roles tested, and party
groups differed in their positive assessments of peacekeeping (32% of
Democrats, 23% of independents, and 19% of Republicans).

**Rotation finding:** institutional legitimacy has at least two clocks:
whether an institution is wanted in principle and whether it is credited with
delivering in a specific role. The atlas should preserve role, place, crisis,
familiarity, and performance attribution rather than collapse them into one
global approval score.

## New platform-consumption signal: speculative attention is expanding through sports, not just politics

The record `us-pew-prediction-markets-sports-attention-2026` adds a
platform-mediated consumer and attention layer. Pew's analysis of Kalshi and
Polymarket data finds combined monthly global notional trading volume rising
from $25.7 billion in May 2026 to $53.0 billion in July. The increase was
largely sports-driven: in July, sports volume was $31.4 billion on Kalshi and
$10.6 billion on Polymarket, while politics volume was only $169 million and
$727 million respectively.

This is a different participation mechanism from polling or political action.
Users buy and sell binary event contracts, so repeated trading, platform
liquidity, seasonal events, and speculative attention can generate large volume
without showing broad user adoption or civic persuasion. Polymarket US also
rose from 4% to 39% of Polymarket's volume between January and July, adding a
jurisdiction and platform-design shift alongside the sports surge.

**Rotation finding:** public attention can be monetized through prediction,
entertainment, and repeated micro-trades before it becomes political
participation. The atlas should track topic, platform, contract turnover,
user reach, money exposure, and civic meaning separately rather than treating
trading volume as a proxy for public belief or democratic engagement.

## New cultural-identity signal: institutional continuity can coexist with retention loss and internal change

The record `us-pew-latter-day-saints-retention-demographic-change-2007-2024`
adds a within-community cultural-demographic layer. Among adults raised as
Latter-day Saints, current identification fell from 70% in 2007 to 54% in
2023-24, while religious unaffiliation rose from 14% to 28%. The remaining
community also became more racially diverse: the White share fell from 85% to
72%, while Hispanic and Black shares rose from 6% to 12% and 2% to 6%.

The change is not a simple secularization or partisan-realignment story. About
three-quarters of members still identify as Republican or lean Republican, but
religion-importance and daily-prayer measures declined, while acceptance of
homosexuality and support for more women in the workforce increased. Existing
communal-trust and volunteering records therefore need to be read alongside
retention and composition: strong ties among current members can coexist with
long-run membership loss and changing internal meanings.

**Rotation finding:** institutions can preserve an outward political profile
while changing through retention, conversion, demographic composition, and
issue reinterpretation. The atlas should track who remains, who leaves, who
joins, what practices persist, and which meanings shift instead of using
membership size or partisan identity as a proxy for institutional continuity.

## New cultural-memory signal: collective memory outlasts personal behavior change

The record `us-gallup-911-memory-lifestyle-security-confidence-2000-2026`
adds a long-horizon memory and security-perception layer. Gallup's latest
anniversary poll finds that 64% say Americans permanently changed how they
live because of 9/11, but only 28% say they personally did so. Terrorism worry
has also eased to 37%, compared with 58% immediately after 9/11 and 24% in
2000. The gap indicates that a public event can remain culturally consequential
without producing an equally large reported change in everyday life.

Confidence in government protection is a separate route. It is 55% overall,
well below the 88% recorded immediately after 9/11, and its current partisan
pattern has reversed: 78% among Republicans, 59% among independents, and 29%
among Democrats, compared with 41%, 53%, and 80% respectively in 2021. The
source also reports higher personal-change shares among Hispanic and Black
adults than White adults, and among weekly religious attenders than those who
seldom or never attend; these are subgroup contrasts, not causal explanations.

**Rotation finding:** collective memory, personal adaptation, perceived risk,
and confidence in state protection run on different clocks. The atlas should
track narrative persistence, observed or reported behavior change, risk
salience, institutional attribution, and subgroup exposure separately rather
than treating remembrance as evidence of behavioral transformation or trust in
government capability.

## New place-based shock signal: public-sector contraction can weaken job confidence before regional life visibly unravels

The record `us-gallup-dmv-federal-cuts-local-labor-housing-pressure-2023-2026`
adds a place-specific public-sector shock layer. In the Greater Washington
region, 55% of residents say they or someone in their household has been
negatively affected by federal job cuts, while the share rating local job
availability excellent or good fell from 65% in 2023 to 51% in 2026. The
source allows “impact” to include lost employment, lost clients or business,
reduced access to federal programs or amenities, and other effects, so this is
an exposure and attribution measure rather than a verified layoff count.

The regional picture is uneven rather than uniformly deteriorating. Grocery
availability, healthcare, restaurants, transportation, and the quality of the
region as a place to raise children retain majority-positive ratings, while
affordable housing is rated positively by only 11%. One-third report being
very or somewhat worried about rent or mortgage payments, rising to 48% among
renters; among residents planning to leave, 58% cite rising housing costs and
59% the broader cost of living as major reasons.

**Rotation finding:** a concentrated public-sector shock can appear first as
weaker labor-market confidence and household exposure, while amenity systems
remain comparatively resilient and housing acts as the mobility constraint.
The atlas should track shock attribution, direct and indirect employment
routes, local demand, housing payment pressure, tenure, planned mobility, and
actual moves separately rather than treating a regional sentiment decline as
either a verified job-loss count or generalized place collapse.

## New infrastructure-governance signal: authorization, obligation, disbursement, and usable delivery are separate clocks

The record `us-gao-iija-ira-funding-obligation-disbursement-review-2022-2026`
adds a federal implementation layer to the infrastructure and state-capacity
surfaces. In GAO's review of EPA, Interior, NTIA, and DOT, the selected
agencies had $574.7 billion in IIJA authority for fiscal years 2022-25, of
which $434.2 billion was obligated and $235.6 billion disbursed. That is 76%
of authority obligated, but only 54% of obligations and 41% of authority
disbursed. IRA execution was similarly staged: 72% of selected authority was
obligated and 60% of those obligations disbursed.

The gap is not only administrative delay. NTIA had obligated $45.1 billion of
$47.7 billion in IIJA authority but disbursed $1.1 billion, in part because
many BEAD awards had not reached implementation. Conversely, GAO notes that
reported EPA disbursements of nearly $20 billion connected to two Greenhouse
Gas Reduction Fund programs were held at a private financial agent and subject
to litigation, so a disbursement record did not necessarily mean recipient
usability. Review politics added another layer: agencies reported about 9,500
approved awards, roughly 800 canceled awards, and 2,528 awards totaling about
$33.6 billion still pending as of different dates.

**Rotation finding:** infrastructure capacity is produced through a sequence
of authority, obligation, review, disbursement, recipient usability, project
implementation, and public service—not by an appropriation headline. The atlas
should preserve each stage, agency, program, legal constraint, review date,
recipient route, and eventual service endpoint rather than treating federal
spending as completed infrastructure or public benefit.

## New judicial-legitimacy signal: approval, favorability, perceived ideology, and accepted power are different surfaces

The record `us-cross-source-supreme-court-legitimacy-approval-favorability-2000-2026`
adds a cross-source institutional-meaning layer. Gallup reports Supreme Court
job approval at 33% after the 2025-26 term, the lowest reading in its 26-year
trend, with 61% disapproving. Republican approval fell from 79% in September
2025 to 58%, while Democratic approval was 12% and independent approval 35%.
Pew's contemporaneous but different measure finds 45% favorable and 53%
unfavorable, with favorability at 69% among Republicans and 25% among
Democrats.

The measurement split is analytically useful. Pew finds 43% calling the court
conservative, 44% middle of the road, and 9% liberal; it also finds 56% saying
the court has the right amount of power, versus 32% saying too much. Thus a
person can judge the court's job poorly, view its ideology as conservative, and
still accept its formal power—or reject its performance without rejecting its
authority. The two sources should not be pooled: Gallup measures job approval
with an undisclosed article-page sample, while Pew surveyed 3,554 American
Trend Panel members and asked different questions.

**Rotation finding:** institutional legitimacy is multidimensional: performance
approval, general favorability, ideological placement, and power acceptance can
move together or apart. The atlas should preserve question wording, source,
field period, party route, demographic exposure, and later institutional action
rather than turn one approval score into a claim about judicial authority or
democratic stability.

## New institutional-capacity signal: public mission depends on physical support space and management information

The record `us-gao-federal-judiciary-administrative-space-utilization-2021-2025`
adds a physical-capacity layer behind judicial legitimacy. In fiscal year 2025,
the federal judiciary occupied 12.2 million usable square feet of administrative
space across 716 of 772 facilities; administrative space represented 40% of
the judiciary's total space and 39%-40% of annual rent from 2021 through 2025.
The judiciary paid more than $1 billion in rent, and 234 of the 272 facilities
whose only space was administrative were commercially owned. Most of those
commercial facilities served federal public defender organizations and
probation offices.

The key gap is informational, not merely physical. GAO found that the
judiciary tracks space and rent but does not collect or review occupancy data,
so it cannot calculate utilization or establish benchmarks. The recommended
control—regular occupancy measurement incorporated into space decisions—remains
open. This does not prove waste: specialized, secure, geographically distributed,
or surge capacity can be necessary even when average occupancy is low.

**Rotation finding:** institutional capability has a material substrate and a
measurement substrate. The atlas should track mission space, cost, ownership,
occupancy, security and specialization, data quality, management action, and
downstream service endpoints separately rather than treating rent as waste or
physical capacity as realized public access.

## New comparative state-capacity signal: external shocks become household and service risk through fiscally fragile public enterprises

The record `comparative-world-bank-malawi-macro-shocks-soe-service-delivery-2026`
adds a comparative macro-to-service route. The World Bank's Malawi Economic
Monitor describes a country facing commodity shocks, inflation, foreign-exchange
distortions, import shortages, unreliable electricity, and weak supply chains.
Growth is projected at 2.8% in 2027, the fiscal deficit was 8.8% of GDP in
2025/26, and reserves remained below one month of import cover. The report also
describes increased food poverty, wider inequality, fragile food security, and
heavy reliance on casual labor, especially among young people.

The state-owned-enterprise channel makes the transmission institutional rather
than purely macroeconomic. SOEs provide energy, water, agricultural services,
and other essential goods, but many rely on transfers, subsidies, and guarantees
while facing losses, weak cash flow, arrears, and poor debt service. Political
interference, fragmented oversight, and limited transparency are part of the
report's diagnosis of recurring bailouts and declining service quality. The
recommended route—independent boards, transparency, revenue collection,
cost-reflective tariffs, and debt management—also creates a distributional
tradeoff: stronger enterprise finances can raise user burdens unless social
protection and service access are tracked alongside reform.

**Rotation finding:** external shocks reach households through fiscal room,
public enterprises, infrastructure reliability, tariffs, and labor-market
options. The atlas should preserve shock, financing, governance, service,
price, household-exposure, and reform-implementation stages separately rather
than turning a macro outlook or an SOE diagnosis into a claim about individual
welfare or successful state reform.

## New subnational-finance signal: infrastructure reach depends on an intermediary and project-preparation route before local service delivery

The record `comparative-world-bank-ecuador-subnational-infrastructure-finance-2026`
adds a subnational financing architecture to the comparative state-capacity
surface. The World Bank approved an $800 million, three-phase program over ten
years for Ecuador, with $200 million in first-phase World Bank financing and
$50 million in parallel AECID co-financing. Banco de Desarrollo del Ecuador
will implement the program for municipalities, provincial governments, and
public service providers, while the first phase also funds project preparation
and technical and institutional capacity.

The announcement expects more than 3 million people to benefit and up to $750
million in private capital to be mobilized across energy, transport, urban
development, disaster-risk management, and affordable housing. These are
forward-looking targets, not realized reach or investment. The intermediary
route is itself a mechanism: it may improve coordination and local project
quality, but can also concentrate gatekeeping, create debt or fiscal exposure,
or leave lower-capacity local governments behind.

**Rotation finding:** subnational infrastructure is not only a capital stock;
it is a financing, intermediary, preparation, eligibility, procurement, and
maintenance chain. The atlas should track who receives finance, who prepares
projects, what gets built, how private capital is counted, how benefits are
distributed, and whether services become affordable and reliable rather than
treating an approved envelope or beneficiary target as completed public value.

## New trade-livelihood signal: logistics infrastructure and coastal resource upgrading join market access to climate and gender exposure

The record `comparative-world-bank-vietnam-trade-connectivity-coastal-livelihoods-2026`
adds a trade-corridor and coastal-livelihood route. The World Bank approved
three Vietnam projects totaling $666.74 million: Hai Phong resilience and
urban connectivity, Mekong Delta highway rehabilitation, and sustainable
fisheries development. The plans include 251 kilometers of highway work, a new
Ring Road 3 section, wastewater and flood protection, and modernization of nine
fishing ports. Construction employment is projected at about 10,700 casual and
2,300 professional jobs in Hai Phong and 10,900 jobs annually for the Mekong
connectivity project.

The fisheries project expects more than 525,000 fishers, aquaculture farmers,
and coastal residents to benefit, with women targeted to represent at least
half of direct beneficiaries. These are program targets rather than observed
income or access outcomes. The chain can improve market reliability and reduce
post-harvest losses, but port modernization can also favor larger operators,
change access rules, raise costs, or shift environmental pressure. A gender
target does not by itself establish control over income, assets, or decisions.

**Rotation finding:** trade infrastructure changes the social distribution of
time, logistics cost, climate exposure, work, and resource access. The atlas
should preserve corridor, port, worker, producer, gender, climate, procurement,
and realized-benefit stages separately rather than treating a loan, job
projection, or beneficiary target as completed livelihood improvement.

## New resource-security signal: water scarcity becomes a growth, labor, service, and household-protection problem through governance

The record `comparative-world-bank-tunisia-water-scarcity-growth-jobs-2026`
adds a resource-security route. Tunisia has about 380 cubic meters of renewable
freshwater per person annually, below the international threshold for absolute
water scarcity. The World Bank estimates that water scarcity could reduce GDP
by 6.4% by 2050 and, without action, eliminate at least 30% of agricultural
jobs by mid-century; agriculture employs 14% of the national workforce and up
to half of rural workers. The risk spills into tourism, agro-processing,
industry, and other water-dependent sectors.

The proposed response is institutional as well as physical. Plan Eau 2050
envisions roughly $900 million a year in investment through mid-century, while
the RESEau program is expected to generate about 4,000 permanent and 13,400
temporary jobs in its first phase. But the monitor says infrastructure must be
paired with water governance, provider financial sustainability, cost recovery,
network-loss reduction, wastewater reuse, digitalization, and irrigation
management—with vulnerable households protected. That creates a clear
distributional hinge: stronger providers and cost recovery can improve
reliability while increasing burdens for users if protection is weak.

**Rotation finding:** environmental scarcity becomes social risk through labor
dependence, prices, service-provider finance, infrastructure maintenance, and
the reach of household protections. The atlas should track resource condition,
modeled exposure, sector and worker dependence, provider performance, tariff
and affordability changes, adaptation, and realized service separately rather
than treating a water plan or modeled GDP loss as an observed household outcome.

## New public-risk signal: politics enters personal safety perception as a cross-partisan social risk

The record `us-cross-source-gallup-world-risk-politics-safety-2021-2026` adds a
public-risk and political-meaning route. Gallup's 2025 World Risk Poll, covering
140 countries, reports that 10% of U.S. adults named politics as the greatest
personal safety risk, up from 6% in both 2021 and 2023 and roughly ten times the
1% global average. The U.S. tied Hungary for the highest country estimate. The
poll reports no statistically significant U.S. differences by gender, age,
education, or party, so this signal should not be reduced to one partisan
subgroup.

The surrounding U.S. context is also institutionally meaningful: 33% named
politics or government the country's most important problem in 2025, while 58%
said the government does not care at all about people like them, 11 points
higher than in 2021. Politics was below roads (34%) and crime or violence
(19%) but above personal health (5%) as a named personal safety risk; the
comparison is a perception measure, not an observed violence or safety event.

**Rotation finding:** political conditions can enter everyday risk perception
and institutional meaning before the atlas can claim a change in physical
safety, disorder, participation, polarization, or institutional withdrawal.
The next tests should preserve the distinction between perceived risk,
reported concern, actual exposure, public behavior, and institutional response,
while checking whether this meaning signal connects to trust, mobility,
consumption, or civic action through separately measured endpoints.

## New civic-architecture signal: public life splits into action, community, attention, and lower-participation routes

The record `us-pew-public-life-engagement-types-2025-2026` adds a cross-domain
participation route from Pew's 2025 Cross-Sectional Engagement Survey. Rather
than placing Americans on one engaged-to-disengaged ladder, Pew classifies them
into Mobilizers (9%), Connectors (28%), Spectators (31%), and Outsiders (31%)
using 19 measures spanning political activity, civic involvement, religious
attendance, and news attention. The classification is a source-defined cluster,
not a durable identity or a time series.

The endpoints separate in consequential ways. Mobilizers and Connectors both
report voting at 87%, but Mobilizers are much more likely to have contacted an
elected official (65% versus 13%) and volunteered (77% versus 42%), while
Connectors report more nonpolitical donations (90% versus 83%). Spectators
follow national news at 84% while reporting much less volunteering (17%) and
official contact (7%). Outsiders report lower levels across the measured
activities, but 41% say they voted in 2024 and 24% attend religious services
monthly. These are not interchangeable forms of participation.

**Rotation finding:** civic and political life is better modeled as a set of
routes—public action, community connection, mediated attention, religious
practice, charitable behavior, and voting—than as one participation score. The
atlas should test which routes are enabled by time, money, institutions,
identity, or information environments, while keeping cross-sectional group
membership separate from causality, durable engagement, efficacy, and social
capital.

## New cultural-access signal: recent arts participation is lower than perceived local opportunity

The record `us-census-htops-arts-participation-community-supply-2026` adds a
current cultural-consumption and cultural-supply route from the Census Bureau's
experimental July 2026 HTOPS tables. In the prior four weeks, the published
weighted counts imply that about 20.5% of adults attended a live music, dance,
or theater performance, 9.5% attended an art exhibit, 22.6% went to the movies,
and 17.9% created, practiced, or performed personal art. These are distinct
forms of cultural activity, not one arts-participation score.

The same release implies that 63.1% agreed or strongly agreed that there were
plenty of opportunities to take part in arts and cultural activities in their
neighborhood or community, while 25.4% disagreed or strongly disagreed. The
contrast between perceived opportunity and recent participation is a useful
screen for conversion: supply, affordability, time, transport, caregiving,
health, preference, and the recall window can all intervene between an
available cultural route and actual attendance or creation.

**Rotation finding:** cultural life should be tracked as a chain from perceived
local supply to available venue or activity, affordability and time, travel and
care constraints, participation, creation, and durable meaning. The HTOPS
figures are experimental and cross-sectional; they do not establish a venue
shortage, cultural disengagement, spending pattern, or causal access barrier.

## New infrastructure-geopolitics signal: undersea cable redundancy is a state-power and economic-continuity dependency

The record `us-uscc-transpacific-undersea-cable-gray-zone-resilience-2026` adds
a physical-digital infrastructure route to the geopolitical atlas. A September
2026 research report posted by the U.S.-China Economic and Security Review
Commission examines 25 operational trans-Pacific cable systems, Chinese
military-civil capabilities, a gray-zone vulnerability index, repair capacity,
and modeled network consequences. It reports that U.S. operator data covers
about 83.5% of trans-Pacific cable capacity, while the global repair fleet has
62 cable vessels, with up to 36 potentially unavailable at a given time. These
are infrastructure and research inputs, not an observed sabotage rate.

The modeled scenarios make redundancy a distributional and firm-continuity
question. Under peak-demand assumptions, disruption of the report's top five
highest-risk cables leaves zero headroom relative to lit capacity; a Taiwan
contingency leaves 1.76%; and disruption of two large cables produces modeled
negative headroom of 69.76%. Guam is especially exposed: the report says that
breaking one of its two continental-U.S. cables leaves less than 0.1% headroom
even after maximum-design-capacity upgrades. The model also connects latency
to financial services, cloud, AI, and internet users.

**Rotation finding:** geopolitical leverage can run through repair fleets,
route redundancy, ownership and supplier capability, maintenance access,
capacity activation, and allied monitoring before it appears as a visible
consumer outage or firm loss. The atlas should keep strategic intent, modeled
vulnerability, infrastructure response, realized disruption, service quality,
and downstream economic effects as separate stages, particularly where a
contracted report's analysis is not itself a verified incident.

## New comparative-capacity signal: growth, competition, formalization, and climate adaptation are one institutional package

The record `comparative-oecd-philippines-productivity-formality-climate-2026`
adds an OECD country-survey route for testing how macro growth becomes—or fails
to become—broad capability. The Philippines is forecast to grow 5.1% in 2026
and 5.8% in 2027, but the survey says reaching the country's 2040 income target
requires roughly three-quarters of a percentage point more annual productivity
growth than its pre-pandemic average. It identifies high electricity and
telecommunications prices, vertical integration, limited access to critical
infrastructure, administrative burden, and corruption as constraints on firms
and investment.

The distributional hinge is labor-market formality and protection: only about
one-third of workers are in formal-sector jobs with comprehensive protection,
while roughly two-thirds work informally; mandatory social contributions are
about 23% of gross wages. The survey pairs lower formalization costs with a
universal basic tier and expanded non-contributory protection, while also
calling for climate adaptation in high-risk areas and stronger natural-resource
governance. The recommendations are not implementation or welfare outcomes.

**Rotation finding:** comparative growth evidence is most useful when it keeps
the chain visible: competition and infrastructure access affect firm capacity;
formalization affects protection and fiscal reach; climate exposure tests state
adaptation; and macro growth can coexist with weak coverage. The Philippines
record is a mechanism and comparison layer, not a U.S. estimate or a claim
that any recommended reform has been delivered.

## New control-route signal: critical-infrastructure exposure can run through ownership, boards, and organizational networks

The record `us-inl-topgear-organizational-influence-critical-infrastructure-2026`
adds a less visible control route to the infrastructure and geopolitical atlas.
Idaho National Laboratory describes TOPGEAR as a tool that links three layers:
business ties and leadership roles, physical and digital infrastructure assets,
and cross-layer influence paths connecting an organization or person to a
specific asset. The stated inputs include SEC filings and Energy Information
Administration data, supplemented by proprietary sources when needed. INL says
the project completed 76 market analyses through DOE's Energy I-Corps program
and is already used by state energy offices.

The publication describes policy responses around foreign ownership near power,
water, and military assets; procurement restrictions involving companies tied
to countries of concern; CFIUS real-estate and agricultural oversight; and
FOCI limits on sensitive U.S. companies. These are policy and implementation
signals, not evidence that any particular investor or board relationship caused
harm. Screening can reveal hidden influence, but it can also raise compliance
costs, constrain beneficial investment, or shift ownership without improving
operational resilience.

**Rotation finding:** infrastructure power is not only about cyber compromise,
physical sabotage, or formal control. Ownership, board access, procurement,
leadership networks, and asset adjacency can create a latent influence route
that requires different evidence and safeguards. The atlas should separate
relationship mapping, suspicious-pattern screening, verified operational
control, realized disruption, and public-service consequences.

## New consumer-finance signal: credit-market expansion is product-specific and risk-tier uneven

The record `us-cfpb-consumer-credit-trends-february-2026` adds a current market
endpoint between macro conditions and household room. In the latest available
CFPB dashboard month, February 2026, seasonally adjusted originations were up
year over year by 5.3% for auto loans, 13.1% for credit cards, 36.2% for
mortgages, and 142.5% for student loans. The product mix matters: the dashboard
also reports credit-tightness indexes below the January 2010 base of 100 for
auto (81.0), cards (79.0), and mortgages (82.9), while inquiry indexes were
well above that base for auto and cards. These are market indicators, not
household affordability or repayment outcomes.

The risk-tier view prevents the aggregate expansion from becoming a single
consumer story. February credit-card originations rose 24.9% year over year for
the deep-subprime group and 13.8% for the superprime group; auto originations
rose 34.3% for deep subprime while falling 1.0% for prime. A sharp tier change
can reflect access, refinancing, program rules, low bases, or reporting mix,
and does not by itself establish beneficial inclusion or harmful lending.

**Rotation finding:** consumer finance should be tracked as product, inquiry,
underwriting tightness, risk tier, originations, dollar volume, repayment,
delinquency, and household adaptation stages. Market expansion can coexist
with unequal access and unresolved cost; the CFPB data adds the credit-route
screen while leaving individual debt burden, affordability, and welfare open.

## New commerce-interface signal: AI is becoming a shopping intermediary before transaction effects are observable

The record `us-niq-ai-assisted-shopping-agentic-commerce-2026` adds a commercial
consumer-behavior route from NielsenIQ's Agentic Commerce Tracker. NIQ reports
that 51% of U.S. consumers used at least one AI-powered tool to support shopping
in the past month, with AI product recommendations at 20% and personal shopping
assistants at 16%. The release describes adoption as crossing the halfway mark
after growth over the prior two quarters and places AI use across discovery,
comparison, value evaluation, choice narrowing, and final purchase decisions.

The firm-side endpoint is also part of the trend: NIQ says brands and retailers
will need stronger product content, digital discoverability, and structured
product information, and that it will measure agentic commerce as a distinct
channel. But the source is a vendor press release based on approximate monthly
samples of about 500 consumers, with limited public methodology. Reported use
is not transaction share, trust, accuracy, savings, conversion, or autonomous
delegation.

**Rotation finding:** the path to purchase may be reorganized by an AI
intermediary before the atlas can observe realized sales, price effects, or
consumer welfare. The next tests should separate tool exposure, recommendation,
comparison, retailer visibility, purchase, post-purchase recourse, and who
controls the product information and ranking layer.

## New financial-access signal: eligibility is broader than realized private-market participation

The [SEC accredited-investor qualification record](records/us-sec-accredited-investor-qualification-private-market-access-2026.json)
adds an access-and-participation route from the SEC Office of the Investor
Advocate's longitudinal THRIVE research. The SEC estimates that 14.2% of the
U.S. population qualified as accredited investors in January 2026, up from
12.1% in the paired 2024 wave; the net-worth criterion alone covered 12.1% in
the 2026 cross-sectional estimate. Status is dynamic: 21.3% of those
accredited in 2024 no longer qualified in 2026, while 5.3% of those not
accredited in 2024 qualified by 2026.

The participation endpoint is much narrower. Only 5.8% of accredited
investors reported owning private-market securities in 2026, while 11.5%
reported plans to invest in the next 12 months. Persistent accredited
investors reported higher ownership than newly qualifying investors (7.8% vs.
1.8%), and 38.0% of those accredited in both waves used a financial
professional for investment decisions in both years. The paper's paired panel,
weighting, thresholds, and self-reported ownership measures make this a useful
institutional-access signal, not a respondent-level dataset or a causal claim
about wealth inequality.

**Rotation finding:** financial access should be tracked as eligibility,
qualification stability, intermediation, offer exposure, ownership, planned
investment, realized transaction, return, and loss/exit stages. A larger
eligible population can coexist with concentrated participation; the next
tests should distinguish threshold expansion from actual opportunity, advice,
portfolio ownership, and household outcomes.

## New civic-culture signal: public-life engagement is plural, not a single ladder

The [Pew public-life engagement record](records/us-pew-public-life-engagement-groups-2026.json)
adds a cross-domain cultural and political participation route. Pew's
cluster analysis of 19 measures identifies four groups: Mobilizers (9%),
Connectors (28%), Spectators (31%), and Outsiders (31%); the displayed groups
exclude adults not assigned to a group. The distinction is behavioral rather
than simply partisan: Mobilizers and Connectors both report 87% voting in 2024,
but Mobilizers report much more contact with elected officials (65% versus 13%)
and Connectors more nonpolitical donation and group participation. Spectators
follow news relatively closely while acting less publicly, and Outsiders are
low across the measured channels but still include voters and religious
attenders.

The political endpoint is deliberately non-binary. Pew finds that each group
contains Republicans and Democrats rather than one group being owned by one
party. The source is a one-time late-2025 survey window and many measures cover
the prior 12 months, including the 2024 presidential election, so the record
does not treat these as stable identities or a time trend.

**Rotation finding:** civic and political capacity should be tracked across
attention, private discussion, voting, volunteering, donation, group/religious
participation, direct official contact, knowledge, and institutional response.
Party labels alone miss distinct routes into public life; low participation in
one channel can coexist with episodic action or meaningful community activity
elsewhere.

## New comparative geopolitical signal: digital services growth is not translating evenly into trade participation

The [UNCTAD digital-services trade record](records/comparative-unctad-digital-services-trade-participation-2026.json)
adds a cross-border production and rule-making layer. UNCTAD reports that
services accounted for 71% of global intermediate inputs in 2022 and 27% of
global exports in 2025; digitally deliverable services grew 7.1% annually over
the past decade and now represent 56% of global services exports. The growth
is therefore not just a consumer-internet story: services are embedded in
manufacturing, agriculture, logistics, finance, and global value chains.

The distributional endpoint is stark. Least developed countries held 0.6% of
global services exports in 2025, and digitally deliverable services were only
16% of their services exports versus 61% in developed economies. UNCTAD points
to connectivity, cross-border payments, skills, computing capacity, finance,
data, AI expertise, and fragmented digital-trade rules as linked capability
constraints. Since 2020, 90% of developed countries, 62% of developing
countries, and 66% of LDCs participated in agreements containing digital-trade
provisions; participation in a rule-making venue is not the same as export
access or bargaining power.

**Rotation finding:** global digital transformation should be tracked as
inputs, infrastructure, payment friction, skills, firm entry, remote delivery,
export value, domestic capture, trade-rule participation, and AI capability.
Headline services growth can coexist with marginal integration for poorer
countries and with unresolved questions about who captures value.

## New household-cost signal: energy affordability is shaped by bills, buildings, access, and policy design

The [IEA household-energy affordability record](records/comparative-iea-household-energy-affordability-2026.json)
adds a global household-cost and infrastructure route. The IEA estimates that
global household energy bills were still 4% higher in real terms in 2024 than
in 2019 after the 2022 shock, when bills rose 16% year over year. In advanced
economies, more than 120 million households spent over 10% of income on
residential heating, cooling, and appliances; adding private transport pushed
the poorest households' energy share above 20%. Electricity now represents
about 30% of residential energy use globally and household electricity
spending exceeded USD 1 trillion in 2024.

The policy endpoint is uneven targeting. The IEA tracked more than 120 new or
updated demand-side affordability policies across 45 countries in 2025, but
only about one-third specifically targeted households most in need. Access and
upfront-cost constraints remain central: the IEA estimates that 730 million
people lack electricity, around 2 billion lack clean cooking, and 40% of people
without electricity in sub-Saharan Africa may not afford a basic provision.
The companion IEA framework treats affordability as a policy-data feedback
loop spanning drivers, impacts, population groups, indicators, and data rather
than a single price metric.

**Rotation finding:** energy pressure should be tracked across wholesale price,
retail tariff, network charge, tax/subsidy, building efficiency, appliance and
transport choice, access, clean cooking, health, utility finance, and policy
targeting. A lower bill or a new support policy is not by itself evidence of
adequate service, fair distribution, or durable household relief.

## New fiscal-policy signal: tax systems are balancing household room, firm incentives, and public-finance pressure

The [OECD tax-policy reform record](records/comparative-oecd-tax-policy-reforms-fiscal-household-firm-2026.json)
adds the fiscal mechanism behind several household and firm trends. Its 2026
review covers reforms introduced or announced in 2025 across 92 jurisdictions.
The broad direction is mixed: governments used personal-income-tax relief and
more progressive measures to address cost-of-living pressure, while social-
security contributions moved toward broader bases and higher rates in several
countries as demographic and social-protection financing pressures grew.
Revenue-raising measures remained relatively limited even as public debt and
debt-service costs stayed elevated.

The firm-side response is targeted rather than a simple tax-rate race. The
average combined statutory corporate-income-tax rate across 145 jurisdictions
was 21.2% in 2025, versus 28.0% in 2000, while corporate base-narrowing
measures still exceeded base-broadening measures. Countries continued using
incentives for R&D, AI, emerging technologies, defence, and strategic sectors;
more jurisdictions also extended VAT collection to non-resident suppliers and
online platforms. Health-related taxes, especially tobacco and nicotine
excises, were among the common tax-increasing measures.

**Rotation finding:** fiscal policy should be tracked across statutory rate,
effective burden, tax base, transfer/relief, contribution, platform collection,
firm incentive, public revenue, service financing, consumption response, and
household distribution. A reported progressive reform or strategic incentive
does not by itself establish who pays, who benefits, or what behavior changes.

## New global-work signal: headline employment stability is masking a jobs-quality and access deficit

The [ILO global jobs-gap record](records/comparative-ilo-global-jobs-gap-quality-inequality-2026.json)
adds a labour-market layer distinct from country-specific work records. The
ILO projects global unemployment at 4.9% in 2026, but estimates a broader jobs
gap of 408 million people who want paid work and cannot access it. Nearly 300
million workers remain in extreme working poverty and 2.1 billion remain in
informal employment, often without basic rights, social protection, or income
security. In low-income countries employment is projected to grow 3.1% in 2026,
but weak productivity and slow formalisation mean that more jobs do not
necessarily mean better jobs.

The distributional and geopolitical endpoints are also connected. Women account
for about two-fifths of global employment and are 24.2% less likely than men to
participate in the labour force; 20% of young people—about 260 million—are not
in employment, education, or training. Around 465 million jobs are linked to
foreign demand, but trade uncertainty, AI adoption, and sovereign debt could
weaken the ability of trade to improve job quality. The ILO therefore points
toward domestic skills, social protection, and labour-institution capacity as
the next policy route.

**Rotation finding:** work should be tracked across labour demand, access,
employment, formalisation, productivity, pay, protection, bargaining,
household income, trade exposure, and technology-mediated change. Low headline
unemployment is not a sufficient endpoint for decent work or social stability.
