## New intergenerational-risk signal: child climate exposure is a compound hazard and service-capacity problem

The [UNICEF child climate-risk record](records/comparative-unicef-child-climate-hazard-service-exposure-2026.json)
adds an intergenerational and infrastructure-service layer. UNICEF estimates
that 1.1 billion children face at least three overlapping climate hazards; its
component exposure estimates include 1.8 billion children exposed to drought,
1.5 billion to worsening heatwaves, 662 million to tropical storms, and 2.3
billion living where air pollutants are detectable. These are modeled exposure
surfaces, not counts of children already experiencing a specified injury,
displacement, learning loss, or health outcome.

The important mechanism is compounding. UNICEF pairs the Global Child Hazard
Database with a Children’s Climate Risk Framework covering health, WASH,
nutrition, education, protection, and social protection. Drought, fire, air
pollution, floods, damaged schools and hospitals, displacement, and disease can
form a cascading pathway when service capacity is weak. The policy endpoint
therefore includes climate-resilient facilities, food and water security,
shock-responsive protection, and children’s meaningful participation in climate
decisions.

**Rotation finding:** climate risk should be tracked across hazard exposure,
compound sequence, service capacity, displacement, learning, health, food,
WASH, protection, adaptation, and child voice. Counting exposure alone misses
both buffering institutions and the distribution of realized harm.

## New social-health signal: loneliness is a population-level connection gap, not simply an individual mental-health condition

The [WHO social-connection record](records/comparative-who-social-connection-loneliness-2025.json)
adds a relationship and community layer. The WHO Commission estimates that
15.8% of the global population experienced loneliness across its 2014–2023
evidence period, drawing on 23 datasets covering 153 countries and territories.
Estimated prevalence was highest among adolescents (20.9%) and young adults
(17.4%), and was higher in low-income countries (24.3%) than high-income
countries (11%). The source distinguishes loneliness from social isolation and
from clinical diagnosis, so the figures are a social-health signal rather than
a respondent-level mental-health finding.

The mechanism spans health, education, work, income, marginalization, life
transitions, and community institutions. WHO frames social connection as a
public-health priority and points toward cross-sector action, including a 2025
World Health Assembly resolution. The policy response remains open: recognizing
the problem or deploying digital/community programs does not establish durable,
equitable improvement.

**Rotation finding:** social resilience should be tracked across contact,
belonging, subjective loneliness, isolation, school/work participation, health,
community institutions, digital mediation, and policy response. A connection
gap can be widespread without having one cause or one universal remedy.

## New digital-safety signal: platform-mediated child abuse is an exposure, trust, and reporting-system problem

The [UNICEF digital child-safety record](records/comparative-unicef-tech-facilitated-child-abuse-2026.json)
adds a safety and institutional-trust endpoint. UNICEF estimates that almost
one in five surveyed internet-using children aged 12–17 across 21 countries
experienced at least one form of technology-facilitated sexual exploitation or
abuse in a single year. The study estimates more than 15 million exposed to
unwanted sexual content, 9 million asked to engage in sexual conversations or
share images against their will, and 4 million having sexual images shared
without consent. Around 60% of cases occurred on mainstream social platforms
and 14% in online games.

The pathway is not simply stranger danger or platform use. UNICEF reports that
57% of cases involved someone the child already knew, while 38% first
encountered the perpetrator online. More than 40% of cases were never shared
with anyone and fewer than 1% were reported to authorities. The report calls
for safer-by-design platforms, trusted reporting and support systems, stronger
regulation, and shifting protection burdens away from children. Its prevalence
and mental-health findings remain bounded to internet-using children and
reported associations.

**Rotation finding:** digital safety should be tracked across access, platform
features, contact, exploitation, disclosure, reporting, service response,
recourse, child trust, and institutional accountability. Platform exposure is
not itself a causal explanation, and low official reporting is not proof that
no informal disclosure or support occurred.

## New displacement-livelihood signal: integration depends on labor absorption and shared service capacity

The [World Bank Kenya refugee-host livelihoods record](records/comparative-world-bank-kenya-refugee-host-livelihoods-2026.json)
adds a comparative migration and local-economy route. A two-wave panel study
summarized by the World Bank follows refugee and host households as aid tightens
and Kenya shifts toward integrated settlements. Employment among camp-based
refugees fell from 13% to 7% between waves, while urban refugee employment fell
from 50% to 37%. In camp settings, 89% were inactive in the second wave and 64%
of those citing no available jobs rose from 41% previously. Host employment near
camps remained low, edging from 35% to 33%.

The mechanism is shared absorptive capacity, not simply refugee presence. Aid
accounted for about three quarters of camp-based refugee revenue in 2024; the
source reports food reduction by 64% of camp households and credit use for food
by 44%. Refugees and hosts also rely on the same constrained schools, clinics,
roads, water systems, and thin labor markets. These are descriptive, bounded
Kenya findings—not a causal estimate of aid cuts or a verdict on the Shirika
Plan—but they create a reusable cross-source test: whether integration policy
is accompanied by work authorization, mobility, childcare, private investment,
shock-responsive support, and enough local service capacity to prevent shared
scarcity from becoming social fracture.

**Rotation finding:** displacement should be tracked across legal status,
mobility, aid, work access, household income, food coping, shared services,
host-market absorption, gendered care constraints, and social cohesion. Formal
integration is not the same as economic inclusion or durable self-reliance.

## New geopolitical signal: perceived risk is moving faster than perceived multilateral capacity

The [UN Global Risk Report record](records/comparative-un-global-risk-perception-multilateral-capacity-2026.json)
adds a global institutional-perception layer. The 2026 exercise drew on
approximately 1,300 stakeholders across 155 countries and assessed 28 risks.
Large-scale war moved toward the center of the risk map, with 36% expecting it
within one year; concern about AI and frontier-technology power concentration
also rose. Climate-change inaction nevertheless remained the most important
risk overall, and joint government action remained the response viewed as most
effective.

The useful mechanism is the gap between salience and capacity. The UN frames
global vulnerability as a difference between how important a risk appears and
how capable multilateral institutions are perceived to be in responding. This
is not an event forecast or an audit of institutional performance. It is a
cross-source bridge for testing whether geopolitical attention, technology
concern, climate exposure, and economic stress are matched by coordination,
preparedness, implementation, and credible public or state response.

**Rotation finding:** geopolitical change should be tracked across risk
salience, hazard realization, preparedness, coordination, institutional
capacity, implementation, legitimacy, and response feedback. A risk moving to
the center of attention is not the same as a changed probability or a closed
governance gap.

## New social-cohesion signal: insecurity and inequality are becoming trust and collective-action constraints

The [UN World Social Report trust and cohesion record](records/comparative-un-world-social-report-trust-cohesion-2025.json)
adds a cultural-social route that is broader than loneliness or civic
participation alone. The report synthesizes evidence that more than half of the
global population has little or no trust in government, fewer than 30% believe
other people can be trusted, and more than a third live on between $2.15 and
$6.85 per day. It also reports a decline in government trust across successive
birth cohorts and growing inequality in much of the world.

The mechanism is not simply an attitudinal mood. The report defines cohesion
through cooperation, fairness and inclusion, belonging, and trust, and links
economic insecurity, public-service delivery, social protection, perceived
inequality, and mis/disinformation to the ability to act collectively. The
figures come from different surveys and country evidence and should not be
collapsed into one global trust index or causal estimate. They do add a useful
cross-source test: whether material security, fair institutional treatment,
credible information, and local solidarity reinforce one another—or whether
insecurity converts into distrust, polarization, and policy paralysis.

**Rotation finding:** social cohesion should be tracked across material
security, inequality, service delivery, institutional legitimacy, interpersonal
trust, belonging, information environments, participation, and collective
action. Low generalized trust does not prove the absence of strong local or
familial ties, and policy recognition is not a verified repair.

## New housing-market signal: digital rental search can convert access pressure into upfront loss and identity exposure

The [FTC rental-scam record](records/us-ftc-rental-scam-housing-platform-losses-2020-2025.json)
adds a concrete housing-search and platform-recourse endpoint. The FTC
identified nearly 65,000 likely rental-scam reports from January 2020 through
June 2025, with about $65 million in reported losses and a $1,000 median
reported loss. In the most recent year, about half of platform-specified
reports said the scam began with a fake Facebook ad and 16% with Craigslist.
The reported mechanisms include copied or fabricated listings, below-market
prices, upfront deposits or fees, fake credit-check subscriptions, and early
collection of identity documents.

The age gradient matters as a housing-access signal: among reports from July
2024 through June 2025, 46% of 18–29-year-old reports indicated monetary loss,
compared with 23% for ages 30–39 and 13% for ages 40–49. These are normalized
reporting patterns, not prevalence or causal age effects; the FTC notes that
most scams are never reported. The distinct cross-source test is whether
housing scarcity, urgency, platform ranking, identity verification, payment
timing, and recourse combine to redistribute risk toward people with fewer
alternatives.

**Rotation finding:** housing access should be tracked across search, listing
visibility, verification, urgency, upfront payment, identity exposure, loss,
reporting, recovery, landlord/platform responsibility, and ability to switch.
An attractive listing or a complaint route is not the same as a safe home or
restored financial room.

## New creative-economy signal: digital cultural production is expanding while value capture and participation remain uneven

The [UNCTAD creative-economy record](records/comparative-unctad-creative-economy-digital-cultural-trade-2024.json)
adds a cultural-production and market-structure route. UNCTAD reports that the
developing-country share of global creative-goods and services exports rose
from 10% in 2010 to 20% in 2022. But the leading creative-services categories
were software (41.3%), research and development (30%), advertising/market
research/architecture (15.5%), and audiovisual services (7.9%), while cultural,
recreational, and heritage services were 0.6%. This is a reminder that
“creative economy” growth can mean digitally mediated business services as well
as cultural production.

Digital distribution also changes the consumer and firm endpoint. Streaming
accounted for 67.3% of global music revenue streams in the cited 2022 data, and
the report describes AI as altering creation, distribution, and consumption,
including use in newsrooms. These shares do not establish worker displacement,
creator autonomy, audience welfare, or equitable cultural representation. They
do create a cross-source test: whether digital scale broadens participation and
market access, or shifts discovery, rights, income, and cultural visibility
toward a smaller set of platforms and high-capacity firms.

**Rotation finding:** culture should be tracked across creation, access,
distribution, platform mediation, intellectual property, worker bargaining,
consumer payment, local identity, export concentration, and who captures the
economic and symbolic value.

## New tourism signal: travel demand has recovered, but place capacity and geopolitical resilience determine who benefits

The [OECD tourism trends record](records/comparative-oecd-tourism-demand-community-resilience-2026.json)
adds a movement, destination, and host-community route. International arrivals
to OECD countries rose 8.1% to 819.5 million in 2024, with tourism directly
accounting for 4.0% of GDP and 19.3% of services exports; arrivals were
estimated to grow another 3.4% in 2025. The rebound is economically important,
but the OECD describes concentrated visitor flows, infrastructure and community
pressures, extreme-weather risk, and the early-2026 Middle East conflict as
forces reshaping safety, affordability, transport, and demand.

The U.S. profile shows the same split between scale and exposure: tourism
directly represented 3.0% of U.S. GVA and 4.0% of employment in 2023, while
2025 international arrivals fell 5.5% from 2024 to 68.3 million—86% of the
2019 level—even as domestic travel reached an estimated 2.4 billion person-
trips. These are sector and trip measures, not resident sentiment or individual
travel motives. They add a cross-source test: whether tourism growth is matched
by transport, housing, water, labor, community voice, crisis preparedness, and
local value retention rather than merely higher arrival counts.

**Rotation finding:** mobility and place should be tracked across demand,
affordability, connectivity, visitor concentration, housing and infrastructure,
worker and host-community benefits, environmental pressure, safety, shocks,
governance, and distribution of local value.

## New firm-finance signal: SME credit is recovering unevenly while long-term investment capacity remains constrained

The [OECD SME-finance record](records/comparative-oecd-sme-finance-investment-2026.json)
adds a firm-side liquidity and investment route. Across the 2026 Scoreboard,
34 of 39 countries had SME interest rates above pre-pandemic levels, 10 of 17
showed higher shares of SMEs requiring collateral, and the outstanding SME-loan
stock was broadly stagnant. Aggregate real long-term SME-loan volumes in the
selected country group fell 11% since 2021, while venture-capital growth was
reported in 18 of 30 countries but concentrated in a limited number of large
AI deals.

The implication is not simply “small firms cannot borrow.” New lending can
recover while stock stays flat because short-term loans are repaid; firms can
also use internal funds, leasing, factoring, equity, guarantees, or fintech
finance. The distinct cross-source test is whether the available instrument
supports working capital only or enables durable investment, digitalisation,
skills, green transition, resilience, succession, and worker stability—and
which firms or regions receive patient capital versus short-term survival
liquidity.

**Rotation finding:** firm capacity should be tracked across loan access,
collateral, cost, maturity, liquidity, investment, alternative finance,
public guarantees, ownership, technology adoption, worker outcomes, regional
concentration, and long-term survival.

## New education signal: access expansion is real, but exclusion and equity-system capacity are falling behind the target clock

The [UNESCO 2026 GEM education record](records/comparative-unesco-gem-education-access-equity-2026.json)
adds a human-capital and public-system capacity route. Global primary and
secondary enrollment reached 1.4 billion in 2024, up 30% or 327 million from
2000, while completion also improved. Yet 273 million children, adolescents,
and youth remained out of school in 2024, the out-of-school population rose 3%
since 2015, and at current expansion rates the world would reach 95% upper-
secondary completion only in 2105.

The bottleneck is not only demand or school places. UNESCO reports that one in
three countries does not report urban-rural disparity and one in two does not
report wealth disparity; fewer than one in ten countries have a sufficiently
strong intended equity-policy focus. Inclusive-education laws and financing
mechanisms have expanded, but target-setting, measurement, transportation,
technology, health, nutrition, and institutional follow-through determine who
can convert formal access into timely completion and future capability. These
are global system indicators, not classroom or individual learning outcomes.

**Rotation finding:** education should be tracked across access, timing,
completion, learning, equity, financing, transport, digital infrastructure,
health and nutrition, teacher capacity, data visibility, household demand,
and later work and civic capability. Enrollment is not the same as timely,
equitable, or meaningful completion.

## New gendered-opportunity signal: equal laws are advancing faster than the systems that make them usable

The [World Bank Women, Business and the Law record](records/comparative-world-bank-women-business-law-2026.json)
adds a legal-rights-to-practice route. Across 190 economies, women hold about
67% of men's measured legal rights; fewer than half of the supportive policies
and institutions needed to make those rights effective are in place, and legal
experts rate enforcement at about 53 out of 100. Only 4% of women live in
economies close to full legal equality, even though 68 economies enacted 113
strengthening reforms between 2023 and 2025.

The implementation gap is the useful mechanism. The framework covers safety,
mobility, work, pay, marriage, parenthood, childcare, entrepreneurship,
assets, and pensions, so formal rights can be connected to time, care,
household room, firm participation, and later security. The U.S. profile scores
legal frameworks above supportive frameworks and enforcement perceptions and
reports no reforms in the measured topics during the covered interval. These
are institutional diagnostics, not individual women's outcomes or causal
estimates; they create a cross-source test of whether legal recognition is
translated into accessible services, safe mobility, finance, childcare, work,
business growth, and retirement security.

**Rotation finding:** gender inequality should be tracked across law, policy
support, enforcement, safety, mobility, care, pay, assets, entrepreneurship,
work, household bargaining, and durable economic security. A right on paper is
not the same as a usable option.

## New health-access signal: coverage gains still leave care costs large enough to crowd out household consumption

The [WHO/World Bank universal-health-coverage record](records/comparative-who-universal-health-coverage-financial-protection-2025.json)
adds a global public-system and household-affordability route. The UHC service
coverage index rose from 54 in 2000 to 71 in 2023, but WHO estimates that 4.6
billion people still lacked coverage for essential health services in 2023.
Financial protection also improved: the share experiencing large or
impoverishing out-of-pocket health-payment hardship fell from 34% in 2000 to
26% in 2022. Even so, 2.1 billion people were estimated to face that hardship
in 2022.

The important mechanism is the conversion of formal or nominal access into
usable care and remaining household room. The revised financial-hardship
measure explicitly treats health spending as harmful when it exceeds 40% of a
household's discretionary budget, linking care access to medicines, food,
housing, work, debt, and other consumption choices. These are population and
system indicators, not respondent-level outcomes or individual causal effects.

**Rotation finding:** health-system pressure should be tracked across service
coverage, primary-care capacity, medicines, timeliness, quality, public
financing, out-of-pocket exposure, household consumption displacement,
inequality, and crisis resilience. Coverage improvement is not the same as
affordable, timely, effective care.

## New social-protection signal: changing work is exposing gaps in who is covered, what protection is adequate, and who pays for resilience

The [ILO universal social-protection record](records/comparative-ilo-universal-social-protection-changing-labour-markets-2026.json)
adds an institutional coverage-and-financing route. The ILO identifies
persistent protection gaps for temporary, part-time, self-employed, domestic,
agricultural, micro- and small-enterprise, and other workers in complex
employment arrangements. It also separates coverage from adequacy: being
formally included does not guarantee sufficient protection through unemployment,
illness, disability, parenthood, old age, or job and sector transitions.

The second mechanism is resilience architecture. The paper links social-security
contributions, progressive taxation, public subsidies, and—where fiscal
capacity is insufficient—international solidarity to the ability of workers,
enterprises, and households to absorb climate, demographic, technological, and
economic shocks. This is a policy synthesis and institutional mechanism map,
not respondent-level take-up data or a causal estimate of benefits on later
consumption, work, health, or political behavior.

**Rotation finding:** social protection should be tracked across legal coverage,
actual reach, benefit adequacy, portability, financing, administration,
employment transitions, care, health, disability, old age, climate exposure,
informality, and household security. Coverage is not the same as usable
protection, and financing design determines who can sustain it.

## New diaspora-consumption signal: cross-border transfers are large household infrastructure, and payment competition determines how much survives the journey

The [World Bank remittance-cost record](records/comparative-world-bank-remittance-costs-fast-payments-2026.json)
adds a cross-border household-transfer and digital-payment route. The World
Bank estimates global remittance flows at $856 billion in 2024, including $653
billion to low- and middle-income countries. Average sending costs remain
around 6%, and only about half of corridors record average costs below 5%.
These flows can support everyday consumption, school fees, housing repair,
savings, small business, and local resilience, while also making recipient
communities sensitive to destination-country labour demand, migration policy,
exchange rates, and payment access.

The infrastructure mechanism is distinct: World Bank-summarized research finds
that a domestic fast-payment system is associated with a 0.3-to-1 percentage-
point reduction in the cost of a $200 remittance, primarily through competition.
That is an institutional and market-architecture signal, not proof that every
recipient receives more, spends differently, or becomes more secure. Access,
identity, interoperability, connectivity, consumer protection, and provider
competition determine whether cheaper rails become usable transfers.

**Rotation finding:** migration should be tracked across mobility, labour
demand, remittance volume, fees, exchange-rate spreads, payment speed,
digital access, recipient consumption, savings, enterprise, housing, education,
diaspora institutions, and policy shocks. A large transfer flow is not the
same as low-cost, reliable, or evenly distributed household support.

## New national-meaning signal: people assemble pride from culture, services, political institutions, and economic conditions

The [Pew national-pride record](records/comparative-pew-national-pride-culture-services-politics-2026.json)
adds a comparative cultural-legitimacy route. Pew asked 33,486 adults in 25
countries an open-ended question about what makes them proud of their country.
In the United States, 22% mentioned freedom while 20% said they were not proud;
in the United Kingdom, 29% said they were not proud. Sweden provides a useful
institutional contrast: 53% mentioned the way the country is governed as a
source of pride.

The public-system mechanism is especially useful for cross-source work. Pride
mentions include democracy, freedom, welfare, health care, education,
infrastructure, safety, and social programs, while criticism includes
leadership, democratic backsliding, prices, wages, debt, jobs, immigration,
and international affairs. Among Spaniards, 24% of those satisfied with
democracy mentioned government as a source of pride versus 5% of those
dissatisfied. These are coded public-attitude aggregates, not individual
identity forecasts or causal evidence that services produce pride or pride
produces political action.

**Rotation finding:** cultural legitimacy should be tracked across pride,
belonging, morality, public services, material conditions, political
performance, freedom, equality, immigration, international standing, age,
ideology, party alignment, and reform demand. National identity is a bundle of
experiences and institutions, not a single stable attitude.

## New food-consumption signal: healthy diets are constrained by value-chain costs and household affordability, not simply by aggregate food supply

The [FAO healthy-diet-cost record](records/comparative-fao-healthy-diet-cost-food-security-2026.json)
adds a food-system and everyday-consumption route. FAO estimates that 645
million people faced hunger in 2025, while about 2.7 billion people could not
afford a healthy diet. The distinction matters: hunger, dietary adequacy, and
the economic ability to purchase diverse nutritious foods are related but not
identical conditions.

FAO reports a healthy-diet cost of $4.28 per person per day in 2021 PPP,
roughly 25% higher than five years earlier. The cost is shaped after the farm
through storage, processing, transport, energy, retail, cold chains, market
information, and food loss, while nutrient-dense foods often cost more than
cheap calorie sources. That creates a cross-source bridge between food prices,
household room, infrastructure, cultural fit, health, work capacity, and
agrifood market power. These are global system estimates, not individual diet
histories or causal nutrition outcomes.

**Rotation finding:** food security should be tracked across hunger, healthy-
diet affordability, incomes, non-food expenses, food preferences, cultural fit,
retail prices, logistics, energy, cold chains, trade, climate, conflict,
market concentration, subsidies, and later health and household adaptation.
Food availability is not the same as affordable nutrition.

## New geopolitical-supply signal: trade integration is both a resilience asset and a shock-transmission network

The [FAO agricultural-commodity-market record](records/comparative-fao-agricultural-commodity-trade-shock-transmission-2026.json)
adds an upstream geopolitical and market-network route. FAO reports that the
value of food and agricultural trade increased fivefold between 2000 and 2024,
with low- and middle-income countries more integrated into global markets.
That integration can diversify supply and create adjustment options, but it
also exposes domestic food systems to extreme weather, conflict, pandemics,
financial crises, macroeconomic shocks, and logistics disruptions.

The key mechanism is transmission rather than a simple “globalization good or
bad” judgment. FAO background analysis says shocks can spread across countries
and commodities, reduce trade connectivity, and create caloric deficits where
import sources are concentrated. International shocks affect domestic markets
unevenly because trade openness, market structure, and policy environments
shape the scale and persistence of price effects. Export restrictions,
import-promotion, trade agreements, reserves, and price stabilization can
dampen or amplify those effects. This is network and policy evidence, not a
country-specific price forecast or household causal estimate.

**Rotation finding:** geopolitical exposure should be tracked across trade
connectivity, import concentration, commodity substitution, logistics,
fertilizer and energy inputs, price transmission, reserves, export controls,
trade agreements, climate, conflict, domestic market structure, and household
food security. Integration is not the same as resilience.

## New displacement-governance signal: forced movement has eased from its peak while return and reintegration remain high-risk institutional processes

The [UNHCR Global Trends record](records/comparative-unhcr-global-trends-forced-displacement-2025-2026.json)
adds a global displacement, hosting, and return route. At the end of 2025,
UNHCR counted about 117.8 million forcibly displaced people, including 41.6
million refugees, 9 million asylum-seekers, and 68.7 million people internally
displaced by conflict or violence. The aggregate total declined from 2024, but
the scale remains equivalent to roughly 1.4% of the world's population.

The useful mechanism is not only movement out of danger; it is the institutional
path after movement. UNHCR reports 14.7 million returns in 2025, while 68% of
refugees and other people needing international protection were hosted in
low- and middle-income countries and 65% lived in neighboring countries.
Returns can occur amid damaged homes, degraded infrastructure, weak services,
limited economic opportunity, or adverse host-country policies. This creates a
cross-source bridge from conflict and governance to housing, education, health,
work, food, local host capacity, and political legitimacy. These are population
statistics, not individual trajectory or voluntariness evidence.

**Rotation finding:** displacement should be tracked across origin shocks,
route governance, legal status, host concentration, service capacity, duration,
returns, reintegration, re-displacement, household assets, education, work,
health, gender, disability, and host-community politics. A return count is not
the same as a safe or durable solution.

## New mobility-governance signal: international movement is limited in aggregate, but pathway design and climate adaptation redistribute risk

The [IOM World Migration Report 2026 record](records/comparative-iom-world-migration-report-2026-mobility-pathways-climate.json)
complements the UNHCR displacement stock with a broader mobility and governance
route. IOM estimates about 304 million international migrants at mid-2024 and
more than 83 million people in internal displacement at the end of 2024. Most
people still live in their country of birth, so the global migrant stock should
not be mistaken for universal or accelerating cross-border movement.

The institutional mechanism is pathway selection. IOM describes regular routes
as increasingly selective and unequally distributed across regions and groups;
restrictive policies can shift mobility into riskier and more precarious
journeys rather than eliminate it. Its climate-mobility analysis links sudden
disasters and slow-onset degradation to migration while criticizing reactive
responses detached from long-term adaptation. Local governments, communities,
Indigenous peoples, diasporas, civil society, and private actors become part of
the response architecture. These are population and policy findings, not
individual route histories or causal estimates.

**Rotation finding:** mobility should be tracked across migrant stocks, routes,
legal status, visa selectivity, labour demand, remittances, climate exposure,
adaptation, local governance, social protection, regularization, risk transfer,
internal displacement, and development outcomes. Restriction is not the same
as reduced mobility; it can change who bears the risk.

## New digital-dependence signal: cyber resilience is becoming an uneven public, economic, and geopolitical capability

The [WEF Global Cybersecurity Outlook record](records/comparative-wef-global-cybersecurity-outlook-ai-geopolitics-2026.json)
adds a digital-infrastructure and cyber-inequity route. In the 2026 leader
survey, 87% identified AI-related vulnerabilities as the fastest-growing cyber
risk, while the share reporting security assessment of AI tools before
deployment rose from 37% to 64%. Geopolitics still influenced cybersecurity
strategy for 66% of organizations.

The resilience gap is the more durable mechanism. Sixty-four percent reported
meeting minimum cyber-resilience requirements, but only 19% said resilience
exceeded requirements; 54% identified limited knowledge and skills as an
obstacle to AI-driven cybersecurity. The report links uneven resources,
infrastructure, governance, organization size, and supply-chain dependence to
systemic exposure. A local breach, cloud outage, or supplier weakness can
cascade through retail, manufacturing, aviation, public administration,
finance, and daily services. These are leader-survey and systems indicators,
not incident counts or individual cyber-harm estimates.

**Rotation finding:** digital dependence should be tracked across AI adoption,
predeployment governance, cyber skills, baseline versus excess resilience,
supply-chain exposure, cloud and cable dependence, geopolitical sovereignty,
fraud, public-service continuity, organization size, sector, and consumer
trust. Connectivity is not the same as resilience.

## New digital-social signal: the wellbeing effects of internet use depend on trust, offline activity, generation, gender, and peer saturation

The [World Happiness Report Chapter 8 record](records/comparative-world-happiness-social-media-trust-generations-2026.json)
adds a distinct social-connection mechanism to the existing platform-exit
evidence. Using four European Social Survey rounds across 30 countries from
2016–2024 and regional internet speed as an instrument, the chapter reports a
negative internet-wellbeing gradient strongest for Gen Z, moderate for
Millennials, near zero for Gen X, and slightly positive for Baby Boomers.
Younger Europeans—especially Gen Z and Millennial women—also experienced the
largest deterioration in trust, perceived social activity, and meeting
frequency. Trust, friendship interaction, perceived safety, and attachment to
country or Europe are positively related to wellbeing.

The important moderation route is social environment. Internet use appears more
beneficial in low-saturation peer environments and increasingly harmful as
social-media use becomes widespread among peers; the chapter also reports
lower wellbeing among people who post political views online. These results
remain heterogeneous by platform, region, generation, gender, and social
connection, and they do not establish a universal technology effect or predict
an individual's mental health or political behavior.

**Rotation finding:** digital sociality should be tracked across exposure,
platform design, peer saturation, trust, offline meetings, perceived activity,
community safety, attachment, political expression, age, gender, region,
income, and substitution or complementarity with real-world relationships.
Connectivity is not the same as connection.

## New water-security signal: infrastructure scarcity becomes a gendered time, safety, health, and governance burden

The [UN World Water Development Report 2026 record](records/comparative-un-water-development-gender-access-2026.json)
adds a global water-and-equal-opportunity route. UN-Water reports that 2.1
billion people still lack safely managed drinking water, while women and girls
spend an estimated 250 million hours each day collecting water. In more than
80% of rural households without direct access, women are responsible for
collection. The downstream mechanism spans health, education, livelihoods,
safety, food security, and lost time for paid, civic, and social activity.

The governance side is equally important: fewer than 20% of formal water-
utility workers worldwide are women, and women remain underrepresented in
water governance, financing, leadership, technical roles, and infrastructure
decisions. Climate change, scarcity, floods, droughts, and disasters amplify
these inequalities. This is a global system and institutional signal, not
individual time-use or causal health data.

**Rotation finding:** water security should be tracked across safely managed
access, collection time, sanitation, safety, health, education, livelihoods,
gender, rurality, climate exposure, infrastructure reliability, affordability,
utility workforce, decision power, financing, and community governance.
Availability is not the same as equal access or usable time.

## New human-development AI signal: public expectations center augmentation, but access and agency decide who benefits

The [UNDP Human Development Report 2025 AI record](records/comparative-undp-human-development-ai-agency-2025.json)
adds a broad human-development and technology-expectations route. UNDP's
21-country global survey reports that 60% expect AI to affect employment
positively by creating opportunities in jobs that may not exist today, while
50% expect their current job could be automated; only 13% fear AI could lead to
job losses. In lower-human-development countries, about two-thirds anticipate
using AI in education, health, or work within a year, and 70% in low- and
medium-HDI countries expect productivity gains.

The useful mechanism is not respondent-level prediction. It is the bridge from
public expectation to development choice: AI may augment work and personalize
health or education, but online access, skills, affordability, local-language
fit, service capacity, and human agency determine whether that possibility
becomes usable capability. This complements the government-AI governance and
cyber-resilience records without claiming that optimism is adoption, job
creation, or improved outcomes.

**Rotation finding:** AI diffusion should be tracked across augmentation,
automation, productivity, connectivity, electricity, skills, language fit,
health, education, work quality, institutional accountability, human agency,
and unequal access. Expectations are not outcomes, and access is not agency.

## New social-connection signal: thinner face-to-face contact is becoming a structural and civic-capacity concern

The [OECD social-connections record](records/comparative-oecd-social-connections-loneliness-2025.json)
adds a comparative OECD route distinct from the WHO global prevalence record
and the World Happiness Report's internet-wellbeing analysis. Across 21
European OECD countries, daily in-person interaction with friends and family
declined between 2006, 2015, and 2022 while daily remote contact increased. The
share reporting never feeling lonely fell from 59% in 2018 to 51% in 2022, with
the youngest group showing a 14-point decline.

The policy mechanism is broader than loneliness as an individual condition.
OECD reports that low income and unemployment are associated with roughly twice
the likelihood of loneliness, while libraries, parks, community organisations,
commercial gathering places, and safe online spaces are possible social
infrastructure routes. This creates a public-capacity and civic-participation
question: social connection can support health, work, education, and civic
engagement, but policy response remains under-evaluated and strong aggregate
connection can conceal unequal relationship quality.

**Rotation finding:** social connection should be tracked across face-to-face
contact, remote contact, loneliness, support, relationship quality, age, gender,
income, unemployment, living arrangement, place, digital mediation, social
infrastructure, health, work, education, and civic participation. Contact is
not the same as support, and policy attention is not proof of repair.

## New child-digital signal: near-universal access shifts the question from connection to design, exposure, and protection

The [OECD child digital-wellbeing record](records/comparative-oecd-children-digital-wellbeing-2025.json)
adds a youth and platform-governance route. In OECD comparisons, 96% of
15-year-olds had a desktop, laptop, or tablet at home and 98% had a smartphone
with internet access in 2022; at least half of 15-year-olds in most countries
spent 30 or more hours per week on digital devices. About 10% of adolescents
reported problematic social-media use in 2021–22, up from less than 7% in
2017–18, with higher reported rates among girls and adolescents with a migrant
background.

The mechanism is not “screen time causes harm.” OECD emphasizes activity type,
platform design, passive versus active engagement, social comparison,
cyberbullying, family context, offline vulnerability, and child voice in policy.
That makes digital childhood a consumer-protection and institutional-capacity
question: when access is ordinary, safety, age assurance, accountability,
rights, and usable recourse determine whether children are protected or merely
connected.

**Rotation finding:** child digital life should be tracked across access,
device time, activity type, social comparison, harmful content, problematic use,
gender, migration, socio-economic status, family context, platform design, age
assurance, parental and school support, child voice, safety, and offline
well-being. Access is not empowerment, and exposure is not diagnosis.

## New platform-governance signal: age limits exist more often than age assurance works at account creation

The [OECD age-assurance record](records/comparative-oecd-child-age-assurance-services-2025.json)
adds a service-level implementation endpoint to the child-digital map. In an
OECD review of 50 services used by children, only two systematically assured
age at account creation, although 26 had some age-assurance mechanism. The
remaining gap is not simply a missing rule: minimum ages often arise from
privacy or contract law rather than safety, vary by geography, and age-tiered
safeguards can be optional or dependent on parental controls.

This makes platform protection a cross-border governance and consumer-recourse
problem. A stated minimum age is not the same as knowing who is using a service;
age assurance can also create privacy, bias, exclusion, and data-minimisation
trade-offs. The source is a public service benchmark, not a test of individual
children, enforcement accuracy, or actual harm.

**Rotation finding:** child online safety should be tracked across stated age
rules, systematic assurance, trigger conditions, age tiers, parental controls,
privacy, bias, geography, platform design, reporting, moderation, remedy, and
regulatory enforceability. A rule is not implementation, and implementation is
not safety.

## New democratic-resilience signal: institutional quality is weakening unevenly while participation remains a separate capacity

The [International IDEA democracy-and-migration record](records/comparative-idea-global-democracy-migration-participation-2025.json)
adds a global political-institution route. Across the 173 countries with 2024
data, Freedom of the Press declined in 43 countries, or 24.9% of those covered.
Participation was comparatively stable, but only 11 countries had notable
changes between 2019 and 2024: nine declines and two advances. Civic Engagement
and Civil Society together recorded 22 declines against five advances.

The migration endpoint is institutional inclusion rather than migrant opinion.
International IDEA reports that out-of-country voting can strengthen democratic
resilience in home and host countries, but diaspora turnout is relatively low
and policy and administrative arrangements are often inadequate. This keeps
press freedom, rule of law, participation, belonging, and voting access as
separate surfaces rather than one democracy score.

**Rotation finding:** democratic resilience should be tracked across media
freedom, expression, courts, electoral integrity, civil society, civic
engagement, turnout, migrant status, registration, out-of-country voting,
administrative access, belonging, and institutional response. Stability in one
pillar is not repair of another.

## New social-consumption signal: drug-use demand and synthetic-market adaptation are outrunning equitable treatment access

The [UNODC World Drug Report 2025 record](records/comparative-unodc-world-drug-report-2025-markets-treatment.json)
adds a global social-consumption and public-health-capacity route. UNODC
estimates that 316 million people aged 15–64 used a drug excluding alcohol and
tobacco in 2023, about 6% of that population versus 5.2% in 2013. Cannabis
accounted for 244 million users, cocaine use reached 25 million, and potential
cocaine manufacture reached 3,708 tons; synthetic stimulant seizures also hit a
record level.

The more consequential inequality signal is the conversion bottleneck. Of an
estimated 64 million people with drug use disorders, only about 8.1% received
treatment; the reported ratio was one in 18 women versus one in seven men.
Service coverage also varies sharply by region and can be concentrated in
urban areas or capitals. This joins market change to gender, stigma, childcare,
distance, capacity, and public-health response without treating use as a
disorder or seizures as consumption.

**Rotation finding:** drug trends should be tracked across prevalence, substance
mix, synthetic production, trafficking, conflict, price, criminalization,
treatment, harm reduction, gender, age, geography, stigma, childcare, service
capacity, and recovery. Market growth is not diagnosis, and treatment contact is
not recovery.

## New macro-capacity signal: sovereign debt is becoming a public-service and everyday-development constraint

The [UNCTAD debt record](records/comparative-unctad-world-debt-public-capacity-2025.json)
adds a macro-finance-to-public-capacity route. Global public debt reached $102
trillion in 2024, including $31 trillion in developing countries. Developing
countries paid $921 billion in public-debt interest, and 61 allocated at least
10% of government revenue to interest payments. UNCTAD estimates that 3.4
billion people live in countries where interest payments exceed spending on
either health or education.

The external-finance mechanism is also unequal: developing regions have been
borrowing at average rates two to four times those of the United States since
2020; external public-debt service reached $487 billion in 2023, and developing
countries paid $25 billion more to external creditors than they received in new
disbursements. This does not prove that debt alone caused a household or service
loss, but it makes fiscal room, public investment, health, education,
infrastructure, and political legitimacy part of the same observable pressure
system.

**Rotation finding:** debt pressure should be tracked across borrowing costs,
interest, revenue, exports, aid, liquidity, restructuring, public services,
health, education, infrastructure, climate investment, household exposure, and
political voice. Aggregate debt is not household incidence, but fiscal space is
not abstract when it governs public capacity.

## New consumer-market signal: digital choice architecture is turning convenience into a route for financial, privacy, and time detriment

The [OECD dark-patterns record](records/comparative-oecd-dark-patterns-consumer-choice-2024.json)
adds a consumer-choice and firm-conduct route distinct from scam-loss records.
Preliminary OECD survey findings across 20 countries report that 90% of
consumers encountered online manipulative design techniques. The relevant
exposure is not just advertising: defaults, false urgency, misleading social
proof, forced disclosure, hidden fees, and hard-to-cancel services can steer
choices, extract data, or consume time. OECD also cites earlier evidence that
50% of online consumers encountered a shopping problem in the prior year and
that cumulative severe e-commerce detriment exceeded $22 billion in the cited
study.

The institutional mechanism is recourse and market integrity. More information
alone may not overcome choice architecture, especially for children, less
educated consumers, or people facing financial and digital vulnerability. But
encountering a dark pattern is not proof of loss, deception, intent, or legal
liability; the next test is to connect interface exposure to payment, privacy,
cancellation, switching, remedy, trust, and durable consumer behavior.

**Rotation finding:** consumer power should be tracked across defaults, fees,
urgency, data disclosure, personalization, device, literacy, age, income,
privacy, payment, cancellation, switching, enforcement, remedy, and trust.
Choice is not autonomy when exit is costly.

## New resource-consumption signal: rising material demand is an unequal infrastructure and provisioning problem, not just a climate statistic

The [UNEP Global Resources Outlook record](records/comparative-unep-global-resources-consumption-2024.json)
adds a material-consumption and planetary-capacity route. Global material
extraction rose from 30 billion tonnes in 1970 to 106.6 billion tonnes in 2024,
while per-capita demand rose from 8.4 to 13.2 tonnes. High-income countries
consume materials at roughly six times the rate of low-income countries, and
built environment, mobility, food, and energy together account for about 90% of
global material demand.

The household-facing mechanism is provisioning. Under the Historical Trends
scenario, extraction could reach roughly 160% of its 2020 level by 2060, with
food-and-fibre biomass around 180% and net greenhouse-gas emissions above 120%.
UNEP also describes a Sustainability Transition pathway in which resource
pressures and inequalities fall, but that outcome depends on infrastructure,
trade, finance, circularity, public services, and social acceptance—not
individual restraint alone.

**Rotation finding:** resource pressure should be tracked across extraction,
material footprints, trade, housing, mobility, food, energy, waste, pollution,
income, infrastructure, affordability, local extraction, circularity, public
provisioning, transition costs, and equity. Aggregate efficiency is not local
justice, and consumption is not a household moral category.

## New work-health signal: psychosocial risk is a work-design and public-capacity problem

The [ILO psychosocial-working-environment record](records/comparative-ilo-psychosocial-working-environment-2026.json)
adds a work-organization-to-health and productivity route. The ILO's 2026
global report estimates more than 840,000 deaths and nearly 45 million DALYs
annually from psychosocial risk factors at work, with an estimated annual loss
equivalent to 1.37% of global GDP. These are modelled global estimates, not
individual diagnoses or a claim about any particular employer.

The useful cross-source mechanism is the conversion point: job demands,
workload, role clarity, autonomy, working-time arrangements, and fair or
transparent processes are treated as design and governance conditions. Formal
wellbeing programmes can therefore coexist with unchanged staffing, schedules,
workload, or worker control. The report makes prevention and management a
shared workplace, institutional, and public-health question rather than an
individual resilience burden.

**Rotation finding:** work-health pressure should be tracked across job design,
workload, autonomy, scheduling, management, fairness, worker voice, sector,
employment status, care demands, health, productivity, compensation, remedy,
and exit. A global burden estimate is not worker-level causality, and policy
adoption is not safer work.

## New adaptation signal: recognized learning is an unequal gateway through digital, green, and care transitions

The [ILO lifelong-learning record](records/comparative-ilo-lifelong-learning-skills-future-2026.json)
adds an education-to-work-adaptation route. The report says 16% of workers
received training in the past year, compared with 51% of full-time permanent
workers in formal firms; access is weakest for lower-educated, insecure, and
small-enterprise workers. Informal learning is widespread, but often remains
unrecognized and therefore does not reliably convert into qualifications or
mobility.

The transition mechanism is broader than technical skill acquisition. ILO
links digitalization, AI, green work, and demographic change to rounded skill
profiles, infrastructure, industrial and employment policy, and the valuation
of care work. Long-term-care employment is projected to rise from 85 million
workers in 2023 to 158 million by 2050. The institutional bottleneck is
delivery: fragmented systems, weak coordination, financing gaps, and limited
social dialogue can leave learning demand disconnected from actual opportunity.

**Rotation finding:** adaptation capacity should be tracked across training
access, informal learning, recognition, qualifications, employer size, job
security, digital and green tasks, care work, wages, infrastructure, public
financing, worker voice, mobility, and exclusion. Skills demand is not skill
power, and training participation is not a guaranteed return.

## New state-capacity signal: development finance is becoming a geopolitical and public-service bottleneck

The [UN Financing for Sustainable Development 2026 record](records/comparative-un-financing-sustainable-development-fragmentation-2026.json)
adds a macro-finance-to-state-capacity route. The UN Inter-agency Task Force
places the annual Sustainable Development Goal financing gap above $4 trillion,
reports that debt-service burdens reached 20-year highs in developing countries
and Small Island Developing States in 2024, and notes that more than 70
developing countries collect tax revenue below 15% of GDP. Official development
assistance fell 23.1% from 2024 to 2025 in the report's comparison.

This is not just a debt-total story. The report links geopolitical
fragmentation to diverted trade and investment, weaker cross-border capital
flows, and greater volatility, while renewable-energy investment reached $2.2
trillion in 2024—about twice fossil-fuel investment. The conversion question is
whether financial room becomes resilient public infrastructure and services,
or whether financing constraints redistribute costs to governments, firms,
households, and vulnerable communities. Neither the financing gap nor the
investment total identifies local incidence or political legitimacy by itself.

**Rotation finding:** state capacity should be tracked across borrowing costs,
tax room, aid, trade, investment, energy transition, debt restructuring,
infrastructure, public services, resilience, ownership, dependency,
multilateral cooperation, and geopolitical leverage. Fiscal pressure is a
condition on public action, not proof of a particular service or household
outcome.

## New digital-capability signal: investment quality is part of infrastructure and state power

The [OECD digital-transformation investment record](records/comparative-oecd-digital-transformation-investment-governance-2026.json)
adds an investment-governance route distinct from digital-government adoption.
The OECD framework organizes digital investment around governance, regulation,
technical and financial support, and information/facilitation, while connecting
investment to competition, cross-border data flows, trade agreements, skills,
and responsible business conduct.

The important mechanism is quality and control, not capital volume. The OECD
uses Morocco, Uzbekistan, and Viet Nam as country examples and draws on FDI
quality, regulatory-restrictiveness, tax-incentive, and investment-promotion
tools. The framework makes domestic capability, data governance, local skills,
and public bargaining conditions part of whether international investment
creates usable digital infrastructure or deeper dependency. It does not itself
measure local service quality, ownership, worker outcomes, or household access.

**Rotation finding:** digital transformation should be tracked across capital
source, infrastructure, regulation, competition, data flows, skills, local
capability, firm concentration, public procurement, ownership, portability,
service quality, and geopolitical dependence. Investment attraction is not
digital inclusion, and policy coherence is not realized capacity.

## New digital-participation signal: connectivity is expanding while meaningful access remains stratified

The [ITU connectivity-divides record](records/comparative-itu-digital-connectivity-divides-2025.json)
adds a population-facing digital-infrastructure route. ITU estimates roughly 6
billion people online in 2025, with 2.2 billion still offline. Yet the quality
and affordability gradient is substantial: 5G coverage reaches 84% of people in
high-income countries versus 4% in low-income countries, and mobile-broadband
service remains unaffordable in about 60% of low- and middle-income countries.

The social mechanism is meaningful participation rather than a binary access
count. ITU reports internet use at 94% in high-income countries versus 23% in
low-income countries, 77% for men versus 71% for women, and 85% in urban areas
versus 58% in rural areas. These gaps shape who can use digital public services,
education, work, markets, platforms, and political information, but they do
not by themselves identify individual preference, harm, or outcome.

**Rotation finding:** digital participation should be tracked across coverage,
quality, affordability, devices, skills, safety, age, gender, income, rurality,
public-service access, platform use, work, misinformation, environmental cost,
and exit or non-use. Being online is not meaningful connectivity, and meaningful
connectivity is not equal power.

## New innovation-power signal: breakthrough technology is concentrated in metropolitan ecosystems while investment momentum cools

The [WIPO innovation-cluster record](records/comparative-wipo-global-innovation-cluster-concentration-2025.json)
adds a firm, place, and geopolitical-capability route. WIPO's 2025 index covers
139 economies and describes AI and quantum progress advancing while innovation-
investment growth slows, venture-capital activity moderates, and patenting
gains remain modest. Its top 100 innovation clusters account for roughly 70%
of global PCT filings and VC deal activity and about half of scientific
publications; the top ten alone account for about 40% of PCT filings and 35% of
VC deals.

The concentration is not just a ranking. Clusters unite universities,
inventors, venture capital, and R&D firms, often across administrative borders.
China has 24 top-100 clusters and the United States 22, while middle-income
clusters appear across Brazil, Egypt, India, Iran, Malaysia/Singapore, Mexico,
and Türkiye. This creates a route from local infrastructure, skills, housing,
and firm ecosystems to national technological capacity and external leverage,
but cluster presence does not establish local benefit, ownership, or reduced
inequality.

**Rotation finding:** innovation power should be tracked across research,
patents, VC, firms, universities, skills, energy, housing, local labour markets,
ownership, diffusion, public procurement, supply chains, and geopolitical
dependence. Innovation leadership is not broad prosperity, and cluster density
is not community control.

## New information-culture signal: social video and personality-led news are reshaping trust and political attention

The [Reuters Institute Digital News Report record](records/comparative-reuters-digital-news-platform-video-trust-2025.json)
adds a cultural, platform, and political-attention route. Across 48 markets,
social-video news consumption rose from 52% in 2020 to 65% in 2025, while any
online video rose from 67% to 75%. Six online networks now reach more than 10%
weekly for news, compared with two a decade ago. In the United States, social
and video networks reached 54% weekly—above TV news at 50% and news websites or
apps at 48%—and the shift is especially strong among younger adults.

The trust mechanism is unsettled rather than one-directional. Fifty-eight
percent across markets are concerned about telling true from false online news,
yet overall news trust remains 40%. AI chatbots are already used for news by 7%
weekly overall and 15% of under-25s; audiences expect AI to make news cheaper
and more current but less transparent, accurate, and trustworthy. The source
also finds that people most often say they would verify suspected falsehoods
through trusted news outlets or official sources, not social media alone. These
are aggregate survey results, not proof of persuasion, voting, or platform
causality.

**Rotation finding:** information power should be tracked across format, creator,
platform, algorithmic distribution, age, political identity, local news,
verification, moderation, AI mediation, trust, misinformation, payment,
participation, and exit. Reach is not influence, exposure is not belief, and
trust is not accuracy.

## New demographic signal: population ageing and continued growth are splitting the policy map

The [UN World Population Prospects record](records/comparative-un-world-population-prospects-fertility-ageing-2024.json)
adds a demographic-structure route. UN DESA projects global population to rise
from about 8.2 billion in 2024 to 10.3 billion in the mid-2080s before easing
to roughly 10.2 billion by 2100, with an 80% probability of a peak this
century. More than half of countries are already below replacement fertility,
while nearly one-fifth are below 1.4 births per woman. This is a split system:
63 countries had already peaked by 2024, while 126 are projected to keep
growing through 2054.

The downstream route is institutional and cultural, not merely numerical.
Global life expectancy reached 73.3 years in 2024; by the late 2070s people
aged 65 and older are projected to outnumber children under 18, while rapid-
growth countries face youth, education, employment, and infrastructure demands.
The same demographic transition therefore changes care supply, work, migration,
housing, family formation, social protection, and state legitimacy in different
places. Projections do not reveal desired family size, individual agency, or
whether longer life is healthy, secure, or supported.

**Rotation finding:** demographic change should be tracked across fertility,
longevity, care, work, migration, housing, education, family time, social
protection, health, disability, gender, consumption, and state capacity. A
population projection is not a household preference, and ageing is not
dependency by definition.

## New place signal: urban growth is concentrated in cities and smaller settlements, not only megacities

The [UN World Urbanization Prospects record](records/comparative-un-world-urbanization-prospects-cities-towns-rural-2025.json)
adds a settlement-structure route distinct from the population-ageing record.
Using a harmonized Degree of Urbanization, UN DESA estimates that cities held
45% of the world's 8.2 billion people in 2025, towns 36%, and rural areas 19%.
Two-thirds of population growth through 2050 is projected to occur in cities,
with most of the remainder in towns; the rural population is expected to peak
in the 2040s and then decline.

The policy mechanism is distributed urban capacity. The number of megacities
rose from 8 in 1975 to 33 in 2025 and is projected to reach 37 by 2050, but 96%
of the roughly 12,000 cities have fewer than one million residents and 81% have
fewer than 250,000. More than half of the projected 986 million increase in
city residents is concentrated in seven countries. This points to planning,
land, housing, transport, digital access, care, energy, water, and local-state
capacity in smaller and rapidly growing places—not just global-city power.

**Rotation finding:** place change should be tracked across settlement type,
city size, housing, land, transport, work, care, energy, water, digital access,
climate exposure, local finance, migration, service quality, and resident
voice. Urbanization is not megacity concentration, and population growth is not
service capacity.

## New generational signal: youth opportunity is moving toward growing lower-income regions

The [UN World Population Highlights 2026: Youth record](records/comparative-un-world-population-highlights-youth-2026.json)
adds a youth-demography and transition-capacity route distinct from the broader
population and settlement projections. The UN estimates roughly 1.3 billion
people aged 15-24 in 2025. Between 2025 and 2050, the youth population is
projected to grow 16% in low- and lower-middle-income countries and decline 24%
in high- and upper-middle-income countries; the lower-income group would contain
69% of the world's youth by 2050, up from 59% in 2025.

That shift changes where education, health, decent work, migration, rights,
digital safety, and political voice become capacity bottlenecks. In higher-
income countries, the publication projects roughly three people aged 65 or
older for every person aged 15-24 by 2050, making youth representation and
intergenerational solidarity an institutional question as well. These are
population projections and policy priorities, not individual attitudes,
respondent outcomes, or proof of a demographic dividend.

**Rotation finding:** youth change should be tracked across cohort size,
education, skills, decent work, health, reproduction, migration, housing,
digital life, rights, intergenerational bargaining, participation, and state
investment. A growing youth population is an opportunity only when institutions
convert it into usable capability and voice.

## New social-capacity signal: youth mental health is shaped by linked institutions and social conditions

The [UN World Youth Report mental-health record](records/comparative-un-world-youth-report-mental-health-determinants-2026.json)
adds a social-capacity route distinct from the youth population projection. The
report organizes youth mental health and well-being around six interlinked
determinants: education, employment, family dynamics, poverty and deprivation,
technology and the digital environment, and society and community. It treats
inequality, stigma, discrimination, and unequal access to opportunity and care
as connected barriers rather than isolated clinical variables.

The institutional implication is broad: supportive schools and social-emotional
learning, decent and less precarious work transitions, family and community
support, safer digital environments, privacy-protective data practices,
prevention, early intervention, and youth-led initiatives all sit in the same
policy route. The publication synthesizes evidence and mechanisms; it does not
prove a single determinant caused an individual's outcome or substitute for
local service evaluation.

**Rotation finding:** youth well-being should be tracked across exposure,
stigma, school, work, family, poverty, technology, community, service access,
prevention, privacy, agency, and institutional remedy. Mental-health capacity
is partly a question of social conditions and usable public infrastructure.

## New macro-consumer signal: geopolitical energy shocks transmit through prices, supply chains, and public capacity

The [UN WESP mid-2026 record](records/comparative-un-wesp-mid-2026-energy-inflation-supply-chain-shock.json)
adds a macroeconomic transmission route. UN DESA projects global growth at
2.5% in 2026 and 2.8% in 2027, while global inflation is projected to reach
3.9% in 2026 after the Middle East crisis reversed the recent disinflationary
trend. The report describes the shock as moving through energy, fertilizer,
food, freight, insurance, transport, and import costs, with low-income
households more exposed because food and energy occupy a larger budget share.

The downstream political-economic route is equally important: higher rates can
contain inflation while weakening growth, while weaker growth and crisis
spending constrain fiscal space. Developing economies face tighter external
financing and greater food-insecurity, debt, and SDG risks. These are forecasts
and transmission mechanisms, not household incidence, product-level
pass-through, or proof of realized political action.

**Rotation finding:** geopolitical events should be tracked across energy
transit, commodity prices, supply chains, household purchasing power, firm
costs, labour markets, financial flows, fiscal response, food security,
inequality, and international coordination. Positive aggregate growth can
coexist with severe distributional pressure.

## New implementation signal: public systems have delivered gains at scale, but progress is uneven and increasingly exposed to crisis

The [UN Sustainable Development Goals Report 2026 record](records/comparative-un-sdg-report-2026-progress-regression-capacity.json)
adds a public-system-results route. Since 2015, the report attributes roughly
one billion additional people to safely managed drinking water and 1.2 billion
to safely managed sanitation, while electricity access reaches 92% of the
world's population, internet access rose from 40% to 74%, and social protection
now covers more than half the global population. This is evidence that policy,
investment, data, and cooperation can produce gains at scale.

The counter-signal is implementation fragility. Among 139 targets with trend
data, 36% are on track or making moderate progress, 49% are advancing too
slowly, and 15% have regressed below 2015 baselines. The report also highlights
273 million children and young people out of school, one in five young people
not in employment, education, or training, a 23.1% fall in official development
assistance in 2025, and major displacement and informal-settlement pressures.
Coverage is not reliability, affordability, adequacy, or individual receipt.

**Rotation finding:** public progress should be tracked across policy input,
infrastructure, access, quality, affordability, reach, durability, crisis
resilience, financing, data, remedy, and unequal distribution. The existence of
large-scale gains does not erase implementation gaps; regression does not erase
the capacity to scale what works.

## New civic-digital signal: online space is becoming more controlled, manipulated, and politically consequential

The [Freedom on the Net 2025 record](records/comparative-freedom-house-freedom-on-net-online-control-2025.json)
adds a civic-digital control route distinct from news consumption and basic
connectivity. Freedom House reports a fifteenth consecutive year of global
internet-freedom decline: conditions deteriorated in 27 of 72 assessed
countries, while 17 improved. Its population-weighted control indicators report
that 81% of internet users live where people were arrested or imprisoned for
online expression, 69% where political, social, or religious content was
blocked, 61% where social-media access was restricted, and 52% where authorities
disconnected networks.

The mechanism is not only blocking. Paid commenters, imitation news sites,
AI-generated misleading content, undisclosed progovernment influencers, and
pressure on satellite providers create a layered environment in which access,
visibility, trust, anonymity, and political action can diverge. These are
expert-coded country assessments and documented control environments, not proof
of individual persuasion, voting, or belief change.

**Rotation finding:** digital civic power should be tracked across access,
content, manipulation, identity, privacy, surveillance, shutdowns, platform
rules, infrastructure, circumvention, journalism, protest, trust, and remedy.
Connectivity can expand participation while the same infrastructure expands
control.

## New material-land signal: forest loss is slowing, but land-use, ownership, and livelihood pressures remain uneven

The [FAO Global Forest Resources Assessment 2025 record](records/comparative-fao-global-forest-resources-assessment-2025-restoration-livelihoods.json)
adds a land, livelihood, and ecological-capacity route. Forests cover roughly
4.14 billion hectares, or 32% of global land. Annual net forest loss fell from
10.7 million hectares in the 1990s to 4.12 million in 2015-2025, but that net
improvement combines slower deforestation with slower forest expansion; it does
not mean ecological recovery is uniform. Naturally regenerating forests make up
92% of forest area, while primary forests cover at least 1.18 billion hectares.

The governance signal is distributed ownership and use. Public ownership is
71% globally, about 20% of forests are legally protected, 55% are covered by
long-term management plans, and only 5% are designated primarily for social
services such as recreation, culture, and education. Formal designation is not
the same as enforcement, community consent, benefit sharing, or ecological
quality; forest area is not a proxy for local livelihood security.

**Rotation finding:** land and ecological change should be tracked across
commodity demand, deforestation, regeneration, ownership, management rights,
protection, carbon, biodiversity, water, food, culture, restoration, community
voice, and benefit distribution. Aggregate environmental improvement can
coexist with local dispossession or degraded ecological function.

## New trade-technology signal: AI may expand global trade, but infrastructure gaps and regulatory fragmentation can concentrate the gains

The [WTO World Trade Report 2025 record](records/comparative-wto-world-trade-report-ai-inclusive-growth-2025.json)
adds a trade, firm-capability, and geopolitical-technology route. WTO scenarios
project that AI could increase global trade by 34-37% and global GDP by 12-13%
by 2040, with digitally deliverable services trade rising 42% across scenarios.
The report frames the mechanism through lower trade costs, supply-chain
visibility, customs and compliance, language barriers, market intelligence,
contract enforcement, and small-firm access—not simply automation.

The distributional hinge is catch-up. In one scenario, halving the digital-
infrastructure gap and increasing AI adoption produces modeled income gains of
15% for low-income and 14% for middle-income economies. But AI-enabling-goods
trade was already about $2.3 trillion in 2023 while quantitative restrictions on
those goods rose from 130 in 2012 to nearly 500 in 2024. These are models and
policy indicators, not realized firm or worker outcomes; access is not ownership,
control, or bargaining power.

**Rotation finding:** AI globalization should be tracked across infrastructure,
skills, data, firms, supply chains, trade costs, tasks, wages, ownership,
standards, tariffs, restrictions, interoperability, labour policy, and local
capability. Aggregate AI growth can coexist with concentrated value and uneven
voice.

## New digital-material signal: digital consumption is becoming an energy, water, mineral, and waste system

The [UNCTAD Digital Economy Report 2024 record](records/comparative-unctad-digital-economy-environmental-footprint-2024.json)
adds a material-footprint route distinct from digital access, civic control, and
AI-trade scenarios. Smartphone shipments reached roughly 1.2 billion in 2023,
IoT devices are projected to reach 39 billion by 2029, and business e-commerce
sales across 43 countries reached about $27 trillion in 2022. Digital devices,
data centres, and ICT networks account for an estimated 6-12% of global
electricity use.

The infrastructure and supply-chain effects are uneven. Data centres used an
estimated 460 TWh in 2022 and could reach 1,000 TWh in 2026; critical-mineral
demand for cobalt, graphite, and lithium is projected to rise 500% by 2050.
Developing countries formally collected only 7.5% of digital waste in 2022,
versus 47% in developed countries, while they often bear extraction and waste
costs without capturing high-value processing. These are aggregate footprint
and value-chain indicators, not household device-use or local causal estimates.

**Rotation finding:** digital change should be tracked across devices,
e-commerce, energy, water, minerals, data centres, waste, repair, recycling,
ownership, value capture, local resource competition, labour, and circularity.
Digital inclusion without material accountability can shift costs rather than
remove them.

## New legitimacy signal: institutions are redefining progress beyond GDP toward well-being, equity, sustainability, and resilience

The [UN Beyond GDP record](records/comparative-un-beyond-gdp-progress-dashboard-2026.json)
adds a measurement-and-legitimacy route. The UN High-Level Expert Group proposes
a 31-indicator dashboard organized around four pillars: foundational principles,
current well-being, equity and inclusion, and sustainability and resilience.
The proposed domains include material conditions and work, health, education,
security, subjective well-being, inequality, poverty, work inclusion, regional
disparities, social cohesion, human and social capital, institutional quality,
natural capital, and environmental quality.

The cultural and political shift is in what becomes visible to decision-makers.
The framework argues that GDP can rise while safety, environmental quality,
health, equality, peace, or social cohesion deteriorate. It recommends
country-owned dashboards and recognizes cross-border spillovers, while UNCTAD
is piloting implementation. This is a proposed measurement framework—not proof
that governments have adopted it or that indicators have changed outcomes.

**Rotation finding:** legitimacy should be tracked across what institutions
measure, fund, communicate, and remedy. Progress metrics can broaden public
judgment, but they can also become symbolic unless data, budgets, accountability,
and local voice follow.

## New firm-geopolitical signal: investment is recovering but concentrating in strategic megaprojects and a narrow host-economy set

The [UNCTAD World Investment Report 2026 record](records/comparative-unctad-world-investment-report-2026-concentration-development-impact.json)
adds a firm, capital, and geopolitical-capacity route. Global FDI rose 6% to
about $1.6 trillion in 2025, but inflows to developing economies rose only 2%
to $901 billion compared with 11% growth in developed economies. More than 80%
of global FDI went to the top 20 host economies, while strategic sectors made up
44% of global greenfield project value in 2025, up from 16% in 2020; AI-related
digital-infrastructure megaprojects were a major driver.

The key conversion question is what capital builds. UNCTAD identifies productive
capacity, jobs, skills, technology transfer, and resilient value chains as the
conditions that turn investment into broader development impact. Announced
projects and FDI totals do not prove completed capacity, local ownership,
quality jobs, technology diffusion, or community acceptance; geopolitical
fragmentation and high financing costs may further intensify concentration.

**Rotation finding:** firm power should be tracked across capital source,
