project completion, sector, host place, ownership, infrastructure, jobs, skills,
technology transfer, supplier linkages, environmental burden, public leverage,
and local voice. Investment recovery can coexist with narrow development reach.

## New climate-education signal: climate disruption is interrupting schooling and future opportunity, not only physical safety

The [UNICEF Learning Interrupted record](records/comparative-unicef-learning-interrupted-climate-school-disruptions-2026.json)
adds an education-continuity route. UNICEF estimates that at least 171 million
students experienced climate-related school disruption in 2025 across 106
countries and territories—at least one in ten students—with 75% of affected
students in low- and lower-middle-income countries. Forty countries experienced
multiple disruption events, making recovery between shocks a distinct concern.

UNICEF estimates that disrupted learning and widening education inequality could
put at least $200 billion in lifetime earnings at risk. The important mechanism
is broader than attendance: school closure interrupts learning, routines, peer
relationships, protection, access to services, and pathways into work, while
resilient infrastructure, early warning, flexible delivery, and recovery support
can buffer the effects. These are aggregate and modeled estimates, not student-
level learning or earnings data.

**Rotation finding:** climate impacts should be tracked across hazard exposure,
school closure, duration, learning continuity, infrastructure, transport,
teachers, digital access, protection, family adaptation, recovery, future work,
and local voice. Education systems are also social and cultural resilience
infrastructure.

## New mineral-security signal: transition demand is colliding with concentrated refining and strategic trade controls

The [IEA Global Critical Minerals Outlook 2026 record](records/comparative-iea-critical-minerals-outlook-2026-supply-chain-security.json)
adds a material, firm, infrastructure, and geopolitical route. Global battery
demand exceeded 1.5 TWh in 2025 and grew more than 35%; the energy sector drove
about 75% of demand growth for key energy minerals. At the same time, the
average share of the top refined supplier reached 70% in 2025, up from 68% in
2020, while export controls and conflict-related chokepoints transmitted risk
into batteries, vehicles, grids, semiconductors, fertiliser inputs, and defence
systems.

The Latin America section makes the conversion question visible: the region
produces large shares of copper and lithium but refines only about one-fifth of
its mined output of key energy minerals. IEA models substantial additional
regional value if processing expands, while identifying finance, power, water,
technology, skills, and local-stakeholder engagement as constraints. These are
market, policy, and modeled exposure signals—not mine-level environmental
incidence, completed projects, consumer losses, or proof of geopolitical
leverage.

**Rotation finding:** transition security should be tracked across extraction,
refining, ownership, trade controls, stockpiles, substitution, recycling,
power and water, skills, local voice, downstream firms, prices, and external
response. Clean-energy and digital expansion can increase capability while
also deepening concentrated mineral dependence.

## New cultural-policy signal: culture is being elevated into development infrastructure while protection and distribution lag

The [UNESCO cultural-policy and creative-economy record](records/comparative-unesco-cultural-policies-creative-economy-artistic-freedom-2026.json)
adds a direct cultural route to the atlas. UNESCO's global policy report draws
on more than 1,200 national and local reports and finds that 93% of reporting
Member States recognize culture in national sustainable-development plans. Yet
the more operational measure is narrower: 85% of reporting countries include
cultural and creative industries in development plans, while only 56% set
specific cultural objectives.

The sector is economically material—UNESCO reports cultural and creative
industries generate about 3.39% of global GDP and 3.55% of jobs—but the support
system is uneven. The companion 2026 creativity report records low public
funding, weak reported protection of artists' economic and social rights,
unequal inward artistic mobility, platform concentration, a large digital-
skills gap, and modeled generative-AI revenue exposure for music and
audiovisual creators. Cultural recognition therefore does not automatically
become creator security, diverse production, accessible participation, or
social cohesion.

**Rotation finding:** cultural trends should be tracked across public
recognition, funding, rights, platform distribution, creator income, AI use,
artistic freedom, education, mobility, local institutions, participation, and
political or social meaning. Culture is both an economy and a governance
infrastructure, with a measurable gap between symbolic inclusion and usable
capacity.

## New public-workforce signal: state capacity is a labor, management, and digital conversion problem

The [World Bank Public Workforce Performance and Prosperity record](records/comparative-world-bank-public-workforce-performance-state-capacity-2026.json)
adds an institutional route between public resources and usable outcomes. The
report frames roughly 400 million public-sector workers as the operational
layer connecting wage bills, staffing, skills, management, integrity,
community engagement, and digital systems to education, health, climate,
revenue, peace, fiscal sustainability, and citizen trust.

The cross-country contrast is substantial: low-income countries typically have
10–20 public workers per 1,000 people, compared with 50–140 in high-income
countries. The public sector accounts for 38% of formal employment globally and
59% in fragile, conflict, or violence-affected states, while women represent
46% of the public workforce versus 33% of the private workforce. Public-sector
pay, management, and digital systems therefore affect both service capacity and
labor-market structure. These are aggregate and organizational findings, not
individual worker or citizen respondent outcomes, and they do not prove that
reform produces better services or trust.

**Rotation finding:** state capacity should be tracked across recruitment,
staffing location, pay, management, digital use, worker voice, service delivery,
fiscal capacity, integrity, community connection, citizen trust, and reform
implementation. A larger budget or a new information system is not yet a
usable institution.

## New consumer-finance signal: digital access is becoming a protection and recourse problem, not only an inclusion problem

The [OECD Consumer Finance Risk Monitor 2026 record](records/comparative-oecd-consumer-finance-risk-digital-payments-protection-2026.json)
adds a market-to-protection route across 60 jurisdictions and 107 government
ministries or financial authorities. Financial scams and frauds were the
leading operating-environment risk identified for consumers. The report also
maps harm through mobile banking, transaction accounts, digital wallets, debit
cards, push payments, cross-border transactions, insurance, credit, investment,
and pension products.

The important consumer pattern is institutional routing. In the banking and
payments sector, scams and fraud were the most common complaint subject across
firms, alternative-dispute-resolution schemes, and supervisory authorities.
Complaint volumes received by supervisory authorities rose in almost 70% of
reporting jurisdictions from 2024 to 2025, but complaint growth can reflect
worsening harm, better awareness, stronger confidence in redress, or improved
reporting. These are authority-level assessments, not individual consumer
respondent or victimization data, and they do not prove loss, remedy, recovery,
switching, or trust change.

**Rotation finding:** consumer-finance trends should be tracked across product
design, digital access, fraud exposure, loss, reporting, complaint routing,
redress, recovery, switching, and trust. Digital finance can expand access and
simultaneously raise the cost of safe participation when protection capacity
lags product and platform expansion.

## New public-institutions signal: administrative quality reaches firms through procurement prices, delays, and regulatory predictability

The [World Bank Institutions and Prosperity record](records/comparative-world-bank-public-institutions-procurement-regulation-private-sector-2026.json)
adds a firm and market conversion layer distinct from the public-workforce
record. Its framework separates four organizational dimensions—personnel,
financial resources, information systems, and management—from three governance
dimensions—independence, accountability, and transparency. The key claim is
that policy mandates do not become market outcomes unless an institution can
implement them with those complements.

The procurement evidence makes the mechanism tangible. Better personnel,
systems, management, compensation, and timely payment are associated with
easier contracting and fewer delays, renegotiations, overruns, and barriers to
smaller suppliers. In an Italy case, the lowest-performing procurement units
paid about 55% more than the highest-performing units for similar products. A
Peru telecommunications case links leadership turnover to delayed regulatory
milestones and stronger transparency/accountability to lower uncertainty. These
are comparative and named-case findings—not firm respondents, consumer price
incidence, or universal causal estimates.

**Rotation finding:** firm and consumer outcomes should be tracked from mandate
through agency capacity, contract design, bidder access, payment, price,
delay, service quality, oversight, and political response. “Regulation” and
“procurement” are not single variables; their organizational conversion layer
can redistribute cost, competition, and market access.

## New information-integrity signal: press freedom is public infrastructure for accountability, while platforms and AI reshape discourse control

The [UNESCO World Trends in Freedom of Expression and Media Development record](records/comparative-unesco-world-trends-expression-media-information-integrity-2026.json)
adds an information-as-public-good route distinct from platform-use and news-
attention records. UNESCO reports a 10% global decline in its Freedom of
Expression Index since 2012 and frames journalism as an institutional condition
for public access to information, accountability, human rights, electoral
integrity, and informed citizenship.

The report connects that decline to weakened governance, hostility toward
journalists, digital repression, platform-policy abuse, algorithmic
manipulation, synthetic or altered AI content, and concentration of economic
and political power among technology companies. It also preserves countervailing
developments—social-media access, collaborative investigative journalism,
subscription models, and community media—so the direction is not simply “more
technology equals less voice.” These are global index and publication findings,
not individual media-use, persuasion, voting, or respondent-level outcomes.

**Rotation finding:** information trends should be tracked across press freedom,
ownership, platform distribution, AI mediation, moderation, journalist safety,
public access, local news, trust, institutional accountability, and civic action.
Access to content is not the same as an independent information system.

## New integrated-development signal: geopolitical fracture, digital capacity, and civic distrust are interacting rather than moving in separate lanes

The [UNDP Trends Update 2026 record](records/comparative-undp-landscape-development-trends-multilateralism-conflict-distrust-2026.json)
adds a cross-domain connector to the atlas. Its 12-theme development landscape
links multilateral fracture, conflict cascades, gender, health, digital and AI
capacity, uneven prosperity, migration, distrust, debt, sustainable finance,
energy transition, and environmental limits. The update reports 59 active
state-based conflicts, 78 countries involved in conflict beyond their borders,
2.2 billion people offline, and protest activity increasing in 88 countries.

The value of this source is the interaction map: defense and aid choices affect
development room; conflict and resource competition affect energy and digital
systems; identity and AI capacity affect access and governance; and grievance
can shift participation toward localized or confrontational action when formal
institutions cannot absorb it. These are source-specific aggregate indicators
in an analytical trends update—not respondent-level data, a pooled causal model,
or proof that any one fracture caused later political or social behavior.

**Rotation finding:** cross-domain trends should be followed across state action,
resource allocation, infrastructure, identity, platforms, conflict exposure,
civic voice, institutional response, and later adaptation. The important
endpoint is not one trend line but whether interacting pressures narrow or widen
the practical room for cooperation and development.

## New geopolitical-labor signal: an external crisis can become a household shock through work hours, real income, migration, and remittances

The [ILO Middle East crisis labor-transmission record](records/comparative-ilo-middle-east-crisis-labour-remittance-energy-shock-2026.json)
adds a dated shock-to-work-to-household route. Under an illustrative scenario
with oil prices about 50% above their early-2026 average, the ILO projects
global hours worked to fall 0.5% in 2026 and 1.1% in 2027—equivalent to about
14 million and 38 million full-time jobs—with real labor income down 1.1% and
3.0%. The transmission runs through energy prices, transport, supply chains,
tourism, investment confidence, labor demand, migration, and remittances.

Exposure is not uniform: Arab States have roughly 40% of employment in
high-exposure sectors, while Asia and the Pacific have 22% of workers in such
sectors. The ILO also reports disproportionate adjustment risk for migrant
workers and early pressure on remittances. These are modeled and aggregate
signals, not worker or household respondent data, realized losses, or a
completed crisis forecast.

**Rotation finding:** geopolitical shocks should be tracked across conflict,
energy, transport, firms, sectors, hours, wages, migration, remittances, social
protection, and household adaptation. A distant crisis becomes socially
meaningful when the transmission reaches work, income, and the practical room
of households and origin communities.

## Energy-policy implementation: shocks are widening the state response

The [IEA State of Energy Policy 2026 record](records/comparative-iea-state-energy-policy-2026-policy-adoption-resilience.json)
adds a distinct policy-response layer to the atlas. The IEA tracks more than
6,500 measures across 84 countries and shows governments responding through
emergency reserves, gas buffers, demand restraint, affordability support,
energy-access initiatives, regulatory changes, and policies for renewable and
nuclear deployment, electrification, and supply-chain resilience. This connects
geopolitical disruption to public capacity, fiscal targeting, infrastructure,
firm incentives, and unequal exposure without treating policy counts as proof
of implementation or household benefit.

The distributional signal is especially important: the IEA reports about USD
220 billion in household energy-affordability support during 2022–2023, while
only 25% of short-term interventions since 2022 targeted vulnerable households
most exposed to price shocks. At the same time, regulatory rollbacks in 2025
affected 30% of regulated energy consumption versus 17% subject to stricter
rules. These are aggregate policy patterns, not respondent-level data, take-up
estimates, or evidence that any specific household gained or lost protection.

**Rotation finding:** energy policy should be followed across shock management,
budget allocation, regulation, access, technology deployment, firm response,
consumer exposure, and later reliability or affordability outcomes. The key
open arrow is whether broad policy activity becomes durable, targeted capacity
that changes practical room for households, workers, firms, and communities.

## Humanitarian capacity: displacement need is filtered through funding and access systems

The [UNHCR Global Report 2025 record](records/comparative-unhcr-global-report-2025-protection-capacity-funding-access.json)
adds a distinct displacement-to-institutional-capacity route. UNHCR aimed to
protect and assist 129.4 million forcibly displaced and stateless people in
2025, while available funding covered 37% of total needs. Available resources
fell by $1.246 billion, and the workforce was reduced by more than 6,600
positions by January 2026. This links geopolitical conflict and displacement to
donor allocation, operational reach, staffing, and service continuity.

The report also shows why “assistance” is not the endpoint: protection services,
legal aid, documentation, work access, cash, national education and health
systems, resettlement, and local integration are different conversion stages.
Tightly earmarked funding reached 44% of the portfolio, which can preserve
specified priorities while reducing flexibility for under-covered or newly
emerging crises. These are institutional and aggregate signals—not individual
service receipt, respondent-level data, or proof of durable protection,
adequate income, safe return, or equal access.

**Rotation finding:** displacement should be tracked from conflict and movement
through donor resources, operational capacity, legal status, work and service
access, host institutions, and durable outcomes. The open arrow is whether
formal protection capacity becomes practical, sustained room for displaced
people and host communities.

## Trade governance: flexibility is becoming a strategy for managing fragmentation

The [WTO World Trade Report 2026 record](records/comparative-wto-world-trade-report-2026-fragmentation-flexibility-ai-services.json)
adds a market-and-institutional route to the atlas. The report describes a trade
system with more dispersed economic power, more diverse membership, complex
frictions, regional and targeted agreements, rising digital services, and AI as
both an opportunity and a governance stressor. Low- and middle-income
economies' share of global trade rose from 23% in 1995 to 45% in 2024, while
more than 380 regional trade agreements have been notified to the WTO.

The report's modeled scenarios make the institutional stakes visible: a
geopolitically fragmented world is associated with a 5.1% global GDP decline
and an 18.6% export decline, while an FTA-only scenario is associated with
larger modeled losses. These are counterfactual simulations, not forecasts or
realized losses. The broader signal is that safeguards, waivers, regional
agreements, and special treatment can preserve cooperation under pressure but
can also raise prices, divert trade, or weaken predictability if disconnected
from capacity and need.

**Rotation finding:** trade trends should be followed from rule design through
firm and sector exposure, prices, technology access, worker and consumer room,
development space, and geopolitical alignment. The open arrow is whether
flexibility widens participation and bargaining capacity or becomes a pathway
to fragmented standards, concentrated leverage, and unequal gains.

## Macro-fiscal resilience: geopolitical shocks narrow the room to respond

The [World Bank Global Economic Prospects June 2026 record](records/comparative-world-bank-global-economic-prospects-june-2026-fiscal-space-shocks.json)
adds a fiscal-space and investment route. The World Bank projects global growth
slowing to 2.5% in 2026 from 2.9% in 2025, with inflation at 4.0%, while a more
severe disruption and financial-stress scenario reduces modeled growth to 1.3%.
Regional exposure differs with energy dependence, strategic reserves, policy
buffers, food and fertilizer prices, and fiscal capacity. These are forecasts
and scenarios, not realized household or firm outcomes.

The deeper trend is institutional room: developing-economy government debt has
risen from under 40% of GDP in 2010 to over 70%, and the World Bank associates
rising debt with higher sovereign spreads and domestic-currency yields. That
can narrow the room for infrastructure, health, education, jobs, and social
protection precisely when shocks increase demand for them. Private investment
and AI adoption are therefore not standalone growth stories; they depend on
finance, infrastructure, institutions, and the ability of public systems to
absorb risk.

**Rotation finding:** macro shocks should be followed from energy and food
prices through inflation, debt service, fiscal buffers, public support, firm
finance, investment, jobs, and household room. The open arrow is whether
stabilization preserves practical capacity for people and firms or shifts the
shock into future public-service and opportunity constraints.

## Work inequality: employment form and opportunity shape the value of work

The [ILO Inequalities in the World of Work record](records/comparative-ilo-inequalities-world-work-2026-formality-opportunity.json)
adds a structural labor-market route. The publication treats inequality as
more than a headline wage gap: it separates vertical earnings inequality,
horizontal differences across groups and labor markets, and inequality of
opportunity tied to circumstances beyond individual control. It also identifies
sector and employment form as major parts of the distributional mechanism.

The own-account and informality signal matters for the atlas because autonomy,
entry, volatility, protection, and bargaining can coexist in the same work
form. The ILO reports high earnings inequality in Sub-Saharan Africa and Latin
America and substantial within-group inequality among own-account workers,
especially in informal economies. These are comparative publication findings,
not individual wage estimates, new microdata analysis, or proof that
formalization alone improves household security.

**Rotation finding:** work trends should be followed from sector and employment
form through earnings, protection, bargaining, time, household room, and
institutional response. The open arrow is whether formalization and decent-work
policies expand practical opportunity without eliminating flexible entry routes
or shifting costs onto already exposed workers.

## Education and digital learning: future-ready capability depends on foundations and context

The [OECD PISA 2025 record](records/comparative-oecd-pisa-2025-future-ready-students-learning-digital-equity.json)
adds a social-reproduction and institutional-capacity route. Across 91 systems,
the OECD reports large declines in reading and mathematics performance, with
one in five OECD 15-year-olds now low performers across science, mathematics,
and reading. The socioeconomic science gap is 85 points on average, while 76%
of students report feeling that they belong at school. These are published
assessment aggregates, not new respondent-level analysis or later-life causal
estimates.

The digital signal is more conditional than a simple “technology helps/hurts”
story: 46% of OECD students report weekly-or-more AI chatbot use for learning,
digital distraction is common, and performance patterns vary with the task and
whether students are taught to assess AI-generated information. Almost two
thirds reach the relevant computational problem-solving level, but only half
also show basic proficiency in science, reading, and mathematics. AI therefore
adds a capability and governance layer to education, while foundational skills,
school climate, family resources, and teacher guidance remain complements.

**Rotation finding:** education trends should be followed from access and family
resources through school capacity, learning, belonging, digital tools, AI
guidance, future work, and civic capability. The open arrow is whether digital
adoption expands practical learning room or amplifies pre-existing inequality
when foundational and institutional complements are weak.

## Care financing: aging and work-form change reshape the social-protection base

The [OECD Financing of Social Protection record](records/comparative-oecd-financing-social-protection-2026-ageing-work-care.json)
adds a social-reproduction and public-finance route. OECD and EU social-
protection expenditure is about 23% of GDP; by 2045, pension spending is
projected to rise by 1.1 percentage points of GDP in the OECD and health and
long-term-care spending by 1.5 points. These are comparative projections, not
individual care needs, benefit adequacy, or household outcomes.

The financing mechanism is unevenly tied to work: social-security contributions
covered 43% of OECD social spending in 2023, down from 47% in 1995, while
self-employed workers represented 14% of EU employment but only 4% of social-
protection contributions. Under the report’s hours scenario, total hours worked
fall by about 9% across the OECD and almost 20% across the EU between 2024 and
2060. That makes care, pensions, family benefits, and poverty prevention a
question of coverage and financing architecture—not only benefit generosity.

**Rotation finding:** care trends should be followed from demographic change
through work form, contributions, informal care, service access, public budgets,
firms, and household time. The open arrow is whether broader financing bases
expand protection without shifting costs onto caregivers, unstable workers, or
families with the least room.

## Health-workforce capacity: care access depends on where workers are and when they exit

The [WHO National Health Workforce Accounts 2026 record](records/comparative-who-health-workforce-accounts-2026-ageing-shortage-distribution.json)
adds a service-supply endpoint to the care map. WHO reports that health-worker
density rose 52% from 2006 to 2025 to 67.9 per 10,000 population, with more
than 70 million health and care workers globally. That aggregate improvement
coexists with a 13-fold medical-doctor density ratio between the WHO European
and African regions and a six-fold nursing/midwifery ratio. These are workforce
and planning statistics, not patient-level access or quality measures.

The demographic pressure is two-sided: nearly one in four doctors is older than
55, the projected global shortage reaches 11.1 million health workers by 2030,
and population aging is increasing demand. WHO also reports that more than 90%
of Member States actively reported workforce data in 2025, nearly 20 times the
availability when the accounts system launched. Better measurement is an
important institutional capability, but it does not itself train, retain,
finance, or redistribute workers.

**Rotation finding:** health trends should be followed from population need
through training, migration, retention, workforce age, geographic distribution,
service access, care burden, and outcomes. The open arrow is whether improved
workforce intelligence becomes actual local capacity where patients and
caregivers need it.

## Purchasing power: energy shocks reach consumers through inflation and support design

The [OECD September 2026 Interim Economic Outlook record](records/comparative-oecd-interim-economic-outlook-september-2026-energy-inflation-consumer-room.json)
adds a consumer/economic-behavior route. The OECD projects G20 headline
inflation rising from 3.4% in 2025 to 4.1% in 2026 before easing to 3.6% in
2027, while global growth is projected at 2.9% in 2026 and 3.0% in 2027. The
shock has been partly absorbed through alternative supply routes, inventory
drawdowns, strategic reserves, additional production, and lower oil demand.
These are macro forecasts and mechanisms, not household-specific price or
consumption outcomes.

The social signal is the policy tradeoff: support needs to be targeted and
temporary enough to protect exposed households while retaining incentives for
energy savings, and public finances face pressure as long-term yields rise.
Meanwhile, rapid AI investment supports trade and growth but increasingly relies
on external financing, creating a concurrent market-correction risk if expected
returns fail to materialize. Consumer room is therefore shaped by prices,
wages, transfers, firm costs, energy behavior, and public support together.

**Rotation finding:** price trends should be followed from geopolitical shock
through energy costs, inflation, wages, substitution, support take-up, firm
pricing, savings, and household participation. The open arrow is whether
stabilization preserves practical purchasing power or simply delays and
redistributes the burden.

## Political voice: formal representation does not guarantee safe or equal power

The [IPU-UN Women Women in Politics 2026 record](records/comparative-ipu-un-women-politics-2026-representation-hostility-quotas.json)
adds a political-participation route. Women hold 27.5% of parliamentary seats
and 22.4% of cabinet posts globally, while only 28 countries are led by a woman
and 101 have never had a woman leader. Parliamentary gains are slow and women
Speakers fell to 19.9%; these are institutional aggregates, not proof of policy
influence.

The access-to-power conversion is also about safety and rules: 76% of surveyed
women parliamentarians reported public intimidation or violence versus 68% of
men, while IPU identifies well-designed and implemented quotas as a decisive
mechanism in several 2025 renewals. This is a bounded publication synthesis of
institutional counts and the cited parliamentarian survey, not a new microdata
analysis.

**Rotation finding:** political trends should be followed from electoral rules
and party selection through candidacy, representation, leadership, safety,
agenda control, policy response, and later trust or participation. The open
arrow is whether formal inclusion becomes safe, durable, and consequential
political power.

## Cultural production: digital reach is expanding faster than creator security

The [UNESCO Re|Shaping Policies for Creativity 2026 record](records/comparative-unesco-reshaping-policies-creativity-2026-cultural-economy-ai-mobility.json)
adds a cultural-production route. UNESCO reports that 85% of reporting countries
include cultural and creative industries in development plans, but only 56% set
specific cultural objectives. Cultural goods trade reached $254 billion in 2023,
yet developing countries accounted for just over 20% of cultural-services trade.
The route therefore connects culture to trade, state capacity, and unequal access
to digital markets—not only taste or identity.

Digital reach also changes the creator bargain: digital revenues rose to 35% of
creators’ income from 17% in 2018, while UNESCO projects 2028 revenue losses of
24% for music creators and 21% for audiovisual creators from generative-AI
outputs. These are publication indicators and projections, not realized
individual outcomes. Skills, mobility, and protection remain uneven: essential
digital skills are reported for 67% of people in developed countries versus 28%
in developing countries; only 38% of developed countries facilitate inward
artistic mobility from developing nations; and only 61% of countries maintain
independent artistic-freedom monitoring bodies.

**Rotation finding:** cultural trends should be followed from public recognition
and education through creation, licensing, platform distribution, mobility,
income, safety, audience access, and cultural participation. The open arrow is
whether digital expansion produces a broader creative public or concentrates
value and visibility in a smaller set of platforms, markets, and creators.

## Housing and place: urban access is a linked system, not a shelter count

The [UN-Habitat World Cities Report 2026 record](records/comparative-un-habitat-world-cities-2026-housing-displacement-climate-delivery.json)
adds a place-and-access route. UN-Habitat estimates that up to 3.4 billion
people lack secure, safe, and adequate housing, including more than 1 billion in
informal settlements and slums. The global housing deficit rose from 251 million
units in 2010 to 288 million in 2023, while an estimated 64 million people were
evicted between 2003 and 2023. These are global estimates, not a city-specific
rent series or individual household panel.

The mechanism is broader than shelter: affordability, displacement, informality,
resilience, sustainability, and liveability interact with transport, public
services, health, employment, public space, and local implementation. Climate
planning is beginning to acknowledge that route: UN-Habitat finds well-developed
urban content in 80% of 142 third-generation national climate plans, up from 49%
in the prior cycle, with housing and informal settlements a priority in nearly
half and concrete urban delivery mechanisms in roughly three-quarters.

**Rotation finding:** place trends should be followed from land and tenure
through rents, housing quality, transport, service proximity, climate exposure,
displacement, social networks, and local delivery. The open arrow is whether
national housing and climate commitments become usable, affordable, and durable
urban access for residents.

## Democracy under pressure: legitimacy depends on conversion into results

The [UNDP Democracies Under Pressure 2026 record](records/comparative-undp-democracy-development-lac-2026-institutions-trust-pressure.json)
adds a regional institutional route. UNDP describes Latin America and the
Caribbean as the most democratic developing region, with more than four in five
people living in democracy, but emphasizes that democratic access alone is not
the endpoint. Democracy, the State, and human development form a connected
triangle: political inequality, weak representation, and fragmented state
capacity can reinforce one another.

The pressure stack includes questioning of elections, weakened electoral
authorities, personalist concentration of power, weaker checks and balances,
polarization, changing information systems, organized crime, human mobility, and
the triple planetary crisis. These are aggregate institutional findings, not a
new analysis of the report’s optional public quiz or citizen responses.

**Rotation finding:** political legitimacy should be followed from electoral
integrity through representation, economic-power constraints, state control,
deliberation, institutional checks, public delivery, development, and trust. The
open arrow is whether formal democracy can turn pressure into renewal and public
promises into visible, legitimate results.

## Acute food crises: conflict and response capacity convert shocks into hunger

The [FAO-WFP Global Report on Food Crises 2026 record](records/comparative-fao-wfp-global-food-crises-2026-conflict-funding-displacement.json)
adds a crisis-escalation route distinct from the existing healthy-diet
affordability record. The 2026 assessment finds 266 million people in 47
countries and territories facing high acute food insecurity in 2025, or 22.9%
of the analyzed population; about 1.4 million faced the most severe level.
Acute hunger has approximately doubled over the past decade, and two famines
were confirmed in parts of Gaza and Sudan during 2025.

The mechanism is not food supply alone: conflict, displacement, economic shocks,
weather extremes, market disruption, access constraints, and financing gaps
concentrate risk. Ten countries account for roughly two-thirds of the assessed
acute-hunger population, while 35 contexts have appeared in every report for nine
years. Funding and data availability have weakened, increasing the importance of
prevention, agricultural livelihoods, early warning, negotiated access, and
resilience alongside emergency assistance. These are aggregate crisis
classifications and response signals, not individual consumption data.

**Rotation finding:** food-crisis trends should be followed from conflict and
climate shock through displacement, markets, livelihoods, acute food access,
nutrition, humanitarian reach, financing, and recovery capacity. The open arrow
is whether early action and resilient local systems can interrupt repeated crisis
cycles before acute hunger becomes famine.

## The generational economy: hidden care and transfers shape fiscal room

The [UN DESA generational-economy record](records/comparative-un-desa-generational-economy-2026-transfers-care-fiscal-capacity.json)
adds a life-course accounting route. UN DESA defines the generational economy as
the social institutions, markets, public transfers, time transfers, caregiving,
production, consumption, and savings through which people receive and provide
support over their lives. Conventional GDP does not fully reveal these flows.

Demographic change alters the balance: declining fertility and expanding
working-age populations can create a dividend when human-capital investment and
employment are sufficient, while ageing increases demand for health and
long-term care and pressures pensions and public finance. The measurement route
is therefore substantive, not merely statistical: National Transfer Accounts,
National Time Transfer Accounts, and National Inclusion Accounts can expose age,
time, care, and socioeconomic-status differences in resource flows.

**Rotation finding:** intergenerational trends should be followed from age
structure through education, paid work, unpaid care, taxes, transfers, pensions,
health, household support, gender, and public budgets. The open arrow is whether
better accounting becomes fairer care, durable fiscal planning, and stronger
social cohesion rather than a new way to pit generations against one another.

## AI markets: capability diffusion and firm power can move together

The [OECD Artificial Intelligence Markets 2026 record](records/comparative-oecd-ai-markets-2026-concentration-competition-strategic-advantage.json)
adds a firm-power route. OECD’s assessment is mixed: foundation-model segments
have shown dynamism over the past three years, while structural risks persist
across the value chain. AI deployment reshapes business performance, cost
structures, and strategic advantage, so the relevant endpoint is not simply
adoption but who can enter, switch, innovate, and capture value.

The concentration risk is especially visible in hardware and data, where
incumbent positions may become entrenched. This complements the World Bank’s
capability-and-dependency route: infrastructure and model access can expand
capacity while still leaving firms and governments dependent on bottleneck
providers. These are aggregate competition findings, not firm-level market-share
estimates or consumer microdata.

**Rotation finding:** AI trends should be followed from compute and data through
models, cloud infrastructure, applications, entry, interoperability, cost
structures, labor control, consumer choice, public procurement, and competition
enforcement. The open arrow is whether AI lowers capability barriers broadly or
converts technical bottlenecks into durable firm and geopolitical power.

## Climate and firms: heat is becoming a productivity, wage, and trade mechanism

The [World Bank MENAAP climate-risk record](records/comparative-world-bank-menaap-firms-climate-heat-2026-sales-productivity-trade.json)
adds a firm-and-worker climate route. Across the Middle East, North Africa,
Afghanistan, and Pakistan, roughly three in ten firms operate in extreme heat for
more than 100 days a year. The World Bank reports that 17 additional hot days are
associated with a 6% sales decline, a 4% decline in labor productivity, and an 8%
decline in wages in its firm-level analysis. These are study estimates, not
universal constants or household heat-stress outcomes.

The mechanism extends beyond local weather: smaller and financially constrained
firms face greater adaptation barriers, while EU and UK carbon-border measures
reshape export competitiveness, investment, and sector access. Adaptation can
also create a double dividend through finance, energy efficiency, green
production, and a projected $46 billion sustainable-cooling opportunity, though
cooling itself can increase energy demand and affordability pressure if it is not
efficient and broadly accessible.

**Rotation finding:** climate trends should be followed from temperature and
water exposure through firm size, finance, energy, work conditions, sales,
productivity, wages, trade rules, adaptation, and worker security. The open arrow
is whether climate policy distributes adaptation capacity or amplifies the gap
between firms and workers that can protect themselves and those that cannot.

## Fragility and poverty: shock exposure becomes durable exclusion through weak capacity

The [OECD States of Fragility 2025 record](records/comparative-oecd-states-fragility-2025-poverty-conflict-displacement.json)
adds a state-capacity route. OECD identifies 61 high- or extreme-fragility
contexts containing 25% of the world’s population but 72% of the world’s extreme
poor in 2024. By 2040, as much as 92% of the world’s extreme poor could live in
those contexts under the report’s projection. This is a context-level projection,
not a household forecast.

The mechanism is cumulative: organized violence, low growth, food insecurity,
forced displacement, gender inequality, weak institutions, limited FDI, and
service-delivery constraints reinforce one another. More than 100 million
refugees and internally displaced people originate in high- or extreme-fragility
contexts, while those contexts received 5% or less of global net FDI inflows in
2023. The relevant response is therefore not only crisis aid but institutional
recovery, social protection, gender-responsive services, peacebuilding, and
better data.

**Rotation finding:** fragility trends should be followed from political and
security shocks through state capacity, public services, markets, poverty,
displacement, gender exposure, financing, and recovery. The open arrow is whether
early institutional investment interrupts the poverty-fragility loop before
crisis concentration becomes a durable global geography.

## Spatial opportunity: place converts infrastructure into usable access

The [World Bank People, Jobs, and Places record](records/comparative-world-bank-people-jobs-places-2026-spatial-welfare-opportunity.json)
adds a place-to-opportunity route. The central signal is that national labor and
welfare averages can conceal persistent differences between neighborhoods,
rural and urban places, and people facing different transport, safety,
infrastructure, and hazard conditions. Access to jobs is therefore not only a
question of job supply or worker skills; it is also a question of where people
live and what surrounds them.

The practical mechanism runs through travel time, housing, local firms, services,
information, safety, migration, and mobility. World Bank GeoE3 work shows how
combining labor-market and geographic information can identify location-specific
constraints and support targeted employment interventions. That precision is
useful but not automatically just: spatial data can improve allocation while also
stigmatizing neighborhoods or enabling surveillance, so resident voice and data
rights remain part of the policy route.

**Rotation finding:** spatial trends should be followed from land and housing
through transport, travel time, safety, services, local firms, worker access,
migration, wages, and neighborhood governance. The open arrow is whether place-
sensitive policy expands usable opportunity or merely maps inequality more
precisely without changing it.

## Institutional justice: formal progress does not guarantee usable voice

The [UN SDG 16 2026 record](records/comparative-un-sdg16-2026-institutions-justice-information-voice.json)
adds a publication-level institutional route across violence, justice, public
information, human-rights oversight, and political representation. The report
shows a mixed pattern: violence indicators have improved in aggregate, while
progress remains uneven; access-to-information laws have spread but compliance
is inconsistent; bribery exposure is sharply unequal across income groups; and
the growth of fully compliant national human-rights institutions has slowed.

The cultural and political signal is that institutional form and institutional
use are different trend layers. A law, accreditation, or appointment can widen
formal capability without ensuring timely information, effective remedy,
independent oversight, or influence over high-consequence decisions. Women and
young people remain underrepresented in influential roles, especially as
conflict, climate, economic insecurity, and technological disruption reshape
public choices.

**Rotation finding:** follow institutional change from legal adoption through
enforcement, public-service access, civic space, representation, trust, and
lived remedy. The open arrow is whether formal institutional expansion becomes
usable voice and protection for people facing unequal exposure, rather than
remaining a paper capability.

## Creative labor: cultural participation depends on work conditions

The [UNESCO creative-industries skills and employment record](records/comparative-unesco-creative-industries-skills-employment-2026.json)
adds a culture-to-work route. The publication treats music, fashion, film,
heritage crafts, and related fields as economically important but labor-market
fragile: 40 percent of workers are reported to earn below their national
minimum wage, while informality, precarity, gender disparities, curriculum lag,
and weak measurement limit who can sustain creative work.

The mechanism is broader than training alone. Changing tools and industry needs
raise the value of adaptable skills, but fragmented policy, outdated
qualifications, teacher shortages, and uneven access to learning can shift the
cost of adaptation onto workers. Microcredentials, intergenerational learning,
and public-private partnerships may widen entry, yet they do not automatically
improve pay, bargaining power, protections, or control over distribution.

**Rotation finding:** follow cultural production from training and digital
infrastructure through pay, informality, gender, platform dependence, labor
rights, and cultural participation. The open arrow is whether skills investment
creates sustainable creative livelihoods or simply supplies more precarious
labor to expanding cultural markets.

## Poverty and protection: the consumption floor is exposed to compound shocks

The [UN SDG 1 2026 record](records/comparative-un-sdg1-2026-poverty-social-protection-cost-shocks.json)
adds a current poverty-and-consumption route. The UN estimates that about 826
million people, or 10 percent of the world population, remain below the $3.00
per-day extreme-poverty line in 2026. Social assistance has expanded, reaching
37.3 percent of vulnerable people, but coverage is incomplete; at the same
time, poverty-reduction aid grants fell 8.7 percent from 2023 to 2024.

The mechanism is a buffer problem rather than a single-income problem. Rising
living costs, conflict, climate shocks, inequality, and weak labor-market
opportunity can push households below a consumption floor, while transfers,
public services, work, remittances, and savings may protect recovery. Coverage
statistics therefore need to be followed through adequacy, timing, take-up,
service access, and the ability to rebuild after a shock.

**Rotation finding:** follow poverty from prices and work through transfers,
care, public services, climate and conflict exposure, household buffers, and
recovery. The open arrow is whether social protection converts formal coverage
into durable consumption security when multiple shocks arrive together.

## Drug markets and public health: harm follows access and institutional pathways

The [UNODC World Drug Report 2026 record](records/comparative-unodc-world-drug-report-2026-markets-harm-treatment.json)
adds a social-behavior and institutional-response route. The report shows a
wide market footprint—cannabis cultivation is reported in at least 155
countries—and a sharp expansion in reported coca cultivation, while drug-related
deaths continue to rise in total even as population-adjusted growth is slower.
Opioids remain the most common substance in direct drug-related deaths in the
available toxicology data, with cocaine ranking second.

The key social trend is not prevalence alone. Treatment coverage remains low
and varies sharply by region; referral routes and intervention types differ,
with criminal-justice systems playing a larger role in some regions and
self/family referral more common in others. This connects market structure to
stigma, care access, institutional trust, public safety, work disruption, and
community harm. Limited reporting also means that the most visible data may
overrepresent people who reach formal services.

**Rotation finding:** follow drug-market change from production and trafficking
through exposure, mortality, stigma, referral, treatment, recovery, and local
institutional capacity. The open arrow is whether public-health systems reduce
harm equitably or whether unequal access and enforcement pathways deepen it.

## Platform attention: convenience is becoming the gateway to civic interpretation

The [Reuters Institute Digital News Report 2026 record](records/comparative-reuters-digital-news-report-2026-platform-attention-trust-action.json)
adds a missing encounter-to-interpretation route. Across 48 markets, social
media and video networks became the most widely used route to online news, used
by 54 percent of respondents, ahead of news organisations' own sites and apps.
The shift is not only substitution: 12 percent now use only social/video
networks for news, casual or passive users have grown, and younger audiences
are especially platform-first.

The important mechanism is the convenience-trust tension. Overall trust in news
fell to 37 percent, while trust in news encountered on social media and through
AI chatbots was lower; concern about fake or misleading news reached 62 percent.
People may therefore rely on a convenient default channel while treating it as
less reliable, creating an open middle between exposure, interpretation,
discussion, and action. The report does not establish that platform use causes
polarization or political behavior, which remains the next empirical bridge.

**Rotation finding:** follow platform exposure through attention, source
selection, trust, misinformation interpretation, identity, discussion, and
collective action. The open arrow is whether platform convenience expands civic
access or produces a thinner, more distrustful information environment.

## Time poverty: care is a hidden allocation system for work and participation

The [UN Women time-use and social-inequality record](records/comparative-un-women-time-use-social-inequality-2026.json)
adds the missing social-reproduction route. Across ten countries, the report
uses harmonized time-use measures to show how paid work, unpaid domestic and
care work, leisure, mobility, multitasking, and well-being combine into social
and economic inequality. Its central contribution is to make time poverty a
measurable dimension rather than treating time as an unobserved personal
constraint.

The mechanism runs through household composition and care continuity. Living
with children is identified as a key driver of time inequality, while
multitasking and mobility reveal pressures conventional employment statistics
miss. Those pressures can reduce paid-work access, rest, social participation,
and political voice, but the same time pattern can also reflect support,
meaning, and chosen family arrangements. Better indicators expose the route;
services, leave, schedules, and responsibility-sharing determine whether it
changes.

**Rotation finding:** follow care from household composition and unpaid time
through work schedules, services, mobility, rest, social participation, and
well-being. The open arrow is whether care policy redistributes time and
continuity or simply records unequal burdens more precisely.

## Digital consumer agency: transparency is not the same as usable choice

The [OECD digital-markets consumer-policy record](records/comparative-oecd-digital-markets-consumer-agency-enforcement-2026.json)
adds the consumer-power middle between market conduct and effective recourse.
The OECD describes personalized pricing, recommendation systems, data-driven
targeting, interface design, drip pricing, tying, and AI-generated deceptive
claims as practices that can implicate both competition and consumer law.

The central mechanism is consumer agency: understanding alternatives, making an
informed choice, acting on preferences, and switching when appropriate. A
market can provide nominal options while algorithms, lock-in, information
asymmetry, or weak remedies make those options difficult to use. The same
conduct can affect consumer welfare, privacy, trust, entry, innovation, and
market power, which is why the report emphasizes coordination between
competition and consumer authorities while preserving distinct mandates.

**Rotation finding:** follow digital conduct from interface and pricing through
understanding, comparison, switching, complaint, remedy, and market response.
The open arrow is whether consumer-protection capacity produces real agency or
merely more disclosure inside increasingly powerful intermediaries.

## Civic space and global rules: state power changes the cost of participation

The [CIVICUS State of Civil Society 2026 record](records/comparative-civicus-state-of-civil-society-2026-civic-space-global-governance.json)
adds a geopolitical/state-response route. Its central claim is that civic
space, multilateral rules, and technology governance are being contested at the
same time: activists and journalists face rising costs of dissent, civil-society
funding is under pressure, powerful states weaken or selectively use global
institutions, and AI creates new surveillance, disinformation, military, and
gendered-abuse risks.

The report also documents the counterforce. Civil society uses litigation,
fact-checking, early-warning systems, secure platforms, community
documentation, and decentralized youth organizing to contest state and
corporate power. This is an external-response mechanism, not just an attitude
endpoint: whether rules are accepted, enforced, resisted, or bypassed depends
on civic capacity, resources, technology, institutional trust, and the ability
to build coalitions across borders.

Because CIVICUS is an advocacy organization, the record preserves its
normative standpoint and does not treat the report as a neutral causal index.
The open arrow is whether adaptive resistance can convert civic participation
into durable accountability when states and firms control more of the legal,
financial, and technological environment.

## Online literacy: confidence, attention, and ability are separating

The [Ofcom adult media-use record](records/comparative-ofcom-adults-media-use-attention-literacy-2026.json)
adds a platform middle beyond reach and news choice. In the UK, overall online
confidence is high, but confidence in judging accuracy and recognizing
advertising exceeds performance on some scenario tests. Social participation is
also becoming more passive: posting, sharing, or commenting has declined, while
read-only use and reduced exploration of new sites remain substantial.

The consumer and social signal is a divergence between access and meaningful
control. People can be online yet rely on one device, lack confidence, miss
scams or sponsored content, spend more time than intended, or experience the
social environment as unkind and mentally costly. At the same time, online
access can broaden understanding, support learning, connect people, and enable
public-service or civic action. The relevant trend is therefore not simply
screen time; it is how platform design, skills, household resources, and
institutional literacy shape the quality of participation.

**Rotation finding:** follow online use from access and exposure through
attention, recognition, safety behavior, consumer choice, well-being, and
public digital-literacy response. The open arrow is whether literacy and safety
capacity convert platform access into agency rather than passive, unequal, or
harmful participation.

## Socioeconomic voice: trust is shaped by whether institutions feel reachable

The [OECD trust and political-agency record](records/comparative-oecd-trust-public-institutions-2026-agency-socioeconomic-ai.json)
adds a public-institutions middle route. The 2026 OECD report links trust gaps
to partisan alignment, financial concerns, perceived discrimination, education,
interpersonal trust, and whether people feel that people like them have a say in
government decisions. In the comparable survey waves, the average partisan gap
in trust in national civil services widened from 10 percentage points in 2021
to 15 points in 2025.

The mechanism is reachability and agency, not attitude alone. Groups with less
material security or weaker perceived representation may trust institutions less
and participate less, making it harder for policy to reach them and for public
agencies to learn from their experience. The same route now extends to
public-sector AI: automated services can improve consistency or access, but
skepticism, transparency, human oversight, and recourse determine whether they
enhance or erode legitimacy.

**Rotation finding:** follow socioeconomic exposure through service encounters,
perceived voice, participation, responsiveness, AI-mediated administration, and
institutional trust. The open arrow is whether public systems convert formal
access into felt influence and credible fair treatment across groups.

## Gender parity and the next economy: representation does not automatically become power

The [WEF Global Gender Gap 2026 record](records/comparative-wef-global-gender-gap-2026-leadership-ai-capital.json)
adds a cross-cutting gender-and-transition route. The index finds the global
gender gap 69.2 percent closed in 2026, but the remaining gaps are concentrated
in economic participation and opportunity and especially political empowerment.
Professional and technical representation is substantially closer to parity than
the representation of legislators, senior officials, and managers.

The forward-looking mechanism runs through the jobs and capital shaping the next
economy. Across 57 economies, women’s representation falls from 46 percent at
entry level to 23 percent at the C-suite. The report also tracks women’s
participation in AI-related roles and financial markets, where growth can remain
concentrated in less technical or lower-paid segments. This links care,
training, hiring, promotion, capital allocation, technology, and political
voice rather than treating a headline parity score as equal power.

**Rotation finding:** follow gender gaps from care and education through skills,
technical work, hiring, leadership, capital, AI transition, and political
representation. The open arrow is whether new growth sectors widen agency and
mobility or reproduce old hierarchies inside new occupations and institutions.

## Child platform life: exposure becomes a time, safety, and consumer system

The [Ofcom Online Nation 2025 record](records/comparative-ofcom-online-nation-2025-children-platform-time-safety-consumption.json)
adds a child-focused platform route. UK children aged 8-14 spend almost three
hours online per day on average, with YouTube and Snapchat together accounting
for a large share of time; a material portion of use occurs late at night. The
same environment supports friendship, schoolwork, well-being, and learning,
while creating questions about sleep, family routines, attention, and safety.

The route also reaches action and consumption. Ofcom reports that many children
encounter harmful content and that a substantial share take action through
reporting, blocking, dislike tools, or telling an adult. Children also spend
money across games and social platforms, often after prompts from customization,
advertising, recommendations, or influencers, with notable regret and
uncertainty about what was purchased. These findings connect platform design to
care responsibilities, consumer agency, safety governance, and children’s own
capacity to respond.

**Rotation finding:** follow child platform use from exposure and time through
family routines, harmful-content response, digital literacy, spending, remedy,
and agency. The open arrow is whether safety and consumer systems protect
children without removing useful connection, learning, and participation.

## Gender inequality across systems: exposure, access, and power compound

The [UN Women and UN DESA Gender Snapshot 2025 record](records/comparative-un-women-desa-gender-snapshot-2025-poverty-conflict-digital-power.json)
adds an SDG-wide route that complements the WEF parity index. It links the
gendered distribution of poverty, conflict exposure, food insecurity, digital
access, unpaid care, work, rights, and institutional decision-making. The report
projects 351 million women and girls could remain in extreme poverty in 2030 if
current trends continue, while a closed gender digital divide is modeled as a
potential source of broad economic and poverty gains.

The key mechanism is compounding exposure rather than a single gap. Conflict,
food insecurity, care burdens, weak services, and limited digital access can
reduce time, income, safety, information, and voice simultaneously. Formal
commitments or representation can therefore coexist with weak implementation
and limited lived power. The source is a global indicator and scenario
synthesis, not a respondent-level explanation of individual outcomes.

**Rotation finding:** follow gendered exposure from poverty, conflict, food,
care, and digital infrastructure through work, services, representation,
financing, and implementation. The open arrow is whether cross-sector policy
capacity converts formal equality commitments into durable access and power.

## Risk perception: geopolitical shocks are being read through social fragility

The [WEF Global Risks Report 2026 record](records/comparative-wef-global-risks-2026-geoeconomic-polarization-disinformation.json)
adds an institutional risk-framing route. In the report's expert survey,
geoeconomic confrontation and state-based armed conflict together account for
nearly one-third of selections for the leading material global crisis risk in
2026. Geoeconomic confrontation also ranks first over the two-year horizon,
while misinformation and disinformation remain near the top.

The broader signal is convergence: the report places inequality, economic
downturn, polarization, trust, disinformation, technology, climate, and
geostrategic change in one interacting risk landscape. This is not a forecast
or an objective probability estimate. It is evidence about how expert and
business institutions are prioritizing threats and linking them to social
stability, markets, public legitimacy, and state capacity.

**Rotation finding:** follow perceived geopolitical risk from conflict and
trade confrontation through prices, supply chains, information, polarization,
institutional trust, and preparedness. The open arrow is whether risk framing
improves coordinated prevention or reinforces narrow elite threat narratives.

## Financial access: mobile connectivity is becoming part of household resilience

The [World Bank Global Findex 2025 record](records/comparative-world-bank-global-findex-2025-connectivity-financial-resilience.json)
adds a consumer and infrastructure route. The database links mobile-phone and
internet access to account ownership, digital merchant payments, mobile-money
saving, borrowing, digital safety, and financial resilience across 141
economies. Account ownership and digital use are expanding, but gaps remain by
gender, income, phone ownership, and geography.

The mechanism is not “technology equals inclusion.” A phone and account can
reduce payment friction or create a saving channel, yet effective control also
depends on affordability, identity, literacy, privacy, safety, product quality,
and recourse. Financial resilience remains connected to employment, transfers,
health costs, social networks, and public systems. The source is survey-based
aggregate evidence, not transaction-level behavior or causal welfare analysis.

**Rotation finding:** follow connectivity from device and account access through
payments, saving, borrowing, safety, gendered control, consumer protection, and
shock response. The open arrow is whether digital finance becomes durable
household room or simply shifts risk and dependency into new infrastructures.

## Social justice: formal protection and lived power remain separate

The [ILO State of Social Justice 2025 record](records/comparative-ilo-state-social-justice-2025-rights-informality-transitions.json)
adds a rights-to-lived-outcomes route. The report places declining progress in
social justice alongside persistent poverty, wealth concentration, informality,
weakening collective-bargaining rights, and a gap between legal protections and
workers’ reported experiences of exclusion or neglect. It also frames climate,
technology, and demographic change as transitions whose distributional effects
must be governed rather than assumed to be fair.

The mechanism is institutional reach and bargaining power. Growth or formal
rights can coexist with insecure work, weak voice, and low trust if enforcement,
representation, and social protection do not travel into everyday employment
and transition decisions. The report’s interview evidence is treated as a
qualitative publication synthesis, not as respondent-level prevalence or a
causal worker panel.

**Rotation finding:** follow social-justice commitments from poverty and legal
rights through informality, bargaining, trust, services, and fair-transition
implementation. The open arrow is whether labour and social institutions adapt
quickly enough to distribute new risks and opportunities credibly.

## Democracy under conflict pressure: institutional erosion becomes a security route

The [International IDEA Global State of Democracy 2026 record](records/comparative-idea-global-state-democracy-2026-conflict-rights-rule-of-law.json)
adds a democracy-to-conflict route. Across 173 countries, the report finds that
57 percent declined in at least one key democratic indicator between 2020 and
2025, with especially broad deterioration in Rule of Law, Rights, credible
elections, legislative effectiveness, expression, press freedom, and access to
justice. It also examines how partial democratic procedures without real
competition or enforceable rights can coexist with heightened conflict risk.

The mechanism is institutional, not simply attitudinal. Weakened checks,
unreliable elections, restricted expression, and poor access to justice can
reduce peaceful channels for contestation while concentrating executive power.
But the report also identifies fragile improvements through transitions and
civil-society mobilization. The conflict relationship is treated as comparative
association, not an individual-level causal forecast.

**Rotation finding:** follow institutional decline from representation and
rights through trust, civic participation, executive constraint, conflict risk,
and international security. The open arrow is whether democratic renewal and
civic capacity can restore nonviolent channels before institutional erosion
hardens into security crisis.

## Freedom and rights: democratic status is also lived civic space

The [Freedom House Freedom in the World 2026 record](records/comparative-freedom-house-freedom-world-2026-political-rights-civil-liberties.json)
adds an independent political-rights and civil-liberties lens to the IDEA
democracy assessment. Freedom House reports a twentieth consecutive year of
global freedom decline in 2025: 54 countries deteriorated and 35 improved. Its
longitudinal framing highlights armed conflict, coups, institutional erosion,
and authoritarian crackdowns as recurring pathways affecting media freedom,
expression, due process, and civic space.

The cross-source signal is not simply that democracy scores fall. Rights and
freedoms shape whether people can contest power, organize, access information,
seek remedy, and reverse institutional deterioration. The assessment also
records resilience: most countries rated Free in 2005 remained Free, and some
Partly Free countries improved through competitive elections, judicial
independence, and stronger rule of law. This remains an expert-coded country
index, not respondent-level rights histories or causal repression evidence.

**Rotation finding:** follow political status from formal classification through
expression, media, due process, elections, civic organization, conflict, and
reform resilience. The open arrow is whether institutional safeguards create
real room for civic action before rights erosion becomes normalized.

## Planetary health: environmental risk becomes a production, consumption, and justice problem

The [UNEP Global Environment Outlook 7 record](records/comparative-unep-global-environment-outlook-7-climate-nature-pollution-consumption.json)
adds a systems-level climate and environmental route. GEO-7 links climate,
nature and biodiversity loss, land degradation, pollution, food, energy,
materials, finance, health, poverty, and economic development rather than
treating them as separate issue silos. Its transformation scenarios project
large long-run gains, but also emphasize up-front costs and difficult choices.

The cultural and consumer mechanism matters: behavior, production, lifestyles,
Indigenous and Local Knowledge, procurement, infrastructure, and finance all
shape environmental outcomes. Individual “green choices” cannot substitute for
changes in prices, systems, corporate incentives, public investment, and
distributional protection. The source is a scientific assessment and scenario
framework, not household behavior data or a deterministic forecast.

**Rotation finding:** follow environmental pressure from energy, food, land,
materials, and consumption through health, poverty, culture, infrastructure,
finance, and public governance. The open arrow is whether whole-system policy
can convert planetary risk into equitable transformation rather than shifting
costs onto exposed households and communities.

## Biodiversity and livelihoods: protection expands while ecological security erodes

The [UN SDG 15 2026 record](records/comparative-un-sdg15-2026-biodiversity-forest-livelihoods-governance.json)
adds a more specific biodiversity-to-livelihoods route beneath GEO-7’s broader
planetary-health frame. The SDG assessment reports expanding biodiversity
targets, environmental accounting, and protected-area coverage, while more than
48,600 species face extinction, forests continue to shrink, and coral risk has
risen. These changes reach people through fisheries, tourism, coastal
protection, food systems, water cycles, climate buffering, and cultural value.

The open issue is implementation and distribution. A law, target, accounting
system, or protected-area designation can improve governance without ensuring
effective stewardship, local consent, ecological connectivity, or livelihood
security. Conversely, community management and restoration can create gains
that global indicators register slowly. This is aggregate SDG evidence, not
individual livelihood histories or local causal ecological analysis.

**Rotation finding:** follow biodiversity loss from species, forests, reefs, and
key sites through food, water, work, tourism, culture, coastal safety, and
conservation governance. The open arrow is whether expanding formal protection
becomes effective, locally legitimate ecosystem security.
